". . . the use of the dying god as a scapegoat to free his wor-
shipers from the troubles. . . . The accumulated misfortunes and
sins of a whole people are sometimes laid upon the dying god.
palming off on someone else the trouble which a man shrinks
from bearing himself. . . the custom of sacrificing the son for
•
the father was common, if not universal, among Semitic peoples.
... All over Western Asia from time immemorial the mournful
death and happy resurrection of a divine being appear to have
been annually celebrated."-Frazer

I
His father was a Greek named Pamphilus, who, before he was
taken by pirates and sold as a slave in Rome, was a schoolmaster at
Antioch. His mother Levana was a Roman of noble birth, but
the mad emperor, Caligula, destroyed her male relatives and con-
fiscated their property, leaving the women in beggary. She was
born in the twentieth year of the emperor Tiberius and was actu-
ally a beggar when Pamphilus, then a slave, met her in the eighth
year of the emperor Claudius. Damon, their only child, was born
in the ninth year of Claudius, and so was fourteen years old in
the year of the great fire which destroyed most of Rome, and fixed
in him that obsessive interest in the Christians which was to en-
dure throughout his life.
His father's master, one Valerius, a distant relative of the
Claudian line and a very wealthy man, was invited by Nero to a
banquet, at his seaside villa in Antium, the day before the fire;
and Damon went along as one of the servants, for at great feasts it
was a custom with rich men to have slaves in attendance on them,
to wash their feet, to fan them, to sing to them, and in other ways
to minister to their whims. Valerius had been depressed and
anxious for many weeks, having heard that Nero intended to
trump up a charge of treason against him and confiscate his hold-
ings. Valerius had told Pamphilus that he would give him his
freedom and settle on him a substantial inheritance. He was fond
of Pamphilus, who had become his tutor in rhetoric and philos-
ophy, and his close companion. On receiving Nero's invitation
Valerius said, "They always invite one to a feast, Pamphilus, be-
fore sending a messenger to give one his choice of the forms of
suicide. I shall eat in preparation for my death." Pamphilus had
9
made no reply. For one familiar with the caprices and tyrannies
of the emperors there was nothing to be said. It was widely known
that Nero was short of funds. An emperor who, when he jour-
neyed, had a thousand mules shod with solid silver, hundreds of
retainers robed in the finest scarlet, and a procession that stretched
out for miles, was usually in need of money.
They arrived early in the afternoon at Nero's magnificent villa.
Damon had been in attendance with his master at some very
sumptuous affairs but he had never been in the emperor's palace
or villa. The time was the 19th of July in the tenth year of Nero's
reign.
Damon's first impression was of huge gardens drenched with
costly scents; his second, of a baldheaded senator, being borne
away by his servants, in a handsome red sedan. He thought the
man was ill. His father told him later that the man had been
taken away to a chamber to be bathed with scented waters and
rubbed with unguents. His father said the perfumes used at feasts
by the more prodigal emperors, or sometimes by patricians of great
wealth, were so fantastic in their cost that guests were expected
to contribute to them. Passions, Damon had learned, were so un-
confined that the host or one of his aides usually challenged at
the door any guest whose face was sad or cynical or sickly; for no
one with an unhappy mind or a weak stomach had any business at
a Roman feast. Indeed, the guests were usually given a promulsis
to strengthen their stomachs: the horrible concoction, Damon's
father said, was made of boiled honey, sweet wines and strong
spices. If a guest sickened on this he was hustled away to a pile
of raw lettuce and cooling herbs to settle his vomit.
Sitting alone together, beyond hearing, Damon's father had
spent hours telling him about the Romans. Since Augustus, Pam-
philus said, all the emperors had been prodigious gluttons. Ti-
berius had sat at a feast and eaten for two days and nights without
rising. Caligula had been worse, Nero was certainly no better.
Nero, whom Damon had seen at chariot races or when singing
in the theaters, was a man under average height, powerfully built
but corpulent. He had blue eyes touched with madness, pale red
hair, a freckled florid face. His neck was short and thick, his hands
heavy and gross and sprinkled with large freckless. He was very
nearsighted, a condition that gave him an air of morbid doubt
and suspicion. The whole world knew that he fancied himself as
the greatest singer alive, a delusion which the sycophants around
10
him did much to nourish. He also fancied himself as a musician
and an actor without peer, and as an athlete of extraordinary
prowess. But those who knew him best, Pamphilus said, thought
him thick-witted, arrogant, self-deceived and monstrously vain;
but they admitted that he was popular with the common people.
A little drunk, when Damon and his father arrived with Va-
lerius, Nero was singing in a very loud voice; and all the hypocrites
whose lives depended on his whims were fawning and applauding,
while in actual truth, Damon was to learn later with astonishment,
plotting his death. A quiet, rather neurotic and thoughtful child,
Damon in his unobtrusive way observed many things this evening:
he was fascinated by a few of the guests-by Petronius because of
his contemptuous silence and elegant sneers; Seneca, because of
his fluent talk; Plautius, because of his incredible gluttony; and
the beautiful Murdia because, though a Christian like his own
mother, she seemed to be wanton. Damon was dressed in livery
and had many tasks but he also had time, as the party progressed
into deeper drunkenness, to look round him and to listen. He had
seen feasts but never so much food or so many kinds. As the vari-
ous courses were brought on the nature of them was proclaimed
by the master of the banquet, who drooled from his tongue and
leered like a pimp. The first course, the gustatio or appetizer, in-
cluded oysters, eggs, mushrooms, all saturated with a sauce of
sweet wine mixed with heavy amber honey. Among the tidbits
were tongues of flamingoes and other birds, the flesh of ostrich
wings, breast of dove and thrush, livers of geese, and a concoction
of sow-livers, teats and vulva in a thick syrup of figs. Of the main
dishes he managed to taste chicken covered deep with a sauce of
anise seed, mint, lazerroot, vinegar, dates, the juices of salted fish-
guts, oil and mustard seed. There were many kinds of fish, all
heavily spiced, rich and dripping; thrush on asparagus; a pastry
of the brains of small birds; sows' udder floating in a thick jelly
that smelled of coriander; roast venison, pig, fowl and hare; and
innumerable sausages and pickled or spiced meats. A dish in
great demand was a jelly of sow-livers that had been fattened on
figs, an invention of Apicius, a famous gourmet under Tiberius.
When the tables were wiped clean for new courses, the servants,
including Damon, were expected to move in dance-rhythms and
to sing. He moved his lips but he made no sound.
Of wines, Valerius had told them while riding over, Nero would
have only the choicest: old crusted Alban and Setine; the famous
11
Falernian vintage of Opimius, said to be a hundred years old; and
Chian. Damon managed to taste them all. They were all very
sweet. They were not, it seemed to him, the kind that would mix
happily with sauces drenched with oils and with hot spices. He
knew that the mansions of the wealthy had vomit-chambers, to
which guests, sickened by eating, could withdraw to empty their
stomachs, and so eat again. Aids were there, including long
feathers with which to tickle the throat, and various emetics of
mustard, salt and nauseous herbs. Servants were there to assist
the glutton, cleanse his face and clothes, and give him cooling
drinks or gentle herbs to settle his stomach, or medicines to pro-
mote his appetite and digestion.
Damon now and then stole near to observe Plautius, whose
gluttony was lengendary. It was the Roman way when eating to
lie belly downward on a couch, and reach out for the food heaped
on tables. When sated, the diner turned to his left side. For what
seemed to Damon a long time Plautius fed ravenously with both
hands, assisted by slave-boys whose long hair he used as napkins
to wipe his dripping fingers. In a moment when Damon was
staring at him he suddenly rolled over and sat up. He leaned for-
ward, his elbows across his knees, his bulging eyes looking hor-
rified and expectant. He started to rise but at once vomited, pour-
ing out of his outraged stomach oysters stewed in garum, fig-
peckers, African snails, sow-udder, mussels, Lybian truffles, honey,
sauces, pastries, wines, fruits. Nobody but Damon thought any-
thing of it. Vomiting was the surest possible way to show approval
of a cook's art and a host's hospitality. Slaves, including Damon,
hastened over to clean up the mess; and the huge fellow staggered
off, clutching his guts with both hands. A little later he returned,
looking cleansed and fit, and began to eat, choosing first an anti-
dote against poison, which was furnished to all guests, and then a
sweet thick wine mixed with boiled honey and heavy cream. He
must have drunk a pint of this when, smacking his lips, he looked
over the tables and chose sorrel and peppers. These were intended
to keep his stomach from heaving. But presently, Damon ob-
served, his own stomach turning sick, the man was devouring
rich hot meat dishes, raw brains in jelly, a paste made of camel-
heels, and huge quantities of Falernian. After a few minutes he
sat up again and stared down like a man contemplating the treach-
eries of his stomach. How many times this monstrous glutton
ate and disgorged Damon was never to know, for his attention
12
was drawn to other matters. Hours later a few of the guests,
deathly sick, were laid out on couches, with slaves fanning them.
Damon fanned one of them until the man fell asleep, when he
went over to hear what Seneca was saying.
Seneca, Damon had heard Valerius say with contempt, pre-
tended to believe in an austere life of self-denial but had never
practiced what he preached. He taught against the accumulation
of wealth but was himself among the richest men in the world;
advocated a brotherhood of man but lent money at usurious rates;
proclaimed that ambition ought to be restrained but aspired to
be emperor of Rome; and in still other ways was an odious ex-
ample to those whom he exhorted.
Damon saw a short man of Spanish extraction, with a great
bald head, a grossly fleshy neck and jowls, and a short pointed
beard that was gray. His speaking voice was not pleasant; it was
nasally resonant and became shrill when he was agitated. His
hands, with which he gestured to silence a disputant, were heavy
and abnormally hairy; and his mouth back in his beard had,
Damon thought, a number of decayed teeth.
It was Nero's whim to invite to his feasts actors, singers, philos-
ophers, physicians, as well as base fops with the stain of low birth
upon them. They reeked of unguents and hovered attentively
on the words of the powerful. Among these sycophants was an
exquisite dandy named Regulus, who had painted a dark circle
round his right eye and covered a part of his forehead and one
cheek with patches; for this was then the fashion with fops.
As Damon moved within hearing he heard a statement and
glanced quickly at his father. If God, Seneca said, had given
man a rational nature, then nothing was more becoming to man
than reason. He elaborated on this by saying that reason could be
improved with study and the pursuit of truth, that philosophy
purified a man of prejudice and superstition, promoting virtue and
self-confidence. He said he was not yet wise, nor would ever be.
"Do not ask me to be equal to the best, but rather to be better
than the base. That is enough for me-daily to take away some-
thing from my faults, and rebuke my errors." Then he made a
statement that caused Damon's father to reach down and clasp his
son's hand. "There is an old proverb," said Seneca. "If one
should tell you that he has sought and found, do not believe him;
but believe him who tells you that he found without seeking."
Why, after Seneca had uttered those words, had his father
13
squeezed his hand? Damon thought he knew but he was not sure.
Regulus interrupted to ask: "What do you think of the Friends?”
He meant the Christians.
Seneca spoke with bitterness tinctured with contempt. He had
heard of these people and he had seen a few of them; he thought
them stupid, ignorant and contemptible. They were dangerous
to the Empire because they would dissolve the ties of custom, home
and friendship, desecrate religious institutions, encourage hatred
of mankind. They despised life, they projected their whole in-
terest into a mythical future. The doings of the wretches were il-
legal, for did they not live in secret societies? In fact, did they
not imitate the rites of the mystery cults, the better to deceive
the unwary and prey on the ignorant? He looked round him and
said: "I have heard that some of them eat human flesh."
Because his mother was a Christian Damon was listening with
all his ears.
"Great philosopher," said Regulus, impudently baiting him,
"they believe in a brotherhood of man. My wife is one." He
turned to a group that was watching naked slave-girls dance.
"Murdia, come here!"
There came toward Regulus a very beautiful woman. She
was giddy, for she had been drinking too much; and she moved
with the air of a woman who viewed with cynical indulgence her
baser passions.
"You're a Friend," said Regulus. "Isn't it true that you peo-
ple believe in a brotherhood of man?" He turned to Seneca. "You
Stoics profess to believe it. You and the Friends seem to have
much in common."
Seneca growled and for a few moments seemed to be listening
to an inner voice. "Who are these people?" he said at last. "Beg-
gars, slaves, the rabble and chaff, the vulgar outcasts, illiterate,
ignoble, barbarous. Is there virtue in them? Is there love of
truth? Do the fools really believe in the immortality of their
flesh and bones?"
"You say slaves?" cried Murdia and poked at him with her
fan. "I'm no slave but to love. Do you know that one of the
noblest women present tonight is a Friend?"
"Who could that be?" asked Seneca, looking round him.
"Equality and brotherhood," said Regulus, pressing his point.
"That's a Stoic doctrine, even though," he added with soft in-
solence, "it is seldom observed."
14
Seneca turned his dark sad eyes on Regulus. "Can the stupid
ever be equal with the intelligent, the cowards with the brave?
Who but imbeciles believe in equality? As for brotherhood,
Cicero said that nature inclines us to love men, that such love is
the foundation of law. Men, he said, are born to help one an-
other-"
"That's all very pompous and empty," said Regulus, leering
round at his circle of intimates. "But is that what you practice?
Would you go among this rabble and chaff and call them
brothers?"
"Yes, would you?" asked Murdia, breathing in his face. "No,
you wouldn't!"
Seneca fixed her with his unhappy eyes. “I observe your costly
raiment," he said. "Your speech is not that of the rabble. Why,
then, are you one of these people who abhor art because their dull
minds think it idolatrous? They despise life—”
"There's another life!" Murdia cried at him. “It isn't this cor-
rupt life of flesh, no, but life in Christ and the Kingdom; and the
Kingdom, sir, will come any day now! There is one who came and
will come again, to right wrongs, to destroy evil, to punish the
wicked and give fellowship to those cast out, peace to the whole
earth!"
Seneca rumbled, as with incredulity. "Peace to the whole earth,
did you say?"
"Peace!"
"Dear woman, you use the word christ but do you know its
meaning? It is a common name over the whole earth for both
slaves and freedmen. Greek, it means the anointed one and over
in Antioch these people are called Christian, after the word christ,
but the Antiochans use the term in reproach and contempt. These
people-'
""
"We do not call ourselves Christians," Murdia said, her dark
eyes despising him.
"Why not?-if you believe in a mythical Christ? Here in Rome
you call yourselves Friends. In other cities, I have heard, these
people call themselves Disciples or Brethren or Saints or the
Elect, or even the New Israel."
"There is another life," she said.
"The woman is mad," he said, looking round him.
At this moment Damon was called away and heard no more. It
was also a whim with Nero, as it had been with some of the em-
15
perors before him, to seek still baser pleasures after a great feast.
Sometimes he and his friends would cover their heads with the
mantles of freedmen and prowl through the dark streets of a city,
to insult the highborn and strip off their cloaks; to get into brawls
with drunken roisterers; to enter the lowest dens of vice and make
merry with harlots. Or, he had heard Valerius say, they would
go to the public baths, some of which were little more than huge
lupanars: on the corridors were private chambers in which men
and women skilled in the erotic arts tried with caresses and bland-
ishments to beget fresh sensations upon the old.
Nero and his friends, Damon soon made out, were preparing
for such a foray. They were drenching themselves with perfumes
and unguents, and with wine, which they poured over one another
like water. Damon's master, who despised such vulgar excursions,
intended if possible to slip away and had called Damon to tell
him to stand at readiness. But Nero's foray this night was not
to be.
There was suddenly a wild outcry. Damon slipped over to his
father to learn what the trouble was. A man named Cornelius,
who fancied himself assimilated to Jupiter, never had sexual in-
tercourse save during thunderstorms. Some noise, possibly a clap
of thunder, had just now plunged him into a dreadful frenzy; for
he lived under the delusion that with the first roar of thunder
he became Jupiter, whose duty it was to embrace all the women he
could find. At once throwing off his garments he seized a woman
and bore her down. Unluckily for him she happened to be the
wife of the senator Scaevinus, a very virtuous lady; she took from
her hair a sharp metal ornament and stabbed him. It was then
that he let off the first wild shout. Bleeding from a superficial
wound he rushed drunkenly from woman to woman, trying to hurl
them to a couch and cover them. Several men then seized him.
Nero was summoned and because he had a wicked sense of humor
or was himself also mad he called to a doctor named Lucius, a
round and pompous toad, who, quite drunk, had beeen declaiming
on his skill in the healing arts. Frothing and bubbling like a man
in poison Cornelius lay stretched out on a couch, with men hold-
ing his hands and feet; and while Nero acted as master of cere-
monies, Lucius came up to deliver a medical commentary over
the patient.
"Has this man constriction of his solid particles?" he asked.
"Does he suffer from atra bilis or strictum et laxum?”
16
"Which?" asked Nero wickedly. The sycophants round him
took up the cry: "Great doctor, which?"
"For years," said Lucius, gazing owlishly at his frothing victim,
"his atoms have been sluggish, but his Majesty's wine has caused
them to stir with such passion that his body threatens to burst
open. Should the physician, as Asclepiades said, interfere with
nature? Was his Majesty, the great Augustus, cured with hot
baths and fomentations? Ah no! Musa doused him with cold
water and filled his belly with chicory and endive—”
"Come to the point!" Nero roared.
"Your Majesty! Have invisible creatures from the marshes and
bogs crawled up his penis? Shall we take Cicero's prescription and
bathe him in the urine of one who has eaten cabbage?”
"Cato," said Nero, who fancied himself as a man of great learn-
ing.
"Your Majesty," said Lucius with a fat bow, "corrects me when
I err, being more learned than the physicians. Indeed it was Cato.
For one who has been stabbed, said Cato, sing Huat hanat huat
ista pista sista domiabo damnaustra et luxato!"
There was applause, in which Nero joined.
While the wounded man frothed and slobbered and cursed,
the impostor went on: "What did Helio do in such cases? He
castrated the man, so that lustful women could use him without
fear of pregnancy. Hah, matron and maid, he said, the sex has
all turned whore; to escape pregnancy and abortion women now
love their eunuchs more than their husbands and pimps! See him
there without his stones!"
"See, see!"
"All that the navel-string could give forth is present but his
beard, and that is only a barber's loss."
The witticism brought forth drunken shouts. "Hear, hear!”
cried the glutton Plautius staggering up from his couch.
"Tell us, great doctor,” said one of the fops gravely. "Is the os
uteri open during menstruation? Is it then that a woman most
readily conceives? And was the poet Catullus right when he said
that vain women use a tampon saturated with gall to constrict
themselves and so appear to be virgins?"
There were ribald shouts.
Another fop took up the spirit. "This is no man for he has no
hair! Masculine vigor is found only in those who are hairy, deep-
17
voiced and well-braced!" The hypocrite turned to fawn on Nero,
one of the hairiest men in Rome.
Nero's laugh was low and sibilant. "I recall," he said, looking
at the fop, “the time Caligula burst with laughter before his
guests." He now looked round at several faces. "Why, they
asked, do you laugh so, great Majesty? And Caligula said, To
think that if I raised my little finger all your throats would be cut
instantly."
Whether this was a drunken sally or a threat possibly nobody
knew but iced water flung over the guests could not have induced
a more dreadful silence. Lucius stood frozen. Plautius sank back
slobbering and breathing hard. Valerius said later that Scaevinus
and Piso exchanged anxious glances.
"Come!" cried Nero, having had his jest. "I will prescribe for
this man." He beckoned to servants to come over. "Take this
Jupiter away and lock him up with the twelve most beautiful
whores here tonight; and see to it, Gavius, that he embraces every
one of them three times before morning. Away with the dog!"
Men seized Cornelius and dragged him off. A little later Damon
heard Valerius telling his father that Eros slew more people in
Rome than the gods Febris and Mefitis. "I shudder to think,"
said Valerius, shuddering, “of this last night on earth of Cornelius-
Jupiter!"
18
II
It was past midnight and Nero and a few of his friends, variously
disguised, were ready for their foray, when one of the royal guards
came rushing in to announce the fire. Damon and his father were
among the first to leave the villa. It was pitch dark outside, and
looking away toward Rome Damon could see no light in the sky.
Then Valerius came out, calling to his coachman; and the thirty-
mile ride to the city, clattering over the stone pavement through
black night, was for Damon a horrible experience. Valerius was
so apprehensive about his properties in Rome that he stood up
all the way, exhorting the driver to flog the beasts to the last throb
of their strength. "Look!" he cried at last, and when Damon stood
up he could see the glow of fires and the clouds of smoke.
The fire, they were to learn, had begun in the wooden shops where
huge quantities of inflammable materials had been stored. Em-
bers carried by the wind soon fired the circus, whose timbers had
been drained of all moisture by the hot summer sun. From the
circus the flames swept up the vale between the two hills, igniting
the dry houses which stood close together on the narrow winding
streets. Before noon of this day the fire had roared up the Palatine
and consumed the palaces of the Caesars, with their innumerable
treasures and works of art; and the ancient temple of the moon, the
temple of Hercules at the base of the Aventine, the temple of
Vesta, the house of Numa, the shrine of Romulus-all these and
many others were soon blackened stone and ash.
Pamphilus said he would help Valerius, and so Damon was left
alone. His first thought was to find his mother. He hastened away
through the smoke and it was long after daylight before he was
able to find a path to the Christian colony in one of the poorer
19
quarters. He stood speechless before what he saw. The Christians
had gathered to watch the fires and everyone of them seemed ut-
terly bereft of his senses. Damon knew that they believed in the
immediate return of their god, the Christ, to usher in the millen-
nium; and that his coming would be proclaimed by prodigious
convulsions of nature, by overwhelming catastrophes, by famine
and earthquake and flood and fire-and by fire above all. His
father, who did not share his mother's religious fervors, had told
him, a little wryly, that belief in fires as auguries was common
among people: even Seneca had written that the world would
end in heat and violence; and the votaries of Mithra believed that
the final judgment would come with stupendous conflagrations
and upheavals.
He stood aghast, looking at the Friends. Some were making
wild animal-like cries of joy, some were shrieking in a strange jar-
gon and he made out that they were uttering prophecies and
judgments; and some were dancing with such demented abandon
that they had torn their clothes off and were stark naked. He sup-
posed they had no doubt, not one of them, that this great and
awful fire was proclaiming the return of their savior, that pres-
ently they would see him, coming on clouds of glory down from
the heavens. Some seemed to imagine that they now saw him, for
they were pointing to the clouds and smoke and wildly embracing
and kissing one another.
Damon went among them but nobody would speak to him. He
could make none of them aware of his presence. These people,
he realized, staring at their faces, would not have been aware of
ferocious lions or of the emperor's assassins. They were simply
and utterly mad with joy. His own mother was one of them and
her face was so transformed that he hardly knew her. He rushed
up to her, crying, "It is I, your son!" and tried to force her to
speak to him, to look at him; he shouted at her and pulled at her
arm; but she was singing!-singing and dancing!-her radiant
face uplifted, her eyes seeking the coming of her Lord. "Mother!”
he said, seizing her arm and shaking her. "Mother, it is Damon!
Speak to me!" She never once looked at him and after a little
while he slunk away, feeling very unwanted and lost. He with-
drew from the insane group and stood at a distance, observing
their incredible energy and their frenzied antics; or looked up at
the sky or over at the fires. He felt confused and frightened, he
felt ill. What did it all mean? Why did these people have to kiss
20
and scream and gesticulate at the sky, why did they have to run
around naked? He looked over at his mother and felt afraid.
Among them was a young girl named Levilla, his mother's
friend. She was almost naked. She was dancing round and round,
pointing, waving, singing. He saw her run to a man and envelop
him with her arms and kiss him. The next moment she was danc-
ing like one about to leave the earth. Feeling sick enough to vomit
Damon turned away.
During the next few days he was to see such terrors and suffer-
ings and madness as he was never to see again. There was panic
as the fires developed; the confusion became appalling. Hundreds,
thousands died in the flames. Some were burned alive when they
were trapped; some perished when they rushed into buildings to
find and save their belongings; and some, out of their minds with
grief, deliberately leapt into the fires and he saw their hair burn
up like torches. The third day, sleepless, white and spent, he went
as close as he dared to the principal fire; he watched the panic, the
suffocation, the trampling underfoot of relatives and friends. He
heard the roar of flames, the shrieks, the wailing of burning chil-
dren, and he felt in himself all the heat and terror and pain.
When he smelled the odor of roasted flesh and of hair burned to
ashes he swayed dizzily and almost fainted.
And still his mother would not speak to him because she did
not know him.
At the end of the sixth day the fires momentarily abated. By
this time the unburned parts of the city were the lairs of thieves
and assassins, the hives of wild and baseless rumors. Everywhere
Damon heard the stories about Nero. One said that he had
planned the city's destruction that he might build a greater one.
Another said that one night he stood on the roof of his pavilion
across the Tiber, where he had taken up quarters after his palace
burned, and twanged a harp and sang a dirge, lamenting the de-
struction of Rome and comparing it to the burning of Troy. It
was said that frenzied mobs heard his singing and resolved to kill
him. Damon did not know if there was truth in any of the rumors;
he did perceive that when minds were unbalanced and passions
ran like fire almost any rumor was possible, almost any rumor was
believed.
One of the rumors, his father told him, said that Christians.
had set the fires. Those who believed this pointed to one of their
favorite sayings, that the end of all things was at hand. Another
21
rumor said that it was the Jews. It was the rumor about the Chris-
tians that spread like a flame over Rome, until some of the mad-
dened survivors, who had lost their property, their families and
friends, cast about vengefully for a scapegoat.
"But some," said Pamphilus, "say Nero did it. Some said that
Augustus lay with his own daughter. Most people believe such
things."
Alarmed by the fury turned on the Christians Damon went to
find his mother. His father had looked very sad when he talked
about Levana, for there was nothing, he said, that he could do
for her. Her soul had caught fire in this new superstition that
called Rome the Beast or the Harlot and looked on the conflagra-
tion as a herald of the Lord's return. Did they know that Roman
punishment of arson was brutal?-those who burned property
were themselves burned. Damon was thinking about these things
as he searched for her; about their belief that the world would
end soon and Christians would ascend bodily to heaven, to have
eternal life and bliss, while all other people went down to perdi-
tion. Shaking his head sadly Pamphilus had asked, “What kind
of citizen is it who rejoices in the destruction of a great city?"
He did not find his mother. He heard that she had been taken
with other Christians and that they were being tortured. He saw
the pitiable broken thing after she was released. Yet, he asked him-
self, should he think her pitiable?—for her spirit was in no way
broken, nor her courage and faith. He saw her but not to speak
to her. In a dark night he saw her again when she was taken
with others to a vacant field, and he trailed along, sensing that
some horrible thing was to be done. Standing back, hidden and
looking out, he saw men set posts in the earth and bind the Chris-
tians to them, with their backs to the posts and their wrists and
ankles tied behind. He saw his mother there. He saw them pile
around these people wood saturated with oil and he stopped
breathing when he saw them set the wood afire. He was too fright-
ened to move. When the executioners went away he sped toward
his mother, determined to break the thongs and free her. What
happened then he was to spend a lifetime trying to understand.
His mother was enveloped by flames but as he rushed up to her
she cried to him in a clear firm voice, “Damon, stay back!"
"I will free you!" he said, sobbing.
"My son, stay back! Go back or they'll kill you!"
"Mother!"
22
"My son!"
"O Mother, Mother!" he screamed, dashing tears from blinded
eyes.
"My son, don't grieve for me. I am all right."
"O Mother!"
She was all right! She was enveloped in flames! An incredible
thing then happened and Damon was to ask himself many times if
he saw it clearly. Though the flames had risen to her breast she
seemed not to be suffering at all. She was smiling at him—or in
later times did he unwittingly cherish and build a picture of her
that was not real? Did she scream? He had no memory of it. Did
she writhe and try to be free? He was convinced that she did not.
He was to recall the noble words of Arria who, when her husband
was commanded by the emperor to kill himself, and faltered, took
the dagger and plunged it into her own breast, and drew it out
and handed it to him, saying, "See Paetus, it doesn't hurt much.”
His mother's whole face seemed to Damon to be radiant, to be
suffused with a light not of this world. Was what he saw the radi-
ance of the fire shining on his tears? Was her face convulsed by
agony? He would never be able to say. It seemed to him that she
spoke other words, that out of the horror came gentle words of
comfort and love; or did he dream it? No, he did not dream it.
She kept talking to him and she kept smiling to the bitter end.
She said he must become one of them-one of those in Antioch
called Christians, in Rome, Friends. She made no effort at all to
free herself; she kept her gaze fixed on the heavens, looking for
her Savior and Lord. This life did not matter, she said. My son,
be brave, she said to him. And there she died.
That a person could die a death so hideous and keep her faith
serene and holy was to be for him a greater miracle than any the
Christians were to tell in their gospel stories. "My son, be brave.
There she stood at last, a skeleton bound to a post, his own
gentle mother; and then the post was burned in two and both
post and skeleton fell. Damon was standing back about a hundred
feet. He was numb with horror. He stood there, blinded, trying
to see her frame on the post. But there was no post now. There
was no post but he still heard her voice, clear and firm: “Be brave.
This life does not matter. .
He turned at last and fled to find his father. He sobbed out his
heartbroken story and Pamphilus put an arm round his son, his
23
cheek to his hair. He heard his son whispering, "Father, what
does it mean?"
"My son, I don't know." Pamaphilus had never understood his
wife's devotion to this strange new cult. He heard his son saying
that he would know, he would find out what it meant if it took his
whole life to do it. "Yes, my son."
Damon could never remember what he did in the first weeks
after his mother's death. The experience went so deep into him
that it possessed his soul and his consciousness; he lived for a while
in a nightmare of terror, but under the terror he felt that he had
been a witness to something beautiful and true. But a witness to
what? He asked himself that question over and over, he put it
many times to his father; and always Pamphilus said gently, sadly,
"My son, I do not know." What was it in this new faith that
crowned a person with such nobility in her last moments of
agony? It was a question that Damon resolved to answer and to
find the answer he became a dedicated man. "Mother, Mother!"
he would sob, weeping, remembering. "Mother, didn't you suf-
fer? Mother, what does it mean?" He would go out to the spot
where she died and he would put his lips to the earth there and
would sink shuddering to the earth to touch it as though she was
in it there, somehow; and out of grief almost too deep to be borne
he would whisper, "Mother, my dear Mother!. . ."
24
III
His wife's death broke the heart of Pamphilius and within the
following year he died. Damon was an orphan and alone. He
inherited enough property to make himself modestly independent
but he was still only a lad and he was very unhappy. Because
he had suffered such deep shock he kept pretty much to himself
for a year or two after his father's death, sitting in somber thought
in the small room where he slept, or going to the libraries to read,
or standing on one of the hills to watch the rebuilding of the city.
For fellowship at last he turned to the Christian colony in Rome.
Murdia was one of the first Christians to become his friend.
She was a riddle to him. Nearly all the Christian women scorned
cosmetics and jewelry and false hair; it was their belief that when
an elder laid his hands on a woman's head to bless her, the blessing
would not penetrate false hair. Christian women were told to
keep quiet in meetings, that if they wished to know anything they
should ask their husbands or the elders. Murdia would have none
of that. Though she seemed to be devout, and possibly was,
Damon reflected, in her own strange way, she dressed lavishly,
adorned herself with costly brilliants, drenched herself with rare
scents, and so stood in astonishing contrast to the poor ragged
women who had almost nothing at all. She would make a charm-
ing face at Damon and say, "Men wear nothing on their heads in
our meetings because they are the image of God and must reflect
his glory; but women, the poor sinful bitches, are only a reflection
of man's glory, and so must keep their heads covered." She would
gaze at Damon, her eyes wonderful and wicked. "You don't
believe it?"
"No," he said, remembering his mother.
25
"You think women are equal to men?"
"Superior," he said, remembering his mother.
"You're a darling!" Murdia cried, and kissed him.
Kissing with Christians was a common form of greeting and
fellowship. Murdia loved to kiss the handsomer men if they were
clean and not too shabby. She kissed Damon with ardor, filling
him with shame because his baser passions were stirred; yet he
knew that Christian kisses were supposed to be holy and in that he
found comfort.
"You darling!" she would say, clasping his face and kissing him
with soft hot passion full on the mouth. "Damon, I love you!"
Damon was tall for his age and well formed but rather unfleshed.
He sensed that what Murdia had in mind was not religious hunger.
The presence of her husband Regulus, the fastidiously scrubbed
and scented fop, made no difference at all. “Never mind him,”
she would say to Damon, when with a cynical leer he was observing
her. "He has his whores. Like Julius Caesar he has seduced the
wives of all his friends. He accepts the equality of women, espec-
ially in a bed-chamber. Why should he complain? He's a sinner
headed for perdition.”
"Sister Murdia!" he said, sneering.
Unmarried and widowed Christian women were organized
in groups and called sisters. Calling a married woman a sister
was a special insolence, but Murdia only tossed her lovely hair
and laughed.
Damon developed a jealous infatuation. Sometimes, when look-
ing at her, he wondered how many lovers she had had. Did she
intend that Damon should be one of them? He was too lonely and
lost and unhappy to want to be that.
Week after week he studied the Christians in Rome but could
find no pattern in them. Some were almost as holy in their emo-
tions and deportment as angels; but what was he to think of a
certain man who had all the marks of a lecher, or of a woman who
sat in the depths of despair making snakes of her fingers and
weeping, or of still another who seemed to be insane? Or of Mur-
dia? In the beginning, a legend said, Christians had shared all
things in common, pooling their resources and giving to each
according to his needs; but it was not so in Rome. Some Christians
were slaves in the rags and filth of beggars; a few like Murdia
made an obscene display of their riches. What was a Christian
anyway? Some, calling Rome the Harlot, hated the city as they
26
""
hated nothing else; some, like Murdia, loved it and said proudly,
"How beautiful it will be! Augustus found it a city of wood and
left it a city of marble . . . After sensing how deeply most
Christians hated the city and the Empire Damon looked round
him to see if he could understand why. He spent much time
reading. Slowly a picture emerged.
Were the emperors gods? Most of the common people thought
so. The emperor, they believed, was no less than Jupiter himself;
he wore the costume of Jupiter, borrowed from his great temple;
rode in a chariot drawn by four horses crowned with laurel; put
on purple robes embroidered with gold; carried in his left hand
a scepter adorned with an eagle; set on his brow a crown of laurel;
and painted his face red. Above him a slave held a massive crown
of gold fashioned to resemble oak leaves. The eagle was Jove's
bird, the oak his sacred tree; and his images were kept rouged.
The degenerate Caligula had called himself Lord and Master and
thought of himself, Damon suspected, as God. When Augustus
was a child he was borne by invisible hands from his cradle to a
lofty tower and there eagles waited on him and fed him; and after
his death an image of him was seen to rise from the pyre and
ascend in glory to heaven. The common people believed such
stories. To his astonishment Damon learned that the Christians
also believed them. But they hated the emperor.
Murdia gave him some reasons why. The slightest disrespect
to the image of the emperor was a capital crime. When a drunkard
accidentally touched an image of Claudius he was denounced as
a spy and a traitor; and a person found undressing near a statue
of Augustus or entering a brothel bearing a coin with his image
on it was punished with death. Murdia puckered her lovely face
up when telling such things. Why, she asked, did people worship
mortal man?
Seeking the answer Damon learned that Empedocles had pro-
claimed himself a god; Plato had thought that departed heroes
became holy angels on earth; Aristotle had dedicated an altar to
Plato; Sparta had a temple for the worship of Lycurgus; Philip
and Alexander both had thought themselves worthy of divine
honors. Damon then learned that worship of rulers had become
with the Greeks a state cult which the Romans had adopted: Ovid
had acclaimed Augustus as a god but the Christians said there was
only God.
He learned that in close intimacy with the worship of rulers
27
were certain vices. Damon had heard his father and Valerius
discuss Roman taste and its vulgar and barbarous excess in orna-
ment and gaudy decoration. There was Nero's Golden House
which he had built after the fire: its gardens, meadows, fish ponds,
game preserves, vineyards, fountains, waterfalls and pleasure houses
covered such an immense area that wags said the people would
have to migrate, the emperor would soon occupy the entire city.
In his new palace Nero had a colossus of himself a hundred and
twenty feet tall, and the circuit of his colonnades covered three
miles. The Egyptian roses for one of his banquets cost a fantastic
fortune. "He squanders money that way when the Friends don't
have enough to eat," Murdia said, and rose with her silks shimmer-
ing to sit down to a feast.
So far as Damon could make out she was typical of the ladies
born to wealth. They seemed to spend most of their time beau-
tifying and adorning themselves; all that costly and colorful
raiment and jewels could do for a woman was done. When she
went to her dressingroom her maids came, each carrying mirrors,
bottles, jars to polish teeth, blacken eyebrows and lashes, curl
hair and dip it in henna and with heated tongs make bangs in
front, leaving only a tiny patch of brow showing; to shave the
lady's entire body except the head; to take off a paste made with
asses' milk, put on the night before; to bathe her and to drench
her with expensive scents. "What," Murdia said, "did that dread-
ful old fool Cicero mean saying that nobody dances when sober
except a lunatic?" And then, showing herself off with the languor-
ous softness and grace of a cat, "Would I excite Hippolytus?"
Most of the poor, Damon observed, imitated the highborn. If
they could not afford pastilles to scent their breath they chewed
some fragrant herb. They massaged themselves not with costly oils
but with plant juices. There were women, Murdia said, too poor
to buy a crust who kept their bodies shaved. Had he ever seen
a statue in Rome showing a woman with pubic hair? As for the
fops, they were even more self-indulgent than the women: they
spent their time trying with massages to keep slim and graceful;
saturating themselves with the costliest scents; and imitating and
perfecting the erotic movements of the mimes.
Damon had seen homes of the wealthy. Murdia invited him to
her own. He saw great beams of Hymettian marble resting on
African pillars; walls of colorful marble or of alabaster slabs bor-
dered with green serpentine; arches gleaming with glass mosaics;
28
tesselated floors; tables of citrus wood on ivory feet, couches inlaid
with gold and covered with Babylonian tapestries; vases of Corin-
thian bronze; Aeginetan candelabra; sideboards laden with an-
tique silver plate; and statues and paintings by renowned artists.
Murdia's possessions astonished him: she had diamonds, pearls,
rubies, silks—and on her table, peaches and apricots and the
syrup of sugar cane. Romans said if you saw diamonds and rubies
you could not be sure that they indicated immense wealth; but
if you saw ripe peaches on a table you knew! Looking at Murdia's
luxuries Damon thought of Poppaea, of whom it was said that
she bathed daily in asses' milk and took a herd of asses with her
when she traveled.
Most Christians pretended to scorn wealth. They pretended to
abhor cruelty—and of all people on earth, Pamphilius had said,
the Romans were the cruelest. Damon had known from childhood
that a Roman father had the right to flog, imprison, kill or enslave
his son, even after the son was grown. No wonder that flogging
usually preceded execution or that the law commanded, Strike
a man in such a manner as to make him feel he is dying. No
wonder that the cross was the form of punishment for slaves and
criminals or that the poor creatures were tortured by having
flesh torn from their bodies with redhot pincers and hot nails
driven through their genitals; or that they were hanged head
downward; or scourged and then sewed in oxhide sacks with
snakes and scorpions; or that they had their hands and feet chopped
off. In one of Seneca's essays he found it revealed that the
Romans had every known infernal machine and method with
which to torture and maim.
Did only the Christians and Jews protest against such brutal-
ties? Well, no: he learned that there had been a few others.
Both Augustus and Tiberius had tried in vain to soften the
barbarous passions loosed by the amphitheater. He learned that
when the Greeks proposed to introduce gladiatorial shows De-
monax had cried, "You will first have to throw down the altar
of Pity!" Even the cynical Petronius had denounced the circus
combat. But nobody denounced them with such vehemence as
the Christians with whom Damon talked; they said the Romans
had placed themselves completely beyond God's mercy and for-
giveness. Was not Caligula a symbol of the Empire?—that gross
degenerate man with his skinny neck, pot-belly, thin legs, who
had had handsome gladiators tortured and strangled because they
29
made him conscious of his beastlike appearance. Had not Claud-
ius loved to watch the agony in the faces of the dying? What hope
could there be for such a people?—who chained bears and bulls
together, or threw men to bulls maddened by white-hot irons
or stuck full of darts that had been dipped in burning pitch.
Had not four hundred bears died in a single day under Caligula?
-and hundreds of tigers and bulls and elephants in a single spec-
tacle under Nero?
Yes, what the Christians said was true. One day he heard
a story that for him summarized all that was most brutal in Roman
life. A knight had a son whose elegance had annoyed Caligula
and the emperor had the son beheaded. The very same day he
summoned the father to dine with him and while the pathetic
man was urged to eat and drink and be gay, wicked spies studied
every emotion in his face. "How," a friend asked him later,
"could you bear such an ordeal without flinching?” And the
father replied: "Because I had another son." Or there was the
story of Julia, daughter of Augustus. When asked why, in spite
of her notorious adulteries, all her children looked like her hus-
band, she had said, “I never accept a lover until my husband has
got me pregnant." No wonder Augustus had banished her to a
desolate island.
Damon had a sensitive and civilized father, he had lived in the
home of a civilized Roman. He had not known that some of the
oldest and noblest families had their wives taken from them by
the emperor to serve as whores for degraded guests. He had not
known that most men of high birth or position had male lovers.
He had not known that in the theaters the loves of Pasiphae and
Leda were acted out literally or told in the most naked language,
or that the theaters represented chiefly betrayed husbands, de-
bauched wives, sodomy, whoredom. The mimus mocked Christ-
ian and Jew and obscenely parodied their rites. As for women,
the Christians told Damon that Messalina was typical of Roman
wives and was not sexual intercourse in competition with harlots
her favorite diversion? Hadn't the poet Ovid been banished by
Augustus because he had discovered the emperor's incestuous
relations with his daughter? Hadn't Caligula's mother, this same
daughter, publicly said that Ovid had been banished for that
reason? Was it not well known that Caligula was incestuous with
all his sisters, and with two of them at the very time when they
were plotting to kill him? Did anyone doubt the story of Nero's
30
incest with his mother? Hadn't the wantonness spread even to the
Vestal Virgins?-those girls chosen for their beauty whose task was
to keep perpetually burning the ancient hearthfire of the city.
No man, Regulus had said with a smirk, but the chief Pontifex
dared to set foot inside the shrine of the virgin goddess; but the
smirk indicated that Regulus knew better than that. He knew
as all the fops knew that Ovid had advised men in search of erotic
adventure to go to the temples and holy places. With another
smirk he had told Damon that the Virgins used the herb agnus
castus to kill their voluptuous desires, but Murdia said the only
agnus castus they used was found on men about fifteen inches.
above their knees.
Murdia, delighting in his shamed and astonished look, told
him so many things. She told him that some of the most degener-
ate libertines presented themselves as the god Anubis and seduced
noble matrons. "They think," she said, "they'll have a god-child.”
She reminded him that statues of Priapus with an enormus red
penis were to be seen everywhere; that on certain holy days, huge
male genitals carved of wood were borne on a cart through the
cities and fields, and at last crowned by a woman chosen as the
most virtuous matron in Rome. Had he seen the people beside
themselves with erotic raptures shouting their worship in obscene
words—and highborn women struggling with one another to place
a wreath on the genitals and bow before them in homage? Had
he observed the fascinium hanging from the necks of small child-
ren or resting above the doors of shops and the city's gates or riding
on the triumphal chariot of military conquerors? Did he know
that the Vestal Virgins worshipped it? Had he seen that Hic habi-
tat felicitas was the sign under the penis and the testicles? Was
that what the poet Horace had meant by the strong stump against
his thighs? Damon reddened, for he was a shy and sensitive youth.
Had he seen childless women kneeling before a wooden penis
and begging it for fertility? His color deepened. Did he know
that it was into the holy chamber of this god that the trembling
bride entered to lose her maidenhead? Had he seen pubescent
girls ravenously eating pastry made in the likeness of the male
organs? He turned away and left her.
But he came again, because Murdia and Regulus fascinated
him. It was a pretty thin line, said Regulus with a yawn, between
harlots who took you for a coin, and wives who took you for a
jewel. Regulus liked to tell of the large brothels close by the
31
circus or just outside the city walls. Horace had called them
fornices; Catullus, lupanaria. Some brothels were only dim filthy
cubicles with a genital image above the door; some were sump-
tuous establishments. Most of the harlots were lodged near the
circus, because after the bloody games men came out with their
lusts hot, and even the most virtuous women wildly flung them-
selves on pander and pimp. Of wandering whores, Regulus told
him, there were the noctilucae or night moths; the bustuariae or
grave-watchers who took men into the cemeteries at night and
hired out as mourners during the day; and the diobolariae or
twopennies, lowest of the order, being unsightly and broken-down
hags. "When you're ready," Regulus said, "I'll show you around."
One day Murdia was talking to him about the horror in which
Christians held all the symbols of fertility when she burst forth
angrily: "What can men expect of women, when more and more
men prefer men as lovers? Have you read Catullus?"
He was staring at her.
"Soon all men will work off their lust with their concubinus,
their handsome male slave! And do some of the gods prefer male
lovers? Damon, have you been to the lunapars for boys?"
He shook his head no.
"Go, then you'll understand a Christian. You know how many
men there are who rent themselves out as girls?—even how many
freemen make their living that way? But when emperors set
the fashion what can the people do? How about Nero's boy Spo-
ros? What can we do when men insist on intercourse a praepos-
terae venere? Like Regulus, the dog! You'll learn some day that
women are what men want them to be. When are you going to
kiss me?"
Most of the Christians came from the slaves, the poor, the dregs
of society. It was Murdia again who gave him the picture: a
third of the people in Rome were slaves, not counting all the
free men who, refusing to compete with slave-labor, were little
more than beggars. But some of the wealthy people, she said,
had estates so large that they employed thousands of slaves. Every
picturesque or sheltered site on the lakes, on the Anio, the Alban
Hills, in the Laurentine forests and on the bays of Campania was
a luxurious country seat. How terrible it was, she said-but then
dimpling with little smiles, she said that the slaves would enter
with Christ into glory. Because his own father had been a slave
Damon did not like the subject. His face had burned when he
32
read in Cicero: "All gains made by hired laborers are dishonorable
and base...the work of all artisans is sordid; there can be nothing
honorable in a workshop." But the Christians said honest toil
was honorable. "Is a slave a man?" a famous Roman had asked,
for under Roman law he was classified as a thing. Cicero had
thought them lower than the beasts-but Christians said they
would be the first in the Kingdom. Ten thousand slaves were
hung on crosses by Crassus, Julius Caesar had sold sixty thousand
Gauls in a single day, Augustus had left it on record that he deliv-
ered up to their masters for execution thirty thousand slaves
during his reign; but the Christians said, Among the first will be
these. As the son of a slave Damon had to ask himself if he was
a man, if he was human-for Claudius had decreed that a woman
who married a slave became a slave, and so it had been with
Damon's mother. In the language of Rome a freed woman slave
and a whore were synonyms. In his reading Damon learned that
many of the famous men of the past had asked if slaves were
human and for the most part had concluded that they were not;
and he wondered about it when, walking over the city's hills
or along the river, he saw so many filthy beggars, so many pale and
skinny children and mothers, so many persons who had crawled
away into hiding to die. He wondered about it when reading
in Seneca; for Seneca had written about purity and had been
openly denounced for his adulteries; about clemency, yet had
abetted Nero in some of his worst atrocities; about vulgar display,
yet had lived in a palace almost equal to the emperor's. If the
greatest of the Romans was so base, perhaps Rome was all that
the Christians said it was. "My own conscience is of more impor-
tance to me," Cicero had writen, "than what men say❞—but he
had thought slaves lower than beasts, and physicians, painters, and
engineers only a shade above slaves.
Over the whole world, he was beginning to sense, there was a
deep and dreadful pessimism and a general expectation of the
end of things. Life and pain were one, a poet had written. Exist-
ence was one perpetual night, Catullus had said. Carpe diem and
then eternal exile where pulvis et umbra sumus, said Horace. I
was not, I became; I am not and I care not was a favorite inscrip-
tion on tombs. Eat, drink, enjoy yourselves, then join us where
eternal night covers all. While I lived I lived well; now my little
play is ended, soon shall yours be; goodbye. Hold all mockery,
reader; nothing is our own. Such were some of the popular epi-
33
taphs. Even Cicero had despaired: Sine ullo sensu. But the
Christians said this life did not matter, that the virtuous would
inherit eternal bliss.
Damon's father had believed in nothing beyond death but
Damon's reading now led him to the realization that all over the
world there was a yearning for another life. He found it in Virgil:
the people were stretching out their hands in longing to the far-
thest shore. He found it in other poets. Mankind, one of them
said, craved more than drunkeness, brutal and bloody games, sens-
ual orgies and the disgust from over-indulgence. Stray preachers
like the Cynics came preaching and some listened and some scoffed.
The world was weary of Empiricus: "Some men say they have
found truth; some say that it is impossible for truth to be appre-
hended; some still search for it." The world was weary of the
Dogmatics, the Stoics, the Skeptics. It was weary of the present
religions. Who, Murdia asked, bringing a foot up to rouge its
nails, could believe in Scabies, the goddess of itch? —in Mefitis,
the goddess of stench? What person with the brains of a pimp
believed that Epona from a niche in a rafter looked down upon
the stable, Neptune upon the ship, Mercury upon the merchant?-
or that Jupiter watched over the sky, Vesta over the hearthfire,
Saturn over the growing seed, Edulia over the sucking babe,
Domiduca over the bride? Now and then she startled him with
an intuitive insight, as when she said that Romans prayed but
never to be morally improved, only to be healthier and richer;
as when she said that they had no idea of sin because they had no
power to feel remorse. Sin for them, she said, looking at him with
beautiful enraged eyes, was only a breach of contract; remorse was
only regret that they had blundered into punishment. They had
no divine laws that called on them to be temperate, compassionate,
self-denying and so found it impossible to understand either
Christians or Jews.
Damon's search for the meaning that had sustained his mother
drove him not only to the libraries, temples, baths and theaters
but also to the graveyards. He would stand and look at legends
on tombstones: This mute stone begs you to stop, stranger, until
it has told you whose shade it covers. Here lie the bones of a
man, modest, honest and trusty-the crier, Olus Granius. That
is all. It wanted you not to be unaware of this. Farewell!
That is all. Farewell. But was that all? In a cynical and des-
pairing age, two peoples said that was not all. One was the Jews.
34
In Rome they lived on the fringe of the Campus Martius and near
the Porta Capena; they were a despised and hated people, they
were also a proud and arrogant people, who believed that Rome
would succumb to their god Yahweh. They were despised be-
cause the fanatics among them said that the Greek philosophers
had taken all their wisdom from an ancient named Moses, that
Moses had taught the Egyptians all that they knew. They were
hated because they would not mix with other people or eat with
them or even talk with them. Seneca had called them criminals.
Other people called them atheists and donkey-worshipers; it was
widely believed that they had an offensive odor and were cowards
and in business dealings more cunning than the fox. They were
held in contempt for their practice of circumcision, their refusal to
eat pig, their manner of dress, their long prayers, their holy days.
The other people were the Christians, whom Romans associated
with slaves, criminals, degenerates. Romans saw in this new relig-
ion a pestilence of superstition and hatred and magic. Christians
ranting against idols had in places ruined the trade of the silver-
smiths. Ranting against war they threatened to undermine the
martial spirit of the Roman soldier. Preaching equality they
threatened, the philosophers said, to reduce all people to the same
level of mediocrity. They had periodic frenzies over the coming
of a king who would destroy Rome. They hid out in illegal soc-
ieties, the punishment for which was the same as for treason:
they could be beheaded, crucified, cast to the beasts or burned
alive.
Having been tutored by a gentle and disillusioned father, who
believed that reason was the noblest mark of man, Damon had no
interest in the Christians until he saw his mother in the flames. She
had said that this life did not matter; his father had said there
was no other life. She had smiled and looked up at the heavens
as though she suffered no agony at all. His father had covered his
face, murmuring, “I don't understand it." Could he understand
it some day?-he, the son, the orphan, left alone and now lost and
lonely? Was there a great new wind blowing across the world?
Were the tides rising in a great new truth? Had his mother stand-
ing there, bruised and bloody, with half the hair torn from her
head, with her poor naked frame looking broken but her face
looking holy-had his mother looked deeper than the philos-
ophers and farther than the prophets?
To his effort to answer these questions Damon was to give his
life.
35
IV
"You've got to become a Christian!" Murdia cried at him.
"Great will be my glory in heaven if I convert you!"
They were sitting in a sumptuous chamber of her house and
Damon was feeling stiff and ill at ease; for though she said she
was troubled about his soul in her magnificent eyes he saw some-
thing else. Her lovely white body was almost visible to him in
her semitransparent robe.
In this brutal world, she said, there had to be a religion of
love. She pronounced the word love with a seductive lisp. She
tossed her brown hair back and laughed at Damon; and he grinned
like any gawky youth seized by the tyranny of his first erotic
emotions. Looking at her a moment he recalled her saying that
the Egyptians were very clean in their personal habits but the Ro-
mans for all their baths were filthy, boasting that in filth they fol-
lowed the worthy customs of their fathers. She had told him that
Seneca, scorning the luxuries of the baths, had praised the bath-
room of Scipio, which was small and dark; the Romans of old had
bathed only once a week and argued that their foul odor was the
odor of heroism. Murdia was as clean as a new willow whistle.
Murdia came over and sat by him and clasped his hands. She
looked deep into his eyes and the sly siren was reading there, as
only an intuitive woman can, the stormy emotions in his soul.
When he tried to draw his hands away she tightened her clasp and
thrust her fragrant hair against his cheek.
Breathing on his throat she whispered, "Do you love me?"
"Who is your god?" he demanded in a loud voice to hide his
confusion. "Not Jupiter, not Apollo-"
“Oh, don't be so damned silly! It's just God."
36
"But your
Christ;-"
"He's a man who became a god."
He had a father who taught him well, he knew about such things
as that. Many men had become gods, he said-Orpheus, Dio-
nysus-
“And Venus," the sly rogue said, laughing softly and touching
her lips to his cheek, "was she once a woman like me?" She drew
back and regarded him gravely. In the tone of a schoolmistress
she said: "His name was Jesus. We call him that but the Jews
among us call him Yeshua or Joshua or some such nonsense. It
all means the same thing."
He had to talk to hide his deep emotions. "Jesus became a
god?"
"Yes, darling," she said with infinite patience. “He was killed.
On the third day he rose to heaven."
He knew all about that too. All the savior-gods rose on the
third day—Attis, Dionysus, Mithra—all of them. He had told her
that. She had said that these other gods were impostors.
Now, to take the look from his eyes, the look like his father's
that asked more questions than a world could answer, she smo-
thered him with murmurings and seductive warmth and Damon
took advantage of the moment to look down at her thighs. She
wore only a tunica interior or intima, an almost transparent silken
thing sometimes called Coan, for the source of the material. He
saw her handsome legs clearly for a moment and remembered
Regulus saying that the softest thing in life was the flesh between
a woman's thighs. Then she was leaning back and looking at him
intently.
“Damon," she said reproachfully, "must you be learned and
dull? Can't you just believe and be happy without troubling
your mind with what pompous old men have said? Who in limbo
cares how many gods rose on the third day?"
He looked at her, all the questions in his eyes. "This Jesus was
a Jew?"
She pouted deliciously. "Can I, a mere woman, answer all
your questions? I believe. I know that our redeemer lives. I
live in the body of Christ and I am happy. When I die I will go to
heaven and be blessed and see God. Do I have to worry about
what old goats like Seneca have thought? I won't. I won't!" she
cried with a shriek and leapt up, looking wild and distraught.
Damon sensed that the need to believe in her was as deep as her
37
heart and he did not see her again for many weeks, save at a dis-
tance, and then he ducked and ran. But somehow, from someone,
she learned where he was living-in one of the ‘islands', one of
those dismal gloomy ugly buildings in which persons lived who
were too poor for fine houses but too rich for sheds, tents, or shelt-
tering trees. She walked in on Damon, smelling like the emperor's
gardens, and found him bent over a book by Plato. She looked at
it and asked with astonishment:
"Can you read Greek?”
"My father was a Greek."
"I never knew that," she said, looking at him curiously. She
turned for a chair and finding none sank to the bed. "Why have
you been avoiding me?" Before he could protest she said, “Don't
lie to me! We Christians hate liars. Is it that you don't find me
attractive!" When he gave no reply she asked: "Shall I show you
my body unclothed? Would you like that?" He was staring at
her. She stripped off and stood naked before him and in the way
of women very vain or unhappily unsure of themselves she asked
at once, almost before he had time to look at her, "Well, do I
please you?"
His blood was racing like a hot wind across hyacinths. Stam-
mering, he asked what this had to do with being a Christian.
"We Christians believe in love," she said, turning to show him
her perfect form. “Well, do I attract you?”
He stared.
"Oh, not my mind. When was a man ever interested in a wo-
man's mind? But in my capacity to love and my knowledge of the
art?" Red-faced, he was gawking at her like a dull fellow. She was
turning, to show herself. Then, putting a hand gently under her
breasts: "Don't you want to be my lover?"
"You have lovers!" he said, almost shouting at her, the shame
burning in his cheeks.
"Lovers?" she said, sneering. "Men are such bulls. How did
Lucretius say it? I grind! How did Petronius say it? Molere muli-
erem! Is to grind a woman to love her? Why do men write about
it that way?—and poets, my dear! Lovers? I've had stupid things.
If it would be molere mulierum with you then I hate you."
"Please dress," Damon said.
The strange woman flung herself at his feet and began to sob.
She was reproaching herself, babbling apologies, begging his for-
giveness. But in a few moments she was up, furious and disdain-
38
ful, her eyes flashing, her red lips drawn into a pout of hurt and
scorn. She cried at him, "What did Ovid say?" and before he could
gasp or protest she was affecting the manner of the theater and re-
citing:
"Although they call it force they love the forcing,
they love to be compelled to give themselves!
If Venus takes a girl by storm she loves it
But if a girl escapes an ardent lover
she may look happy but she will be sad!"
"You're not ardent?" Damon's foolish grin did not hide his feel-
ing of abysmal silliness. Murdia put on her garments and turned
to the bed, saying simply like a child, "I will pray now." She knelt
and seemed to be considering. Without turning to him she said,
"Jews face the west when they pray but we face east. Why is that?”
When he did not reply she asked, “Am I facing east?”
"This is east," said Damon.
She half-turned and then prayed aloud. "On you do I call,
Savior and Lord, the God of all flesh and Lord of all spirits, the
blessed one and giver of all blessings, to keep this young man
from temptation and grant him wisdom and understanding, yes,
and love, yes, and tenderness. Grant that he may see the evil of
his ways and join us. Grant that he may love me, even if ever so
little. Please send him to more of our meetings. All this I ask in
the name of the Christ forever."
She arose and went to the door but came back, framed Damon's
face with her hands, looked for a moment into his eyes, and gave
him a pious little kiss on his mouth. "Be sure to come to our
meetings," she said, and then was gone.
Christian meetings in Rome, secret and therefore outlawed,
were held in graveyards in the first dusk of morning; or in under-
ground caves; or once in a while in a house. Damon had been to
some of the meetings and had been as confused by them as by
Murdia. He had gone for fellowship and to see the girl Levilla,
whom his mother had hoped he would marry. As a dutiful son
he was trying to fall in love with her.
Soon after Murdia came to his room he went to a meeting.
Christians met on a fixed day just before dawn and sang hymns to
one called Jesus as though he were a god; bound themselves with
a dreadful oath against theft and adultery; kissed one another and
talked of the all-absorbing love in the body of their Christ; and
often wept. At a former meeting Damon had heard one of them
39
say that a gospel of love had to be born into the world because of
its rapacity and cruelty, and degradation of man by man. Damon
was not able to tell what they meant by love: it seemed to be a
kind of mystical and other-worldly thing.
The Christians here, he had learned, regarded themselves as an
ancient people of God, the remnant of Israel, that had been saved
by accepting the Messiah. They had no fixed organization, no
body of doctrine. Their principal belief seemed to be of them-
selves as an elect and chosen group in the mystical body of their
Christ. Some claimed to be able to impart esoteric lore; some, to
cast out demons and heal the sick; and some to express the will of
God by babbling in strange tongues. A few believed in circum-
cision but most of them said it was not necessary to salvation.
There was a young man of sad countenance and halting speech
with whom Damon talked. He told Damon their Christ had
taught that evil would not be blighted or extinguished by rulers
nor by war, neither by evading it nor making light of it, but by
meeting it head-on, with love and self-sacrifice, with suffering,
even with descent into hell, even with death on the cross. Fixing
his sad dark eyes on Damon he said that Christians believed not
in punishing the sinner but in healing him with love. The times
had been fulfilled; the Kingdom was at hand. They would all be
perfect as their Father was perfect. Yes! They would take his
yoke upon them and learn from him, for the yoke was easy, the
burden was light. Ah yes! But what did he really mean? He said
that a pure person encompassed the universal. The universal,
God had housed in flesh, so that truth could be detached from the
accidents of time and place and revealed in the pure heart of
Jesus. He said that one named Paul had in a moment of vision seen
all the scattered fragments of idea and purpose coalesce in one uni-
versal moment that was the eye of God.
This was the first time Damon had heard of the one named
Paul. He stared at this sad man and listened but found nothing
in his words to explain his mother.
After the hymns and prayers a shaggy and ragged elder began
to shriek in a high shrill voice, and at once the people gathered
round him. Murdia whispered to Damon that God was speaking
through the man in the ancient and forgotten language of the
chosen people. Damon thought it plain gibberish but he saw
that the man's face was bursting with sweat, that he seemed like
one consumed by inner fires. After the man's transport passed
40
he spoke to his fellows in a quiet voice, telling them that the end
of things was imminent, that it would be heralded by destruction
of the Jews. Damon shuddered. He shuddered again when he
saw that the man's words had made them so happy that they were
hugging and kissing and dancing. He was staring unhappily
around him when Levilla rushed up and kissed him.
Ordinarily a quiet sober girl, with deep gray thoughtful eyes
set far apart, hair the color of wet myrtle, a clear comlexion and a
sweet mouth, Levilla like Damon was an orphan. She had been
born a slave. Her kiss lured up from his depths a wish to embrace
her; he thought it was because they were both orphans, both lonely
and lost. He thought it was his duty to marry her and one day
he spoke of marriage.
She fixed him with a sad smile touched with pity. "Can you
think of marriage when our Lord will return any day? Repent
and prepare yourself."
He was offended. "It was my mother's wish,” he said.
"Your mother? Is that your reason?"
Damon fought the truth down. “No,” he said, bravely lying, “I
love you."
""
"Oh, but you've not learned the meaning of love!"
He had known when she rushed up and kissed him that the kiss
was not for him. In another meeting she kissed him again and
rushed away crying, “Marana tha!" They were all crying these
words. A Jew had told him they were Aramaic and meant, Our
Lord, come! "Marana tha!" Levilla cried, and rushed away to
kiss another man.
Then one of the elders began to hop up and down like a huge
demented toad, shrieking all the while, "After the body is wasted
away the soul, like a swift horse freed from its harness, leaps up—"
as he was leaping “and mixes itself with the pure air of heaven,
hating the sinful house of the flesh in which it lived! It leaps-ah!
—it mingles-it ascends-it rises to God. . . ." They were all
leaping, trying like earthbound creatures to ascend-trying to
burst forth from the corrupt prison of flesh, their open mouths
sucking in the holy air, their eyes lit up like candles in heaven...
Then another strange thing happened. An old woman shrieked
and, tearing at herself, pitched downward, writhing and frothing;
and from screaming voices Damon made out that a demon had
entered her. Those around her were showing anxiety and fright.
A man who held some kind of official position came up to her and
41
in a loud voice commanded the demon to depart. He put sharp
questions to the tormented writhing woman and after a few mo-
ments her struggles ceased and she listened. "Do you know that
you are divine by nature and a daughter of God?" He repeated
the question over and over, his voice rising in the scale of anger
and rebuke; and at last the woman said softly, "Yes." "Is it the
vision of God that imparts new life?" "Yes." "Is your soul fixed
on this vision?" "Yes." "You are I and I am you and we are one
in the Christ." "Yes!" "You have had a birth of understanding.
The demon has fled. Arise." The woman got up and turned from
one to another, beaming on her friends.
