•
That was a miracle. Such miracles convinced these people that
they alone were the chosen of God. Murdia slipped up to ask,
"Now do you believe?" Before Damon could stammer a reply a
number of the Christians were seized by religious transports. The
demon had fled. They knew that God was in their presence. One
of them cried, “I stretch out my hands and I approach my Lord—”
he stretched his arms to the sky and others did likewise and all
were crying: “—and I approach my Lord, for the stretching of my
hands is his sign! I am of no account to those who do not take
hold of me, and I shall be with those who love me. ." Murdia
seized Damon's arms and raised them. "Look, Damon! Such is
his love for those who believe in him!" This chant, she said, was
from the Odes of Solomon. "Like the arm of the bridegroom over
the bride-See!" she said and put his right arm round her, and
glancing at Levilla he saw that a man had his arm around her.
"-so is my yoke over those that know me! And as the couch.
that is spread in the house of the bridegroom and bride, so is my
love over those that believe in me! . . ." All the women were
now encircled by the arms of men, all eyes but Damon's were
staring at the heavens. He was looking at the emotion in Murdia's
face and asking in his heart, Mother, is this it?
Murdia wanted Damon to be baptized, but first, he said, he
would witness a baptism. It was early one morning. A middle-
aged woman, a slave, was being accepted as a member, and when
Damon came on the scene, rubbing at the sleep in his eyes, they
were gathered round her chanting these words:
Awake, you sleeper,
rise up from among the dead
and he who is Christ the Lord shall enlighten you!
The sun of the resurrection
42
begotten before the dayspring
giving life by his beams!
Baptism, so far as he could tell, was an act of purifying, a cleans-
ing of the body by immersion, as well as a symbol of repentance
and new life. It was the rite of admission to membership. It
united the initiate in a mystic bond with his Christ. This woman
was immersed in his name and received the sign of the Christ,
that she might be recognized as one of his own on the day of judg-
ment.
"Do you see now?" Murdia whispered.
After the baptism came hymns and prayers, followed by a holy
meal. At this meal, Murdia told him, their Christ was present.
The meal itself was very simple; it was a small piece of black bread
for each person, and an earthen cup of wine that was chiefly water.
As Damon ate the piece of bread Murdia told him that he was
eating the flesh of the Christ; and when he sipped the wine and
water he was drinking the savior's blood. He looked at her with
horror-for it was this way in the rites of Mithra! Yet it was clear
to him that these childlike people actually believed that they were
absorbing the flesh and blood of their Lord and infusing their
bodies with his mystical and holy essence.
The one who served as the chief official then uttered a prayer:
"When you have eaten enough this shall be your thanksgiving:
we thank you, holy Father, for your holy name, which you have
caused to dwell in our hearts, and for knowledge, faith and im-
mortality, which you have made known to us by your servant
Jesus! To you the glory forever and ever! We thank you!"
He then called on those present to search their hearts and con-
sciences, to be sure that they were pure. The pure in heart,
Murdia whispered to him, would see God. When the sun came up
they all arose to hail it, and from some book he had read Damon
recalled the words, the sun of the resurrection. That was his first
clue but he missed it then. He wondered why they saluted the
sun, and why, when shouting hosannahs to it, they turned so
wildly to kiss one another. He looked at the sun as he had never
looked at it before.
Levilla also begged him to become a Christian but he held back,
for lodged in his mind were his father's words: “There are many
religions. If one of them has more truth than another, you will
know that only if you are familiar with all of them." He remem-
bered how his father had squeezed his hand when Seneca said,
43
"If one should tell you that he has sought and found, do not be-
lieve him; but believe him who tells you that he found without
seeking." Yet who, he wondered, smiling grimly down in himself,
ever sought more deliberately than he was seeking now! He
wanted to leave Rome. He wanted to become a student in one of
the great halls of learning-at Alexandria or at Rhodes or at
Antioch; and he might have gone forth before his twentieth birth-
day if a dreadful thing had not happened in the world.
At this time Damon knew little about the Jews, though he had
talked to a number of them. He knew that they were aloof and
clannish, that they were held in contempt for their strange prac-
tices and beliefs and despised throughout the Roman world. One
of their beliefs was that a Jewish king was coming, the root of
Jesse, a scion of David, though on the nature of this king and his
powers Jews seemed not to agree. A Jew told Damon that the king
would destroy Rome and enthrone the Jews above all people on
earth. But would he do this with love or with a sword? The
fanatical zealots said he would do it with a sword and they were
eager to assist him—so eager indeed that they plunged their people
into war with the mighty Empire. For them it was unthinkable
that the unclean heathen should rule over the chosen people,
whose coming king would be infinitely more powerful than all the
Caesars put together.
The awful struggle was begun in spite of the counsel and oppo-
sition of influential Jewish leaders when a Roman seized a part
of the temple treasure in Jerusalem and the Jews thereupon re-
fused the daily sacrifice to the emperor. That refusal was a declara-
tion of war. It was only later that Damon learned what happened.
The Roman general Vespasian laid siege to the holy city, and the
bodies of thousands who died from hunger were thrown outside
the city walls, where they rotted in the sun. Then Titus laid
siege with a tremendous force of four legions-it had taken only
three to conquer Armenia. The Jews fought with a desperation
and a courage that Romans had never before encountered on the
field of battle; hundreds of Jewish towns were completely de-
stroyed; hundreds of thousands of Jews perished from disease, star-
vation, fire or the sword. It was such a slaughter as Roman legions
had not made since Julius swept like a plague over Gaul.
The news began to reach Rome almost at once and again the
Christians were beside themselves. They looked at the sky and
waited. But it was a dreadful war that would run almost four
44
years, ending with the destruction of the temple and the sack of
Jerusalem. Damon was again amazed by the behavior of the Chris-
tians. They thought their God was destroying the Jews for their
presumption in not accepting the one they called the messiah.
They thought that this destruction foretold the second coming of
their Lord. When at last Jerusalem fell and the temple was re-
duced to ashes, the joy of the Christians in Rome knew no bounds:
they were far more violent than they had been when Rome burned:
a fire might herald the return of their Lord; the destruction of
Jerusalem and its temple did herald it, beyond all doubt. What
other sign could possibly be of such magnitude as this casting
down by the Almighty of a people who had rejected the savior,
born among them and one of themselves?
Not a Christian in Rome had the slightest doubt that their Lord
would descend from the sky at any moment. The few who had
property gave it away. When news came prematurely that Jerusa-
lem had fallen, Damon one night was observing their horrible
frenzies and thinking of his mother when Levilla rushed upon him
like an insane woman and began to dance him round and round.
It took him some time to realize that she was maneuvering him
away from the group and into a grove of trees; and what happened
then he would never be able to recall clearly. He was shocked
and horrified when, coming to his senses, he knew that they had
fallen down or flung themselves down and he had embraced her.
He was even more shocked when he perceived that Levilla was not
at all aware of it. She did not know that she had just lost her
maidenhead. She was so completely out of herself with religious
and erotic passion that she did not know that she was with a man,
or where she was; and Damon was so disgusted with her and so
shamed by what he had done that he slunk away, loathing himself,
swearing by the memory of his father that he would never see her
or the Christians again.
The war dragged on and the frenzies in the Friends subsided,
for the reason that they were exhausted and spent. They took
up their vigil and some of them watched the sky day and night.
Even a woman as vain as Murdia came to look like an unwashed
hag; and Levilla became so weak that she could only whisper
when she prayed. To heap madness upon madness, messiahs and
prophets, both Christian and Jew, began to appear-and Damon,
mixing with both people, was amazed by what he saw and heard.
Jews were saying that on the day their temple was destroyed their
45
king was born: would his reign on earth last forty, sixty or seventy
years? They did not agree on that but they did agree that in forty
or sixty or seventy years the kingdom of the Jews would be ushered
in. Whom, Damon wondered, was a person to be more amazed by,
whom pity more, the Jews or the Christians!
He did not keep his vow to his father but went to the cemetery
where the Christians kept their vigil. They were so famished and
weak that some of them were now unable to stand, or to utter words
in more than a whisper; but they tried to keep up their chanting,
their prayers, their welcoming cries. They believed that the dead
in their graveyard would stand up the moment their Lord ap-
peared; and so it was that they divided their attention between
the sky, where he would show himself on a cloud, and the earth,
from which their dead would rise. Some of them looked back and
forth, back and forth.
•
Damon went to Levilla, lying on her back, her white starved
face looking up. As so many times he looked at her belly, though
almost a year had passed since he lay with her.
"Levilla-"
"Hush," she admonished him in a feeble whisper.
"Levilla," he said sadly, glancing up at the sky, "there's no one
coming."
"Please be still."
"Was this what my mother died for, this madness?”
"Shame on you."
"Are you going to lie here till you die? There's no one coming.”
"You blaspheme!" she whispered, her voice hoarse from praying.
"I'm going away, Levilla. To some university to study. There
is no truth to be found here."
"You are wicked."
"Maybe I'll go to the land of the Jews. Maybe someone there
still lives who saw your Jesus or heard of him." He saw her lips
moving. He felt anger when he realized that she was praying for
him. When her lips were still he said: “Even if your Lord is com-
ing, why do you think he'll come to Rome? Wouldn't it be more
logical to think that he'll come to the land of his birth?”
"You are wicked," she whispered.
"Will you go with me?"
She was silent a few moments. Then she said: "If-if he doesn't
come here, I'll go with you."
He completely misunderstood her. He thought she meant that
46
if her Lord did not come to Rome she would be his wife or mis-
tress. He learned a few weeks later that she had not meant that at
all.
"How do you mean you'll travel with me?"
"As your sister."
"Sister! But what would people think of us?"
"I'm pure," she said. "I'm a bride in Christ."
She baffled and annoyed him. There came to him lines from
Catullus: My dearest, unless I love you desperately, always, for-
ever, more than the fondest lover in the world, may I be dropped
in the African desert to face a greeneyed lioness! What did she
mean that she would love him as a sister? He asked her that.
Her smile was indulgent, pitying and very tired.
What had the poet said?—I'd kiss your honeysweet lips five
hundred thousand times, yet have never enough, not even if our
kisses sprang thicker than barley! How could she be unaware of
their intimacy that night? Did she sense nothing in the poet's
words-Suns disappear and again return; when our brief light
burns down and dies, darkness remains and endless sleep! No:
she would see her Lord soon, she would be his bride, he would
take her to heaven.
She was looking at him. "How much you have to learn," she
said.
Yes, he thought, how much! He had to learn what his mother
had meant, and what this girl meant when she said that she was a
bride in Christ. He had to learn what she meant by the word
sister.
To put her off he said, "Travel on the sea is dangerous."
"I'm not afraid."
"I may never come back to Rome."
"When our Lord returns there'll be no coming back."
"There is the risk of your being abducted and sold into a
brothel."
"Our Lord watches over the pure in heart."
"It'll cost me more if we have separate beds."
"I can sleep with you and still be pure.'
""
That dashed him. Was the woman mad? "Why should you go
with me at all?”
"To learn if our Lord has come somewhere else."
"Thanks," he said, his vanity stung. "But what about the signs?
You Christians say when he comes there'll be fires in the sky,
47
stricken stars, cloudbursts, a bloody moon, the sun falling out of
its course. You say when he comes this city of Caesars will be
consumed by a flame from heaven, that this Babylon whore will be
a lake of fire and brimstone. Well," he said, looking over at the
city, "it still stands."
"The Devil is on you," she said.
"But be reasonable, Levilla. You have said, you have all said,
that this land will proclaim his coming more than any other, for
it has Aetna and Vesuvius and Lipari and other volcanoes and
beds of sulphur."
She turned her head to look at him. Her eyes were steady and
cold, her mouth prim. “When do we go?" she said.
48
V
Damon decided to wait until the season of storm had passed, be-
cause travel on the seas was dangerous at best. There was also
the matter of time. The average summer passage from Rome to
Alexandria took about eighteen days, but was shortened during
the period of the Etesian winds. The imperial post-service was
the speediest on land; they took this to Puteoli, the center of sea-
borne traffic in Italy. They then embarked on a corn-ship for
Alexandria, and at once to spare his purse Levilla offered to share
his bed.
It was not necessary, he said. He had funds.
"But you don't know how pure I am."
He exploded. "O great God, yes, I do! Yes, that's one thing I do
know! You're so damned pure I expect you to take off for heaven
before we reach Alexandria, with a whole flock of angels singing
your praises!"
"You never will," she said.
He looked at her hard for a moment and turned away.
He felt homesick, leaving Rome, leaving the spot where his
mother had died, his father's grave; and he searched over the ship
to learn if any Christians were there. Most of the professions were
migratory: there was constant movement from land to land of
lecturers, physicians, prophets, sorcerers, actors, merchants, as
well as a multitude of sharpers engaged in one practice or another,
such as abduction of the unwary into slavery or whoredom. David
fell in with a friendly and learned Jew who took ship along the
way.
This man, a merchant, knew more about the Christians than
49
anyone David had talked to in Rome. He said there were several
sects, perhaps a score or more, all violently disagreeing with one
another. The sect that lived in Jerusalem before that city fell had
all been Jews-an impoverished illiterate fanatical group who be-
lieved that the long-expected Jew-king had come and had been
hanged by the Romans. He told Damon there had been many
false kings, all of whom the Romans had destroyed. This Jerusa-
lem sect were Essenes. They had fled to Pella. He did not know
much about them, he said-only that they had quarreled with
one named Saul who called himself Paul, and that they called
themselves Nazarenes because their messiah had lived in a place
called Nazareth.
Was it true, Damon asked, that Jews awaited a king who would
overthrow Rome. The response to the indiscreet question was a
polite shrug.
"The saffron around Mount Corycus is of fine quality. I im-
port it to Italy where it is used for medicines and scents."
Damon flushed. He knew that he had been rebuked.
The Jew said: "Most people have some childlike beliefs, haven't
they? Why are you so interested in these matters?"
"My mother was a Christian. They burned her to death.”
The Jew lightly pressed his arm and turned away.
After they became better acquainted David ben Huna talked
more freely. It was true, he said, that Artapanus, a Jewish his-
torian, had written a history of the Jews, in which all the knowl-
edge and institutions of the Egyptians had been taken from Israel;
in which Abraham instructed the Pharaohs and Joseph taught
them better forms of cultivation; in which one called Moses gave
them their gods.
David shrugged. "Some of my people say, The King of the
world shall raise us up, who have died for his laws. What are
these but hawkers with a bundle on their back? A son of David
will come to destroy Rome? Who is it who gleans the last kernels
in the barley field?"
Damon was silent. He was confused by the man's habit of talk-
ing in riddles and parables.
"Have you seen the Christians in Antioch?"
"No," Damon said, wondering why these things amused David.
"They have a savior or lord but they also call him the messiah.
Messiah means simply to anoint. So where did they get the notion
50
of a savior-god? Not from Jews. For Jews such an idea is blas-
phemy."
Damon struggled with a question. "For these Nazarenes, is
the messiah only a man?"
"Only that. He couldn't be more for a Jew who is still a Jew.
For the Christians in Rome he was a man who became a god, for
those in Antioch he was a god who took up mortal flesh. For most
of those in Alexandria he is neither." David smiled. “My dear
man, if you've undertaken to learn who the Christians are and
where they got their beliefs you're going to be busy a long time.
Have you read the writings of Paul?"
"No," Damon said.
"You will, of course. You'll talk to Christians who hold by
gnosis and be astonished. You'll see their secret mysteries in Alex-
andria and be astonished again. You'll see them flogging beasts
and scourging one another in Corinth.”
Damon was staring at the man's face. "You mean there are as
many kinds of Christians as there are places where they live? You
mean there's no Christian but only Christians?"
David smiled. "You do have intuitive insights," he said. "There
is no stone but only stones, no truth but only truths. You know,
I'd think you should go to Pella first."
"Why?"
"Because the old men there are the originals. Maybe some of
them saw Jesus and remember him."
"I'll go there,” Damon said.
"What surprises are ahead of you! You'll find Christians who
say their messiah was not killed but lived to be an old man and
died a natural death in bed. You'll find some who say he had
golden hair. So," said David drily, "has Apollo. You'll find
some who say he was black. So is the Egyptian sun-god. Are
you familiar with what Xenophanes said?"
“No,” said Damon, feeling borne down by the burden of his
ignorance.
“He said a Negro's god has a flat nose. The Thracians have
blue eyes and so their god has blue eyes. He said that if an ox had
a god it would of course look like an ox. And I'm sure that Yah-
weh looks like me."
Damon gave off a snort. He liked this man. He thought David
was about thirty-five and very sophisticated, cynical and life-weary.
"If you ever wish to learn about my people go to Jamnia. The
51
wise men are there with beards ten cubits long. Did you know that
men are always more wicked when together than when alone?"
"No," said Damon, staring at him.
"Philosophy would be impossible if that weren't true."
When they left the ship Damon asked Levilla to go overland
with him to Pella. She was offended. If these people were Jews,
she said haughtily, she had no wish to see them.
Damon said gently: "But your Jesus was a Jew."
"I'll stay here," she said. She meant in Caesarea.
He gave her money-she would accept only a little—and then
hired a guide with two donkeys and set out for Scythopolis, which
was close to the river and across from Pella. The guide spoke
Greek. Knowing that they were just south of the land called Gali-
lee Damon asked him if he had ever been in the village called
Nazareth. The guide had not, and he knew of no such place. Had
he ever heard of Jesus of Nazareth, a Jew who was hanged by the
Romans? He had not.
This journey into the land of the Jews was for Damon a vivid
experience. He was beguiled by the lovely hills and flowers and
the breezes blowing in from the sea, until he descended into the
hot sweltering pestilential abyss of the Jordan River. He thought
he would suffocate. The air was almost palpable heat; the sun
above was a golden furnace. After finding a ford and crossing
the river the guide turned back; and with a packet of food and
water over his shoulder David climbed the hot desolate hills to
Pella, the shabbiest and ugliest village he was ever to see.
He was appalled by the people he found there. Nomads fresh
from the desert heat and winds and sands could not have looked
more sunbaked and timeless than this handful of Jews. David
had warned him to be careful of his speech with these people; he
had said that if he spoke of Jesus as the son of God or of these peo-
ple as worshipers of a Lord they would rend him limb from limb.
Damon felt at once their implacable hostility and was afraid. He
realized afterward that they were a wounded people because of
the recent destruction of Jerusalem and its holy places and the
killing or selling into slavery of tens of thousands of Jews.
As he approached the village no dog barked. David had told
him that these people were dog-haters. A gaunt old man, a man
who might have been a hundred or a thousand years old, came
forward and faced him; and when Damon saw the hostility in the
black eyes he trembled with fear. What had he come to say? To
52
ask who their Jesus was, what powers he had, how he had lived and
died. To ask why they hated the one named Paul. When at last
he was able to stammer into speech he realized that the old man
did not understand a word: he had been foolish enough to imagine
that these Jews would speak his tongue or the tongue of Rome.
Damon said he had come to learn about them and he might as
well have spoken to a man stone-deaf. The man indicated that
he was to go away, and when Damon made no move to go, other
men came forward, their bodies full of menace.
Damon turned away because there was nothing else to be done.
He looked back at the village, recalling what David had said, that
these were the original disciples of the one called Jesus. He sat
on the desolate hillside and looked away toward Rome and felt
forsaken; and after his tears were falling he murmured again,
"Mother, what does it mean?" He arose, weeping, and walked
away and came to a goatherd who spoke Greek and who told him
some things about these people. He said all other Jews despised
them, they were pariahs and outcasts; they spent so much time
praying that their knees were as calloused as their heels; those who
would listen to them they summoned to repentance and prayer;
and they did indeed believe that the long-awaited Jew-king had
come, but with peace instead of a sword. Damon asked who were
the parents of this Jesus and the goatherd said he thought their
names were Joseph and Mariam. What had Jesus been like? The
goatherd said he was a wonder-worker who traveled through the
land, healing the sick, raising the dead, calling the people to re-
pentance. The Romans had hanged him on a tree, as they had
hanged a hundred other men, or a thousand, who had claimed to
be the messiah. All these things, the man said, had happened be-
fore he was born.
With the whole thing slipping way from him into the mists
Damon asked if any of the people in Pella had known this Jesus.
The goatherd did not know. He had heard that Jesus had brothers
and sisters. What did these people in Pella call themselves? The
Disciples, he said.
From another he learned that they not only hated Paul but told
this story about him. Paul, they said, was a Greek, not a Jew; he
had gone to Jerusalem and fallen in love with the daughter of the
high priest, when, hoping to win her, he became a proselyte and
was circumcised. Failing to win the girl he became the angry and
bitter enemy of Jews and wrote books that denounced their cus-
53
toms. Damon also learned that these people gave no account of
the birth and childhood of Jesus, did not claim for him a miracu-
lous origin, did not believe that he had risen from the dead. Jesus
for them had been only a Jewish teacher, a kind of prophet with
great powers, who was normally born of mortal parents and was
hanged because of his teachings.
Trudging along Damon told himself that if there had been a
Jesus and if there were any people in the world who had known
him in the flesh they were certainly among the elders in Pella.
He toyed with the idea of learning to speak their language, of try-
ing to win their confidence but this terrible land was a blight on
his spirit and he hurried back to Caesarea, asking himself over and
over one question: if the Disciples did not believe in a miraculous
birth, resurrection and ascension, where had other Christians got
these ideas?
He found Levilla among the Christians in Caesarea. She greeted
him coldly, her eyes full of suspicions and unhappiness. She had
not accepted the Christians here as Christians, for they did not be-
lieve in a risen god, they only scoffed at some of the things she had
said. She was eager to go to Antioch. So they took a ship up the
coast, with Damon staring through the mists of Galilee toward
Pella; and they came to the mouth of the Orontes and by smaller
boat sailed up the river to his father's birthplace. On their way
he told Levilla that if anyone still alive had known Jesus he must
be a Jew-for Jesus was a Jew and had lived all his life in the land
of his people. Silent, brooding, Levilla stared at him and said
nothing. Surely, Damon said, some of the old men in Pella had
known him, yet they did not believe that he arose from the dead
and ascended to heaven. So what did it all mean? Did it mean-
he had been turning this thought over and over-did it mean
that Jesus and the Christ were utterly different things?
She allowed her frozen face to give him a faint smile of pity. "I
believe in our Lord," she said.
Gently Damon asked her, "But who is he?"
"The one who came to redeem us from sin."
"A son of God? But Jews say their God has no son."
"Jews are stupid," she said.
"No, Levilla. Jews may be many things but they are not stupid."
He was silent a little while. Then he said: "Will you read with
me in the libraries here?"
Her lips curled. "Do children born of slaves know how to read?"’
54
"I was born a slave. I will teach you."
She shook her head no in the sad stubborn way that baffled him.
She said whether Christians could read did not matter at all, for
they had the truth, all of it, and the world would soon end.
"If I leave Antioch will you go with me?"
"Yes."
Astonished, he looked at her. "You mean you'll go with me

"I love our Lord," she said. "When you become one of us you
will know not the sinful love in the flesh but the holy love in the
spirit."
Sometimes he wanted to shake her. "You'll never marry me?"
"Never."
"Nor anyone?"
"Nor anyone."
On the way to Antioch she had sat, indifferent to the beautiful
sea and the unfolding panorama of city and coastline, her gaze
turned inward to some vision that obsessed her. So it seemed to
Damon. She ate a little but seemed unaware of what she was eat-
ing. He understood now that she could share his bed and be
wholly unaware of his presence in it.
The people of Antioch, he had heard, were excitable, fickle,
keen, quickwitted, irreverent, dissolute, with an inordinate love
of handsome buildings, theaters, temples, baths and public spec-
tacles. Their lives opened outward upon their senses. Those who
could afford to wore broad-brimmed hats dyed purple; the richly
embroidered chlamys; garments shot with gold thread; and fine
ornamented boots. In spite of their tireless pursuit of pleasure
there was, it seemed to him, something sad in the Romans. In the
Antiochans there was only joy. They drank a great deal and many
of them were never sober; and in the park of Daphne he was to
see sexual ardor possess whole multitudes in unashamed and child-
like innocence.
Because his father had been born in Antioch and had been a
scholar there Damon felt a sentimental interest in the place. He
had been told that it was the most beautiful city between Parthia
and Spain and he thought it so. Rome was a city of marble and
it was cold. Most of its streets were ugly crooked paths. At night
it was lighted only by moon or stars or by slaves carrying torches to
guide their masters. Antioch had been laid out by an architect
on the lower Orontes, against the myrtle-clad hills of the Casian
55
range. The clear sweet waters of a river ran through it. At first
view, as Damon approached it, the most impressive feature was the
line of the wall-it had a circumference of seven miles-where it
climbed precipices, crossed ravines and torrents, and fell in spans
like an immense cable from tower to tower. In the north part of
the city the river divided and encircled a lovely island. In the
south part it rested on steep slopes. In the southwest were the
finest rose gardens he was ever to see. The main street was wide
and clean; fresh water poured down the gutters, bearing away the
refuse, which in most cities accumulated and rotted. After dark
the principal streets were well-lighted, making Antioch unique
among all the cities of the Empire.
The gardens of Daphne, which he soon explored, were in the
shape of an amphitheater amidst beautiful wild scenery; they had
many cascades, numerous fountains bursting from laurel groves,
and flowers literally everywhere. Of colored marble, the temple's
pride, and the city's, was an enormous statue of Apollo designed
by Bryaxis. This monument, reaching almost to the temple's
roof, was of vinewood covered with golden peplos, the exposed
parts of the body being of white marble, the hair of gold inter-
twined with golden laurel wreaths, the eyes of huge brilliant
jacinth. Apollo held a bowl in one hand, a harp in the other.
Damon gazed at the god's image, remembering David's statement,
that for some Christians their Lord had golden hair.
Damon loved the city. After a few weeks of exploring he wanted
to make his permanent home there but his home was always to be
with his mother. He liked the climate. The air and water were
purer than Rome's. The breezes were laden with the sweet odors
of sea, river and flowering hills. Why not, he asked himself, be-
come a scholar like his father, and was delighted with the prospect.
He had left his properties in Rome in the hands of a broker, who
from time to time sent him letters of credit in sums far beyond his
needs. He was young, healthy, independent, of good appearance;
he could choose his own way of life. That is what he thought but
he did not know how deep into his soul terror and loneliness had
gone. He walked back and forth over the city wondering on what
spot his father had been born, in what house he had lived; and
he would look at the hills with their myrtle and think, He looked
at that; at the river, He looked at that. His father had been fond
of Seneca's words: No man can live happily who regards himself
alone... it behooves you to live for another if you would live

- for yourself. He was willing to live for Levilla but she was living
for her brothers and sisters in the new faith. On arriving in Anti-
och he had supplied her generously with money, and weeks later,
looking at her wan face and ragged garments, he asked her if she
was out of funds. She said she was.
"I gave you a lot," he said. "Levilla, what do you do with it?”
When she looked at him with hostile eyes and was silent he asked,
"Do you give it away?”
"Yes."
"If I were to give you all I have I suppose you'd bestow it on
beggars."
"Beggars, do you say? They are my brothers and sisters in
Christ."
"You starve yourself and wear rags-

With an angry gesture she interrupted him. Those who lived

"Each day I'll give you enough for the day's needs."

What was he to do with her? She never ate enough to nourish
her body; she wore only the coarsest garments; she used no cos-
metics or scents, believing that all beauty was in the spirit; and
she got so she would not sleep in a room, not even in the least ex-

new faith some great force for good, some oasis of love, some shel-

As for Levilla he imagined that in some strange way, too dark
and deep for his insights, she loved him, not as a woman loved a
man but as some kind of symbol, as a strength above the weak (or
was that it?), perhaps as birdsong above the screams of the arena.
Whatever it was, she was to be his always, and he was to be hers,
in a way that he would never understand.
This time he spent three years in Antioch and gave most of his
hours to books. Levilla gave her time to the sick and old or to the
57
small orphans among her people. Damon read chiefly the philos-

he strove with almost frantic zeal to understand the sources of

Christian beliefs.
And always, day and night, he was eager to listen to the scholars
who came his way.

VI
He said his name was Socrates but Damon thought that possibly
he was jesting. A man in late middle age he wore only rags but
they were clean, and carried a hawthorn staff in which he had worn
depressions almost as deep as his fingers. Most of his teeth were
gone and he spoke with a sibilant lisp but you were not aware of
that if you looked at his eyes. They were a very deep blue and they
danced a perfect commentary on his thoughts. Such marvelous
lights of kindliness and goodwill, of subtlety and humor, Damon
was seldom to see in a human face. His hair was gray, with some
streaks much whiter than others; his brows were white; but his
short thick beard was not. Damon suspected that he dyed it, re-
serving beyond the discipline of his rigorous standards this one
vanity to soothe his last lonely years.
Socrates came to the library now and then, where it was his
habit to go from reader to reader and peer over their shoulder, to
see what engaged their interest; and one day, finding Damon lost
in a strange wild thing called the Book of Enoch, he whispered in
his ear, "What a waste of time!" When he left the building Damon
followed him and they sat on a bench by the river and talked.
"You think I waste my time?"
"Fantasies by foolish men written for children! Doesn't it say
that the final assault of world powers will be wrecked on the rock
of Zion, and one with a head as white as wool will ascend the
throne, and with him one whose countenance is like that of man.
His name was before the stars were formed. On the day of judg-
ment the godless shall be burned in a lake of fire, and a great bull
will come forth with horns. Young man, I ask you, what non-
sense is this?”
59
Damon said he had been reading the book only because cer-
tain Christians were devoted to it.
"Ah, those wretched and deluded people! What are they but a
council of frogs croaking? For their sakes the world was created!"
"But this book," Damon said, looking at the man's marvelous
eyes, "was written by a Jew."
"The Jews! Another council of frogs. Upon them, the chosen
of God, rests the salvation of the whole human race. Can human
presumption go farther than that? Jews and Christians, both
dangerous because self-idolatrous. Tell me, young man, why
should a person consider himself so superior to the lion and the
horse as to assume that God has human form? Or tell me why
God chose to show himself to mankind as a Jew."
Damon was silent a few moments. He said: "Christians be-
lieve in love."
"What is love? Virtue alone is good."
"But can there be virtue without love?”
"You are younger than I thought. Self-control, my brother, is
the essence of virtue. Is it a virtue to hate those who will not ac-
cept your beliefs?"
A few days later Damon told the gray philosopher that what
he most wanted to know was where the Christians came from.
Did anybody know?
"An offshoot of the Jews," said Socrates, waving toward the
south with his stick. "Yonder the superstition was born and now
it is spreading everywhere, to women, children, idlers, quacks,
fakers, impostors, slaves. What is the great appeal of this doctrine?
This," he said, poking at Damon with his stick. “Be baptized, re-
pent, inherit eternal life, all in the time it would take you to pick
off a scab or blow out a sneeze."
But was repentance that simple, Damon asked, thinking the old
man too glib and superficial. And was there not more to it? The
common people had no religion; in Rome worship was the priv-
ilege of wealth, and even in Greece foreigners and slaves were ex-
cluded from religious feasts. Was not religion everywhere only
for those who could afford it?—but Jesus had said, Come to me,
all you who are weary and heavy-laden. The Christians were say-
ing to all people, Come-to the poor, the outcast, the sick, the
homeless-all. Had any religion ever said that before?
The eyes twinkled on Damon, amused, tolerant, kindly. "If it's
a religion for the stupid and illiterate of what good can it be?
60
Only the few have the power to sense the promptings of the spirit.
For all others it is ritual, a slipping into heaven by the back door."
Damon knew he was no match for such a facile adversary. He
said: "But don't people deserve more than their rags? If they
must eat lentils and black bread and leeks and locusts and
go half-
naked and be enslaved and despised and worked to death and then
cast out to die would you deny them this comfort of a faith?”
"Oh no, no—but deny to an intelligent man like you the right
to see in it more than is there."
"For the first time in human history—
99
"Yes, brother, yes, I know what you'd say. It sounds like a good
thing to you, does it? But look, a religion that the simple can
understand must make the way to heaven pretty easy. Isn't that
so?"
"I guess it is," said Damon, wondering if a snare was being laid.
"Very well, now follow me. If a religion is accepted solely on
faith and makes entrance to heaven simple and easy, in time the
whole of mankind may be infected with such mediocrity of the
spirit and drag all life down to that level. Had you ever thought
of that?"
Damon had not. He said he would think about it.
Socrates went on: "Philosophy can't elevate the imbecile but if
enough imbeciles get in positions of power they can drag philos-
ophy down to their level. The forces in mankind that would des-
cend are stronger than those that would climb, and may always
be. Very well, then, this new faith, which appeals to the ignorant,
and only so far as I can see to them, may some day debase man-
kind. For look! The Jews don't go to heaven the easy way. They
get there with meditation and good deeds. But this rabble called
Christian, how do they get to heaven? Why, bless you, simply with
faith and an act of grace. Any idiot or scoundrel in the world has
only to come crying repentance and to believe and the kingdom
is flung open to him.”
"But if he is sincere?"
"It's too easy. It's a thousand times easier than the Jewish way."
"You don't think this new faith can ever appeal to enlightened
people?"
"It can conquer them.
How many times in after years Damon was to recall those
words: It can conquer them!
61
On still another day Damon asked the old man a question:
What was the chief thing in this new faith?
"Denial of life in this world," Socrates said. "And what would
that lead to if it should ever become supreme? The end of man-
kind, would you say?"
"I don't know," Damon said, feeling wretched. The old phil-
osopher made him so unhappy that he would go away and sit
alone by himself and weep, thinking of his mother in the fire,
of his father's broken spirit in the last days of his life. And then
with renewed frenzy he would read again, determined to find
somewhere in books a great truth that no philosopher could throw
down.
It was during this time that he met Elisha, a Jew who was to
become a close friend. Elisha was a few years older and for Damon
extremely learned and extremely skeptical. Hearing one day that
Damon was reading what Elisha called the fables of the Jews—at
⚫the moment the stories of creation and of Moses-Elisha was cur-
ious to know why. Damon said feebly that he was looking for
similarities between the faith of the Jews and that of the Christ-
ians. Did Elisha think there had been a Jew named Jesus who
was crucified?
Oh, it was possible, Elisha said. He had once heard that such a
man had wandered over Israel with five disciples, named Mattai,
Naquai, Netser, Buni, and Todah. "But what," he asked, “is Net-
ser but a pun on Nostrim, meaning Christian?”
Damon stared at him. It was impossible to tell when Elisha
was serious and when he was spinning fables of his own.
Elisha told him a story-and Damon was to find variants of it
all over the world. A certain Jew named Yochanan espoused one
Mariam, a virgin. Mariam, a very lovely girl, attracted the interest
of a handsome scoundrel named Joseph Pandera, who seduced
and betrayed her. He did this under cover of darkness, when the
innocent Mariam, believing the wooer to be her husband, gladly
surrendered. Great was her shame when she learned that the man
was not her husband. Great was Yochanan's anger when he found
his wife pregnant by another man. He fled to Babylon.
Mariam gave birth to a son and called him Yehoshua, corrupted
to Yeshu, which in Greek was Jesus. It was a precocious child
and now and then confounded the sages. In the holy temple-so
the story ran-Jesus learned the ineffable and secret name of the
Jewish Yehweh, and to preserve it wrote it on a piece of leather,
62
which he then sewed under the skin of his thigh. He gathered a
group of disciples, proclaimed himself the long-awaited king, and
performed miracles and wonders by using the secret name.
Alarmed, the wise men arranged for one of their number, Yehuda
Iskarioto, to learn the name and also perform wonders. Jesus was
at last brought before the rulers, condemned to death and taken.
to Tiberias and imprisioned; but his disciples, who believed him
to be the king, rescued him and fled with him to Antioch. Some
time later Jesus went into Egypt and a second time to Jerusalem,
where he was discovered and hanged on a tree on Passover eve.
Lest he defile the Sabbath he was taken down a few hours later
and buried, but a gardener removed the body from the tomb and
disposed of it. When the disciples returned and were unable to
find the body they believed their king had risen from the dead, and
they proclaimed this belief as the glad news. Among these disci-
ples was a man named Shimeon Kepha—the Jewish word Kepha
meant peter or rock in Greek—who kept the faith alive among the
followers.
"But the sect in Pella don't think he rose from the dead."
"I know it."
"Maybe some of them knew him."
"Maybe."
"If he came to Antioch maybe there are people here who remem-
ber him."
A little sharply Elisha said: "You seem to be taking the story
pretty literally." He filled their cups with wine, pressed the tips
of his fingers together and said: “Who is this Jesus? Ben Stada,
the false messiah in Egypt who promised to throw down the walls
of the holy city? Jesus ben Pandera, hanged on a tree more than
a hundred years ago? Are the Christians confusing him with
Dionysus, or the Phoenix, or Jason, or Joshua? Jason equals
Joshua equals Jesus. Joshua with twelve helpers passed through
the Jordan. Jason with twelve went after the golden fleece. The
sun wanders through the twelve signs of the zodiac. If there was
a Jesus and he had disciples their number must be twelve, I sup-
pose, by analogy to all the other wonder-workers."
Damon nodded. His reading had told him that.
"Is it only the old sun-myth in a new form? What do you make
of Judas?"
"Who?"
"You haven't heard of Judas?"
63
"No."
"Well, some of the Christians say he was the twelfth apostle
who betrayed Jesus. In the religion of sun-worshipers the twelfth
month is the betrayer of the sun, that sickens at the winter sol-
stice. So what do we have?"
Silent, Damon lifted his cup of wine.
"By analogy to sun-gods when would Jesus be born?"
"At the winter solstice. You think the whole thing a sun-myth?"
"I don't think much about it. Isn't any religion chiefly myth
and legend?"
"Yours?"
"That of my people, you mean. Joshua and Samson were sun-
myths, I suppose. What Moses was I can't figure out but it's as
sure as sunrise that he wasn't Moses."
"What does his name mean?”
"I haven't found out. It seems to be the same as mesu, meaning
child or water-child. He was found in the bull-rushes, was he?"
Damon smiled.
Elisha put his fingertips together and stared down between his
hands. If Damon had known him well he would have realized
that the man was formulating some kind of symbol. When Damon
asked if a king was coming to the Jews Elisha looked up.
"Opinions differ," he said, his smile enigmatic. "Will he be
Daniel's son of man, coming on a cloud? — or Isaiah's maimed
and despised scapegoat, doomed to dishonorable death? Will he
be a powerful king like David? Will he rule four hundred years
and then die? Will triumphant Israel march at the head of a pen-
itent mankind?" He looked at Damon, a dry wry humor playing
over his features. "Isn't his coming foretold in the psalms and in
Ezekiel?"
Not knowing what to say, Damon said, “Is it?"
"That depends," said Elisha, "on how unhappy you are when
you read them."
Damon flushed. With what ease this man led him into snares!
Looking at Damon as if he had never seen him before and was
speculating on his nature Elisha said: "Since Jews keep a fire
burning for him in the Chamber of the Hearth what can he be
but the same old sun-god?"
They were silent. Damon would glance at Elisha's eyes and
look away and back. He was troubled. He said at last, "You mean
your people were sun-worshipers?"
64
"In a refined and subtle way they still are, aren't they?”
"All people?"
"All people. For Christians isn't their Christ a sun symbol?"
"Judas?"
"Oh, more than Judas. You see," Elisha went on, gazing be-
tween his hands, "the world was made to exist six thousand years,
our wise men say. Shall I challenge that? No. At the end of the
period the rule of their king will begin. This king was born the
day the temple was destroyed. Where is he now?”
Damon started. Was the question rhetorical?
"The wise men don't know where he is but he is somewhere,
growing up. How old is he now? Anyhow he is growing up, some-
where. Somewhere," said Elisha.
Again Damon was startled. He had not known that merely by
uttering a word a person could charge it with so much meaning.
Somewhere!
"Eventually," Elisha went on, “he will proclaim himself. Wild
beasts will then love men. Even more incredible than that, men
will love one another."
Damon was looking at the mischievous light in the man's eyes.
"People will live to be a thousand years old and will be neither
old nor weary. Women will bring forth without pain. The dead
will rise from their graves and proclaim the glory of God." Elisha
was silent, looking at him. "What does one say about the illusions
of childlike adults?-how sad or how beautiful?"
If only, Damon thought, my father could have known this man!
Curious to know what response he would get he said: "The Christ-
ians say the king has come."
Elisha looked at one of Damon's eyes and then the other, back
and forth, until Damon became ill at ease, thinking he had found
some imperfection or uncleanness. He knew, though, that it was
only another of Elisha's subtleties.
"I had heard that," Elisha said.
Damon was silent. He felt stupid and defenseless.
Making with his two hands what Damon took to be another
symbol, Elisha went on talking, gently, as though speaking to a
child. A Jew, he said, concluded his prayer with the word amen;
a Christian with the word verily. The Jew said the king would put
himself, like Judas the Maccabee, at the head of an armed force;
the Christian expected his Christ to come down on a cloud, accom-
panied by a host of singing angels. The Jew said a man could be
65
saved only with good deeds. The Christian said he could be saved
with faith alone. The Jew felt great joy in contemplating the
presence of God in the world. The Christian was borne down by
a chronic melancholy. For the Jew, sin was an act of willing and
men had free choice. For the Christian men were born in sin and
could be saved only by divine intercession and mercy. The Christ-
ian said, See the flowers in the field, how lovely they are, yet they
toil not nor spin nor take thought of the morrow. The Jew said,
We become godlike through constant willing and striving.
After completing his gently acidulous catalog of differences
Elisha looked Damon straight in the eye and asked a question that
he was never to forget. “How could a Jew ever exalt himself enough
to be a Christian, how could a Christian ever humble himself
enough to be a Jew?"
Damon considered the question a few moments and said:
"Maybe some day I'll know what you mean."
"If you'd understand these Christians in Antioch why don't you
go to the secret rites of the mysteries? Have you read the letters of
Saul who changed his name to Paul?"
"Two or three."
"Read all of them. You'll then perceive that his attitude toward
such things as baptism and what he calls the Lord's supper he took
straight from the heathen mysteries. Often his language is the
same. Baptism for Paul is a symbolic death and rebirth. The
holy meal in the mystery cults is an eating of the body and blood
of the slain god, as it is for Paul. One thing above all, I'd think,
you should understand now."
Damon looked at him and waited. He felt uneasy. More than
in Socrates he felt in this man a denial of his
quest.
Elisha said: "There isn't much in the religion of my people
that will help you understand Christians. You'll have to go to the
mystery cults of the heathen."
Again there was silence. To break it Damon asked: "Why
doesn't Paul ever mention Jesus?"
Elisha looked at him a few moments and then smiled. "Man,
you're getting close to it with that question. Paul was interested
only in the lord and savior idea, which is not a Jewish idea at all.”
Elisha fixed him with a stare and Damon knew that the man
was formulating another statement that would summarize an
infinity of questing and thinking. "Damon my friend, if you
would know the difference between Jews and Christians, keep one
66
fact always with you: Jesus and Christ are not the same thing.
A person may believe in either but no one in his right mind can
believe in both. If there was a Jesus he was a Jew, and probably
a fanatic, as so many of my people have been and are. The Christ
is the heathen son-of-the-father savior-god."
When alone Damon said the words over and over: Jesus and
Christ are not the same thing. Then what were the Christians
doing? What was the magic, what was the truth, in this new
faith? One day he heard that in Rome the Christians had pro-
duced a book and trembling with excitement he took the news to
Levilla.
"In Rome?" She looked at him with grave eyes in which he saw,
as always, quiet pity. "Who cares what they do in Rome?"
"But a book," Damon said. "A book that tells the whole story.
It was written by a man named Mark."
"Mark? I knew him."
"You knew him? Levilla, you knew him?"
"Mark, yes."
"Oh, but maybe that was another Mark."
As if it didn't matter at all she said: "I knew a Mark."
"Yes, but this Mark has written the gospel story. It tells what
happened."
"In Rome," she said, "they don't know what happened."
With pity and indulgence that enraged him she went on: "Here
we know. Our Savior lived before the sun and stars were made.
He was a pre-existent being. He was before the earth was. He
came down and took the sinful flesh of mortal man that we who
believe in him can be saved. Even you," she said, "when you come
to your senses.”
""
"How sweet you are," he said. "But in Rome, Levilla, they say
he was mortal man who became a god.
"Nobody cares what they say in Rome."
"But they're Christians too."

"What do you mean?"

With a blandness that staggered him she said: "Toward it.
Rome is away from it."
He looked at her, feeling as defenseless as when he looked at

"Damon," she said, her tone gentler, "become one of us and

"It's not a sister I want but a wife."
"Will you never realize," she asked, softly, quietly, infuriatingly,

"Levilla, look round you. The earth is fair and smells sweet,

"Paul says it's better not to marry."
"Paul? Who told you that?"
"Luke."
"And who is Luke?"

“Oh, what a foul and infamous and abominable lie!" Damon

With a blandness that maddened him she said, "We know they

"You know nothing of the kind! If he had to die to save such
arrogant little chits as you does it matter who killed him? God

68
and universe wasn't it? Do you condemn someone who had no

Levilla burst into tears. She came over and took one of Damon's

"I'm only a slave!" she murmured, sobbing. "I have nothing-
no parents, no brother or sister, no people. I have only my faith
and you would take that away."
His hatred dissolved into compassion. He put both arms
around her and tipped her face up and kissed her hard and full
on the lips. She did not resist. She astonished him by drawing
his head down for another kiss.
Like a weary child she asked, "Will you come back?"
"Yes," he said, looking across her hair at the world. "Yes, Le-
villa, I'll come back."

VII
The ship on which Damon took passage went down the coast
of Cilicia and he strained for a glimpse of Tarsus, where Paul
was born, but was told that it was inland on a river. It did not
matter; he would see it before his journeying was done. From
Rhodes, in whose university halls he was later to spend many
hours, there came aboard a huge fellow who took an instant fancy
to Damon. He regaled him with prodigious laughter and an
outpouring of obscenities all the way to Corinth.
He said his name was Optatus. About six feet four inches tall,
with broad shoulders as square as a box, a huge chest out of which
came his thunderous voice, and a great square face that matched
his form, he looked to Damon like a pirate but said he was a mer-
chant whose affairs took him over the world. With a huge hand
that could have inclosed two of Damon's he drew him aside to
talk confidentially.
In the hills north of Corinth, he said, he had a big slave-farm.
Knowing that it was the custom with some parents to put un-
wanted babies out to die, to abandon them in parks or other public
places, he had agents everywhere whose work it was to pick up
these babies, as some men gathered orphan lambs in lambing-
time; and all these children he took to his farm to raise and sell as
slaves. He also did a lucrative business in runaway slaves, and
told Damon that if he would become one of his informers he
would pay him a handsome commission on all sound babies and
escaped slaves turned over to him.
When Damon protested, Optatus paid no attention to him.
"The babies," he said, looking into Damon's eyes, "don't have
to be abandoned. Any babies will do." Then, after studying
70
Damon's face for a moment, while his own twitched, the man
roared with laughter. The incredible fellow seemed to think that
he could not speak without being witty. His laughter was so loud
and shattering that it assailed Damon's senses like a hard slap
across his ear; and his breath, which he blew upon him, smelled
of sour wine and garlic.
Damon could not stomach the insufferable brigand, but if he
made the slightest move to get away, Optatus would seize his arm
and shout, "Listen to what I've got to tell you!" And there would
come again that overpowering burst of bad breath and laughter.
When Optatus wished to emphasize his words he would thrust
out his big red tongue and strike his right thumb across it. Then,
with the four fingers turned inward to his palm and his thumb
thrust out, he would gesture at Damon with the thumb, from time
to time wetting it with a swift powerful stroke across his tongue.
"So you're going to Corinth," he said, though Damon had told
him a dozen times that he was going to Rome. "A journey to
Corinth is not every man's business!" With a blow he almost
floored Damon, though the jest was not original with Optatus.
Corinth was a byword in the world for every form of debauchery.
Its theaters were said to make those in Rome seem as pure as a
Vestal Virgin's vows.
"A journey to Corinth is not every man's business!" Optatus
roared, staring at Damon; and he was off again, his huge frame
shaking like a gladiator trying to settle his armor. "You'll want a
girl there." As though that were a gem of pure wit he erupted
again.
Damon said he did not want a girl and he was not going to
Corinth. When Damon spoke Optatus stared at his lips, and his
own lips moved as if tasting words. If Damon had told him that
he knew the whereabouts of a thousand abandoned infants the
man could not have looked more devilishly secretive.
He completely upset Damon by howling in his face, “By the
gods, how you hate the Christians! I hate them too! If I were em-
peror of Rome I would cook every one of them in hot pig-oil.
May the gods curse and castrate them!" he roared, his eyes popping
with horrible fury. Then, with a quick change, he burst with
wild ribald laughter; this ridiculous man could not talk even as a
conspirator or an outraged merchant without pouring upon his
words the soured wine of his being. "Montani semper liberi!"
71
he thundered, and then looked hard at Damon's eyes. They had
been talking in Greek.
"Montani semper liberi," said Damon, and Optatus watched
his lips. "Mountain men are always free men."
"Christians!" Optatus snorted. "They hate the pagans, do
they? Ask them in Rome."
Damon was startled. Pagan was the Roman word for any coun-
try person: did the Christians despise rural folk? As though
divining his thoughts, Optatus prodded him with a thumb and
shouted: "They hate the pagans! Why? Because the pagans won't
join their damned religion! Why? Because mountain men are
always free men—and so would all country men be, if they could.
Only the city's rabble become Christians! Am I a liar?"
"No," said Damon feebly.
"You know what these Christian pigs are doing? Destroying
the merchants! What have they done in Ephesus? Put the silver-
smiths out of business!" He leaned back, looked hard at Damon,
and bellowed until the bench shook on which they sat. He
struck his thumb across his tongue and poked it like a pike at
Damon's chest and said: "In Corinth I will show you what the
Christians are like." He leaned close to Damon to look into his
eyes. "Ho-ho-ho!" he thundered. “A journey to Corinth is not
every man's business! Boy, how are you in the three situations?"
"I don't know," said Damon, feeling angry and silly.
In Corinth, said Optatus, with another quick change, the Christ-
ians were so depraved that when not watching the clouds for the
return of their Lord they spent their time in carnal orgies, believ-
ing that all their sins would be instantly forgiven when their Lord
appeared. They had practically taken over the famous temple of
Aphrodite, and there, day and night, they rode one another like
amorous dogs. By all the broken staffs in hell would he show him
what Christians were like!
"You're going into business with me," he said, and two powerful
hands seized and shook Damon. Had he heard of Glycera? He
would show her to him. She was the love-goddess of this degener-
ate horde. Ah, beautiful, beautiful!—with a form and a face and
a head of hair that God had made only once and would never
attempt again. "After one night with her a man is a eunuch for
a month!" He gazed raptly at Damon to see if he was fully pos-
sessed of the wonder; when, exploding like a small planet, he stag-
gered to his feet, grasping his midriff, working his fingers in and
72
out as though to relieve pain. Staring down with tear-filled eyes
he said: "You will lie with her! Boy, if a man is a good man he
must be good in three situations—in a fight, in an inn, and in bed
with a woman! How do you rate in those things?"
He struck his thumb across his tongue, gave Damon painful
stab in the ribs, and then like a huge demon who must enjoy his
jest alone he laughed until he collapsed to the bench.
Damon did not want to stop at Corinth but there was no way
to get out of it. When the boat moored at the landing the mons-
ter seized him, whipped him around in front of him, and propelled
him off the ship, calling meanwhile to porters to bring the luggage.
Damon was badly frightened. He was not sure that the unmanage-
able brute was not hustling him off to his slave-farm. Presently,
though, he realized that the man's intentions were honorable:
for some reason Optatus had taken a fancy to him and wanted to
set before him the best food and wine in Corinth. He wanted
Damon to be filled with wonder at having met Optatus, the
wealthy merchant in slaves, who was as good as the best in all three
situations.
While porters followed with their luggage, Optatus propelled
Damon through the city and into a tavern. Most inns, in Corinth
or anywhere, were evil places, being little more than brothels
where the unwary were drugged and robbed; but this inn was a
sumptuous place. And it was plain that Optatus was one of its
most lordly patrons. In his great voice he called for the best
wines, and almost at once brimming silver flagons stood before
them. This giant, whose appetites were as elemental as the sea,
raised his flagon and drained it and drew a huge hairy hand across
his bearded mouth.
“Drink!” he said, beaming upon Damon. "My boy, I'll test you
tonight in only two situations. Hah, by the gods! Why should I
ask my friend to show his prowess in battle?" Looking round,
he saw a lovely girl and summoned her after first wetting his
thumb. She came over, wagging her hips in such a way as to toss
her garments and expose them, and mocking Optatus with her
eyes. "Sit down," he said, “and talk to my friend here. Say witty
things, like Aspasia did." He roared at his jest and picked up
another flagon. He bent forward and looked into Damon's eyes
and said: "A journey to Corinth is not every man's business! Boy,
tell her
your name.'

"Damon," said Damon, wondering how he could get away from

“Darian?” said Optatus, who had been watching Damon's

"Damon."
“Demas? Hah, that means the popular one! Well, Demas, this

heat and the third watch. Demas, aren't you drinking?” he asked,

Thalia clasped Damon's face and he did not turn away, for he
thought she would give him only a light caress; but she came to

"Wonderful girl!" said Optatus. "I teach my girls how to kiss

Eva kissed Damon with the same soft exploring warmth, and

rather too much of this. He had emptied a flagon and the wine
was threatening to turn loose his emotions and tongue.
"If you're not a man now," said Optatus, with great jovial good
will, "you'll be one before morning. In two situations at least,
my boy. Ho-ho-ho!" He looked up at Damon. "Boy, are you all
right?"
Damon was not all right. He had never been drunk; when he
left this inn he was reeling like a poetaster at dawn. Still making
his inane jests, still bellowing like a bull, Optatus propelled him
along the streets, either by grasping an arm to guide him or by
walking behind to support and push him. He said he would show
Damon the Christians. Damon was never to know where they
went. Optatus said the next day that they went to the temple of
Aphrodite to watch a Christian named Glycera beguile the men.
Damon's senses were so fogged with wine that he could never
clearly recall the events of this night.
74
They entered a large handsome building and there Damon saw
the worst hag he was ever to see. Garbed in the most costly raiment
and crowned with a thousand gleaming jewels she sat on a kind
of throne, draped with great swaths of gold and purple cloth; and
around her were a dozen or more naked men and women, deliver-
ing themselves over flesh and soul to every kind of posture in
sexual lust. Glycera looked very old, as old as sin itself, and she
was very lean, and her face had the weathered appearance of a
discarded boot. Her nose was a long hooked beak and flaring
nostrils, filled with hair. Her mouth was toothless and caved in,
giving a monstrous prominence to her chin and nose. She had a
whip and from time to time she descended from the throne to
flog the sprawled creatures when they rested in their orgies. Other
people roundabout wildly applauded when she struck with her
whip and shrieked her obscene gibberish. After flogging a man or
a woman she would ascend her throne, lay the whip across her
knees, suck her mouth in, and stare down at the votaries of the
goddess.
Damon did his best to observe clearly and to keep in mind
the fact that he was in Corinth; but he was sick and the temple
was filled with an incense that stunk like the underworld.
"Via dolorosa!" the old hag cried.
Optatus alternately bellowed with joy or thrust a thumb at
Damon's ribs, crying, "Boy, how do you like it? Is there fire in the
ashes? Susirium et decus puellarum! Are you, boy?"
"No," said Damon.
"See how she scourges and how she loves it! The old hate the
young. Did you know that? What did a poet say?—I deserve to

no sign to show I was man! Isn't that what the Christians would
do?... Boy, isn't it?"
"Jews say if a member offend you, pluck it off.”
"Look, Demas, would that woman excite the pure Hippolytus?
Ah, mea vita, my little Ipsitilla, what a wiggle! See how she trem-
bles, quivers, sways her loins! Demas, would she make Hippolytus
forget himself?"
Damon suposed he meant Hippolytus, the chaste stepson of the
amorous Phaedra, who had passed into common speech as a sym-
bol of virtue.
"Look!" said Optatus. "Do you observe how she uses her
hands? ...'
75
But Damon was not interested. He wanted to get out of this
place, and when he indicated his wish to Optatus by taking his
arm and moving toward the door, the giant at once propelled him
out of the temple and back to the tavern. While Damon's head
rang with strange wild music and a babble of voices within his
skull, Optatus called for flagons of wine and for dancing girls,
but from this point on Damon could remember nothing but some
of his dreams when he awakened the next morning.
Optatus, he supposed, had put him to bed, and had put a girl
to bed with him; but he had sunk too far below the conscious level
of life to know what was done. His first awareness was of a terrible
dream, a sleeping torment overborne by nightmarish horrors.
He was a dwarf with thin legs and a huge head which his legs
could barely support; and he was walking on the sea like the water-
treading gods; and his head grew until it filled the sky. His skull
was a huge heavy planet peopled with demons and all sorts of evil
things; and two of these, lying prone in his aching brain, looked
out through his eyes and saw Levilla's virtuous face reproaching
him. These demons saw disembodied hands drawing her away
to a brothel, and at that moment Damon stirred and tried to
arouse himself. In doing this he became aware of his bed partner
and vaguely apprehended that it was a woman; and thinking it
was Levilla he put an arm across her and slept again.
He had other dreadful dreams. In one, all the Christians he
had known assumed the configuration of a monster that had been
unsexed, and walked, sad and bleeding, across the earth. In an-
other he was his own flagellant; he scourged himself while a god-
dess with the aspect of a hag murmured in the sweetest imaginable
voice Virgil's terrible lines:
Beneath the blow his jaws shattered,
his face was torn away; thick with his mangled brain
blood rushed through his nostrils; in black gore
from crushed head and smashed sockets, dripped his eyes.
So it had been with Scaevola when a Carthaginian smashed his face
with a stone; and so in Damon's dream it was with him. Then in
this dream or in another came the lines,
upon their groaning fathers
half-murdered children, at the gate of life
sobbing their souls away!
So it was with him during the remainder of the night and far
76
into the morning, until at last he came awake. His head was
simply bursting. He sat up, dizzy and foolish, and strove to realize
where he was, to recall what had happened; and after a few mo-
ments he became conscious of a pair of blue eyes steadily watch-
ing him.
He had never seen this girl before. She was very young. She
was hardly more than a child. She looked very innocent and
lovely, lying there, studying him with grave childlike eyes.
"Are you sick?" she asked.
"Sick as a poisoned dog," he said and groaned. "Who are you?”
"Just a woman," she said.
Just a woman! That was about all most women could say of
themselves in the Roman world.
"You may go when you wish to."
"Can I bring you anything?"
"No, thanks. I'll be all right."
"Shall I tell my master you're awake?"
"No."
She left the bed, slipped into her garments and was gone.
Damon thought that as soon as he could appease the furies in his
skull he would steal away and resume his journey. He observed
that his luggage was close by his bed. He took off his money-belt
and examined it: nothing had been taken. Well, Optatus was a
pretty good fellow after all. The huge buffoon had only been
trying to show him a good time and to learn if he was a man in
the three situations. Great God, he had proved himself a man in
none of them!
In this barren room was a small table, and on it an earthen pot
filled with water. By the pot was a towel. Damon soaked the
towel, wrung it out, returned to the bed, and was cooling the
fevers in his brow and eyes when the door was flung open and
there stood Optatus, looking as fit as Hercules. He stared at
Damon a moment and burst with laughter.
"Demas, how are you? Do you feel all right?”
Damon shook his head no.
Optatus came over and sat on the bed and fixed him with his
large dark eyes. "By the gods, boy, where's your woman?"
Damon gestured at the door.
"You're not a man in any situation, are you?"
Damon shook his head no and turned the cooler side of the cloth
down.
77
"You need some wine," said Optatus; and overpowering Da-
mon's protests he left the room and bellowed for wine. "Some
wine of Phocis," he said, returning. "It's light and it's mixed
with pine cone juice and it eats up the poisons and fevers. Me,"
he said, "I'll have the vintage of Laconia." He was regarding
Damon with obvious compassion, and compassion in this man
was as big as his lusts. When the wine came he emptied a flagon,
wiped his red lips, and pressed the other flagon on Damon. Da-
mon drank a little to please him. It tasted more like herbs than
wine. "You feel better, Demas?"
Damon nodded.
Optatus now turned the cover back and looked at the bed.
Damon knew what was in the rogue's mind. “And she was a
virgin," he said, his eyes reproaching Damon. “Just about the
last one I had left."
When Damon had dressed, Optatus gathered his luggage and
seized his arm and took him down to eat. He ordered a number of
rich hot dishes, and the aroma of them sickened Damon; but this
man, with the health and vigor of the elements, ate like a glut-
ton. He devoured an astonishing quantity of wine and spiced
food. He had planned an excursion for them: they would go to
Delphi and consult the oracle; thence by boat to Chios, where the
wine and food were the best in the world; and then over to his
farm, back in the hills, where Damon could prove himself a man
in the three situations and take his time about it. He said that
when Damon left his farm he would be mightier than the gladi-
ators in battle, and in love he would be wiser than the poets.
But Damon's senses were in a turmoil of fever and nausea and he
wanted to get out to a boat and the sea breezes. Slowly, with
growing shock, he had realized that Optatus was stone-deaf and
he did not know how to make his wish known. He called one of
the girls and told her, and she talked to Optatus, who watched her
lips. Optatus demanded from Damon a promise that he would re-
turn soon and accept his hospitality for an indefinite period of
time.
He asked Damon if he was a man of means; and when Damon
nodded to indicate that he was, Optatus said gravely: "You will
need a lot of women—at least a thousand before you turn back to
dust. There are none more beautiful than mine."
"Yes, yes," said Damon, dying to get away from him. Was he
married? No. Was Damon in love with some faithless wench?
78
Damon said he was not. Very well, then, he could have a life of
pleasure, for nothing stood in his way: wine, food, women: what
else was there? This peninsula had a marvelous climate; its wo-
men were the loveliest; its food and wine were of the best. Sadly
he urged Damon to return and become his friend and neighbor.
He hinted that he might be willing to admit him as a junior
partner in his flourishing slave-business. Because Damon so des-
perately needed to get away he gave hypocritical assent to every
proposal, and a solemn promise to return.
Optatus went to the wharf to see him off, bearing his luggage,
and escorting him as though he were a frail old woman. He
pressed on him a jug of fine old wine. He offered to send one of
his girls all the way to Rome with him; Damon could use her and
bring her back when he returned. Had he plenty of money for
the voyage? Was there anything he could do for him that a father
might do for a son?
As the boat drew away Optatus stood on the landing, and Da-
mon observed that there were tears in his eyes. He looked very
lonely and sad. Beyond Optatus Damon could see a part of the
temple of Isis and a little of the great white stadium, both re-
nowned for their architecture. Optatus struck a thumb across his
tongue and waved goodbye but it was farewell. Damon was never
to see him again.
He was never to see Corinth again but tales of Christian de-
baucheries there were to follow him all his life. Memory of the
place was always to make him feel ill. He drank from the jug
of wine, recalling what Optatus had to say about Corinth's wo-
men, looking on himself as a weakling. Optatus told him that
Ionian slaves were of surpassing loveliness; the Alexandrian were
famous for their skill in stimulating jaded passions. He gazed at
the dim and withdrawing shore, thinking, Farewell, strange and
generous and lonely man! Via dolorosa! Farewell!
Then he tried to put Optatus out of his mind by thinking of
Mark. Montani semper liberi: he hoped Mark would turn out
to be a mountain man.
79
VIII
He had written Murdia that he was returning to Rome but before
revealing his presence to her he spent a week looking for a copy
of the book by Mark. When he could find none he went to her.
She scolded him as though he was trying to abandon her for an-
other lover.
"You shameless cheat!" she cried and roughly kissed his mouth.
"Did you bring a mistress with you?"
"I have no mistress."
"You have become worldly, which means that you have learned
to lie." She was appraising him. "How tall and handsome you
are! How self-assured!"
"Oh yes, self-assured."
"But you are handsome. Darling, have you brought sweet
scented flatteries to your Lesbia?" Having no felicities of her own
she had got a great deal of Ovid by heart and when her own in-
ventions failed her she lifted verses from the poet. She now beamed
upon Damon and lisped:
"At sight of him the dying flames revived-
her cheeks blushed crimson and her face glowed hot!--
as when a little spark, smothered by ashes,
takes food and energy from a passing breeze,
starts up and rises to its former might,
so now Murdia's languid waning love
blazed up again, fanned by her Damon's beauty!"
When he moved to turn away she clutched him and kissed him,
breathing into his mouth:
"And he was handsomer than he was wont
that day-too handsome to condemn his lover!"
"Please!" he said, trying to put her off. "Where is Mark's book?"
80
She was pouting at him. "You know how long you've been

fess
your sins, and tell me about all the women you have lain with,
you abominable man." Behind the mask of her girl-like naivete

chit named Levilla?"
"No."
"Why not?"
"She wouldn't marry me," said Damon, looking round him for
the book.
"God still saves fools from themselves. But you should never
marry, my dear. Would you fetter yourself to one lady? Impos-

beautiful women share you. You have known so many of the east-
ern women and it is said they are very passionate. Are they?”
"I know nothing about them," he said. He found her very tire-
some in this mood.
She was off again:
"Give all your gifts without her rival's knowledge.
Vary the times of your adultery;
vary the places where you meet your mistress
in case a rival knows her secret too!"
She was looking at him reproachfully. "Damon, how long will

"I never was," he said, wondering if she really had Mark's book
or had lied about it.
"I am," she said. "Next to you that's the greatest joy of my life.”
"Do you really have the book by Mark?"
"Yes." She was looking at the corners of his eyes, from one to
the other. "You're not very tidy," she said.
"Have you read it?"
"It can wait. First you must tell me all about your lovers. And

I can look into the mirrors of your eyes and see their faces, a
81
hundred of them." She moved close, looking into his eyes. She

"What?" he said, startled.
"The ugly one.”
"Oh, that must be Glycera."
"Glycera," said Murdia, staring. "And I can see Camilla-Zel-

there?"
He strove to speak but she clapped a hand to his mouth.
"Don't lie to me. We Christians think lying is a sin. Darling,

--and you must take them if she will not give. Perhaps she'll fight
at first and call it outrage-have you learned that if you fight a
little it makes it sweeter? The females of some of the animals
know that."
"Where is the book?"
"What did he say?—for all her fighting, still she hopes to lose.
Only beware, you must not kiss her roughly or make her weep at
your brutality. And after kisses, if you go no further you are un-
worthy even of a kiss!"
He stood up. "Murdia, do you have Mark's book?"
"Oh, to the gutter with Mark's book! It can wait. Tell me if
you know as much as Ovid knew. Do you know that a kiss is
next to the last fulfillment and to stop there would not be virtuous
but dull?"
Damon was trying to hide his annoyance. “Are you going to
show me the book by Mark?"
"He's not half as interesting as Ovid."
"Do you have a copy?"
"O Lord!" she breathed and he knew that he had offended her.
She left the room and returned with her copy of Mark's book
and at once Damon began to read. The first page told him it was
quite illiterate; its grammar was poor, its choice of words was some-
times barbarous. Presently he came to the words, And all things
whatsoever you shall ask for in prayer, believing, you shall re-
ceive. He read them aloud and said, "That is like Paul's state-
