Elisha the Jew-"
"Don't talk to me about Jews!" she cried and snatched the book
away. “Do you want proof that our Christ is the Lord and Savior?
82
It is here." She sat by him and as she turned pages he observed
that a number of passages had been marked, for him no doubt, or
for some other skeptic. "Listen!" she said, giving him a furious
glance. "It says and the unclean spirits went out and entered into
the swine; and the herd ran violently down a steep place into the
sea and were choked in the sea. Could anyone but our Lord cast
out unclean spirits and drive them into pigs?"
He looked at her and was silent.
"Listen again. It says he took five loaves and two fishes, and
looked up to heaven and blessed and broke the loaves-" She
turned to search his face.
"Yes?"
“Then," she said, bearing down hard on every word, “five . . .
thousand... people... and were... filled! Did you ever hear of
a wonder greater than that?"
"No."
"Do you believe now? Will you believe he was the Lord if he
walked on water?"
"Other gods have done that."
"Who?"
"Poseidon."
"Poseidon!" she said. "That impostor. Well, what kind of proof
do you want? Listen, it says he took the blind man by the hand
and led him out of the town; and when he had spit on his eyes and
put his hand on him he asked the man if he could see. The man
looked up and said, I see men as trees, walking.... Well, do you
believe now?"
"What else did he do?" asked Damon, marveling at her child-
like simplicity.
"If you won't believe," she said angrily, searching for a passage
with which to astound him, “why should I bother?... Well, here:
There appeared unto them Elias with Moses-" She looked at
Damon queerly and said: "You know who Moses was?"
"My friend Elisha says he certainly was not Moses."
"Now don't try to be witty. He was an ancient. He came down
from heaven and talked to our Lord. And do you know what hap-
pened? You know what Junius did?-or didn't you know Junius?"
"I don't remember him."
"Our Lord said here-somewhere here," she said, her finger
rambling, “if your eye offend you pluck it out. That's what
Junius did."
83
"Plucked his eyes out?"
"They offended him, so he blinded himself. He looked on wo-
men and lusted." She was quiet a few moments; she seemed to be
musing. “But our Lord didn't mean for him to blind himself,
did he? Can't he still lust, even though blind?"
"I suppose he can.”
"And Terence, he read this book and castrated himself."
"Oh no!" said Damon, staring at her.
"But he did. For our Lord says here in this book, if your hand
offend you cut it off. Terence said it was not his hand but some-
thing else and he cut it off. But I think maybe he was silly. You
know why I think that?"
"Can't imagine," said Damon, still staring at her.
"Because it says here, Verily I say unto you, all sins shall be
forgiven unto the sons of men. If all sins will be forgiven," she
said, looking at him wide-eyed, "Terence was stupid. He could
sin and be forgiven. Any Christian can, for it says so here." Sud-
denly sounding stifled she clapped a hand to her mouth. "O God!”
she moaned. "Does it include women? Does it mean only men?”
"Why don't you ask Mark?"
"O Lord, if it doesn't include women I'm doomed! Oh!" she
said, and for a moment hid her face. "But why should I be?" she
asked angrily, turning to him. "Women are just as worthy of for-
giveness as men, maybe more so. Aren't they?"
"I'd think so."
"But that's the way our Lord said it." She read the verse again.
"He says all sins will be forgiven of the sons of men." She was
silent; but then, bright and cheerful, she burst forth: "But only
if you have faith. Do you know what you can do if you have faith?
Wait." She turned pages; then: "For verily I say unto you, that
whosoever shall say unto this mountain, be you removed and be
cast into the sea, and shall not doubt in his heart, but shall believe
that those things which he says shall come to pass, he shall have
whatsoever he says." She turned to look at Damon, her eyes filled
with wonder. "I guess I don't have much faith, do I? I not only
can't move a mountain, I can't even make you love me. O Lord
our God, I don't even have enough faith to make you sleep with
me tonight."
An involuntary snort burst from Damon. He arose, convulsed
by pain, pity and astonishment; and the incredible Murdia said:
"Do you laugh because I have so little faith? What is the mat-
84
ter with you anyway? If you had faith equal to a grain of mustard
seed you could walk on rivers, stop the sun and the winds, turn
stone into gold, live forever without dying and force all people
to love you. If you're a Christian. For it says so here. If you
don't doubt, anything that you ask for will come to pass. What
would you like most?"
"To know the truth."
"Then you must be a Christian, and soon, for did you know
that our Lord is coming again?”
"He was coming-wasn't he?—when Rome burned and again
when Jerusalem fell."
"Please don't scoff at holy things. This book tells you that the
sun will be darkened and the moon will not give her light, the

He was standing, looking down at her beautiful hair, remember-

The story which Mark had to tell was short, the script was leg-

IX
As with those unwary creatures which certain flowers seduce and
envelop, covering them over with the fragrance and velvet of their
being, so it was with Damon. He thought he would soon pall on
Murdia and he watched her for signs of boredom but she seemed
devoted. Sometimes he thought her whole joy lay in teaching him
all that she knew about love and passion—and of these she knew
more than Damon was ever to know. He lived in her handsome
house and took his meals with her and they spent many hours
roaming the city. They never went to the circus or chariot races:
neither cared anything about them; nor often to the theater and
only rarely to the baths. They went to the temples, museums, li-
braries, parks, or idled in her houseboat on the river. And all the
while he wished to be away.
One day hearing a great din in the street they hastened out and
saw a procession coming. It was one of the rites of the goddess Isis.
Her cult, Damon recalled, had been introduced to Rome in the
time of Sulla but only Caligula had officially recognized it. Ti-
berius had destroyed some of her shrines and had contemptuously
hurled her image into the Tiber, because, it was rumored, her
priests had dishonored a noble lady. Ovid had told his readers
not to shun the temples of Isis; for her, women became what they
became for Jupiter. The worship of Isis (Ovid said) offered to
weary lovers a good excuse to get away from their mistresses.
The heralds came, crying, “Make way for holy things!" Damon
was watching Murdia's face. She professed to be a Christian but
she had an eager childlike curiosity about other religions, like
a woman dining out with a man and looking round her at other
men. She had asked what the difference was between Isis and
93
Demeter, Hera, Semele, Io, Tyche and a host of others. Just be-
hind the heralds were women strewing the earth with flowers,
sprinkling it with balsam and myrrh and other costly scents; some
with gleaming mirrors on their backs to reflect the sun; some with
symbolic gestures arranging and adorning the hair of the goddess.
A number of musicians were blowing on pipes, and with them
danced a choir of youths and maidens, gowned in the pure white
ritual robes, singing songs. Behind all these came the throng of
initiates, persons of all ages from children to graybeards, all
dressed in white linen. The hair of the women was confined with
thin shimmering veils; the polled heads of the men, annointed
with oil, shone like the stars in the Lady's heaven. The high
priests, clothed from head to feet in white linen, bore various sym-
bols of the gods: one held a lamp in the shape of a golden bowl,
from which arose a sheet of pale yellow fire; a second bore an
altar; a third a palm branch and the serpent staff of Mercury; a
fourth the symbol of justice, a deformed left hand with out-
stretched fingers. The fourth priest also had a golden vessel in
the form of a woman's breast, and from this as he walked he spilled
drops of milk to the earth.
In a whisper Murdia wanted to know what it all meant. Damon
said he did not know.
After the priests came the gods, the first being the dog-headed
Anubis, messenger of heaven and hell, holding in his left hand a
serpent image, in his right a palm branch. With him was a priest
bearing on his shoulders the image of a cow, symbol of the fruitful
Mother. Another carried the ark of mysteries, the womb; and a
third the image of a snake, reared, with swollen neck, which for
all people, Damon had heard, was a symbol of the male organ. He
thought Murdia was gazing at the serpent with fascinated interest.
No other goddess in Rome evoked such devotion and piety as
Isis. The initiate spent many days and nights in fasting and
prayer, with the goddess now and then appearing to him to urge
him to repent. He was told that the powers of life and death and
the gates of hell were in her hands, and that this act of self-dedica-
tion was a symbolic death, to be followed by a new birth and a new
life. At the font he was sprinkled with holy water. He then went
to the temple where the holy secrets were imparted to him. To-
ward sunset he was led, clothed in purest white linen, into the very
womb of the holy place, where he drew near to death and looked
upon the threshold of the underworld, beholding the sun in full
94
splendor in the dead of night. When the next morning the rites
were completed he was adorned like the sun and seated as a god
on a veiled platform; and when the curtains were drawn aside the
awed and wondering multitude saw him endowed like the sun it-
self with everlasting life.
Damon had told her about the initiation-had told her all that

"All that I know of."
"What do we have? Mary?"
""
"But Mark says she was only a mortal woman.'

Damon told her that if Jesus became a god, as Mark said, he

Damon was so flabbergasted that he left the house. He went

He learned that all those things were indeed written down in a

In her lovely breast Murdia nourished anger and outrage and

Sometimes when they lay together, propped up with pillows,

to my rebuke, come into your place. And all the bugs marched

She reached down to pinch his organ. "You don't believe it?"

Murdia, like most people, believed in demons. She said these
creatures had been degraded from the rank of angels and cast
down, to roam the earth and torment the living. They usurped the
powers and honors of the gods. One, she had heard, took the at-
tributes of Jupiter, another of Venus, a third of Apollo; they
lurked in temples and other holy places; they invented fables to
deceive the unwary; they spoke as oracles, worked wonders, and
in many ways were indistinguishable from the powers of good.
Christians felt that any mark of respect to another religion, or
even tolerance, was the work of demons.
She had dwelt on these things. She had searched him for signs
that a demon was in him. She found the signs: he scoffed at
miracles, he did not believe in the Lord. He said that all the
things written or told about Jesus had been told about various
heathen gods.
She clawed her way up his frame and dropped her eyelids and
hissed: "Habet Diabolus Christos suos!"
He lay back, thinking of the words. Satan has his Christs! But
who was Satan? So far as he had been able to learn, Satan was a
Persian myth.
Murdia suffered from terrors in her sleep. One night she
awakened him with bloodchilling screams, and in the gloom he
saw her sitting up, clutching her hair, her bulging eyes staring.
When she could speak she told him that she had seen Satan.
101
Satan had been standing on the bed leering down at her, with a

the serpent that was his tongue had a red swollen face smeared
with blood. The tongue flickered at her, and the eyes, shining like
beryl, bade her come.
The next day Damon asked her what the body had been like.
The body, she said, was a big snake, covered over with little snakes.
"How do you know it was Satan?"
He had told her he was Satan. He had told her that he had
been ready to overthrow God but then God sent his only son to
earth for human sins and Satan had lost most of his power.
"And he came to tell you these things?"
"Satan always shows himself to a beautiful woman."
O Lord, what is this thing called man! Damon was young and
for him Murdia was a riddle. Why was she so venomously un-
happy? Why did her visions of Satan cast upon her a kind of
spell? Sometimes she was as sweet and gentle as any woman could
be; in other moods and for no reasons that he could divine like a

could a woman do?
"I'll disfigure myself!" she cried one day, her face moist and
red and unlovely from weeping. "I'll cut off my breasts! Then
men will hate me!"
"For shame," he said.
“I will, I will, I will!" Bitterness too painful to listen to was
pouring out of her.
He spent almost a year in Rome and became more unhappy as
she became more tormented. The time came when he could no
longer endure her sneers and reproaches, her obscene insults, her
wild and venomous furies. He went to the spot where his mother
had died but there was nothing there any more. There was
nothing at the bench where his father had sat. He looked east,
remembering that all the religions came from there. He looked
round him at Rome and found it spiritually empty, and one
morning he slipped away and took the first boat out.
102
X
He felt that he had been wasting time. Once or twice just at
daybreak he had seen Pliny, notebooks in hand, hastening over to
the emperor Vespasian, also an early riser; and once in bitter
weather he had seen him in a coach, his hands encased in heavy
mittens, his shorthand writer and a pile of tablets at his side.
What Damon needed, Damon thought, was not a mistress, but
Pliny's industry and singleminded devotion to a task. And what
was the task, after these years of thinking and searching?
To find a meaning for himself, he supposed: when he found
that he might also find the truth that sustained his mother in the
flames, or the truth that made such a gentle philosopher of his
father on the garden bench. He had accumulated many notes.
He had copied the more significant passages in the story told by
Mark and had written down the names of a few persons whom he
hoped to see. Who was Thekla? He had heard that she had been
converted by Paul and had then left her family and gone forth
alone to spread the glad tidings. Who was Johanan ben Zaccai?
He had heard that when Titus laid siege to Jerusalem Johanan
had escaped in a coffin and fled to Jamnia, where he had estab-
lished a school. Who, actually, was Apollonius of Tyana? His
name was already enshrined in a constellation of legends. One
of these told how, when still a lad, he had adopted the strict rules
of the Pythagorean brotherhood, renouncing marriage, meat and
alcoholic drink, giving his property to his kinfolk, and as prepara-
tion for saintliness maintaining complete silence for five years,
after which he set out to teach. It was said that he cast out demons,
healed the sick and raised the dead: so many wonder-workers did
these things! He taught his disciples to shun slander, jealousy,
103
covetousness and hate, and to love all men. Were Christians
taking some of their doctrines from him? Who was Elder John?
Some of the Christians held him (or the legends about him) in
special veneration: it was said that he was now a very old man,
that he had known Jesus.
He made it a habit when on a journey to learn if any Christians
were around him. On this boat he found three. One of these
reminded him of the vulgar maxim, You would be afraid to play
morra with him in the dark. Petilian-for such was his name-
was a tall man and extremely emaciated. He had morbid eyes
that lit up with strange fires when he looked at Melosa, his com-
panion. He had a number of facial mannerisms which Damon
found fascinating: he would draw the skin of his yellow cheeks
and long thin nose up to his eyes, almost concealing them, and
then stare suspiciously round him and sniff. Damon supposed he
was trying to smell out demons, for it was believed that these
creatures had a peculiar and unpleasant odor that betrayed their
presence.
Melosa was a stout untidy and sullen woman who rarely spoke
but seemed preoccupied with her task of being the divine thought
in human form. For that is what Petilian believed her to be, the
incarnation of divine thought, and that is what he called her.
Melosa would not speak to Damon, and Petilian did so with ob-
vious distaste.
On learning these things about them and that they were Chris-
tians Damon stared at them as he might have at visitors from an-
other planet. He would go away and look at the sea and come back
and look at them; and at last he began to ask his usual questions:
had Jesus raised the dead, had he become a god or was he a god
who came down as a man? When Petilian only scowled or turned
for succor to Divine Thought, Damon said:
"Was he the Lord and Savior?”
Damon had been speaking in Greek. Petilian now turned to
Divine Thought and said: "Quot homines, tot sententiae!"
Switching to the Roman tongue Damon said quickly, "Yes!—
as many opinions as men and as many Christian opinions as there
are Christians. And are any of them original? Where, for instance,
did you get the idea of an only begotten son of the Father? . . .
From Plato?”
Petilian went on scowling. Damon looked at them, back and
forth, wondering about them, thinking that possibly he should go
104
away. But he wanted to know why Christians worshiped the fish.
In Antioch he had been told that in ichthys, the Greek word for
fish, were the letters standing for the words, Iesous Christos Theou
Uios Soter-Jesus Christ, son of God, Savior. Was the Christian
religion a fish-cult?
Petilian continued to scowl. In a whisper he said something to
Melosa.
Damon reminded them that Jesus was to usher in the golden
age. But was not that an old myth? The fawning courtiers and
oracles had for ages told the kings of Babylon, Egypt, Persia that
they would conquer all people and restore the golden age of inno-
cence. What would the Christian golden age be like?
Petilian and Melosa were silent and Damon wondered if they
were Christians after all.
"Did your Jesus rise from the dead and if so, on what day?"
"The third," said Petilian.
"But his very earliest followers-they live at Pella-say he
didn't. Nowhere does Paul mention it. Would Paul have left
out such a miracle if he had known about it? Would Paul-"
"And who is Paul?"
"You have never heard of Paul?"
"No."
With eyes almost closed Petilian was looking at Damon. He
then shrugged and got up and went away, followed by Divine
Thought. Damon paced a deck, wondering again if they were
Christians or only pretended to be, for some evil reason. Then
he met one named Ribad.
Damon learned now, with wonder and astonishment, that there
were Christians known as Gnostics from the Greek word gnosis.
He learned that there were more than a dozen sects but he was
never to know which one Ribad belonged to. This man was only
about thirty-five but he was jaded and almost worn out by self-
indulgence. This was so, he explained, his eyelids sinking wearily,
because certain Gnostics believed that after the immortality of the
soul had been won, the body, being vile and corrupt from birth,
could be and indeed should be put to every carnal use. Ribad,
having secured his soul when he became a Christian, had been
trying to exhaust and destroy his flesh by debauching it with every
conceivable pleasure. He had been to Corinth and had got himself
into a horrible funk of sadness and melancholy. Post coitem omne
animal triste est! Damon thought, looking at the man. Ribad
105
utterly despised women because he thought they were the dedi-
cated agents of Satan.
“Long ago,” he told Damon, in his slow sad almost stuttering
speech, his pale thin hands rising feebly in gestures of contempt,
“men were pure but women introduced them to evil. Why call
them bitches? The bitch is a decent beast. Now men are sunk in
evil but our Lord came and saved our souls. Our bodies don't
matter. We know now who we were, what we have become, where
we were, whither we have been flung-" he paused as though
momentarily too weary even for speech, made a grimace with his
nose and with an effort concluded "-whither we hasten, whence
we are redeemed, what is birth and what is rebirth."
That, it seemed to Damon, was quite a lot for any man to know.
Ribad explained, with interims of rest and dozing, that human
beings were of three kinds. The highest of all were the pneu-
matics, the pure spiritual type; the lowest were the hylics, who
were grossly material; and between the two were the psychics, who
might rise to the upper level or sink to the lower. He said that
he himself was a psychic who had got hopelessly entangled in
matter. His wife, almost as dumb as an ox and with a bad odor
that no scent had been able to extinguish, was a hylic. "Whither I
go," said Ribad, fluttering a pale hand, "she cannot come."
Those words at this time meant nothing to Damon. As a mat-
ter of fact he understood little of what Ribad in his stuttering
melancholy way was trying to tell him. Ribad said, for instance,
that God had projected a pair of energies, mind and truth, and
from these had descended a series of offspring known as aeons, be-
ginning with the logos, and coming down through man to the true
religion, which was Christian. All other religions, he said, had
been only fumbling attempts toward the truth.
Damon gazed at him in astonishment. "But your Jesus was a
man, at least to begin with."
"No, oh no," said Ribad, raising a thin wasted hand and re-
proaching him with black Egyptian eyes. The only true Christians,
he said, were in Alexandria: all the others were charlatans and
impostors of various degrees of malice and wickedness.
"If Jesus wasn't a Jew in the flesh then what was he?"
A phantom, Ribad said, and sighed. There was no mortal man.
There was a phantom that deceived the foolish.
Looking at the man, Damon was silent. What under heaven
was a Christian anyway?
106
He said at last, speaking to closed eyes: "In Rome the Christians
say Jesus was a man who became a god. In Antioch they say he was
a pre-existent god who came down as man."
Ribad raised a lid halfway on one black eye and looked at Da-
mon. The lid fell, he clasped his thin fingers and rested his chin
on the points of his thumbs. Jesus, he was an illusion: that was
the first thing to understand. His suffering, death, all of it was
pure illusion. Were men children, to believe such fables? This is
how it was. God came down and on his descent he took elements
from all the spheres to form his body; and he took passions from
the spheres and discharged them on his ascent. To pass the gates
where his enemies waited he made himself invisible with a chrism
of oil and water, and then ascended to the highest sphere.
It sounded, Damon thought, a little like Plato, but even more
like pure nonsense. Again he saw half an eye looking at him and
became aware that Ribad had a misshapen head, as though it had
been flattened at birth and rounded out by awkward hands. He
wanted to know why Ribad thought the whole earth corrupt.
"Have you eyes?" asked Ribad, looking at Damon's eyes. "What
has reduced me to the thing you see before you? Of course the
earth is corrupt. You are corrupt." He looked at Damon with
critical interest a moment and went on: "I suspect you're a hylic.
You'll descend eventually where everything is corrupt and stay
there. I'll ascend through gnosis to pure spirit." He closed his
eyes and sighed. Suddenly in a moment of astonishing passion
he cried: "How do the imbeciles in Antioch pray who call them-
selves Christians? Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven!
What gross idiots! The earth is too vile for God to bother with.
For see us here, we wander in a maze of darkness and evil, lying,
fornicating, robbing, killing; and most will sink and a few will
rise to eternal light."
Damon was looking at him and asking himself, Was the man
mad? Ribad seemed to have got himself pretty well entangled in
Plato and Pythagoras. As for Christians, for some of them Jesus
was only a Jewish rab, for some a phantom: what a world of philos-
ophy and belief and habit lay between those two points of view!
He felt that his journey should go deeper into the East.
"Why try to destroy yourself with vices?"
"Because the only sin-look, hylic, mark this well-the only sin
is opposition to our appetites. God gave us these, didn't he? Sur-
render to them is part of the divine purpose. God gave it to me
107
to lust after women and I have lusted till I am sick and tired of
them. God gave me the urge to steal: I'd make off with your
money-belt if I could and I've been wondering how I might do it.
God gave me the will to lie: I would lie to you if I were not so
weary and if you were not a hylic. There is no joy in lying to
women or fools." Again he closed his eyes and seemed to rest.
Opening one halfway and fixing Damon he said: “God gave me
the urge to kill. If I get a chance I'll try to knock you overboard
into the sea. He gave us these hungers to use, to exhaust us and
wear us out. It is the blackest sin not to do what he willed. If he
was omnipotent and omniscient and did these things, knowing that
we would be too frail to resist our passions, then how can any but
fools argue that our surrender is not a part of his purpose?"
Damon wished that Murdia and Levilla might hear this man.
"In fact," Ribad went on, his eyelids drooping with tiredness,
"it's our duty and a part of the divine purpose to find out if we
have unindulged appetites. Would you embalm a person full
of his own dung? Would you rape a woman and then cut her open
to watch your sperm and the egg unite? Would you tell me God
was so stupid as to endow us with urges we were not meant to fol-
low? If you believe that, you believe there is no purpose in life. If
I felt an urge to kill you-at the moment I do not, because a
wench has exhausted me-the divine purpose would be fulfilled
in
your death, as it is in torture and war. If I asked you to be my
lover-but a hylic always gags my stomach."
Still staring at him Damon said: "Christians say these things
come from Satan, not God."
"You incredible idiot, there is no Satan. God is in all things
and all things are in God."
"Whose God?-Plato's?—Vespasian's-Isaiah's?"
Ribad gasped and made a hideous face. "Why didn't you say
Moses? His account of the creation and fall is only the gibberish
of a hylic Jew. The Jews have no god. They have only their own
magnified image. Having read no philosophy what can they know
of things?"
Damon went away and stood looking at the sea, his emotions
churning. He wondered if it would not be a good thing to bring
Petilian and Divine Thought to hear this strange and incredible
man. He hastened away to find them but Petilian would not
come. Damon returned to find Ribad and to ask him if he would
go to Petilian. Ribad made feeble protests but arose at last, sigh-
108.
ing and moaning, and shuffled along at Damon's side. The mo-
ment he saw Petilian he muttered, "I've seen that fraud before,
but possibly it was in a nightmare." He slumped in a chair, too
weary to move anything but his eyelids.
Damon looked back and forth from Ribad to Petilian and Di-
vine Thought, remembering David's words, that there was no
stone but only stones. Petilian was manifesting no interest, until
Damon told Ribad that the lady here was the reincarnation of
divine thought: he then turned his stare on Ribad. Ribad fixed
one lidded eye on Melosa.
"She looks to me," he said, “like a dull and dowdy Roman,
with untidy habits, and the waste matters of sleep in the corners
of her eyes. A poor quality of hylic."
Petilian, looking at Ribad, sneered.
Damon told him that Ribad was from Alexandria, that many
Christians were there. He tried to explain a little of Ribad's
philosophy. When he said that if a man lusted he should surrender
to his lust, if he felt an urge to kill he should kill, Petilian opened
his eyes wide in horror.
"No Christian believes such wickedness."
Ribad was still studying the man with one sad black eye. “Tell
me," he said, "have you lain with this woman?"
Melosa looked as if she were in the presence of Satan.
"You long ungainly impostor," said Ribad, softly, without mal-
ice, almost without emotion, "why haven't you? Would you have
the woman hating you even more than she hates you now? For in
the matter of lust, you lackwit, women have always read the divine
purpose more clearly than men. Are you a hylic?"
Petilian was angry. He scowled and stared.
"Would you imagine," Ribad asked Damon, "that such a man
has a knowledge of celestial topography? Does he know the name
of the god or demon at each of the thousand gates? Does he know
the passwords the soul must use journeying from one celestial
sphere to another?"
Melosa said in a low voice to Petilian, “Make no reply to fools."
Ribad turned an eye up to Damon, who was standing, still
looking back and forth. "Nothing makes a woman so dull as want
of a lover. Is there not some word in Greek that means as dull as
an old virgin?"
"I don't know," Damon said.
Ribad looked again at Petilian. "Do these persons know that
109
the soul of a sinner is many times dearer to God than the soul of
a saint?"
Damon had never before heard that thought from a Christian.
He looked back across distance to Rome and Murdia.
"As for this man," Ribad was saying, “even if he is not offensive
to God he is offensive to me... There's smug self-righteousness all
through him like lard through a pig. Do you know," he asked,
speaking directly to him, “why only the ablest young men are
used as soldiers? Because the lust to kill is stronger in them. Do
you know why women prefer the young lover, who is fumbling,
to the old lover, who has mastered the art? Because lust is feeble
in the old, and mating with age is an affront to God. When the
fires of passion burn out we can only sit and wait."
Melosa was now staring at Ribad as if she had become fas-
cinated with him. She said, "Our Father coinmanded us not to
kill."
"I suppose," he said, almost closing both eyes, "you mean the
Jewish Yahweh. He is only a third-rate god, if indeed he is a god
at all."
"Our Lord also commanded us."
"You mean the phantom called Jesus?"
She looked for comfort and aid to Petilian. When he refused to
look at her she said: "He was not a phantom."
"A mere illustion to deceive hylics like you. My dull untidy
woman, if you haven't the mind to understand the divine purpose
why bother your head with it? Concern yourself with what you
can understand, such as your contempt for this fraud who has
never embraced you."
"You are wicked!" she cried, bugging her eyes at him.
"It may be," he went on, in his weary melancholy maddening
way, one eye closed, the lid on the other rising and falling, "that
you have picked up from the Orphics this nonsense about original
sin. But if a woman called Eve seduced an ancient called Adam,
don't you suppose that was part of God's plan? Or was omnipo-
tence overthrown by a naked woman in a garden?”
The next day Ribad told Damon that some gnostics were pro-
Jew, some were anti-Jew; some were licentious and some were
ascetic; some favored the ideas of Plato, some, those of the Per-
sians. Some said that Jesus was a man; some, like Ribad, that he
was an illusion; and still other gnostic Christians believed that he
had two manifestations. Those friendly to the Jews accepted Yah-
110
weh as the supreme God; those unfriendly rejected him as only
a minor demon.
Ribad urged Damon to stop off at Alexandria to see a gnostic
named Cerinthus; but Cerinthus, Damon learned in the city, was
far away in Galatia. One thing he did discover that was to open
a whole new realm to him: for some Alexandrian Christians their
Lord was the savior-god in a mystery-cult. If, it seemed to Damon,
Jesus, or the Lord which Jesus had become, was like Mithra, Dion-
ysus, Attis and other gods the central figure in a rite he might have
the key to the riddle. At this time he knew little about the mys-
teries but he had seen some of the initiates. Mithra's had a tiny
scar on their forehead, where they had been seared with a hot
iron. Some of the Attis votaries had unmanned themselves.
Excited by his discovery but unwilling to tarry here and learn
more he hastened to Antioch to find Levilla. Though several
times he had sent her money from Rome he found her in rags,
looking starved, but with an unwavering serenity in her wide-apart
eyes, in her pale face. It seemed to him that all her features were
touched by a quiet holiness.
She let him clasp her hand a moment but refused his kiss. His
morbid shyness returned. Looking at her he said, "Are you glad
to see me?"
"I don't know," she said.
"You still say you'll never marry me?"
"Never."
"You are happy as you are?"
"We are happy who serve our Lord.”
His thoughts turned to Ribad.
At this time of year the hills around Antioch were surpassingly
lovely. Damon asked her if she would go with him some after-
noon and sit among the flowers, and at first she said she would not,
but yielded after he promised not to touch her or to speak of any-
thing that she found offensive. Within sight of the city they sat
almost hidden by myrtle and laurel.
Damon told her he had prospered. His holdings had doubled
in value and he was now able to give a wife just about anything
that a wife might ask for.
Looking into his eyes, intently, a little self-consciously, she
said: "You will never understand. There are many reasons why a
Christian should not marry. One: our Lord will come again any
day, any moment; two: he commanded us to leave parents, wives,
111
husbands, children and go with him; three: the flesh is corrupt,
the kind of love you want is sinful." She looked away from him at
the hills, her hands in her lap, her fingers moving back and forth
over myrtle leaves. "Besides, Damon, you're not a Christian and
you'll never be. How foolish to speak of marriage to me!”
"I'll be a Christian when I find the truth in it."
"You promised not to talk that way," she said quietly. “I'm
not a Murdia, so do not blaspheme in my presence. Don't you
see how it is?" she asked, turning to look at him. "You haven't
won me, yet you abuse me. If I were your wife and your legal right
was established you'd probably beat me."
Grinning all over inside he said, "I would! With the utmost
enthusiasm I certainly would."
"You think virtue is very dull, don't you?"
"It sometimes seems so."
"And wickedness is always attractive?"
"For some it seems to be."
She plucked a flower and in the quiet and patient voice of the
dedicated teacher she said: “All passion is wicked-carnal love,
envy, hate, jealousy, greed-these are all wicked. Our bodies are
corrupt, only our souls can be pure and free. I tell you again, if
you ever become a Christian and accept our Lord and his bless-
ings I'll be happy, yes, really happy to be your sister."
He looked at her a little while, studying her face, wondering
why he wanted to marry her. "I don't want a sister. I want a wife.”
"You've never had a sister."
"Nor a wife either."
She turned to look at him. "You saw Murdia in Rome?”
"Yes."
"I knew you would. That's why you went, isn't it?"
"No. I went to read a story by one called Mark. Have you
Christians here ever read it or wanted to?"
"You saw Murdia," she said, her eyes intent and unhappy. “Did
she-?" She gave him a wan smile of pity. "I suppose she se-
duced you into sin?"
Damon colored a little. "We were, I guess, what you would call
sinful."
She sprang up, agitated and very angry. “Damon, can't you see
how wicked you are? You lie with this evil woman, yet ask to
marry me! Me!-who has been consecrated in our holy Savior.
Have you no sense at all of what a horribly wicked man you are?”
112
"I guess not," he said.
"You promised not to talk to me about things that offend me."
"It was you who brought Murdia up."
"But what you call love is offensive to me. Don't ever mention
it again. Never."
Damon arose and faced her. "You mean you don't wish ever to
see me again?"
"Oh, but you don't understand. We Christians see all people,
for our Lord commanded us to go into the world and teach the
truth. I'll see you, as I'll see others. I'll pray for you every day
of my life."
"That is most kind of you,” he said.
"I'll ask God to show you the way."
"For your kindness I will give you money."
"Oh no, Damon. I'll take no more of your money. It is wicked
money, though I tried to do good with what you gave me. It is
money that will take you to Murdia again.”
"But you have none."
"Our Lord provides. He gives and he takes away."
Baffled, a little angry, Damon looked at her. She was as much
of a riddle as Ribad and, he now suspected, a lot like him.
"Shall we go?" she asked.
"Not yet. You say it is evil money with which I'll see Murdia
again. But you did not want to go with me. Would you again
sleep with me? I mean would you be willing to?”
"No. You might use force."
Her words filled him with something close to nausea. He forced
himself to say: "You once told me you'd like to go to Ephesus,
Athens, all over the world, so that you could see what Christians
were like in many places.'
"I no longer feel that way. I'm happy here."
"But are you aware that Christians in other places don't believe
as you do here?"
"We have the truth here."
"You'll never care to read Mark's story?"
"No."
“You think it has no meaning for Christians?"
"None."
His nausea and his wonder growing he persisted. "Only the
Christians in Antioch have the truth?"
"I don't care to talk about it."
113
Stung by her pitying aloofness he concealed his malice: "But
if you don't talk about these things how shall I learn? You say
you Christians here are the elect, the remnant, the chosen, the new
Israel, the people set apart and sanctified. I wish to understand
these things."
"I think you never will," she said.
"Levilla?" he said softly, too softly.
"Yes?"
"Let's sit again. I have one thing to say and then I'll go."
Reluctantly she sat, but at arm's length from him. "Well, what
is it?"
"Have you no sense at all of what it means to be devoted to pur-
suit of truth only for the sake of truth?”
"If you were like that you'd be devoted to our Lord. He came
to bring all the truth there is."
He felt helpless. He tried again: "You can see no sense at all
in my wish to study Christians everywhere and discover the sources
of their belief, the reasons for their differences?"
"No, not with our Lord coming any minute."
"Are men no longer to seek the truth after he comes?"
"Damon, are you trying to be stupid? I've told you that all
the truth has been revealed."
"If I don't believe that I'm a sinner?”
"I'll pray for you," she said and turned away.
He stood looking after her as long as she was in sight. Then he
looked round him at the hills and west toward Rome and south
toward Alexandria and north toward Persia. He turned, then,
to face the east. It seemed to him that people like Levilla be-
longed in Rome-by which he had in mind the West; that even
Greece belonged to the West, if one was to compare a Dionysus
with a Buddha; that as a man went into the East he found people
more subtle and more poetry in their religions.
Or was he right?
114
XI
There was buoyancy in the thought that the mystery-cults might
offer a clue to the Christian riddle. He would see them or some
of them but which ought he to see first? Cybele Romans now
called the Great Mother, Isis and her nuns were popular in Rome,
Mithra was winning the devotion of the Legions; but upon all
of these had been cast the unimaginative blight of the West. Over
in Egypt Osiris and Attis were popular; in Syria, Adonis. It
seemed to Damon that people had got the gods mixed up: Dion-
ysus, whom Aristophanes had called the lighter of nocturnal feasts,
Sophocles the lighter of the stars, and Nonnus the night-shining
one, was popular not only in Athens but also in Thebes, in Thrace
and elsewhere. Because his popularity was so widespread and be-
cause in all his rites he was a god who died and came to life again
Dionysus seemed the logical choice for Damon's first adventure.
The rites at Eleusis a few miles from Athens on the Rharian
Plain were world-famous. He thought Levilla would wish to see
them but she was horrified; she said all mystery-cults but her own
were the work of Satan. She said that Satan had foreseen the com-
ing of her Lord and had counterfeited various analogies to de-
ceive the foolish. So in August Damon left her, knowing that the
rites would be held in September. Out on the sea he again had
the strange feeling that when sailing into the West he was sailing
away from his problem or at least away from the answers to it.
...
He had thought Athens a beautiful city but compared to Anti-
och it was nothing, save for a few features. It stood back from
the sea in an unlovely area of eroded valleys and denuded hills.
Its plain was watered by three rivers all of which when Damon ar-
rived were dry; and the country roundabout looked gray and bar-
115
ren, with little vegetation besides gorse-bush and patches of mois-
tureless thyme. Eleusis stood on a rocky bluff on the coast of Attica,
opposite the island of Salamis. The twelve miles across the hills
between it and Athens were known as the Sacred Way, because
on this path the initiates held their processions.
Damon had read that members of the mystery-cults were bound
by dreadful oaths, passwords and promises, and that those who be-
trayed the secrets were hunted down and slain. All the cults
seemed to believe that the dying god made known to his initiates
the secret of his immortality. It was this secret above all others
which they were sworn to keep and not once in all his reading
had Damon found a hint of it. He was now excited by the thought
that he would learn the secret.
He was to discover that the Dionysian mystery, to which women
and slaves were freely admitted but not murderers or magicians,
was quite elaborate in parts of it. After Damon and the other
initiates were chosen, heralds went forth to proclaim the sacred
truce to all people; and multitudes then gathered for the festival.
The worshipers assembled to hear the instructions of the hiero-
phant; when, after nightfall, a solemn march began from Eleusis
to Athens. Like the other initiates Damon bore a sacred object
and walked under an armed guard. It seemed to him, looking
round at them, that all the sacred objects were emblems of the
genitals, male and female. He was carrying a curiously shaped
bowl. In an impassioned speech the hierophant had explained
the secret nature of these objects, and Damon, listening and look-
ing down at his bowl, in that moment got a new idea of the womb.
The sacred objects were placed in the Eleusinion at the foot of
the Acropolis. Then a loud voice cried, "To the sea, initiates!
Wash away your sins!" They all scampered down to the harbor
of Phalerum and plunged in. If sins, Damon was thinking, could
be washed away so easily, all philosophers, indeed, all teachers
were superfluous. The next night—the mystery of Dionysus was
nocturnal-priests told them of the marvels and wonders which
they would behold; of their countless blessings because they had
been chosen; and of the need to prepare themselves with absti-
nence, prayer and pious thoughts. They would be vouchsafed the
holy light, while other people huddled in the filth and fog of
mortal ignorance. Damon saw in the countenances of his com-
panions the light of simple faith.
Up to this point he had not minded it but he was appalled by
116
the rite of sacrifice. Each of them carried a small pig; they had
been told that this was a symbol of the god and they were to devour
it. Most of the initiates had been brought to a state of frenzy with
drugged wine and exhortation, and now fell on their poor helpless
little beasts and literally tore them limb from limb. They had
been told that if they ate the flesh while the blood was warm and
the heart was still beating they would absorb from their deity a
greater measure of his divine essence. While the little pigs
screamed, the demented initiates, both men and women, gouged
off the living flesh with pieces of sharp stone or with their own
teeth. Damon had deliberately spilled his wine. He was not
drugged. In the gloom he turned away and set his little fellow on
the earth and let him escape. He would also have fled if he had
dared to but there were armed guards around the whole area.
Lest he be suspected of impiety he took blood from other car-
casses and smeared his mouth, face and garments, wondering mean-
while how with barbarism people expected to find their way to
God. He listened: the last squeal had been silenced; all around
him were crouched figures, their mouths eating and their eyes
looking up now and then as if to see a Lord coming.
In the fire that burned Rome and in the Christians there he
had seen human passions rise naked to the surface. He saw them
again this night. He was to see them still again when in one of the
nocturnal processions they set out under a sky gleaming with
torches for the hall of initiation at Eleusis. There they had a con-
secrated feast at which again they ate the flesh of their god, and
drank his blood in the form of a heavy bitter liquid that set pas-
sions aflame. Damon felt giddy and foolish but instead of partici-
pating in the wild scene he pretended to be ill, and so remained
apart to observe. At first the initiates began to dance and sob and
cry out that the holy spirit was descending. Everywhere torches
were glowing. There was sad wild music. The dancing initiates
began to leap and shriek and whirl, more and more violently, until
it was impossible for Damon in the weird gloom to distinguish
their faces, or man from woman. Some were weeping horribly and
some were screaming and trying to fly; and some with anguished
strangled moans were wailing, "It is coming! I can feel the holy
spirit!" Female voices were shrieking, "The god is entering me!"
and for a few moments Damon listened to them and wondered
what they meant. He became aware that all these people were
experiencing pleasurable erotic sensations. He moved among
117
them, dancing a little, so that he would not be suspected and chal-
lenged; and as the scene increased in violence and the initiates be-
gan to flagellate one another Damon found himself almost frantic,
trying to dodge the blows. Men and women ran sobbing and shrill-
ing against one another, trying to hurl one another down, to exam-
ine one another's private parts; and when dancing or leaping or
struggling they made copulatory movements. The women, it
seemed to him, were wilder and more wanton than the men. Then
some of them were rushing around on all fours like beasts, acting
like beasts in the darkness; for they were making beastlike sounds
and riding one another and wrestling wildly in their sexual ma-
neuvering. If, either man or woman, one assumed a sexual posture
he was immediately covered, by either man or woman, for it
seemed to make no difference. Damon's thoughts turned to Mur-
dia and the legend of Hermaphroditus. Some tore the garments
from their bodies and, white-naked, leapt and howled and reached
up to the torches, or fell back and then scurried around on hands
and knees like ghostly things; and now and then a woman would
plunge headlong to assault a male and rend his parts from him.
And it was all Damon could do not to abandon himself to this
wild and obscene orgy. It was that, the way it took hold of his pas-
sions and senses, and his mind almost, that was to leave him dazed,
and filled with wonder and questions.
The rites culminated in a sacred drama, which was acted by
the priests of the cult. Nothing that Damon had seen in a Roman
theater was more literal or shameless. The drama set forth the
rape of Persephone, daughter of Demeter, the earth-mother; and
with bulging and fascinated eyes the initiates saw Persephone cap-
tured and raped, while she was playing in the fields of Eleusis, by
Hades, king of the underworld. What, Damon wondered furi-
ously, did this mean-the rape of the daughter of mother-earth by
the symbol of evil and darkness! After raping her Hades dragged
her underground with him. Then they saw the disconsolate wan-
derings and heard the lamentations of Demeter, searching for her
child.
The second part of the drama showed the union of Zeus and
Demeter and the birth of the god Dionysus. When Demeter went
away wailing, some of the initiates lost control of themselves and
rushed off, crying to the god to come. But in the third part of the
drama, after a priest had said they would now see the holy light
and hear the holy word, there fell upon all, including Damon, a
118
sense of the deep and impenetrable mystery of life. So, at least, it
seemed to him; and he found himself thinking with what intimacy
the jungle brooded over the most intellectual minds.
He did not know how the scene was managed but it was brilliant
and impressive. They wandered in darkness over sharp stones
and obstacles and barriers-lost, O lost!-for they represented the
sufferings, the endless loneliness, of the uninitiated dead in the
afterlife. Nearly all of them were weeping and wailing. There
was no moon, no torches, no lights-only the infernal blackness
of hell itself, and its accursed, moaning. They wandered and wept,
fell and struggled and rose again, reached out to touch one an-
other in their fright; when, suddenly, there was a faint glow that
slowly and miraculously grew in size and intensity, like a great
bold sun coming up out of midnight depths; and bathed in this
light they beheld a cradled babe-god, borne by twelve angels.
Breathless, with all their evil passions hushed, their baser natures
stilled, they hastened toward it; and while they were running, a
vast and blinding light enveloped the world. With amazement
Damon looked into the heavens and heard heavenly music, soft
and sweet and far away. He was never to know how much he
actually saw, how much was illusion, induced by fasting, exhaus-
tion and the bitter potent god-drink. While looking at the god-
child they were now instructed in the secret phrases and formulas,
bound by a solemn and terrible oath to be forever mute, and ad-
mitted to the immortal life of the blessed.
Awed, chastened and bewildered, Damon took ship to Antioch;
and this time by preference he journeyed alone, for his mind and
his soul were filled. Above all his thoughts there at last stood one,
like the light he had seen in the night: truth developed through
myth but an accretion of legends at last destroyed it. Or cast a
pall over it like an opaqueness over the eye? Or just what was
the nature of it? Did they in all these cults mix the sun and mid-
night; mankind's love of the child with the grossest sexual pas-
sions? In their frenzied wish to be immortal had they not learned
with the Jews not to eat helpless beasts alive but to look on them
with compassion? He decided that he ought to see some other mys-
teries and he went to the library to read everything he could find
on the Persian god of light.
From the beginning of time, one of the stories said, there had
been an unceasing, desperate and inconclusive struggle between
Ahuramazda, the god of goodness and light, and Ahriman, the
119
power that presided over evil and darkness. Whether Mithra was
another name for the supreme deity or was his son Damon could
not determine. In any case, Mithra seemed to be the immemorial
sun-god, because his birthday was at the winter solstice, about De-
cember 25th. He was born from a rock and was sometimes called
the Rock, a word in favor with both Jews and Christians; and his
birth was attended by shepherds, who made offerings to the new-
born god. He had celibates and holy virgins in his service but
there seemed to be no goddess in this cult and women were not
admitted to its rites. With his own death Mithra insured the tri-
umph of good over evil and the happiness of mankind. His wor-
ship comprised baptism, fasts, communion and a holy supper; his
votaries were called brothers or soldiers in the Lord; and his
moral code was as austere as that of the Stoics.
Mithra's worshipers, like those in some of the other cults, were
organized in guilds, with a roll of members, a council of decurions,
and with masters and curators. They depended on their wealthy
members for the erection of chapels, altars and crypts; and they
emphasized such virtues as chastity, courage, honor and self-denial,
and purity in thought and deed. Mithra was born of a virgin.
Why, Damon wondered, did the Christians say that their Lord
had not been?
With the other initiates Damon was baptized, for in this, as in
other cults, it was believed that water had mysterious power to
purify the soul of guilt and wash sins away. They were plunged
into water and told that, in a mystical sense, they were stripping
off the old man and putting on the new. They became holy. They
became one of Mithra's chosen, one of his sons. In this symbolic
act they were somehow identified with the death and resurrection
of the god—and that, it seemed to Damon, was exactly what bap-
tism had meant to Paul. The priest attending them said, “I now
wash away your sins in the name of Mithra the Father and of his
holy spirit.'
In another part of the initiation Damon was taken into a cave
or crypt deep in the earth, which was arranged, he had learned
from his reading, as a macrocosm, with the ceiling representing the
sky. Along this crypt were tiny chambers. Damon was stripped
naked and told to crawl into a womblike darkness and to draw his
legs up against his belly and to feel that he was not yet born; and
there he lay, a little frightened, for what seemed hours. Everything
around him was pitchdark. He listened for human breathing on
120
either side of him but there seemed to be none. There was ab-
solutely no sound of any kind, not even a gurgling in the mother's
belly: he understood that he was in a womb and was to be born
again.
·
Cramped and aching and full of wonder he was summoned
forth with a blaze of lights and a priest intoning, “You shall have
an immortal and incorruptible body, as well as an immortal soul;
and you shall obtain the kingdom of the Holy One! You who have
lived on earth and known the heavenly King shall be a friend of
God, no longer in the thrall of lusts, suffering and sickness! For
you have become divine, and all that pertains to the godlike, God
will now bestow on you, for you have been born again!
""
The god himself was born again in what Damon thought a very
impressive tableau. It was deep underground, and in complete
darkness and silence the initiates waited. The management of
light, Damon thought, was even more spectacular than that at the
birth of Dionysus. A figure representing Mithra seemed to be
born literally out of the great rock vault, which, Damon supposed,
was intended to symbolize the underworld of the sun. In the bril-
liance of a vast illumination, which unfolded from splendor to
splendor, Mithra came forth; and the priests were crying, "We see
him coming! The god is born!" Shepherds, heads bowed in awe,
came forward to attend him; there was rejoicing and singing. This
part of the rites was handled with such imagination and skill that
Damon found himself responding with a sense of reverence to all
the hidden and undivined depths in the mystery called life.
But the god had to die for the sins of his people—and in a flash
it came to Damon that the human father was taking on himself the
transgressions of wayward sons. The god's death, shown in a beau-
tiful transfiguration on a hilltop, was also managed with extra-
ordinary skill in the use of light. The god-figure was illumined
and made to appear very mysterious. On his brow was a crown of
thorns, intended to symbolize his degradation and suffering; and
over his face and naked body was the impression of bloody sweat.
The god died there for the sins of men and was then buried in a
rock tomb; but on the third day he came forth, the risen god, the
resurrected one, and the rejoicing was tumultuous. Ah, what a
triumph over death! How childlike, Damon thought, this tableau,
yet how beautiful in its expression of human hope and longing!
Another part of the rites astonished him, that of the taurobolium
or slaying of the bull, and the blood bath. The initiate stood in
121
a pit, with the bull above him on a plank screen. A man repre-
senting Mithra then thrust into the bull a long knife. A priest,
also in the pit, threw his head back and extended his arms, and
even opened his mouth, the more completely to be drenched with
the downpour of blood. Damon came out, bloody from head to
feet. He had seen many figures of the Mithra bull: his tail and
ears and other parts of his body were represented as barley or
wheat or other succulent plant-life. The mystical taurobolium
seemed to say that Mithra, with a heroic act, poured on the earth
the great fertilizing powers of the bull, in his own rich blood. The
initiates were literally washed in the blood of the bull and for sev-
eral days had to wear their blood-stained garments. Damon knew
that some cults used a lamb instead of a bull and that the initiates
in these were washed in the blood of the lamb. In the taurobolium
the one representing the god said to the initiates, “Come unto me,
all you who are weary and heavy-laden, and I will give you rest."
The Mithra rites were concluded with a last supper or holy meal,
when the god sat with his companions before returning to the
celestial realms. The votaries of Dionysus used wine in the holy
meal; those of Mithra, more austere in their habits, used plain
coarse bread and water. Here, as in the other cult, it was explained
that in eating the bread and water the initiates were literally taking
into themselves the blood and body of their god. They were then
promised that the god would soon return and that all the dead
would rise to meet him.
Damon thought, How simple, how credulous, yet how beauti-
ful it all is! How much it encompassed of human striving between
the womb and the womb! So far as he could tell he was the only
skeptic: none of the other initiates had a Pamphilus with them:
with the innocence of children they looked forward to the god's
return and to a blessed life everlasting. Men in middle age wept
like children, scattering in a flood of tears their doubts and anxi-
eties and hastening with streaming eyes to kiss one holy object and
another-men who in another mood and another hour would
have cut one another's hearts out. Damon, watching them, was
trying to see more clearly the sources of human baseness and hu-
man nobility; to see the well-springs of faith; to understand what
the Christians were doing with these old myths, these ancient
houses of truth.
After the rites of Dionysus, after the rites of Mithra, he had to
be alone a few days with neither friends nor books around him;
122
with only the sense of what he had felt, as he strove to throw the
feeble light of his mind back across the centuries to the primal
sources. When at last he asked Elisha what the taurabolium meant,
Elisha said what could it be save a refinement of the ancient prac-
tice of eating the god. Damon had come across that idea in his
reading.
"You think they actually ate the god?"
For a few moments Elisha regarded him. “Why does the cow
eat the placenta?"
Damon hadn't known that the cow did. He thought about it.
"But if they ate the god what was it they ate?”
"A man. A surrogate." Elisha gave him a thin smile. "Don't
think they wouldn't have eaten the god if they could have got hold
of him. Did you ever hear an adoring mother say to her babe,
Oh, I'm just going to eat you up? Our wish to absorb what we love
or admire is something on the path you have set for yourself."
"No doubt," Damon said, his mind bursting.
But when he told Levilla about the Mithra rites she was horri-
fied. She said they were the work of Satan. He asked if the Chris-
tians in Antioch had similar rites and she turned away, evading
him. He knew then that they had. But he knew that to participate
in them he would have to become a Christian. Would that be
more shameful than pretending to accept the fellowship of Dion-
ysus or of Mithra? His mind told him that it would not but his
emotions still felt around them his mother's arm with more pres-
sure than his father's.
He told her he was going to Alexandria again because at least
one of the Christian sects there had a drama of death and rebirth.
"Would you like to see it?"
"They're not Christians," she said.
"Levilla, after all these years of reading and seeking I have
learned, I think, at least one thing: there are Christians but there
is no Christian. I imagine that may be a great thing to have
learned. For how terrible if all people believed alike!"
She said quietly: "We all have to believe the same to be saved.”
"I begin to see that that's the surest way to be damned." He
asked her if Christians in Antioch washed themselves in the blood
of the lamb and she looked at him with horrified eyes. "But you
speak of your Lord as the lamb or of the lamb as your Lord, don't
you just as those with Mithra speak of him as the bull. You
call
your Lord the Dayspring from on high, the Light, the Sun of
123
righteousness. Those are all terms in the Mithra ritual."
"Please!" she said, shuddering. “You promised not to offend
me.”
"I have never promised to stop trying to understand. You might
help me. For instance, Paul says Jesus took bread and broke it
and said, This is my body which is broken for your sake. He took
a cup of water or wine-which was it, I wonder?-and said, This
is the new covenant through my blood, drink it in remembrance
of me. No Jew could have said that. Those are things they say
in the Mithra rites. You could tell me if those are things you say
here."
She was looking at him as she might have looked at Satan.
"Please say no more."
"But I wish to understand."
"You will understand only when you believe."
"Levilla, will you listen a moment? There is in this world what
we call realities. It is a reality, a fact, that thought of drinking
blood is abhorrent to a Jew. So Paul must have taken these ideas
from the heathen cults.'
""
"I care nothing about Paul."
"Have you no interest at all in—in—”
"No."
"You've no interest in the fact that Mithra people say they have
put on their god?-and that Paul says that in the communion of
the blood and body of your Lord you put him on? You've no in-
terest in the fact that Paul's explanation of baptism is exactly that
of Mithra worshipers?"
"None."
"No wonder that Jews are amused and amazed by what Chris-

a Jewish rab in Israel and the heathen sun-god."
"I care nothing about Jews."
"And nothing about the wonder in all these things?”
"Oh, you're wicked!" she breathed. Her eyes darkened. “It
does no good to pray for you."
Damon was sitting on a wooden bench by the river. Levilla,
standing, was looking down at him, her eyes smoky with contempt.
"No good at all," he said.
"You evil man! Always you do the talking, you long-winded
tiresome preacher! Now you listen!"
"I will," he said, looking at her eyes.
124
She drew a deep breath. "There is God and there is Satan.
They struggle against one another. Satan-'
"The Satan idea came from the Persians."

Again she drew a deep tortured breath. "Satan wants to over-

"Habet Diabolus Christos suos."

"I was only muttering."
