XII
Damon set out for Egypt, planning to spend a few months in Alex-
andria's libraries, the finest in the world with more than a mil-
lion volumes. He liked Alexandria itself: it didn't have clear
water rushing down the gutters but it was a handsome city. It had
a good climate. Even in the heat of summer there was a cooling
breeze from the sea; and for the evenings there were gay pleasure
boats on the canals that led to Lake Mareotis and Canopus. Its
people were a gay people.
Soon after arriving he sought out Ribad and found him looking
like a filthy sick beggar and living like one. Damon gave him
money, which he accepted as his due and at once squandered; the
moment this strange man recovered a little strength he spent it in
whorehouses and inns, determined to wreck his body before his
soul left it. He slept with harlots, daily drank himself into a
stupor, ate food so highly seasoned that it made him vomit, and
tortured himself with drugs and mild poisons, as well at with un-
natural postures of his limbs.
"A curse on it," he said, fixing Damon with his sad black eyes.
"When we destroy the flesh we liberate the spirit.”
Damon was more than fascinated by this man. He felt affection
for him. This perplexed and troubled him and he tried to look
to the source of it.
When he said he wanted to see the Christian rites Ribad intro-
duced him to several of the leaders, including a very dark and in-
tense man named Markekas. Markekas assumed that Damon was
a Christian and Damon did not undeceive him. He kissed Damon's
forehead with infinite passion and cried, “O truly holy mysteries!
127
O stainless light! You will be illumined! You'll be sealed and
you'll become a god!"
Damon stared at the man. What sort of Christian was this?
The principal rites here, Ribad told him, were held at the
spring equinox. Damon was not surprised, for at this season oc-
curred the death and resurrection of most of the gods in the secret
cults. Before seeing the Christian rites he would take an oath, be
anointed, baptized, and anointed again; and then share the holy
meal. In his oath he swore by the holy Lord, who was over all
men, and by the Good One, to cherish and protect the secrets with
his life. Parts of the oath were passwords to the highest and holiest
mysteries.
He was startled to learn that Christian baptism here was not
with water but was a kind of mystical receiving of the holy spirit.
They anointed him with oil, smearing his hair, ear lobes and the
point of his nose and chin; and he was told that he could then see
what no eye had seen and hear what no ears had heard, for there
had sprung up in him the waters of eternal life. They filled a
chalice with milk and honey and gave it to him to drink. Next
came the oil of thanksgiving; two of the officials in turn sealed
him with it by marking a cross on his forehead.
The holy meal was held in gloom behind barred doors. They
drank wine, which Markekas blessed with these words: "We thank
you, our Father, for this blood of your holy son, Jesus the Savior
Christ. To you be the glory forever.” In similar manner he blessed
the bread. They then together gave thanks and offered a prayer
of supplication. All this was very simple. It was more mystical
and symbolic than the Christian rites which Damon had heard
about in other cities.
What he was eager to see was the drama. This was presented in
a large empty building that looked like an old and abandoned
warehouse. A herald proclaimed the mysteries as imminent, and
challenged those assembled. Was there anyone present who had
not put away evil? Was there anyone who nursed a grievance or
grudge or bitterness toward a neighbor? They were then told
that they would behold the unseen who sees all things, the uncon-
tained who contains all things, the unneeding whom all things
need and through whom they exist, incomprehensible, eternal,
incorruptible and unmade, who made all things by the word of
his power, which was his divine son.
They then proceeded with the drama, which filled many scenes
128
and a considerable period of time. It was after dark. There were
many small lights representing stars but the gloom was deep and
it was impossible to see clearly. In the first scene a woman, who
took the part of Mary, the mother of Jesus, was alone in a garden;
and there came down from above a light symbolizing the holy
spirit and this light softly touched her. She was then shown trans-
figured in a weird illumination, looking up at the heavens, her
hands clasped, her lips blessing God. Damon understood that the
spirit of God had touched Mary to impregnate her with the divine
child and that for these Christians, at least, the god was virgin-
born.
Next came the birth scene. Mary was in what seemed to be a
stable, and in a basket in the manger lay the child. Shepherds
came in to look at the child and rejoice. Overhead three stars
came slowly out of the east, and under them, guided by their light,
came three magi or wise men; and these also looked at the babe
and rejoiced. Then from a concealed position, as though from far
away, came soft music and soft sweet voices singing. Damon sup-
posed they were angel voices rejoicing over the child's birth.
For him it was a tender scene, well done. After the angels had
sung a few moments most of the people assembled joined in the
song, and Damon thought they must have sung together many
times, for they harmonized well. The singing was accompanied
by low music. Well! Damon was thinking. The Christians did some
of these things better than the other cults did them! Did they have
something that the worshipers of Dionysus and Mithra did not
have? Yes, there was something: he thought the word for it was
tenderness.
Next came a scene which showed the man Jesus on the bank
of a river, with a hairy and almost naked man in a goatskin girdle.
The man in the girdle baptized Jesus in the waters and a strange
thing happened. Damon stared up at the roof but he was never
to know how in the world they managed it: a pure white dove
came down from the ceiling and rested on the shoulder of Jesus.
A loud clear voice from somewhere up in the roof spoke, saying,
"This is my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased! Hear him,
my children, and listen to his teachings!" Damon looked round
at the faces: these Christians were gnostics but they certainly did
not accept Ribad's notion that Jesus had been only a phantom.
This Jesus before them was a man in the flesh. He looked like an
Egyptian.
129
There now came a series of scenes in which Jesus healed a leper;
cast seven devils out of a woman; raised a child from death; and
performed other wonders. When the seven devils were cast out,
seven persons in the skins of beasts went squealing away on all
fours into the darkness; and a woman sitting not far from Damon
clutched her throat and began to pray. The dead child got up and
walked, saying, "A moment ago I was dead but now I live. Blessed
be the Lord our God." The leper gave thanks and cast away his
unclean garments.
How simple and childlike it all was!
The final scene represented what Alexandrian Christians called
the Passion—the suffering and death and resurrection of the savior-
god. A voice back in the gloom announced the events to come.
Jesus had come up to Jerusalem, where the Pharisees, who hated
him for his wonder-working, were bent on his death. He would
be betrayed by the Jews. Damon saw Jesus go away with a few
of his followers to the far side of the building, where, the voice
said, he would pray, knowing that his death was imminent. A man
who was identified as Ioudaios came now with officers and went
over where Jesus was praying; and presently the officers came back,
bringing Jesus with them. In the gloom Damon could make out
that Ioudaios had a great crown of flaming red hair and at once
he related him to Typhon, the red-haired one, who in Egyptian
myth was the slayer of Osiris. The red hair symbolized the rays
of the sun. Reflecting on these things Damon thought, Good
heavens! Is it only a sun-myth after all?
The announcer said that Jesus was brought to trial before the
high priest and condemned to death. The next scene showed a
mock scourging, and then a hanging. The man's arms were drawn
above his head and bound, his feet were bound to a platform at
the base of the tree. A crown of thorns was placed on his brow.
Those crucifiying him mocked and reviled him. Damon was
thinking that in practically all the heathen cults the god was
scourged before he was killed.
After a few minutes Jesus was taken down from the tree and
buried in what the announcer said was a rock tomb. He said that
three days would pass. Then the hall was slowly filled with light
as braziers were kindled, to symbolize the coming of morning;
and a woman-Damon was never to learn which one-came to
the tomb and found an angel there robed in white. The angel
told her that Jesus had risen from the dead but would come again,
130
to usher in the kingdom. The assembled multitude now took up
the chant, "Our Lord is risen! . . . and there was heavenly
music and the voices of angels singing. Markekas now spoke to
the people, calling them to repentance and prayer and promising
that those who accepted the risen Lord would be saved to life ever-
lasting.
Parts of the drama were crude but on the whole Damon thought
it quite moving. It was more refined and tender than the rites of
Dionysus or of the Magna Mater, and less austere than those of
Mithra. He wondered if any other Christian group enacted the
drama of a dying and rising god. He was sure that those in Rome
never had. It was at last clear to him that the Christian faith, at
least in part of its rites, was only another mystery cult. All these
cults had in common the assurance of purification, forgiveness of
sins, communion with the deity, regeneration of the soul, and im-
mortality in another life. So far as he knew they all had a re-
deemer-savior who died and rose from the dead. They offered sal-
vation through sacramental rites. The initiates were identified
in a mystical and mysterious way with their god. They all ate some
symbol of the god, either lamb or bull or an effigy of him baked
with flour. He learned from Markekas that in one of the Christian
rites in Alexandria the image of a baked god-child was eaten and
that this had led to rumors of cannibalism among Christians.
It was on this visit to Alexandria that Damon learned that some
of the customs and beliefs there were wholly unknown to Chris-
tians in Rome, Antioch or Caesarea. Markekas had cried, "O mys-
teries truly! O stainless light!" Paul would have understood that,
but not the Christians in Rome. Markekas had cried, "The da-
duchs lead me on to the epopt of the heavens and of God! The
Lord is the hierophant and seals the mystes for himself, himself
the photagogue!" Damon smiled, wondering what Levilla would
make of that. He was sitting in the library one day thinking about
these things, when in a flash of illumination there came to him a
simple fact: the beliefs of Christians were being determined in
various places largely by the local beliefs and customs in those
places. Two cities as different as Rome and Alexandria would have
to have striking differences in their faith. The farther East you
went-? Maybe that was it.
Damon spent many months in the city's libraries or talking to
Christians. He could find no one who had seen Jesus in the flesh
or who would talk about Jesus as a man. He could find no one
131
who gave a hoot about Paul or who had ever heard of Mark or
Luke. He began to feel that he lived in a world of myth, envel-
oped by fold upon fold of soft gloom, in which light glimmered.
No Christian in Alexandria had ever heard of Peter! When Da-
mon told of the wonders in the book Peter was said to have writ-
ten Markekas dismissed it as the work of a vagrant impostor.
One day he saw David ben Huna and asked him to a tavern for
wine and talk. David was turning gray and he seemed more world-
weary. Elisha's mind was scholarly, his emotions were rather cold;
David's emotions came welling to the surface like bubbling waters.
Elisha's smile was pale and dry; David's was like the light inside
his soul.
"Well, so you saw the god die and rise from death. Don't they
all? Krishna climbs through the firmament of Indra. Some of my
people think that Enoch and Elijah went straight to heaven with-
out bothering to put on a shroud. Absit omen! Is it all too foolish
to be beautiful? You mentioned the Passion: that's what Egyptians
call the ascension of Osiris. Why bless you, Christians have even
taken from Egyptians the sign of the cross.
"By the
way, did you ever go to see the old men in Pella?"
"They were very hostile."
"Well, of course they're not Christians really, for they have no
christ. Yet how strange, for they were the first believers in Jesus!"
"Yes. It seems to have started in Israel and got transplanted."
"By Paul chiefly. With what earnestness he labored to transform
a Jew into the sun-god! What did you think about the betrayal of
Jesus?"
"In what way?"
"If everybody knew him, why pay a man money to point him
out?"
"It seemed pretty childlike," Damon said.
"I'll tell you what it means. Ioudaios is a word that means not
Jew but all Jews. Judas is a short form of it.”
"Oh?" said Damon. "So that's it."
"That's it. Just why they insist on representing the Jewish peo-
ple as the betrayer I don't know. Maybe it's only a part of their
childish attempt to fulfill what they take to be prophecies. Had
you thought of that?”
"No. What do you mean?"
"They have found that Zechariah said, And I said unto them, if
ye think good, give me my price; and if not, forbear. So they
132
weighed for my price thirty pieces of silver. In their drama here
they have Ioudaios betray Jesus for thirty pieces of silver."
"Holy heaven!" said Damon, staring at him. "Are they that
simple?"
"If they can show that prophecies are fulfilled they will give
their religion divine sanction and prestige. But they should be
more careful of historical facts. Under Roman law Jesus could not
have been tried before a high priest."
"I had learned that," Damon said.
“They are determined that Ioudaios shall be Typhon."
"Yes." David filled their glasses. Damon looked at him and
said: "A philosopher named Socrates once asked me a question.
He wanted to know if the scapegoat has ever collected his fee."
"His fee? What on earth is that?”
"What would it be? Socrates says mankind would not be in such
a mess if the scapegoat had been paid his fee.”
"I don't understand what you mean."
"You don't?"
David drank off half a cup of wine. He was looking at Damon.
Then suddenly his face lit up, his whole face from his throat to his
hair. He said, "Who but the Jews have ever tried to pay him?
The Stoics maybe, maybe the Cynics. Why, yes, all the wisdom in
the world is summed up in the question the philosopher put to
you. Job came close to seeing it. The Preacher in his bitterness
missed it. Our prophets thundered around and around it but
did they ever see it?"
"Well, are the Cynics trying to pay him?—with self-denial.”
"Maybe, but isn't the best coin good deeds? What do the Chris-
tians say? Just what Paul said: all thing are yours. All things are
yours! Did Paul offer the scapegoat his fee? On the contrary, to
the slave, the sinner, the blasphemer all things belong. A Chris-
tian can lie, steal, rape, murder, deny his parents, copulate with
beasts, blaspheme God; but if in the few moments before dying he
repents and asks to be forgiven he is saved to glory."
Damon was thinking of Ribad. "But is that all of it? Is this
faith only the easiest of the ways to heaven?"
"Well, it clearly is a matter of repentance and of divine grace.
Did the scapegoat die for them so that they can indulge in every
possible vice and yet be saved?"
"But your people are saved only with good deeds?”
"Chiefly with good deeds. That anyway is supposed to be it.
133
But it looks to me as if Paul placed the whole burden on the scape-
goat, on this son of God who came down to die for sins. When
under heaven have human beings so completely ducked their
moral responsibilities?"
"But you have a goat for Azazel."
"That's true. On our day of atonement he is dragged into the
wilderness and flung over a precipice, with the sins of Israel on
his head. That's an old and primitive idea that still survives.
Nowadays we place more emphasis on good deeds, on free will
and moral choice."
"Your goat for Azazel doesn't count for much now?"
"Not much, I think. Damon, do you get my point?"
"I'm not sure."
With a fresh flow of energy David returned to it. "But first,
more wine." He called for wine and filled their cups. "Now let's
put it this way: who are the Christians? Chiefly the common peo-
ple-slaves, beggars, idlers, foolish women, the diseased, the crip-
pled, the stupid. What if this belief were to conquer all other be-
liefs?"
"You don't think it could?"
"Oh, it may be only a fad that will pass soon enough. But sup-
pose it should endure and triumph with its doctrine that the last
shall be first. What would happen then? The uncommon people
would be enslaved by it. Can you imagine a time when common
people would occupy the positions of power?"
"No."
"Can you foresee a time when most or perhaps all art will be
for the common people?"
"No."
"Can you foresee a time when most of the books will be written
for the less intelligent part of mankind? Well, the thought is in-
credible enough. But wouldn't that be the logical development
of Paul's idea? If the last shall be first, if the ignorant and incom-
petent are to find special favor with God, then common men in
all Christian lands will some day rule. There might even come
a time when they will talk about the age of the common man."
The thought was so horrible that they dropped it. A few days
later, again drinking wine, David raised a problem that had be-
come a nightmare to him and his people. Why, he asked, were
Christians blaming Jews for the death of their god? In a Roman
province and that is all Israel was-Jews had no jurisdiction
134
over such things. Besides, said David, smiling, gods were not born
in Israel.
"But they're blaming us and hating us for what we never did.
In how many places are they praying for our destruction? When
Titus razed our holy city, how the Christians rejoiced! They want
us wiped off the face of the earth."
"That seems to be true," Damon said.
"If they triumph what will happen to my people?"
"But will they triumph?"
"I sometimes think they may. They have a faith that appeals
enormously to simple minds. Have you heard what happened re-
cently in Italy?"
"No."
"Whole mountains of boiling rock erupted and engulfed cities.
Pompeii was completely buried. What did the Christians in Rome
do?"
“I can imagine it. I saw them when Rome burned and again
when Jerusalem was sacked."
"Damon, they went mad with joy. I was there. They said their
Lord was coming again and that oceans of boiling rock would flow
over Rome. After that act of divine mercy all Jews would be put
to torture."
Damon shuddered. He was remembering his mother.
“Paul is chiefly responsible for this hatred of my people. Do
you know why?
""
"I know a part of it."
"Yes, you know that he threw away parts of the Law because
they offended other people. You know that he interpreted our holy
books to suit himself. Or did you? You know that he crammed
himself full of the doctrines of Philo and then transformed a Jew-
ish rab into the heathen sun-god."
"Yes, I understand that part of it.”
"I'd put it this way, to put it simply: Christians hate us because
we make the way to heaven too difficult."
Damon looked at him a moment and then smiled. “All right."
"It wasn't enough to blaspheme our religion by saying our
Holy One had a son. They then had to accuse us of killing their
man-god. Some of them have said to me, Your people killed our
Lord. What can we do against such a monstrous lie?"
"It is a monstrous lie. But if God had a son and sent him down
to die, I can't see that it matters much who killed him. His assas-
135
sins fulfilled the divine purpose. Christians wouldn't be on such
an easy way to heaven if someone hadn't killed him."
"Thanks for the ironies," David said drily. "But really—"
"Yes, yes, I know what you mean.”
"I tell you they insist on our accepting their blasphemous notion
that our God had a son and we killed him."
"Yes, I know that."
"Isn't one goat for Azazel enough?”
Damon smiled. One, he thought, was more than enough, and he
was in full agreement both with David's passion and his logic. He
could think of nothing in the world more terrible than what Chris-
tians were doing to Jews. Many times he drank wine with David
and they talked about it. Greater horrors, David said, would come.
Or now and then Damon would talk at random out of his con-
fused and unrelated data and David would gather the parts into a
formula. As when, after Damon told him about Ribad, he said:
"There it all is in a simple parable of lust and penitence. All
things were Ribad's. He spent his flesh and saved his soul with an
act of regret and an act of divine grace. To inherit heaven he
didn't have to perform a single good deed in all his life. Did he?”
"Not according to his notion of the Christian faith.”
"Isn't his notion the common one?"
"It may be."
Just before Damon left Alexandria, Ribad died. Damon was in
the library reading when a Christian woman came to him and
said, "Ribad wishes to see you. He's very sick."
Damon found Ribad in a filthy stinking room in a brothel. He
looked ghastly. Sweat stood out in big drops like dead eyes all
over his face, and his own eyes were burning like hot black jewels.
His lips were red and cracked with fever. His ears looked like
dried and warped slices of tomato. He was suffering and Damon
knew that he was dying, but like some weird purity in a fable he
was a man who had found peace.
With a pale hand, moist and hot, he clasped Damon's. He
turned up to him a thin ghostly smile.

In such manner a gnostic Christian died. He died in faith as
serene, in peace as deep, as any Damon was ever to know; and
there, as he had said it would, lay his foul and wasted and empty
body, fit food for the crows. In one moment he was talking to
Damon, his friend; in the next he was gone. What did it mean, O
Lord, what did it mean? Or was there any meaning in it? Was
this faith, as some said, only childlike superstitions housed in the
illiterate and the stupid?—or was there in it a deeper truth, yet to
be found?
He slipped out of the room, leaving Ribad's body to the harlots.
138
XIII
Going up the coast he thought of putting ashore at Jamnia but
changed his mind. Some day he would go there, to talk to Israel's
most learned men but now he was content to stand in full sun
and sea-mist and gaze inland. As far as he could see, the country
looked barren and desolate, as desolate as the area around Pella:
different lands, he had decided, nourished different philosophies
and religions: Judea was a desert between the Orontes and the
Nile. Trying to sense the thing he stared south where Egypt lay
warm and sensuous; and east at the stony wastes of southern Is-
rael, thinking of Jeremiah; northeast to the rich Euphrates and
Babylon, from which had come so much myth and legend; and
north to the pale blue phantomland of Persia. As he stood, look-
ing round him, maps continued to develop, superimposed upon
one another-maps that had been emerging in his mind for years
now: of the geographies, the religions, the philosophies, all subtly
and inextricably related, somehow, in ways that eluded him; and
above them all the sun, the sun-god, the seven heavens. No, not
above them all: not above Judea, not now: a difference was there
and Damon felt that it came from the nature of the earth: all
around it were provinces richly colored, sensuous, drenched with
sun and wine and laziness . . . there was a meaning here but it
still got away from him. A part of it was in Paul's words where
he said that though he had known the Christ in the flesh he would
now know him no more. A part of it—he was still gazing at the
stony wasteland—was in the horror with which Jews rejected the
notion that God had a son: they would make a parable of a fig tree
in a land where life was a miracle: where life was lush and abun-
dant the Ribads would treat it with contempt. Yet how strange
139
that the Christians should be claiming the Jewish holy books as
their own, and calling themselves the New Israel—as though the
Orontes and the Jordan or the Nile and the Jordan could be ex-
changed with a mere effort of will! As though Plato would not for-
ever be an alien, or Philo, among the circuses and theaters and
baths of Rome, or Isaiah and Ezekiel in the gardens of Daphne or
Diana!
He sailed up the coast, studying his notes, trying to arrange the
maps in his mind, wondering if Levilla would be glad to see him.
She was, in her cool detached way, though his sudden appearance
did not at all disturb her tranquillity. Damon was past thirty
now and looked it; Levilla did not look a day older than twenty.
Her eyes were as clear as a child's, her face as soft and smooth; and
her body was as perfect as on the day when, frenzied and hysterical,
they had lain together. Having eschewed passion she seemed to
be in possession of eternal youth.
When Damon, teasing her, asked if she was ready to marry him
she turned on him those wide clear deepset eyes and said gravely:
"Paul says if you live after the flesh you shall die."
"Only recently I saw a Christian who lived after the flesh. He
died with the logos singing all over him."
She shrugged and arched her brows.
"Have you heard what happened in Italy?”
"I don't care."
"Boiling eruptions buried whole cities and shook the Romans
in their graveyards but your Lord did not come."
"He'll never come there."
"In that case isn't God wasting his signs?"
"You wicked man. You must talk to Luke."
"Oh yes, Luke."
"Luke is wonderful."
"And handsome? A Jew? Old? Do you love him? . . . Levilla,
does thought of him tie your tongue?"
Her eyes flashed. "Oh, you longwinded man, you ask questions
but never let a person answer them! No, he's not a Jew. Yes, he's
handsome. No, I'm not in love with him."
"In what way is he wonderful?"
"He can answer all your questions."
She wearied him. "Mankind has asked a lot of questions that
Luke can never answer.”
Luke was a man of medium height, with brown hair, soft brown
140
eyes, and a pale and rather ascetic face. He seemed to Damon to be
intelligent, alert, persuasive and astonishingly fluent. Almost
daily Luke taught such groups as would listen to him and one after-
noon Damon went with Levilla to hear him teach. He first offered
a prayer and a blessing. When he talked his words flowed with
such ease that Damon suspected he had memorized what he had
to say.
"Brothers and sisters," said Luke, facing a hundred people,
"when a multitude gathered round our Lord he healed them and
made them whole. He lifted up his eyes to the heavens, saying,
Blessed are the poor for theirs will be the kingdom of God. Blessed
are they that hunger for they shall be filled. Blessed are they that
weep and
cry out of sorrow.
·
·
""
Damon looked round discreetly at the listening faces and saw
in them the hope, the yearning, the childlike faith, the complete
lack of cynicism and humor-in all of them, in the whole ragged
stinking multitude of them, sitting on the earth and gazing up at
Luke.
“Our Lord said unto us, Love your enemies, do good to them
who hate you, bless them who curse you, pray for them who treat
you with contempt and spite. If a man strikes you on one cheek,
offer him the other; if he asks for your cloak, give him your coat
also...
""
Damon put an ear to Levilla and pretended to listen. Then he
turned and whispered: "Kings, robbers, thieves, they should all
love a doctrine like that."
"Be still," she said.
"A certain famous ruler said to our Lord, Master, what shall I
do to inherit eternal life? Tell me. And our Lord said to him, You
know the commandments. Do not kill, do not steal, do not com-
mit adultery, do not bear false witness, and honor your father and
your mother. And the man said, All these things I have done.
Our Lord said to him, One thing you have not done: sell all that
you have, give it to the poor and follow me. . .
""
Levilla poked Damon in his ribs. "You," she said.
He moved so close that he could feel one of her thighs. He knew
that she liked to have him stand close, when with an excuse, such
as Luke speaking, she could pretend to be unaware. Or was she
unaware? He studied her face. "But if everybody sells, who buys?
Who keeps the world going?"
"The world is coming to an end."
141
"When our Lord saw this rich man grieve he said to him, How
can those with riches enter the kingdom of God? It would be easier
for a camel to go through the eye of a needle. . .
"That's you," Levilla said, poking him again.
He moved more snugly against her.
99
"How shall we pray?" Luke was asking. "I will teach you. Our
Father in heaven, hallowed be your name, let your kingdom come.
May your will be done, as in heaven, so on earth. Give us day by
day our bread. Forgive us our sins, as we forgive those who sin
against us. Lead us not into temptation. . .
"You," Levilla said.
""
Very gently he moved his thigh against hers to see what she
would do. She did nothing. Studying her face again he saw that
her admiration for Luke was a glowing thing in her eyes, a warmth
in her face, a stillness in her breathing. He looked at the people
and saw the Adam's apple in the throats of men moving up and
down; saw little old ragged unclean half-starved women dabbing
at their eyes with gnarled fingers; saw a child looking back and
forth between Luke and his mother. And Luke was asking the
Father not to lead his children into temptation!
The next day Levilla asked Damon if he would listen while
Luke told him the whole story.
He looked at her a long moment. Then he said, "My dear
girl, I know more about the story than he knows."
"May God forgive you!"
"But I do. I'm sure I do."
"You're afraid of the truth. You'll listen to our enemies-"
"I'll listen to Luke, if that will please you."
The three of them found a secluded spot by the river and spent
an afternoon there. Fixing Damon with his eloquent eyes Luke
said, without the slightest trace of self-consciousness, yet without
sounding arrogant, that he had full and perfect understanding of
the truths revealed by his Christ and Lord. Did Damon wish to
hear them?
"If I may interrupt now and then. I have spent almost fifteen
years studying this matter."
"Time wasted," Levilla said.
Luke looked at Damon a few moments, as though reflecting on
his wickedness. Then he began the story, his voice low and gentle.
The angel Gabriel, he said, appeared to an old man named Zacha-
rias and told him that his wife Elisabeth, who was elderly, would
142
conceive. A little later God sent Gabriel to a city named Nazareth.
Nazareth! This was the first time Damon had heard the word
on the lips of a Christian. He wanted to ask Luke if he had ever
seen the place called Nazareth.
Luke said there was a virgin espoused to a man named Joseph,
a Jew, of the house of David. The angel came to the virgin and
Isaid that she had found favor with God, and the Lord was with
her, and she was blessed among women. The angel said she would
conceive and bring forth a son and call him Jesus. Mary, the vir-
gin, said, But how can this be, since I have not lain with a man?
The angel said the holy spirit would fall upon her and overshadow
her and she would conceive.
Damon's eyes were popping. He was holding up a hand as a
sign that he would interrupt. He was saying, "Your Lord was
born of a virgin? I hadn't heard that before. I've been waiting
to hear it, because the other savior-gods are. But in the book by
Mark he is not. Have you seen it?"
Luke shook his head no.

Levilla wearied him because she had nothing to argue with but

153
analogies, but all these she and Luke and such people as she and

be born in Judea. If Jesus was actually born in Galilee, Christians
would have to doctor their story to make it appear that he was
born in Judea. By doing so they would claim to have fulfilled an-
other prophecy.
In his pale dry way Elisha said: “In getting a Lord who was a
Jew they inherited a dreadful pile of problems."
Damon told Levilla that in one of the Christian writings Jesus
was born in a cave and then taken to a stable and laid in a manger.
He told her that the god Krishna was born in a stable; that Dion-
ysus and other god-babies were cradled in baskets; that most of
the savior-gods were born in a cave, which, certain philosophers
believed, was meant to symbolize the underworld, out of which
the sun-god was born. Levilla only looked at him with eyes of pity.
Luke's story that Jesus at the age of twelve confounded the wise
men Damon also laid before Elishsa; and Elisha pointed out that
this was another Jewish tradition. Moses at twelve left home to
teach. Samuel began to prophesy at that age. At that age the god-
boy Horus ran away and his mother searched for him. Elisha
thought that possibly this part of Luke's story showed an Egyptian
influence.
To all such analogies Levilla and her fellow-Christians had a
reply that never varied: Satan and his imps had been busy every-
where with copies and counterfeits to trick the unwary. Satan
was a wise one, she said, with power to foresee the future. Ah, but
he was a cunning monster! Long ago, anticipating the birth of
Jesus in a cave or stable, and his choice of twelve disciples, Satan
had invented such tales about other gods, knowing that his in-
ventions would confuse all those of little faith. She astonished
Damon by saying that everything in the Jesus story could be found
in stories about other gods. She had no doubt of that. Would
Satan, a wise and powerful one, have been so stupid as not to have
prepared all these things?
Damon stared at her, thinking, How can a person make head-
way against such arguments?

probe had raked Levilla savagely down the back of her neck.
Staring at Murdia Damon asked, "Now what have you done?"
"Kissed her," said Murdia innocently.
"Look!" said Levilla and bent forward to show her neck. There
was a shallow wound from which blood was running.
"Why'd you do it?" asked Damon.
"God told me to. The moment I kissed her, God said, Gouge
her a good one, for she wearies me with that pious face. God
can tell his saints from his sinners, even if men can't."
Damon led Levilla away to find a doctor. Murdia followed
them. When Damon looked back she flashed a smile at him and
indicated with a gesture that he was to dispatch Levilla. She
made the sign of the thumb down.
The experience seemed to turn Levilla's thoughts more com-
pletely toward her faith. One day she asked Damon if he had met
Ignatius, their bishop.
He had not known that Christians had bishops. "No," he said.
"He's a very holy man."
"Holier than Luke?"
Ignatius, she said, had two personalities. God had put a second
person in him so that he would be like the ancient prophets.
"You mean the Jews?" asked Damon.
The name of the second person was Theophorus. That meant
God-bearer. She wanted Damon to meet him.
Murdia had been spying on them and now came up. Damon
took Levilla's arm to protect her and was astonished when she al-
lowed him to enfold her waist. Some sort of leaven, he thought,
is working here!
"When shall I see him?"
"At our next meeting."
Murdia pinched Damon and tiptoed to whisper in his ear,
157
"Don't go, the man is horribly dull." In the next breath she said
to Levilla, "He's a Syrian hog-butcher."
"He's a prophet," Levilla said.
"He's a cannibal. He drinks blood."
"It's the blood of our Lord and it is love."
"He eats the flesh of Jesus."
"It's the bread of God," Levilla said.
They had become shrill. They were shouting at one another
across Damon and spitting like cats. He broke away from them.
"You damned Christians are all sects and schisms and hate.
Just how many sects do you have already?"
"How many?" asked Murdia blandly, with something evil in
her eyes for Levilla.
Damon said: "Dozens, scores, and your Jesus dead only half a
century. How many will there be finally?”
"I say seven times seventy plus three," Murdia said, looking
gravely at Levilla. "How many do you say?"
"There'll always be the true one," Levilla said.
Murdia turned to Damon. "In Rome it's Mark. Here it's Luke.
In Alexandria-?"
"There are twenty in Alexandria," Damon said.
"In Corinth?"
"I don't know."
"In Athens, Tarsus, Caesarea, Pella, Rhodes, Ancyra—How in-
teresting it will be when there are a thousand."
"All claiming to be the true one," Damon said.
“And all true," said Murdia, "for those who believe."
Damon looked at her, considering her words. Not even Elisha
had more power to startle him.
"Only Luke has the truth," Levilla said.
Murdia turned to Damon. "You see what I mean? You under-
stand now why God finds her so tiresome?"
Damon was unable to restrain a smile, though he knew that
Levilla was watching him with veiled eyes. He said to her, "May
I bring a friend, a Jew?"
"Bishop Ignatius doesn't like Jews."
“That's all right,” Damon said. "Jews are used to that by this
time."
158
XV
Bishop Ignatius was a very dark man with black eyes and hair
and a heavy black beard. He was short and thickset. He seemed
to Damon to be almost illiterate and crude, but pompous and
bold. Levilla was right when she said that he hated Jews. A more
intense and implacable Jew-hater Damon was never to meet among
Christians. In the first hour of observing the man Damon felt
that consuming desire for martyrdom that was to overwhelm Ig-
natius in his later years.
Damon had brought Elisha along to hear the bishop preach.
The congregation was chiefly the converted slums of Antioch-
slaves, beggars, the diseased and blind and dumb, the outcasts from
a dozen different people. Were these typical Christians? Elisha
asked, and Damon said that ninety per cent of the Christians he
had known were much like them.
Ignatius had a deep strong voice and he roared like thunder.
He made savage gestures by striking a clenched hand into an open
palm, by running big thick fingers through his crown of black
hair, or by thrusting an arm in the direction of Rome, the Babylon
and Beast. He aroused his audience to passion but not to thought.
"Put aside the evil leaven!" he shouted.
Damon turned to look at Elisha: Ignatius meant the teachings
of the Jews.
"It has grown old and sour. Turn to the new leaven, which is
our Christ and Lord. Be salted in him that none of you may be
corrupted. It is wicked to accept the love of our Lord if at the
same time you cling to the abominable practices of Jews. Hear
me!" he shouted, smiting his palm. "Glorify the Christ who has
glorified you, for he is the bread of God. . . .”
159
"He seems fairly consumed by love," Elisha said.
As though he had heard the words Ignatius now demanded,
"What is Love? It is the blood of the Christ! I tell you to have
no taste for corrupt food in this life. Eat the bread of God, which
is the flesh of our Lord, and drink his blood, which is the only in-
corruptible love. There is in the whole world only the one flesh.
of our Lord, only the one cup for union in his blood, only one
altar, as there is only one bishop. . . .'
99
Damon was making notes, and with curious and rather cynical
interest Elisha was watching him. Damon glanced at him and said,
"I may write a book about all this some day."
Ignatius was shouting: "A prisoner for Jesus I was! So are all
men who will be saved! Everyone who will not confess that the
Christ has come is antichrist! Whoever does not confess the wit-
ness of the cross is of the Devil! Whoever corrupts the truths of
our Lord to his lusts and denies the resurrection and the judg-

who say that the son of God came as a phantom are Satan's fiends!
Those in Rome who say a man became a god are hell's own imps!
"
Damon looked over at Levilla and found her staring at Elisha.
He looked at Murdia. She winked.
"Who was Jesus?” Ignatius was asking. "No Jew can tell you
but I can tell you. Jesus the Christ was descended from David,
and was also of Mary; he was truly born and did eat and drink.
He was truly persecuted under Pilate. He was truly crucified and
truly died, in the sight of beings in heaven and on earth and under
the earth. He was truly raised from the dead. He was the pre-

Elisha turned away. He said he did not want to hear more. He

Was Elisha tortured by some inner conflict? Was he struggling

"All things will pass," he said sadly. "Even the Christians some

“What man can tell?”
"I sometimes think that most people miss truth by trying to fer-
160
tilize old myths with their prejudices. Don't we all cling to the

"All," Damon said. "Seneca told us that God gave us a mind
intending that we should use it."
"That should be obvious even to a child. But can you see the

Damon had wondered about that. "If before they hope people

"Maybe not. But aren't hopes doubly false that rest on hate?
And is it true that you intend to write a book about this new

"Is it new, or is it only a patchwork of what is old?”
"You hope to answer that question?"
"Some day."
Ignatius so wearied Elisha that he would not go to hear him
again, but Damon went a number of times, to listen and take notes.
It seemed to him that the man was obsessed with his position and
power as bishop, for again and again he returned to his belief that
only the bishop could speak for the Lord.
"You must have respect for your bishop as for our Lord himself.
Your bishop presides over you in the likeness of God, but pres-
byters and elders are only in the likeness of the apostles. Your
bishop is the type of our Father; where he appears, there let the
people be. No Christian can have a clear conscience who under-
takes anything apart from his bishop.
""
Damon was incredulous but he wrote these things down.
Ignatius believed himself to be under the control of God, who
used his voice as an instrument. He was filled with the holy
spirit. He spoke as the prophets of old. "Hear me! I can tell you
of the heavenly things. Hear me, lest you be confused and choked.
For though I am in the bonds of the flesh I understand the heav-
enly things and the arrays of the angels and the musterings of the
principalities, things visible and invisible. Like Paul, I have had
visions and revelations; I have been caught up into the third
heaven and heard unspeakable words. I speak to you as to babes
in Christ, and I unveil the glory of my vision. .
•
99
Feeling shame, Damon looked down over himself, asking again,
What is man? Could he ever have this man's incredible arro-
gance? He supposed he could have, or any other man: would
any other man, or he, still look in a mirror?
161
Ignatius poured information into the bosoms of his breathless.
flock. He told these rapt listeners that their Lord was begotten
and unbegotten, God in man; that the pre-existent god had come
in the flesh; that Christians were children of the Light, who would
drive Satan from the earth. "Follow your bishop, as Jesus our
Lord follows our Father. Know God and your bishop and do
nothing without them. . . ." While listening to such words,
Damon's mind turned in speculation to his own father.
One day, drawing all his courage to the surface, he forced him-
self to ask questions. It was plain that Ignatius looked on him as
an upstart. Who am I, Damon thought, a heathen and one of the
damned, to dispute with the bishop of Antioch, whose second per-
sonality speaks for God himself! Did mortal man presume to have
argument with the Most High and his agents on earth? Just the
same he wanted to see this man's credentials. How did Ignatius
know that he was inerrable and that all other Christian sects were
in the service of hell? Because he had been caught up like Paul
to the third heaven?
Levilla said all his doubts would be answered if he would only
go to their meetings. In Rome, when Damon was there, Christians
had met in old underground chambers or in graveyards, stealing
away like criminals. In Antioch they met in the open, usually in
a small park or by a cascade on the river, where the music of waters
mingled with their songs and prayers. Their worship opened with
a prayer, followed by the bishop's reading of letters from distant
friends, or of verses from a Jewish holy book, or by spontaneous
speaking by those filled with the holy spirit. Sometimes in unison
they chanted until they became intoxicated and beside themselves.
One day their chant was the words, Jesus is Lord! which they
cried over and over, it seemed to Damon, a thousand times. He
was thinking that other cults had similar chants-One Zeus Ser-
apis! or, Mithra is Lord! Or their chant might be, One God, the
Father, through whom are all things and we through him! Levilla,
usually so poised and tranquil, became another person in the
frenzy of the chantings, her eyes glowing up at the sky, her hands
and feet beating out the rhythm.
Sometimes they sang.
Let them take our life,
goods, honor, child and wife:
let all these go!
162
Yet is the gain not theirs
for the kingdom is ours!
On Sunday morning-these Christians abhorred the Jewish
Sabbath-they would face the sun and sing:
The first of days the light beheld
bursting from the gloom—
and Christ, our true eternal Light,
arising from the tomb!
Or they would sing:
Lord, on the cross your arms were stretched
to draw us to the sky!
O grant us then that cross to love
and in those arms to die!
Sometimes they had antiphonal chants, in which the congrega-
tion gave the response.
"Blood of him who for us was made flesh!"
"Jesus the Christ!"
"Blood of him who was born from the holy mother of God!"
"Jesus the Christ!"
"Blood of him who appeared among men on earth!"
"Jesus the Christ!"
"Blood of him who was baptized in the Jordan!"
"Jesus the Christ!”
"Blood of him who offered himself as a sacrifice for our sins!”
"Jesus the Christ verily!"
Extemporaneous prayer was popular with them. It was not un-
usual for two hundred of them to be uttering prayers aloud at the
same moment. After prayer they invariably kissed one another,
some kissing one or two persons, some a score or more. Some wo-
men kissed only men; some men, only women. When Damon was
present Levilla always kissed him first and she always kissed him
full on the mouth. Feeling the passion in her he was amused. He
knew that she was unaware of her passion, unaware that she had
a good excuse only after prayers had been offered. When the kiss-
ing was done the bishop blessed them.
Damon had heard that in some places Christians in their secret
meetings gave way to sexual orgies. That was true, he supposed,
in places like Corinth. It was never true in Antioch when he was
163
present, and he doubted that such Christians as Levilla and Luke
would have been willing members of any group that made sexual
license a part of its worship. It was true that kissing was some-
times carried to extreme abandon, it was plain to Damon that
erotic passions were aroused.
Now and then some who were not Christians would draw near
to listen, and one now and then was bold enough to hurl a chal-
lenge or deliver a harangue. One day a Jew said out of a clear
sky, "Most of your proselytes are women! Why? Is it because
women don't have to be circumcised and are more easily con-
verted?"
At once voices tried to silence him but he would not be silenced.
He told them they were heathen, for had they not taken their rites
from the mystery cults? He said they were envious of the Holy
Name's people, the chosen, and that when God wearied of them
they would all perish. From the heavens would fall fiery swords
and flaming torches and cauldrons of boiling oil; and the earth
would be shaken by the Almighty in his wrath. He would rend
the peaks asunder, the darkness of the abyss would become visible:
clefts in the mountains would be filled with their corpses and the
stones drenched with their blood.
Angry Christians with the bishop at their head gathered round
the man, to villify and denounce him. The Jew's face turned
purple with rage but he continued to hurl his imprecations. "God
will execute judgment on all of you—”
"Close his mouth!"
“Silence him!”
"-with the sword, with brimstone, with plague, with fire—”
"Shut him up!"
"—and in the whole earth will be the sound of your wailing-
"Kill him!"
""
In their awful passion the Christians, Damon thought, would
kill him. They seized him and shook him and pounded on his
skull; but frothing and glaring round him he continued to shout.
What would the signs be, he asked, when the king came? Swords
would appear in the heavens toward evening and toward morning.
Clouds of dust. . . . They hurled him to the earth and Damon,
moving in, saw that they were stuffing his mouth with earth. The
rocks would drip with blood. They smote him and kicked him,
they yanked him to his feet and hustled him away and out of sight.
He was still shouting. When the moon came up through the earth,

and fires.

Again they were pounding him on his skull.
After they got him away and out of sight the Christians were
stirred to such depths of passion that some stood white and trem-
bling, some wept. Damon stared at them. For the first time he
sensed that between Christians and Jews there was some terrible
thing that he had not understood. They outraged and infuriated
one another. They drove one another almost to murder and mad-
ness. He went over and sat by Levilla, who was weeping and moan-
ing. He looked at her and waited.
After a while he said, "Why weep? If you have the truth can
a Jew disturb it? If you're not sure you have it why don't you
listen? If your gospel is love why do you smite a man and try to
kill him? If you are to turn your other cheek—”
"Oh, please!" she cried, sobbing.
Damon left her and went over to Luke and stared at him with
fascinated interest. Luke had been deeply shaken. He was white.
"Luke, why in hell don't you Christians practice what you
preach? Why didn't you turn the other cheek?" When Luke gave
no reply Damon said: "Did your Lord bring a gospel of love or
of hate?"
"Love," Luke said.
"Was it love that I witnessed? Why do you hate the Jews?"
"I don't," Luke said.
"It doesn't seem to me you're very fair to these people. You're
even trying to take their holy books. You tell them their God
has rejected them, that they are accursed. I can see reasons why
they should hate Christians but no reason why Christians should
hate them. Can you explain it?”
Luke was silent.
"It seems to me," Damon went on, "that you people are a lot
closer to Plato than to Moses. And what did Plato say?—that the
righteous man in an unrighteous world must expect to be thrown
into prison, scourged and racked, tortured, impaled, castrated,
crucified. That's Plato's idea. The Jew's idea is that a messiah-
king will come in glory and be triumphant. You're trying to make
Jews accept Plato's notion and because they won't you hate them.”
Damon was silent a few moments, studying Luke's pale and un-
happy face. "You say the Jewish books foretell the coming of a son
of God. They do nothing of the kind. Jews would explain it to
you but you won't listen. Why persist in finding meanings in
their writings that aren't there?"
165
Luke turned on Damon his soft quiet eyes. He said: "The mes-
siah came. The Jews rejected him. We're the new Israel that was
created before the sun and the moon, for which the earth was
made."
Damon gazed at him, silenced. For him the man's arrogance
was all the more terrible because so softly uttered.
Luke was telling him that circumcision was no longer necessary.
Abraham had been saved by faith alone, before he was circumcised.
"That's what you say. You take their writings and tell Jews
what they mean and you can't even read their language! To equal
your presumption they would have to tell you what your books
mean. How would you like that? Why can't you have your re-

"You don't understand," Luke said. "When they rejected the
messiah-"
"Please, please, I don't want to hear that nonsense again. I re-
peat it, you hate the Jews because they won't put Plato on the
throne of Moses."
"You speak of arrogance," said Luke with some spirit. "Have
you been to Jamnia?"
"I'll go there some day."
"Damon, we pray for you. Some time you will understand."
"Understand what? That there was a Jewish teacher of some
sort named Jesus? That he was killed by the Romans? That pos-
sibly he had a few followers who began to teach after his death?
And if he had, they were Jews, weren't they? Understand? That
some other Jews, chiefly Saul who changed his name to Paul, took
up this new faith and transplanted it? Understand that they
couldn't make converts as long as they kept certain Jewish cus-
toms, and so abandoned the customs? That the Christians have
borrowed and are borrowing ideas and rites from other religions?
That they have assimilated a Jewish rab to the heathen savior-

Jews that baffles me.'
""
Luke had been protesting, in his gentle mild-mannered way. He
raised his delicate long-fingered hands to silence Damon.
He said: "We Christians are a heavenly people, reaching down

"Oh no, Luke. Pardon me a moment. Those people were the
166
Jews. They'll tell you there were seventy nations present out in

"You believe that?"
"No, no. I don't think all the people of the earth left their
homes and countries and went to a desert to listen to Moses. That's
a fable for children."
"Damon, you are simply a man without faith. You don't believe

"No. You're trying to usurp the historic position of the Jews,

andria or Rome or Antioch. They wouldn't listen to you."
Ignatius had come over and was looking at Damon. Luke bowed

"We were having a discussion," Damon said. "You wish to

"The saved and the damned," said Ignatius, in his heavy deep
voice, “don't speak the same language. When I speak, the holy
spirit speaks through me in righteousness, and I am mighty to
save. When you speak it is as Satan's Theogonius.”
Damon flushed. Theogonius was an idiot in the time of Claud-
ius, whose name had become proverbial.
"Satan's Theogonius," said Damon hotly, "has read the philos-
ophers. Have you? I have talked to Christians in many places.
Have you?"
"The blind fall in ditches," said Ignatius. "There you'll be
when our Lord comes. Luke, we must go."
They left Damon, stung, angry, mortified. My fine arrogant fel-
low, he thought, I'll have all the truth about your religion before
I'm done with it! I'll talk to learned men—to Plutarch and Jo-
sephus and Oenomaus and Epictetus and Gamaliel and Pliny's
nephew! I'll go to Tarsus, Ephesus, Ancyra, Scythopolis, Jamnia,
Jerusalem, Rome! I'll read all the writings by Christians, from
Spain to Babylon! I'll know the truth yet, you insufferable bigot!
But then he felt shame. What were those wise and ancient
words? If you seek to find, no man can believe you; but if you
find without seeking, it may be that you have the truth. He had
no point to prove, no moral, no preachment: he wanted to know
the truth for the sake of the truth alone, that and nothing more.
That, he deeply felt, would be enough.
That might even be God.
167
XVI
He spent the next fifteen years in extensive reading and travel.
Murdia wanted to go with him and he had great difficulty in
putting her away. She was abject: "I won't bother you, I won't
speak to you except when you tell me to speak"; she was lost: "I
have no one now, no husband, parent, friend, no one but you";
she was fearful; "If Luke is right I am utterly damned, for peni-
tence can never redeem such a sinner as I"; she was eager to assist:
"I will be your mistress or your mother or nun, sister, secretary or
slave."
Damon arranged with his bank to pay her a monthly sum and
slipped away.
He met Ayla when he was about forty years old. It happened
in a tavern. On leaving Antioch he went up the coast and inland
to Tarsus, a pleasant city on the Cilician plain. The clear ferti-
lizing waters of the Cydnus flowed through it, and some miles in
the background were the lofty peaks of the Tarsus mountains. He
went there hoping to gather information about Paul, but Paul
had been dead a long time and those who had known him told dif-
ferent stories about him. They all agreed that Paul had had a
physical infirmity; but one said he was a dwarf, another that he
had violent seizures, a third that he had a maimed arm. Damon in-
quired in Tarsus for the man known as Elder John and was told
that he was in Hierapolis. In which Hierapolis? Nobody knew.
One city by that name was a hundred miles east of Ephesus, the
other about the same distance east of Antioch. Because Damon felt
that the deeper he went into the East the closer he came to the
truth he went to the one east of Antioch and found a God-forsaken
168
place without a Christian in it. By caravan he then endured a
tedious journey to Damascus.
This city he fell in love with at once. Watered by many streams
and irrigation canals, it had on every side odorous gardens, or-
chards and vineyards. The drying of fruits here was a large in-
dustry—of olives, apples, pears, pomegranates, dates, figs, plums,
and several kinds of nuts. The inns and taverns in most cities were
little more than brothels but in Damascus he found a fine hostelry
run by an enterprising Greek; it opened on gardens and admitted
only persons of quality. Ayla was one of the girls who danced and
sang to entertain the guests.
She was for Damon one of the most extraordinary women he
was ever to know but he was never to be able to define the qualities
in her that appealed to him so strongly. In physical appearance
she was striking. Her skin was so fair that it colored when she
laughed, but her eyes were dark, deep, soft and glowing, like those
in so many eastern peoples. Her mother had been a Jew; her
father, she thought, had been a Greek. Ayla's lashes were long
and heavy with an unsweep in their curve; her mouth was red and
just a little too full and quite wide; her hair was a golden brown.
But it was her personality rather than her appearance that ap-
pealed to Damon. He had always been shy, introspective and
rather morbid: Ayla was gay, spontaneous, forthright, though
down under all that, he was to learn, was a deep quiet that bor-
dered on the tragic.
She came one evening to sit at his table and talk. He was sur-
prised to learn that her people had been Christians. Ayla had
been married-when Damon met her she was about twenty-two
years old to an intensely serious man who had taken certain
Christian teachings literally. That for a long time was all that
she would tell Damon about him.
When Damon told her that he was trying his best to learn who
the Christians were and where their doctrines came from, Ayla
laughed and said:
"Maybe I could help you.”
A few days later they made a pact. She said Damon would have
to give a few pledges if she were to go with him.
Uneasy, he looked at her. "What?"
"First, never ask me to be your boy."
"Is that all? I have never yet had a man as a lover.”
"That isn't all. If you ever tire of me, say so."
169
"I promise that."
"In trying to please me as a lover don't exhaust yourself or do
things you don't wish to do. For then you would hate me."
It was like Ayla to name the things one by one; she had a logical
and orderly mind.
"Anything else?"
“If a man likes a woman as a lover he will stay with her until
he finds something that he doesn't like. If you find anything, tell
me."
"I promise that also. Any more?”
"Yes. If you are faithless don't try to hide it. Women are sharp
about those things."
Damon was feeling foolish. "I promise that," he said. "I am
not, though, much of a woman's man.”
"What promises do you want me to make?”
"I can think of none."
"That's like a man. I'll think of them for you. First, you won't
want me to nag you to marry me. I won't. Two: you won't want
me to be greedy about your wealth." She smiled. "I'll try not to
be. I'll spend what you give me and ask for no more. Three: you
won't want me to have other lovers. Or would you?"
"God in heaven, no. I'm a jealous man."
"You won't want me to make fun of this silly quest you're on.
I'll try to believe that you will discover some great truth."
"Thank you," he said and kissed one of her palms.
"I'll not ask about your past. You must not ask about mine."
"Agreed."
"Well," she said, flashing her wide bright smile, “here we are,
Damon and Ayla, journeying over the world to learn about the
Christians. Will we travel a lot? I'll be your secretary."
"Quite a lot."
"I've never been farther from Damascus than Tyre. Will we go
to Rome? Of course we will. And we'll be very fond of one an-
other," said this strange woman, looking into his eyes and then
pinching his nose. "One question, though, and then no more
questions. Have you loved another woman?"
"Honestly I don't know. I thought I did. But when I look into
your eyes I find that her face is receding in memory."
"Peppercorn, listen. You don't have to flatter me—ever.”
That was Ayla.
Damon was eager to read as many of the Christian writings as
170
he could find. They were now appearing in many places and were
of many kinds. He had heard of gospels according to the Ebion-
ites, the Egyptians, the Syrians, and according to various men, in-
cluding a Barnabas and a Bartholomew; of little books called acts
according to various Christian leaders; of letters said to have been
written by Jesus, Mary, Mary of Magdal, Joseph, and the brothers
and sisters of Jesus. He had been told that there were quaintly
detailed lives of Adam and Eve and of other persons in the Jew-
ish holy books; and apocalypses of Noah, Abraham, Joshua, Isaac,
David, Solomon, Elijah, Enoch; and testaments of Isaac, Jacob,
Enoch, Daniel and others. There were books about Solomon tell-
ing of the incredible wonders he performed; various booklets that
related wondrous prophecies and foretold miracles to come; and
many books about persons who were said to have known Jesus, in-
cluding two Roman emperors. It seemed to Damon that Chris-
tians everywhere must be forging a vast literature to further their
purpose and suit their needs. The childlike nature of it all was
suggested, for him, in one of their books that he had seen by the
statement that in the beginning men and beasts spoke the same
language.
He soon learned that there were many books on the infancy
and childhood of Jesus. The first of these he read in Edessa. It
told how the child Jesus saw a man who had been metamorphosed
into a mule, and with a magical word transformed him into a man;
how he expelled from the child Judas a demon that slunk away in
the form of a dog; how with his own small hands he shaped ani-
mals of clay and brought them to life; how he caused the sun to
stand still for seven hours; how as a child he moved mountains,
stilled the seas, hushed the winds, made barren trees give fruit, and
with still other miracles showed that he was the long-awaited di-
vine child.
Another book told of the amazing things that happened at his
birth. The temple of Apollo at Rome burst asunder and fell
down; the earth opened in such wide clefts that the doomed souls
in hell were able to come up and peer out; and all the stars sang
a great song that Rome heard in far-away Israel. There was no
pain or blood in the birth of the child, and after birth the mother
was still a virgin. One midwife who attended the birth had a
withered hand but the moment she touched the babe her hand
was well. Juno came to life and was renamed Mary. Bacchus and
all his satyrs rejoiced at the birth and confessed in loud voices that
171
they had been adulterers, liars and thieves. Satan was so appalled
and terrified that he uttered wild shrieks and lamentations that
were clearly heard above the rejoicing of the angels.
Damon read the book aloud to Ayla and she shrugged. She said,
"What does it mean?"
Damon said the Christians seemed to be taking old myths and
applying them to their god. For instance, was Jesus born in a cave?
So were Apollo, Cybele, Demeter, Hercules, Hermes, Mithra,
Poseidon.
"One thing puzzles me: Hermes was born in the cave of Maia,
but Maia and Mary are the same word."
"So what is Mary?"
"The underworld, I suppose. The sun is born from the womb
of the underworld and the savior-gods are only the ancient sun-
god."
She gave a strange laugh. Only men, she said, could be inter-
ested in such nonsense.
Luke had told how Mary wrapped the babe in swaddling clothes
and laid it in a manger, meaning perhaps the Greek liknon. Ex-
actly the same story was told of Hermes, Dionysus and Ion. Some
Christians believed that in an effort to destroy the child King
Herod had caused all the small male children in Bethlehem to be
slain. Damon had been able to find no record of that. What he
learned was that in old myths there had been many attempts to
kill the royal son or the god-child: Nimrod tried to kill Abraham;
a pharoah had tried to kill all the firstborn sons; Joab had tried to
slay the child Hadad by killing all the boys in Edom. Indeed, he
read in a book that the Roman senate with a slaughter of infants
had tried to destroy the infant Augustus.
He sensed some deep truth in this myth and he thought and
thought about it but there was no light.
Ayla asked impatiently, "Why did they want to?"
"I wish I knew. The superior child is usually hidden to save its
life. Moses was hidden in an ark of bullrushes; Sargon, the first
king of Babylon, in a basket. The mother of Trakhan, later king
of Gilgit, sealed her royal infant son in a box and hurled him into a
river." He found a scholar saying that people believed that the
planted seed is menaced before it can ripen, that this was true of
all royal and god-sons. Had not Euripides said that children
sprung from the gods had an unhappy and difficult life? Hadn't
the mother of Melkarth leapt into the sea to save her son, and
172
Leto, pregnant by Apollo, fled into hiding? Had not the infant
Dionysus been sent to a secluded island where he was guarded by
Athene? Had not Zeus in infancy been hidden from the wrath of
his father? Had not the infant Poseidon been hidden among the
sheep and fed with the lambs?
Yes, the fathers had wanted to kill their sons.
"But why?" asked Ayla, staring at him.
"It's a riddle. The king doesn't want his son to grow up and
replace him? Is my friend Elisha right when he says the myth has
grown out of hatred of fathers for their sons?”
"You mean all sons are hated by their fathers?"
"I don't think I was but I was a slave-child, not a divine child.
Don't ask me what the myth means. There it is and the Christians
have copied it."
It became their habit to talk after they had gone to bed or had
awakened in the morning. Usually Ayla would listen, and wonder
about the strange matters; and Damon, more confused and ex-
cited as his knowledge expanded, would talk endlessly, hoping to
capture a truth. One evening when they were lying together he
said:
"Some Christians say that Jesus talked immediately after birth.
So did Krishna, Apollo, Hermes, Confucius. A Christian book
says that Jesus murdered several of his boy playmates. The same
is told of some of the gods. They tell us that Mary cried, Out of
Egypt have I called my son. Well, Isis fled to the swamps to bear
Horus; Rhea to Crete to bear Zeus; Latona to a grove to bear
Apollo; Evadne to the bushes to bear Iamos.
"Take the curious story of the birth in Bethlehem. Elisha
points out that it is an effort to fulfill Micah where he said,
From thee shall come forth a ruler over Israel. The word Bethle-
hem, he tells me, means the house of bread; near it is a grove of
trees sacred to Adonis, the god of corn. Christians say Jesus called
himself the bread of life. Have they confused him with or are
they trying to identify him with the corn-gods?
"Or take the day on which Jesus is supposed to have been born.
Christians don't agree on it. Some say it is the same as that of
Julius Caesar; some, the same as that of Dionysus; and some, the
twenty-fourth of July, which is the thirtieth of the Egyptian month
Epap and that is the birthday of the god-child Horus. Still others
say the twenty-fourth of April or the twenty-fifth of May. They
don't even agree on the year. Some say he was born when Herod
173
was king, others that he was thirty years old in the fifteenth year
of Tiberius."
Sleepily she squeezed his hand. “And what do you say, pepper-
corn?"
He grinned up into the dark, knowing that she would rather
sleep than listen. "I guess nobody will ever know."
"What do the Christians think they're doing?"
"I don't suppose they're thinking about it."
"But you are, poor dear."
"I am watching a thing grow, a very wonderful thing. Who can
say what it will grow into?"
"Aren't the Christians inventing anything new?”
"Nothing I know of. They are taking from others and chang-
ing it here and there. Maybe all religions come into being that
way."
“Haven't they anything that doesn't belong to other people?"
"Nothing, I guess. Well, look, this is the way it goes. Some
Christians talk about three Marys but Paul never mentioned them.
We must ask, what is Mary?"
"You once said the womb of the underworld."
"I don't know. But look. The mother of Adonis is Myrrh; of
Hermes, is Maia; of Buddha is Maya. Maira is the name of the
dogstar, the star of Isis. All those words are the same word."
Ayla pinched him. "Mary is Myrrh is Maia is Maya is Maira.
All gods, then, have to be born of Mary. And what is Mary?"
"I wish I knew. Here's another curious thing. Many years ago
a Jew named Jesus ben Pandira was hanged on a tree on Passover
eve. A Jewish writing I've seen says his mother's name was
Miriam Magdala, that is, Mary the hair-dresser. The goddess Isis
is sometimes called the hair-dresser. Christians talk about a Mary
of Magdala. They say she was full of devils. So was Miriam in the
Moses story. Mary of Magdala in the Christian story was a har-
lot. Among Jews hair-dressing is a profession associated with
whoredom. So what do we have?"
"There was no Mary of Magdala. That much is sure."
"Why in the world do Christians have three named Mary?"
They were silent a few moments. Then she said: "You've told
me Mary is the child-bearer, the Mater Dolorosa, mourning for
her slain god-child. Is Mary only the eternal Mother protesting
against the slaying of the son by the Father?"
"You mean the death of Jesus-?" He broke off and lay in
174
thought. After a while he said: “I've just learned that in the cata-
combs of Rome the Christians now have statues of the mother and
child. Mary is represented as a black woman. Who is the black
goddess? Isis. Have they confused Mary with Isis?"
"Peppercorn," she said, squeezing his hand again, “doesn't it
occur to you that maybe Mary and Isis are the same thing? Do
many names make more than the one Mother?"
"No, I guess not. . . . Well, let's take a simpler matter. The
sun-gods all turn water into wine when they ripen the grapes.
What a childlike act when Luke has Jesus the Jew turn water
into wine! Jesus walked on water. So did Poseidon, Dionysus,
Hercules. But I've no idea what the symbolism is in that. Jesus
healed the blind. So have all the gods and some of the emperors.
That surely is another symbolism but all the Christians seem to
take it literally. They're going to spoil their story with just that,
taking it literally.”
"But they don't in Alexandria."
"Some of them don't."
Ayla sat up and bent over, laughing. "Lord!" she said. "Do
people ever know what they're doing? Men, I mean.”
"Maybe not."
"It's not that they like to fool themselves?"
"No. They're just trying to grow taller than they are.”
“Such as Elijah going straight to heaven without dying?”
"Yes."
"Such as Joshua stopping the sun?”
"Yes. And Poseidon impregnating women by whispering in
their ear. Zeus when he seduced Alcmene made the night twenty-
four hours long so he'd have time to create a very powerful god."
Ayla turned, trying to see his face in the gloom. "Are you teas-
ing me?"
"No."
"You mean men have the idea that twenty-fours hours of it with-
out pause might produce a god?”
"Zeus had that idea."
"Which means that men have it. Poor Alcmene! And was Zeus
successful?"
"I don't know. Some have believed that he was the father of
Alexander, Venus the mother of Caesar, Nero the child of Zeus
and Hera, Pythagoras the son of Hermes, Apollo the father of
Plato-"
175
"Enough," she said, putting a hand to his mouth. "Women will
take the shorter embraces and the shorter sons, if Alexander,
Caesar and Nero are what we get."
They were silent again and he thought she slept but out of dark-
ness she asked at last: "You don't believe there are any gods at
all, do you?"
He said drily, “I have need to, now that my father is dead."
"But greater need if your father were alive and you hated him.
What is God? Is it only father?”
"Some have said mind."
"But love is greater."
"Could they be the same thing?"
She was thoughtful a few moments. "If I love you that means
I'm intelligent?"
"Oh, by all means. But we're talking of a love above the man-
woman."
"If God is mind the Christians must all be atheists. They rest
everything on faith—and if you have faith you don't need mind,
do you?"
"It's possible that faith is an aspect of mind. We know so little
about these things. But right now, Ayla, I'm wondering what will
happen if the world becomes all Christian. What will happen to
philosophy and science and art? Christians hate all three. They
say painting and sculpture are idol-worship. And they hate doctors
too because doctors, they say, interfere with God's plan of punish-
ment."
"You make them out as horrible things. Is there no good in
them?"
"Tenderness, I think. Their religion seems to have more tender-
ness and compassion.'
""
"And more riddles?"
"Riddle one: Christians hate the other gods and call them
demons but they name their children for them-Phoebe, Apollos,
Daphne, Diana. Riddle two: they hate the Jews but they use
their system and call the days of the week by number rather
than by planets. Riddle three: They say Jesus was perfect but
tell us that he was tempted by Satan and that his own family
found him queer and didn't believe in him. Riddle four: they
say sexual love is sinful but they call a lot of their books testaments
which comes from the same Roman word as testicle. Riddle five:
they say baptism is necessary to salvation but not one of them pre-
176
tends that any of the twelve were baptized. Riddle six: nearly all
