"That's because the Jews have none."
"I'll ask some learned Jew about that."
"You don't have to. The smart half of me is Jew."
"Then tell me what God means for Jews."
"Father. The head of the house and the heavens. O Lord my
God, mine holy One, you die not. I can tell you what God meant
for my Jew mother."
"All right."
"For her God was a personal being but apart from the world.
He was spirit, not flesh, as Isaiah said. He did not have the form
of man, as Abraham thought. He is omnipresent, as is told in
one of the psalms; he is in the world, but outside of it. He revealed
himself in a bush to Moses to show that no spot is without his
presence. He is close to those who are virtuous and far from those
who are sinful. He punishes as well as rewards but his compassion
exceeds his justice. A rabbi has said, if a man moves toward him
an inch, he will run toward the man a mile. That's the meaning of
Luke's prodigal son story, which you don't like. God is great,
mighty and awful, the Father of the Jews, who chose his people
in love.
"I know my mother's favorite prayer. Sovereign of all worlds!
Not because of our righteous acts do we lay our supplications be-
fore you, but because of your abundant mercies. What are we?
What is our life? What is our piety? What shall we say before
you, O holy Name and holy Name of our fathers? Are not the
wise as if without knowledge, and the understanding as if without
discernment? That was her favorite. I know others."
"Tell me another." He clasped one of her hands. He thought it
the sweetest thing about her that she always returned his pressure.
"Our Father who is in heaven, may your will be done there
as on earth; give calm spirit to those who fear you, and what is
good in your eyes, do. Amen."”
"The Christians have taken that one, with changes. Here is a
Christian prayer: Grant us to hope in your name, the first source
of all creation; open the eyes of our heart to know you, that you
alone are the highest among the highest, and remain holy among
the holy ones."
177
"That's another Jewish prayer," she said. "They are all prayers
that men have made. Are there no prayers that women have made
or do the mothers always pray in silence?”
One day she wanted to know what the gods of the Romans were
like. Damon said they were pretty silly. Februus assisted women
to be fertile. Mena presided over the menses. Domiducus con-
ducted the bride to her new home. Iuga presided over the court-
ship. Virginensis presided over the rape by the stone-god's penis.
Volupia granted the capacity for pleasure in copulation. Pertunda
presided over the first copulation, and Prema thereafter. Cinxia
loosened the girdle. Fluonia stopped the menses after conception.
Rumina caused the breasts to fill with milk. Nona determined the
date of birth. Vagitanus opened the babe's mouth for the first cry.
Edusa taught it to take food. Ossipaga promoted the growth of its
bones. Parca determined its destiny. Venilia promised it success.
Locutius taught it to talk. Iterduca guarded it to and from school.
Latius-
"You mean the mother has nothing to do?"
"She presides over her adulteries."
"Are the Roman women pretty wicked?"
"Most Christians would think so."
"Do they all have lovers?"
"Many of them.”
"Was one ever-but no, we promised not to ask such things.
Well-what is the Christian God like?"
"He loves the sinner more than the saint."
"Oh no!"
"The angels sing when the sinner comes home. The virtuous
dolt who sticks around and does his duty doesn't get either feast
or praise. Christians are fascinated by evil.”
"But really, what is their God like?"
"He says sexual pleasure is wicked."
"Then why did he create it?"
"Christians say that is the work of Satan."
"Hosannahs for Satan! Jews don't think it is wicked.”
"That's one reason I like Jews."
They often talked far into the night. Damon, whose mind daily
exhausted his energies, was a person who needed a lot of sleep,
but Ayla did well with four or five hours of slumber. Damon some-
times wondered if she was afraid to sleep because of her dreams.
She had terrible dreams and would awaken with a strangled cry.
178
In one dream, which recurred again and again, Satan, she said,
had his arm down her throat and was choking her. Damon felt
that her dreams were related to some tragedy in her life but she
did not want to talk about her life.
One morning she awakened Damon and he sat up sneezing.
She had been tickling his nose. She laughed and said:
"Up, lazybones. Where do we go next?"
"Where do you want to go?"
"Rome. I want to make an offering to the goddess who presides
over sexual pleasure." He drew her down and toward him but she
resisted. "No, no."
"All right. We'll go to Rome next. There's a Christian there I
want to see. Then we'll go to the land of the Jews because some-
where there is a man named Matthew who is writing the story.
Then we'll go to Alexandria."
"To Rome!" she cried, leaping out of bed, white and naked and
beautiful. She snatched up her garments. "I had a funny dream
låst night. I was the Mater Deum Magna Idaea. Imagine it! Do
all women sometimes dream they're the mother of God?"
"I don't know," Damon said, watching her eyes.
"I offered a hundred eggs to the emperor to ward off the chills
of autumn."
Damon was silent. He was looking at her.
“Then I sat on the eggs and they all hatched."
He supposed she was teasing him. "Hatched into what?"
“A hundred children, fifty little boys and fifty little girls."
"You mean you want a child that bad?"
Observing her closely he saw her eyes fill with tears. He reached
over to draw her to him but she broke away and for a moment
stood up straight and white. Then, tossing her hair back and
choking on the emotion welling up, she flung herself wildly to
the bed and wept like one whose heart was breaking. What deep
unhappiness, he wondered, was in this woman?

XVII
He gave Ayla a sum of money on their arrival in Rome and she
was off to the venders of silks and perfumes in the vicus Tuscus;
to the shoemakers in the Argiletum; and to the jewelers in the Via
Sacra. Damon headed for the unfashionable quarter of the Aven-
tine, where some of the early Christians had lived, and was told
that the man named Clement had a house between the Caelian
and Esquiline hills.
On meeting him Damon was self-conscious because he did not
know by what title to address him. He doubted that Clement
called himself bishop; the Christians in Rome designated their
meeting-place as a synagogue and were closer to Jewish practices
than those in Antioch. Damon settled the matter by addressing
him as Friend, and Clement at once asked him if he was a Friend.
Damon said he was not but his mother had been one. He then
told him that he was gathering materials for a book about the
Friends and had come to learn from his lips, having heard about
him in distant lands.
Clement seemed pleased. He seemed to be a gentle and kindly
man. But soon a subject brought the red anger to his face: it was
Rome, which he called the Harlot. When Rome was mentioned,
Clement spoke with the passion of a Roman schoolmaster, while
exhorting Damon to believe that to be in a passion was wrong.
Damon supposed that Christians here had renewed their contempt
for Rome because Domitian was another vain and wicked em-
peror. It was common gossip that he had driven his wife insane
because he had found her in bed with the famous actor Paris.
In his favor, on the other hand, he had forbidden the castration
of boys and had tried to revive the unpopular Scantinian law
180
against sexual intercourse by men with boys of free birth. He in-
flicted the severest punishment on erring Vestal Virgins and their
lovers. Still, according to stories told, he took morbid and in-
satiable pleasure in killing flies, and revealed his prodigious vanity
by adding the word god to his titles and filling his palace with
statues of himself. He had proclaimed the divinity of his father,
brothers and sister, as well as his own, and had organized a new
order of priests, the Flaviales, to attend to the worship of himself.
His official documents called him Dominus et Deus Noster, and
all visitors had to prostrate themselves and embrace his knees.
Clement repeated a part of the gossip to Damon and went on
to tell another story. Domitian invited to a banquet some of
Rome's noblest and most influential men. He had the banquet
room entirely hung with black and furnished with black benches
without cushions. In pitch darkness the guests were shown into
this room unattended, and each one found by his seat a tombstone
with his name on it, illumined by tiny lamps. There entered the
room, then, naked boys, painted black to represent specters. Food
and drink came in black dishes, as though for the dead; and while
the guests ate, Domitian delivered, in a loud sepulchral voice, a
harangue on murder and sudden death. The guests all shook
with terror, expecting assassins at any moment; but Domitian dis-
missed them unharmed, though they were accompanied home by
fierce strangers who menaced them with gestures of killing. Later,
each guest received his tombstone as a gift.
Could God, Clement asked, withhold his wrath much longer
from such a man? Or from Rome?
"No," Damon said because there was nothing else to say. He
had heard that Clement was a Jew. He was unable to tell whether
he was a Jew or not. The man was short, stout and squarely built,
with a strong face, black eyes and hair, and a short beard. Present
with him after a few moments was a strange and extraordinary
woman named Grapte. It was Damon's impression that she was
called a deaconess and that she served as Clement's secretary. She
was a spare, flat-chested and rather ungainly woman, with pale
which she never took off Damon's face.

to place you disagree on many things. What do you think of the

Clement held Damon's gaze and Damon could tell that the

Damon wrote a few moments. Then: "Who is Jesus? How

"He is the beloved servant. Let all nations know that God is

“We Friends are a new people, a new race, whose citizenship

Damon wrote the words down and said, "But the Jews—"

Damon looked down at his notes. "Some Christians say their

For us Jesus died that all men might be saved.”
Damon thought, If only I knew what the symbolism is in that!

The question had slipped out. Damon was sorry. To cover up

"There is one other remission of sins possible. That is when we

Damon had heard that before. "You mean you people wish to

They were sitting in what Damon took to be Clement's study,

"“A man once said to me, There is no Christian but only Chris-

"We are the new Israel."

"We don't. We hate only evil."

"Our Lord suffered death for us. We must suffer to be saved.

My mother, Damon thought, is among their martyrs. "Well,

suffered such death. If so, you should welcome those who perse-

"What did our Lord command? Be merciful, that you may ob-

it to be done unto you. As you give, so it shall be given. As you
judge, so you shall be judged. That is the essence."
"All those things have been taught by the philosophers a long

there's anything new in your religion."
"It's all new."
Damon studied Clement's grave face, and Grapte's, which was
even more solemn, and knew it would be useless to press the point.
He looked at his notes a moment. "Jews say their messiah will be
a man, not a god. Do you people think you have a right to tell
them that their messiah has come?"
Clement's face, Damon thought, was touched with pity. "Our
Lord's coming was foretold in the Jewish books but their wise
men did not understand what was written. What was the red cord
that spies used to mark Rahab's house in Jericho? It was a pro-
phetic sign of the blood of the Christ, by which believers are re-
deemed from sin. Isaiah says, The ox and the bear shall come
together. What did this mean? The ox, a clean animal, means
the Jews. The bear, an unclean animal, means those who have re-
jected our Lord. The ox and the bear will come together when
all people see the light and come to us."
All that, it seemed to Damon, was simply fantastic misinterpre-
tation. He gazed a moment into Clement's eyes and changed the
subject. "I'd like to ask you about Paul. Jews say that a man
should cover his head. Paul said you should sit with heads un-
covered. Was that another getting away from Jewish practices?"
"No. Paul said that a man praying with his head covered dis-
honored his head, because he is the image of God. But a woman.
ought to cover her head, for she is only the image of man."
184
"Are you serious when you say man is the image of God but
woman is only the image of man?"
"That is so."
"May I ask on what you base that belief?"
"Man was created in God's image, woman was then made from
man's rib."
Damon with difficulty kept his face grave. "Then you people
don't accept Plutarch's high sense of female excellence and the
nobility of a woman's love? For if she is only the image of man—”
"It is all revealed by God in the story of the creation. Do you
have other questions?"
A little incensed, Damon studied his notes. "Yes sir, I have.
Paul taught that Jesus came to fulfill the Law and to end it; but
Jews have a saying, When a man is dead he is free from the cere-
monial laws. Paul taught that man and sin are identical. For
Jews that is a horrible thought. Paul taught that Jesus not only
ended the Law but Jewish nationalism also and their position as
a chosen people. Now it is true-"
"We are the chosen people."
"Yes sir, I understand that. Paul taught that the Law had
brought about sin and death and was to be replaced with divine
grace.
""
"That is true."
"But there are many contradictions in Paul."
"Satan," said Clement.
"Satan? He's a pretty handy fellow for you Christians, isn't he?"
"Friends," said Clement gently. "Satan is everywhere, con-
fusing and deceiving. The full truth has been revealed to us and
Satan's treacheries can deceive none but the foolish."
"You mean if Paul uses the same phrases and ideas found in
the mystery cults—”
"The mystery cults are Satan's work."
99
“You mean that such phrases as new creation, born again-
"Satan had foreknowledge and counterfeited these things."
I wonder, Damon thought, what they'd do without him! He
knew it was a waste of time to argue these things with a Christian
and he thought it would be well to withdraw as gracefully as he
could. But he had other questions and he had come a long way
for the answers.
"Most people believe the soul survives death but not the body.
185
Paul taught that the body will be raised from death. Do you
Friends believe that?"
"We know it. Our Lord's body rose from death. Those who
believe in him will rise, body and soul, to immortal life."
"The bodies of unredeemed sinners will perish?”
"Yes."
"And their souls will suffer everlasting torments?"
"Yes."
"But didn't Jesus teach mercy and compassion and forgiveness?”
"There can be no mercy and forgiveness for those who reject
him."
"That will be most of the people on earth, including those now
alive who will never hear of him? But again, no matter. Enlighten
me in regard to sin, for I am confused. Nowhere in the books by
Jews can I find a doctrine of original sin, such as Paul taught.
Jews tell me they have none. They believe that men have free
will and moral choice, a choice between good and evil. They make
the proper relationship between God and man depend on man's
conduct. But you people say we are all born in sin, depraved at
birth, that Jesus came to redeem us. Is that true?"
"That is all true."
"Please be patient a moment. Plato said sin is nothing but
ignorance. Some people believe it is a physical disease. The Or-
phics have a doctrine of original sin, and in the catacombs here
in Rome, I have heard, Jesus is represented as Orpheus with a
lyre. Could it be that Paul-"
"Satan," said Clement, "is also in the catacombs. He is every-
where."
"You don't think Paul took the idea from the Orphics?"
"Satan-"
"Sir, pardon me. Where did Paul get it? Not from Jews."
"When Adam sinned he brought death to the human race.
Adam-'
""
"Sir, again pardon my interruption. Jews deny that. They say
that is not the correct interpretation. If Paul was familiar with
Orphic doctrine-"
"If Adam had resisted temptation, God would have found some
innocent way to reproduce life."
Damon stared at him, speechless. At last he said, almost whisper-
ing, "You think the production of a child is sinful?"
"Both the child and the manner of it. Where have the demons
186
come from? The angels transgressed the divine ordering, and
by intercourse with women produced the demons.”
Damon looked at Grapte. "I also understand now that for you
people Eve is indispensable. Eve and Satan. You would tell me
that Satan has confused the philosophers, who say there are no
demons. Jews, who ought to know what their own books mean,
say that Adam's seduction had nothing to do with sin in the world.
They say the tendency to sin is born in men. That is all."
“Why did Satan tempt Eve?”
"Jews have different explanations. Some say—”
"Have you read the fourth book of Ezra?”
"No sir."
"Read it. It says all mankind sinned in Adam. The sin of Adam
brought a sentence of death on the whole human race. Our
Lord-"
""
"Yes sir. Have you read Philo? He was a learned Jew. He says
the story of Adam and Eve is a parable, not to be taken literally—'
"Have you read the apocalypse of Moses? It gives all the details
of the lives of Adam and Eve. No, it is not a parable. It is just
this, that Satan in the form of a serpent used Eve to tempt Adam,
and Adam fell, and in his fall we all sinned."
"Sir, you people seem to talk much more about sin than about
virtue. You seem to me to be obsessed with the idea. Sin-"
"The whole world is lost in sin. Our Lord came to redeem and
save it. For the sin of Adam he died on a cross.”
"I suppose," Damon said, more to himself than to Clement, "I'll
never know just what the sin of Adam is. I'd think, though, that
Jews know best how to interpret their own stories."
Raising a hand and gently shaking it at Damon, Clement said,
"They have wandered from the way. They no longer know how to
interpret anything."
"Did Jesus teach you not to judge?"
"To judge rightly.”
Damon smiled. He wanted to say that Paul's teachings seemed
to be a mingling of Mosaic and Greek and Persian ideas; that
Plato's war against the senses had been taken over by Christians,
who thought all erotic pleasure anti-spiritual; that Christians
seemed to be missing the wellsprings of holiness. But these, he
decided, were too difficult as subjects. He would ask two or three
more questions and go.
"I don't want to weary you," he said, "but there is still another
187
matter on which I'd like to hear you speak. In some of the stories
about Jesus there are things that simply could not have happened.
I don't mean the miracles. It is written that the Pharisees were
about to stone a woman for adultery when Jesus stopped them.
The Jews had not stoned women for adultery for a hundred years
before Jesus was born. In Samaria the adulteress was still stoned
but not in Judea. Is it possible that some Christian writers are in-
venting stories intended to discredit the Pharisees?"
"No," said Clement. "We Friends speak with the holy spirit."
"But this is simply a matter of historical fact. Very well, I have
still one other matter. What did Jesus actually say, what are you
people having him say? You tell us he commanded us to love
our neighbors as ourselves. Cicero said that. The Jews have been
saying it a long time. In an old Jewish book I found in Damascus
are these words—may I read them to you? A man should love his
neighbor as himself, and sustain the poor and needy, and each seek
the welfare of his brother, nor bear any grudge from one day to
the next.
"You Friends tell us further that Jesus said a rich man cannot
enter the kingdom. That also is an old Jewish saying. You have
Jesus say that if struck we should turn the other cheek. The Hindu
has a proverb, The gods regard with delight the man who when
struck does not strike back. Jesus might have said all these fine
things that had been said before him but I sometimes wonder if
you people are having him say what you feel he should have said.
"If that isn't so, why didn't Paul record at least one saying by
Jesus? Doesn't it seem strange that Paul, who lived closer to his
time, had no knowledge of what Jesus said? For surely if he had
said these fine moral things Paul would have put them down if he
had known about them. It seems plain," Damon went on, carried
away, "that you people have no actual record of anything Jesus
said, and so are putting in his mouth what you think he might
have said or what you feel he ought to have said. In doing this you
are taking moral precepts from all people. Now that seems to me
to be all right. It is a form of poetry, isn't it? It is a creative act
undertaken by whole communities that belong to this new faith.
But in my book-"
Damon broke off suddenly. He had gone too far. Clement had
risen, and Grapte with him, and Damon knew that he was dis-
missed. Clement went to a desk and returned with a sheet of
paper.
188
"You keep returning to Paul," he said. "Paul wrote this. Read
it sometime.”
"Thank you. I will.”
"Goodnight."
"Goodnight."
Outside, Damon stood looking at the house, feeling stunned,
feeling that he had come close to windows and missed them; think-
ing of Clement and Grapte, of Luke, of Ignatius, of Ribad and
others; thinking of the whole mysterious riddle that was called
Christian. He was more determined than ever to understand it,
to look into all its darkness, yes, and all its light.
189
XVIII
He was eager to go on to Jamnia and Jerusalem but Ayla thought
they should go to a seaside resort for a rest.
"They're expensive," he said.
"We can live on the sands in a tent."
"At Baiae or Apulia among the fops?"
They went to Baiae. At this season it was occupied by the most
elegant parasites in Rome, their hair scented and curled, their
silken raiment drenched with perfumes, their frames weighted
with jewelry. Now and then one had so many rings, of silver and
gold, on his fingers that his hands were barely visible, and the
more aristocratic ones had two complete sets, one for winter and
the other for summer wear. They fascinated Ayla, particularly
those who, grown bald, had hair painted on their skulls, or those
who showed off their private parts through a transparent robe.
"Do they all have boy lovers?" she asked, watching them play
their games on the sand.
"No doubt. But as a pastime they seduce the wives of their
friends."
"Wounded serpents," she said.
He looked at her quickly. He did not like the remark.
During the cooler part of the day the wounded serpents played
various games, such as backgammon, hard ball, three men in a row
and dice, though dicing was illegal in public. The best possible
throw in dice was a Venus, the worst, a dog; and when they threw
a Venus or their opponent threw a dog the fops would leap up and
down in shrill glee, slapping their thighs and shrieking.
"The Romans aren't a very clean people, are they?"
"A lot cleaner than they used to be."
190
Ayla sniffed. A breeze was coming from the fops her way.
A man in costly garments came strolling across the sands, wear-
ing a patch on his forehead. Ayla stared at him and turned to
Damon. She shrugged.
"And what does that mean?"
"Maybe he was once a slave and was branded. I suppose the
patch covers a brand.”
Ayla gave a low laugh. "But doesn't he look silly, if everybody
knows the reason? You imagine he has slaves?"
A Roman of social standing, Damon explained, had to have
quite a few slaves-at least a valet, barber, wardrobe keeper and
secretary, all devoted exclusively to him; as well as cooks, pastry-
makers, waiters, doorkeepers, footmen, litter-carriers, butlers, gard-
ners, coachmen. The very wealthy also had librarians, accountants,
stewards, architects, physicians, poets, philosophers.
"You say poets here have no social standing?"
"None at all. They sit below the salt. That's a Roman expres-
sion meaning you're little better than a slave. Writers, actors,
physicians, philosophers, architects, artists all sit below the salt,
with now and then an exception. If a Roman senator were to ask
a famous writer to a party he would show his contempt this way.
For himself and guests of equal social rank, fine old Setine or
Falernian would be poured into goblets of silver and gold; while
some sour stuff grown on the Sabine hills would be poured into
an earthenware mug for the writer. The senator and his friends
would feast on turbot and pheasant, the writer on pike out of the
Tiber."
"Do they invite the writer just to insult him?”
"Sometimes."
"Then why does the idiot go?"
"Writers feel inferior and they love to be insulted."
Ayla laughed, her gaze on his face. "Do you?”
"I have asked myself that. If you're born a slave you never get
over it. Persons born slaves and set free become very humble or
very arrogant."
"Which are you?"
"Neither, I hope, but I know that my social rank is that of a
potter. Here. In Roman society the artist, doctor, lawyer, school-
master, writer, philosopher are classed with butchers, bakers and
potters.
""
Ayla clasped his hand. "Poor darling neither humble nor arro-
191
gant! I suppose they must have awful contempt for people like
me."
"Unspeakable. For a Roman, no conceivable virtue or talent
can take the curse off poverty. If you're rich you can wear the
slave's brand and get away with it. If you're poor, yet are the
world's greatest philosopher and scientist all in one, you'll be given
pike out of a stinking river. Being born poor is the greatest of all
crimes. For a Roman the fop over there scratching his sex organs
is as far above a poet as Caesar was above the sixty thousand he
butchered one day in Gaul."
"What a horrible people! As for the fop he must have lice. He
scratches like that every day."
One afternoon a senator came along, a corpulent and pompous
and haughty fellow, so vain of his citizenship that he wore his
toga in the heat. Damon whispered to her that a senator was
coming; she could tell by the broad band of purple down the
front and back of his tunic, and by the silver or ivory crescent on
his black shoes.
"I don't see purple."
"It's hidden under his toga."
Gazing at the man she said: “If I were to greet him what should
I say?"
"I don't quite know, though it seems to me I should. The em-
peror would be his Highness or his Mightiness and he might offer
a hand to be kissed, or a foot. Caligula offered his foot but he was
a god."
"And how would he greet me?"
“Oh, if in right good humor he might say, My dear Ayla. If his
humor was not so good he would say, Dear Ayla. If he had you
marked for execution he would simply ignore you."
The senator had passed and she was looking at his broad back.
"How many slaves do you suppose that pompous old toad has?”
"Maybe a few hundred, maybe a few thousand."
"What would he think of the Christians?”
"My dear Ayla, his awareness of life doesn't descend that low.
If it did he would call them atheists, because for Romans they are.
Like Seneca he would think them a pestilence, a rabble of half-
wits and criminals, a plague of locusts. If he had his way he would
put them all in old ships and drown them."
"Does a man like that love his wife?"
"Ovid said no man can be a gentleman who hasn't been in love.
192
So we must assume that he has loved some woman, perhaps the
wife of his closest friend."
"Peppercorn, you sound bitter today. Seriously, now, do you
suppose he has loved some woman?"
"Possibly. That such a man can feel love is one of the riddles."
"Does the wife here take the husband's name?"
"Oh no. If we were married you'd be known as Damon's Ayla."
"I'd like that."
"I would be Ayla's Damon."
"Oh, that is wonderful!" She raised his hand to her lips. "Tell
Damon's Ayla if Roman wives love their husbands."
"Some do. But some poison their husbands, one after another.
One wife is said to have poisoned eight husbands in five years.
Such wives reckon time by the husbands they have had. They will
say, That was in the year when I married Lucius; or, That was the
year I divorced Silius; or, That was the year I killed Flavius."
She looked at him incredulously a moment and burst with
merry laughter.
One afternoon she said: "If human beings are half as bad as
you tell me they are nobody in the future will care about a book
if you write one. The Christians will burn it. So let's eat and swim
and drink and love, and not even think about the Christians for
a whole month."
But Damon had wearied of the scented fops and idlers, the strut-
ting litters and red sedans. Ayla's lust for life, he knew, was much
greater than his own; she enjoyed every minute with a child's in-
nocence, swam daily in the surf, browned herself in the sands,
drank more wine than was good for her, and exclaimed with de-
light over every dish that she had not tasted before. Damon al-
ways felt a small sense of shame when indulging his appetites. Was
that, he wondered, because he had been born a slave, or because
his mother was burned at a stake, or because all but a few of the
world's people were poor and cast down? When he and Ayla
feasted on pheasant, partridge, turbot, ortolans, sweetmeats and
fine old wines he could not put out of his mind the millions of
slaves who had only black bread, pulse, roots, and (when any at
all) the cheapest sour wine mixed half and half with water. Ayla,
he supposed, was good for him. She loved rich foods, wines, silks,
furnishings, jewels, and the sweet luxury of idleness. With the
money Damon gave her she had bought for herself only the cost-
liest clothes, jewelry and scents; and she would touch a piece of
193
fine cloth as a lover might touch the breasts of his woman. She
scattered gems in her lustrous hair, and hung from her neck a
chatelaine glittering with stones. She had golden brooches on her
dress and silver buckles on her shoes. Damon was a little ashamed
of her.
And, he admitted to himself, a little weary. She was a more
ardent lover and she exhausted his energies, though he sometimes
suspected that her ardor was chiefly for a child. Having sub-
merged him in stuporous sleep she would steal away at daylight
for a swim, returning refreshed and vital and hungry, to tickle
his nose and whisper in his ear. After a full day she would want to
dance and sing and drink all night. Damon preferred to arrange
the cushions on his beach-chair and sit, looking out over the waters,
studying the aquatic birds, watching the delicate play of light on
the people around him, while thinking of the riddle called life
and of the place of the Christians in it.
One evening there was music and dancing in a pavilion. Da-
mon was tired and went to bed early, leaving Ayla with the pleas-
ure-seekers; but after a while she came to him and sat on the bed
and clasped one of his hands. He knew by her manner that she
was deeply disturbed.
"Peppercorn, you asleep?”
"No. Why have you left the dancing?"
"It isn't fun without you. You know I love you very much?"
"I know when you say that you have something on your con-
science."
“Anyway, as soon as you're out of sight I feel lonely. Is that
love?"
"Or fear."
"You cynical men are quite a problem for a woman."
"You women who don't look to your motives are quite a prob-
lem for men.”
"All right. Some of these fops have been trying to seduce me."
"You're attractive."
"You don't think they have?"
"I haven't been thinking about it.”
"Then you don't love me. Do you have to think about the
Christians all the time?"
She annoyed and wearied him. "What did Ovid say? She is
chaste who has no wooer: unless she is a bumpkin she will woo."
"Then I must be a bumpkin."
194
"But don't want to be? Take heart, said Ovid, you'll conquer
every woman. Not one in ten thousand will refuse. Refuse or not,
they'll love you more for asking."
She sucked her breath in. He sensed that she was angry.
"I found her body faultless everywhere. What arms, what
shoulders! How sweet her breasts, ready for an embrace! Be-
neath a molded waist what a smooth belly! What a rich flank and
what a slender thigh!"
She had released his hand. In a low voice she breathed at him,
"You son of a bitch!"
"How sweet to lie and hear the gale, holding my mistress to my
heart! How sweet-"
She struck out, smiting him through the bedclothes. She was
weeping. She was saying, "We've never quarreled, not once! We
don't love if we never quarrel."
"A poet said it better. Love is uncertain if it never rages. May
my worst enemy have a placid girl.”
"Damon?"
"Yes, Ayla."
"You think all women are whores at heart?"
"What do you mean by whore? Plautus said a whore is like a
prosperous city, she can't get on without a lot of men." He sat
up and stared at her. “Well, what it is? Have you set your heart
on some man here?"
"There's a man here crazy about me and he's a hell of a nuisance.
I want you with me after this."
"Lest you not be the one in ten thousand?"
“Oh, I've asked myself that. Can you be fair with me? After
all, a man can be a dreadful pest. If all but one in ten thousand
surrender, is it because they are worn out, beaten down?"
"That is possible."
"Do you know how some of these studs persist? If I slip away
in the morning for a swim, when I return to the beach there he
sits with some damned gift in his hands. If I dance, the moment
I am released there he is at my elbow, asking will I join him over
pheasant and vintaged wine. If in the afternoon while you're
taking a nap I go out to sit on my chair, he comes up and drops
at my feet and wants to know if I prefer rubies or pearls. If I say
I prefer to be alone he wants to bring me a book to read. If I re-
mind him that I have a lover he gives me a polite leer. If I rise and
leave him he'll be there the next time I sit, dance, swim—”
195
"I know," Damon said. "Some of them are far more persistent
than rams, bulls, studs." He drew her weeping into his arms.
"Now you understand?” she asked, sobbing.
"Yes, more than you've told."
She threw off her shoes and clothes and crept into bed. She put
her lips to his throat and asked: "Why are men so horrible?”
"I think the male senses something in the female. A bull will
torment and worry cows, or a stud the mares; a ram will chop at a
ewe; a rooster will chase a hen into the next province.
God seems
to want to be sure that all things will multiply as the sands of the
sea and the Christians don't like that."
"What is it that I haven't told?"
"That you want a child. This man must sense that in you. He
may regard himself as an instrument of God's will."
"I
guess that's it," she said. "When a woman wants a child so
bad she doesn't have much resistance, does she?"
"We'll have a child before too long," he said.
A little later he thought she had fallen asleep. He was lying on
his back, staring up, thinking about her when she said:
"I met a wonderful man. Anyway, you say he is. He's old and
ragged and has long gray hair and beard and he carries a staff.”
"Not Demetrius?”
"Yes."
"Not here! Not in a place like Baiae!"
"I asked him why. Great and worthy man, what are you doing
here? Guess what he said."
"Something witty?"
"He said, Does one teach virtue to the virtuous? Does the
physician heal the well or the sick?"
Damon sat up as if to go and at once Ayla seized him to draw
him down. "I want you to hold me," she said. "Hold me close
and tell me you love me, even if you have to lie."
"I do love you. How does a man say it?"
"How do you say it?"
He lay down and drew her to him. "The poet Horace held his
harlot and said, I needn't fear a husband interrupting me in my
pleasure, shouting, smashing locks, dogs barking, slams, cries, the
woman leaping out of bed. He liked a cheap and easy love, said
Horace. I like things that come hard-such as the woman who tells
me she can't be the one in ten thousand without a little under-
standing from her man."
196
"Damon's Ayla thanks you!"
"And out there is Demetrius, bless his old gray soul. Tomor-
row I'll see him.”
All the next day Damon searched for the man and thought he
had gone but just at dusk, while they were reposing on beach-
chairs, Demetrius came along. He had only one coarse garment,
and a pouch slung from his shoulder, in which, Damon supposed,
he had a piece of bread, possibly a wallet of beans and a small jug
of water. He was an old man: Seneca had known Demetrius.
Seneca had said he was a great man, even when compared to the
greatest. All his life Demetrius had endured cold, hunger and a
hard bed with cheerful fortitude, wandering over the world, teach-
ing those who would hear. Not even the vile age of Nero had
been able to corrupt him. In bed Damon had told Ayla: "When
Nero threatened him Demetrius gave a reply that has become
famous: You threaten me but Nature threatens you."
For Damon Demetrius was possibly the greatest man of his
age. As the old philosopher approached, Damon arose, awed by
his presence, afraid of his conscience and his knowledge. Deme-
trius called himself an ambassador of God. Damon was glad to
recognize him as that. He asked the old man to take his chair but
Demetrius said no, and looked at Ayla, his gray old weather-worn
face softening.
"Is this the man you told me about?"
"Yes sir."
Ayla had also risen. "Sit," said Demetrius. "I stand, for I think
a man can see more clearly when standing."
Damon asked him if he would like to go to his villa to refresh
himself. Demetrius declined with thanks. Damon reproached him-
self for his obtuseness, for he knew that philosophers of this kind
did not like houses. They preferred the sky, the sun and stars,
the odors of sea and land and wind.
Darkness soon fell and the stars came in one by one. A breeze
moved over them with the smell of deep sea water. In the gloom
Damon felt more at ease and more able to talk and he asked the
old man what he thought was the greatest thing in life.
"Reason, because it is part of the divine mind. That much we
know and that is enough if we cultivate our knowing."
Damon wanted to ask him what he thought of the people called
Christians and was trying to formulate the question when Ayla put
it.
197
Nearly all people, said Demetrius, worshiped a being that they
called God. They all had much the same illusions and fables, the
same low level of poetry in their worship, the same fixation on a
sublimated image of themselves.
Damon asked: "What, then, is God?”
Demetrius touched him with his staff. "My dear man, only
among the most arrogant does the finite try to define the in-
finite. No man can ever hope to know God, for if we could hope
to know what God is, there must some day be an end of aspira-
tion."
Damon then asked what he recognized a moment later as a fool-
ish question. The Christians in Rome, he said, told that Apol-
lonius competed in wonder-working with a Jew named Peter, said
to have been a disciple of a Jew named Jesus. Did Apollonius try
to work wonders?
"Have you lived so long," asked Demetrius, peering at the gray
in Damon's hair, "and learned so little? Does a philosopher who
kept absolute silence for years to prepare himself then match his
powers against an ordinary sorcerer?"
Damon was feeling the sting when Ayla spoke up and at the
same moment reached over to touch his hand. "Sir, do you think
prayer does anyone any good?”
"Only as confession. God grants us our due, and our supplica-
tions for special favors over our fellows God does not hear. If our
wish is reasonable it will be granted, without prayer, if unreason-
able it will be denied. Except as confession prayer is useless.”
Ayla asked: "Do you believe in immortality?”
"Woman, look round you. Do you see anything that is not im-
mortal? What did my disciple Demonax say to the prophet in
Smyrna? If you can alter the course of destiny you should de-
mand higher fees. If everything happens by the will of God of
what use are you? Thus he spoke to the prophet." Looking down
at Ayla he went on: "Why this mad passion for continued ex-
istence, when so few learn how to use the years they have? Do
they wish to make their capacity for bungling eternal? Dear wo-
man, are you afraid?"
"Sometimes."
"Fear of the unknown has peopled the universe with specters,
ghosts, demons, devils. Whither, people ask, can they flee from
the awful unknown? Not in sleep, for their dreams may be night-
mares. Not in death, for then they wander as forlorn shades. The
198
thing these people fear is their compulsion toward evil. Around
you everywhere," he said, making a wide sweep with his staff,
"you see the shrines and altars and wailing-walls of a people try-
ing in vain to deny their own conscience. Conscience is the price
God put on mind. Listen! Can you hear the night? Can you smell
the stars? Can you feel the heart of the earth beating? Everywhere
life calls us to a common brotherhood of man, to peace and good
will; but everywhere people corrupt the goodness they were born
with and beget new vices upon the old. Man is born innocent and
good, but at once fear and error and ignorance confuse him, until
he flees before his own specters. There is nothing to fear but his
illusions and he has created all of them.”
Damon said: "You think man is born innocent? The Christians
say he is born in sin.”
"Are these people authorities? Is there a single philosopher
among them? Aren't they only another cult rearranging the pat-
terns of ignorance? Are you two Christians?"
"No, but some of our people have been.”
"Sir," said Ayla, "I have another question. Are all the oracles
false?"
"Have you heard of Alexander, who established an oracle?”
"No sir."
"This Alexander decided to become wealthy by exploiting hu-
man ignorance-and is there any other way? He was a very cun-
ning man. He went to a secluded place and buried some tablets
and at the appropriate time he unearthed them. He then had a
Sibylline oracle herald the approach of a new god. In magnificent
raiment he suddenly appeared on the scene, affecting those signs of
insanity which the vulgar take to be a sign of divine powers. The
people hailed him.
"Alexander had sealed in a goose egg the form of a small ser-
pent. This
egg was now fished out of a lake. The broken shell re-
vealed the foetus of a new god. A little later he used the form of
a fullgrown serpent, to which he had artfully attached the likeness
of a human head. What did the people do? Ran rejoicing to
spread the glad tidings through half of Asia. As the new god's
prophet, Alexander now accepted questions on sealed tablets but
he knew the trick of breaking and resealing the seals without let-
ting the people know it. He divined questions and gave answers.
The people were thunderstruck.
"Well, before long, of course, he had a huge staff of secretaries,
199
missionaries to spread his fame, bankers to handle the enormous
sums which he collected. He became as rich as a Caesar. Multi-
tudes came from afar to hail him as a god and stars, it is said,
guided them. People of royal birth went to kneel before him.
Rulers bowed to him. And now his cult is spreading over Italy."
Demetrius paused a moment, looking at their upturned faces.
"My friends, men like me devote their lives to virtue and truth.
We talk to all who will listen. We accept no money, no food, no
lodging, but spend all our time trying to destroy the evil super-
stitions and fears that have housed themselves in the brain of man-
kind. A few, a very few, listen to us, but multitudes lay their treas-
ure and their souls at the feet of fakers. For one who listens to me,
or five to Epictetus, there are ten times ten thousand who rush to
the cults that promise immortality for a little fasting and prayer.
They are all alike, these cults, whether it is the Jew-god Jesus or
the Persian god Mithra or the Egyptian god Osiris or the Greek
god Dionysus. There is no salvation through such childish short-
cuts as baptism and holy meals. Repentance is only a mask that is
worn over conscience. God gave us moral responsibility and free
will and God calls on us for good deeds and good works. All the
rest of it is fables."
"Will it always be this way?" asked Damon. "Will people always
flee from the truth to the Alexanders?"
"As far into the future as any man can see there'll be ecclesiasti-
cal and political messiahs to dupe the ignorant, and a crafty priest-
hood of both kinds to extort their earnings. What did Varro tell
us? that it is in the interest of states to have their people deceived
in religion. What did Livy tell us?—that Numa used fear of the
unknown as the most effective way to control multitudes. What
did Polybius tell us?—that all rulers use superstitions and terrors
and scenic effects to hold the mass of people in bondage. So it will
be as long as brother exploits the ignorance of his brother. Have
you," he asked, looking at Damon, “ever speculated on the dread-
ful vanity of man?"
"On my own," said Damon.
"That is the reply of a civilized man. I like you better."
"Is God the image of man's vanity?"
“Oh, much more than that. Let me say it this way: since woman
is the womb of birth, how strange that the god of the Jews made
her out of a man. Would man make himself the vessel of birth,
and so rob woman of her marvelous powers? Have you asked
200
yourself why men have debased women and made them chattels?—
or why these foolish Christians say that man is the reflection of
God's glory but woman is only the reflection of man's glory? Don't
you understand that it is man, not woman, who set up an image
of himself and called it God?"
"But isn't it good for man to have an image of something bigger
than himself?"
"Not only good but necessary. But having done this, shall he
no longer look into the depths of his vanity and presumption?"
Ayla slipped an arm around Damon and on his left side her
fingers moved across his ribs. "He means the rib you once had
that is now me."
The old man looked at her, his eyes amused. Then he looked
at Damon and said: "Have you asked these Christians why three-
fourths of the world is enslaved by the other fourth?"
"Christians believe in slavery."
"There's not a priest from Tingis to Bactra who doesn't think
that slavery is God-ordained. People so sunk in folly must be en-
lightened; so why don't you sell your property, give it to the poor
and come with me? Say with Antigonos of Soko, Be not like slaves
who minister to their master with the hope of being paid; but
serve without hope of pay and for the love of your fellowmen.
Say with a great Jew, The reward of the good deed is the good
deed. The kingdom of heaven is within us. The joys of heaven or
the woes of hell are all in the human mind."
Ayla said: "Do you like Jews?"
"My dear woman, a philosopher likes good wherever he finds it.
The Jews are the only people in the whole wide world who say
that men should feel compassion toward the beasts. Isn't that the
essence of religions? I talked not long ago to Gamaliel. On three
things, he told me, the world rests: on truth, on justice and on
peace. He said to me, The first question that will be asked at
the day of judgment is this: Did you deal justly with your neigh-
bor?
"But the Christians, feeling horribly sinful, say that we must
die and be born again. Alexander of Macedon asked the wise men
of the south, What shall a man do that he may live? The wise men
said, Let him kill himself. And what shall a man do that he may
die? And they said, Let him live. That is an old myth that the
Christians have taken over. Is it somehow related to a death-wish
in people? There is a death-wish in every Christian I have talked
201
to. They borrow these things from others but do they understand
them? Seneca said, He who would do injury to another has already
done it. Aelian said, If you look on a man wishing to kill you
have killed; on a woman wishing to rape you have raped. That is
true, and so they have Jesus say it. But do they understand the
things they are having him say?"
“You think, then, they are having him say what they hope he
said?"
Demetrius fixed his gaze on Damon, looking him over from
head to feet. "You seem to be a man of wealth," he said. "Tell me
why some men accumulate so much property, while so many
starve."
"Do you think nobody should have property?"
"Can you really feast, knowing that most of your brothers and
sisters are moaning with hunger? Can your woman really wear
all these jewels I see on her, knowing that at this moment thou-
sands of babies are dying for want of something to eat? Can peo-
ple call themselves human who are so cold toward suffering?" He
tapped Damon's knee with his stick. "Well, my good man, I've
told you all that I've learned in a long lifetime. Now I must rest.
Goodnight."
"Goodnight."
"Goodnight."
After the old man had gone Damon looked up at the sky, turn-
ing over and over in his mind the things the old man had said.
"See how bright the stars are over Baiae. Is that because De-
metrius is here?"
202
XIX
A day or two later Demetrius left Baiae and Damon then was
eager to be off, while there was good sailing weather. They went to
Ostia at the mouth of the Tiber and shipped on a large corn vessel
with cabin accommodations. As they pulled away from Italy,
Damon stood on a deck looking back at it, believing that he would
never see it again. He had no wish to. All that Rome could give
him had been given: his journey, his seeking, his hunger now lay
toward the sun and the East. With the West against his back he
felt the chill of it like the autumn chill that came over things after
the sun had set.
While putting his notes in order he found the page that Clement
had asked him to read. It was part of a letter that Paul had written
to the feuding Christians in Corinth.
"Listen," he said to Ayla. "Though I speak with the tongue
of men and of angels and have not love I am as clanging brass or
a tinkling cymbal. Do you like it?”
"Yes, peppercorn."
"Love is long-suffering and kind, love envies not, love boasts
not, love is not arrogant, nor acts rudely, nor is self-seeking, nor ill-
tempered. It pardons all things, believes all things, hopes all
things, endures all things."
"If love believes all things it's not very smart, is it?”
Damon went on reading. "When I was a child I spoke as a child,
I understood as a child, I thought as a child. But when I became
a man I put childish things away. For now we see in a mirror
darkly but face to face." He looked at Ayla. "Is that it? Is that
what this new religion means?"
"A religion for children?"
208
"A child's seeing."
"Demetrius is a child, isn't he?"
"Demetrius?"
"I saw the child, the lonely lost little boy, looking out of his
eyes when he was conscious of us as two persons in love. Pepper-
corn, isn't it childlike to ask people to give all their property away?
What would the world be if everybody did that? Whose ship
would this be? If there were no property there could be no so-
ciety. So what kind of sense do Demetrius and the Christians make
when they say give everything to the poor and follow me?”
"It doesn't seem to be sense at all."
"Oh, but you think Demetrius is a very wise man. He wanted
you to give away all you have. Why? So that beggars can squander
and waste it? If Jesus said give away all you have and follow me
was he crazy?"
"He was expecting the end of the world any day."
"Wasn't that childlike?"
"Demetrius and Jesus don't like to see rich men and poor men
in the same world."
"I say they're both silly. Property is for those who know its
value. Fine jewels are for those who regard them not as ornaments
to adorn their vanity but as lovely things to shine on their spirit.
If we followed Demetrius and these Christians and gave all to the
poor, they'd waste it, being ignorant. What would the world then
be like?"
"Jesus, I tell you, was expecting the end of the world."
“Only a child would be expecting it. Do they still think the
end is coming any minute?"
"Oh yes."
"Maybe you're right. Maybe this new faith is for children.”
"That's not the way I put it. I said with a child's seeing."
"Instead of looking in a glass darkly? For a woman that doesn't
make sense either."
Among the Christians on the boat Damon found what he
thought was a queer pair. The woman was tall and skinny, with
a long nose that had a hump in it, as though it had been broken.
Her eyes were small and olive green and too close together; her
hair, falling over her shoulders and down to her waist, looked as
if it had never been washed or combed. Because her voice was
deeper than a woman's Damon wondered if she was a man in wo-
man's garb. Her name was Hymne.
204
The man's name was Stephylus. He was shorter than his com-
panion and quite stout and very dark. His lips were red and full
and looked almost obscenely alive back in his beard. His fore-
head was low and deeply furrowed; his hair was short, thick and
curly, with a high gloss. The garments of both were of fine mate-
rials but were frayed and unclean.
Damon thought that possibly the woman was mad, but if this
was so, her lunacy was a thing she possessed and controlled, as
though it were her genius, her hidden fires, which now and then
blew up, lighting her gaunt features and making her green eyes
shine like jewels.
One evening Stephylus and Hymne were sitting after dusk, with
Damon and Ayla, when Hymne went to the ship's edge and said
in her deep voice, "The bridegroom comes."
Stephylus turned to Damon and said: “She means our Lord is
returning."
"Is coming, you mean?"
"Hasn't he always been coming?"
Damon looked over at Ayla. He thought she was bored. "And
will always be coming?"
"Yes, but always here for those who can feel him. The degree
in which we sense his coming is a measure of our faith.”
Damon stared at the man, wondering if he was glib or profound.
Hymne, still gazing up at the sky, said that the Lord had golden
hair, and Stephylus turned on Damon his oily black eyes and said:
"Her father had golden hair."
Hymne said the Lord had blue eyes. With a blandness that was
chilling, Stephylus said: "Her father had blue eyes.”
Hymne said that the Lord was tall.
"You have observed," said Stephylus, "that I am quite short."
What sort of parable was this? Damon again looked at Ayla.
Ayla winked.
"She has the gift of second sight," Stephylus said. “She also has
our Lord's wounds.”
"His wounds?" said Damon, his astonishment growing.
"Where?"
"In her feet and in her left side."
"You mean she actually bleeds?"
"Sometimes."
Out in the Egyptian desert, Stephylus said one day, there were
Christians fasting and praying and weeping for their sins. Some
205
of them prayed seventy-two hours without pausing. Like their
Lord they were wrestling with Satan—for was not the desert the
home of imps and demons? It was into such a wilderness that
Jesus had gone to wrestle with Satan for forty days and overthrow
him.
Damon said to Ayla: "When I think I'm about to understand
the Christians something new comes along. He says their Lord
came and for a night exchanged hearts with this woman. What
can that mean?”
Ayla said: "She wearies me just to look at her."
"She says that sometimes when asleep her Lord comes and gives
her soul a soft kiss. Once he took her for a short visit to heaven."
"I just can't bear to think about her.”
One evening they all sat together, with Stephylus now and then
casting a discreet glance toward Ayla's bosom.
Damon said: "Do you Christians still think the end of the world
will come soon?"
"Yes," said Stephylus, nodding.
"Berosus, a historian long ago, said everything will be con-
sumed by fire."
"Was he a Christian?"
"He couldn't have been. He lived four hundred years ago.'
"Christians have always been,” said Stephylus, his gaze linger-
ing on Ayla.
"Have always been!"
"Yes, even before the beginning.”
"Beginning of what?"
"Before God made the world he made the souls of Christians."
Damon stared at him, incredulous. "Then the number of Chris-
tians was determined. If that is so why labor to convert people?"
"Many who were determined have to be converted."
"If they weren't converted they'd still have Christian souls,
wouldn't they?" He looked over at Ayla. Her eyes were closed.
She seemed bored. "If Christians were made long ago then it's
easy to see why so many people have stolen their holy rites, such
as baptism, the Passion, the last supper."
Stephylus was nodding. His red lips were smiling back in his
beard. All the Christian rites, he said, were ordained in the be-
ginning. Other people had stolen them.
Damon was thinking, With what cunning evasion they get
around this thing! He was looking at Stephylus when he heard
206
Ayla ask Hymne if she was married. Stephylus in his sly way
looked over at Damon to see what he made of that.
Quickly, Damon said: “In some places Christian women go into
brothels."
"She was there," said Stephylus blandly. "Our Lord spoke to
her."
"In a brothel? What did he say?"
"He asked her to leave it and come with him."
"And do you live a continent life?"
"With us," said Stephylus with dignity, "what is forbidden to
women is forbidden to men.”
"But marriage isn't forbidden."
"Adultery is. Did you ever see an adulterous wife sitting in the
stocks, while the wife of the guilty man flogged her with a leaded
whip? I have seen it. I was the man."
A religion, it seemed to Damon, that could lift up the adulterer
and the whore had a lot of good in it. A deep and abiding good-
ness seemed to nourish Hymne, for all her strange ways and dreams.
Damon had decided to go to the desert to see the mystics. He
told Ayla they were burying themselves up to their necks, hanging
stones from their scrotum, searing their penis with hot irons, sit-
ting in mosquito bogs and in other ways wrestling with Satan.
Ayla looked at him to see if he was serious and then burst with
laughter. "Why don't they just cut their stuff off and be done with
it?" She framed Damon's face and kissed his lips, looking into his
eyes, her own eyes laughing. “It's not the Christians you're trying
to understand. It's men."
"Men? That may be."
After dark they sat together smelling the salt water and the night
and the wind blowing from the south off hot desert sands. They
breathed deep of the ancient odors of earth and sea and watched
the first light come into view. Damon knew that it was the light-
house on the island of Pharos, a marble structure four hundred
feet high, whose beams were shed for miles out over the sea. They
were close to ancient Egypt and it seemed to him that the farther
he went into the East, the older and subtler became the odors and
the meanings.
While they clasped hands and watched, the whole brilliant il-
lumination of it came fully into view. It was as though ten thou-
sand stars had formed a great cluster low in the sky. The stars
just above and around it winked and faded and disappeared, and
207
then came again, winking faintly. Damon thought of the strange
lights in the mysteries of Dionysus and Mithra, that heralded the
god's birth. They could not see the lighthouse itself but only the
light, which stood in the sky as unsupported as the sun or the
moon. The deep and gently rolling water roundabout was black,
but little by little it began to catch the light; and as they ap
proached the harbor the whole scene awoke in a wonderful and
glowing brilliance. They could see the separate waves and the
marvelous things they did with the light as they formed their
crests of fire and folded under, broke and were drowned. Looking
up at the sky and down at the sea and over at the light, Damon said
at last:
"Maybe all this is what it means. Or maybe this is what the Jews
mean. And people shall come to your light, and kings to the
brightness of your rising.
208
XX
Ayla begged Damon to forget the mystics out in the sands but he
gave her money and told her to wait; and one morning with a
guide he set out on muleback for another view of men. The one
the guide took him to was as bald as marble and quite elderly. He
was buried in the earth to his chin and there he stood, with only
his head out, his scalp roasted a tomato red, his long gray beard
spread out on the sands before him.
Damon stood behind the old man and looked down at the red
skull. Who fed him? The guide said the man did not eat, but
fasted, though a disciple came daily to offer him water. How long
had he been here? The guide did not know. He said the man
would stay here until he became senseless, when the disciple would
dig him out.
Damon sat off to one side and studied the man's face. The eyes
were large and black and bloodshot but he thought there was no
lunacy in them, only gentleness and peace. He thought the man
was an Egyptian, for he had a conical head and the Egyptian nose.
Damon was not to speak to him, the guide said. Silence was
part of his punishment.
"Punishment for what?"
"Master, I don't know."
Recalling Ayla's words Damon said, "For being a man, no
doubt." The gray beard was aswarm with insects. "Are there
many like him?"
The guide knew only of this one buried at the moment but
farther out was another one who lay with a pile of stones on his
chest; and another who, stark naked, had climbed a tree in a
swamp to let the mosquitoes eat him alive; and another some-
209
where in a cave who stood on his head and collapsed and stood
again. Oh, there were many others: some had made incisions in
their flesh to let the worms in, a few had cut their testicles off.
Damon got to his feet and looked round him, feeling queer.
What a world it was and what a thing was man! He again viewed
the red skull with its fringe of gray hair. Parts of the scalp had
blistered and broken open, and at the edge of the wounds tiny ants
seemed to be drinking. Feeling that he was about to lose his
humanity and reason Damon walked round and round the skull,
looking at it, trying to realize that a man's body was buried there
in the earth, with arms thrust down, inert, with the heart dully
beating. And there, deeply hidden from women, were his parts.
Did he belong to the people called Christians?
The guide did not know.
"How long will it be before he is senseless?"
It might take three weeks or a month. Some of the mystics were
rugged and could endure a lot of punishment. The disciple would
come some day with a skin of water. He would find him uncon-
scious. He would dig him out. He would restore him with water
and put him on a donkey and take him home and then the holy
man would perform great wonders.
"Such as what?"
He would walk on water, he would heal the sick and raise the
dead.
"Well," said Damon, "all things have I seen in the days of my
vanity. If you see violent perversion of judgment and justice,
marvel not. For all his days are sorrows, and his travail grief; yea,
his heart takes no rest in the night. This is also vanity. Then I
said in my heart, as it happens to the fool, so it happens to me; and
why was I then more wise? What is the cause that the former days
were better than these? There is an evil among all things that are
done under the sun." Observing that the guide was staring at him
open-mouthed Damon said: “I was reciting the words of a great
Jew."
Damon turned away, feeling ineffectual and depressed, feeling
for the first time in his life that it was a terrible thing to be a man.
After he had gone forty or fifty paces he looked back: there was
the skull, like some strange red desert plant, and there under it
was a man, a votary of the Christ or of Osiris or of Serapis; a man
who felt out there that he was sinful and vile but who in the city
later would feel that he was a saint and a prophet. "Vanity of
210
vanities, says the preacher, all is vanity, and folly is set in great
dignity." He pointed back and said to the guide: "And folly
is set in great dignity.”
"Yes, master. You want to see the one with stones on his chest?"
"Oh no. No. The other evening I saw the wonderful light of a
lighthouse and the beauty of it on the sea and in the sky, as a ship
came in out of the black water and the night; and today I see a
man buried to his chin out in a wilderness of sand."
"He will perform great wonders," the guide said.
"If the jackals don't eat him."
On returning to the city he found Ayla and he was so filled with
the horror of the old man out there that he did not observe at once
that Ayla had been weeping. He took her to a tavern for a drink.
He told her what he had seen, and she shuddered; he spoke of the
follies set in great dignity over the earth. Then he saw the tear-
stains on her cheeks. He asked her what had made her unhappy
and she shuddered over against him and said she was afraid.
"Has something frightened you?"
At once tears welled to her eyes. She was trembling. He had
never seen her so agitated except when she woke screaming from a
nightmare. Putting an arm round her and kissing her cheek he
whispered, "What is it?"
She said she had seen a man killed. She sobbed against him, un-
able for a little while to say more; and then she said the man was a
Christian and a mob of men had beaten him to death because he
was, they said, an atheist.
"Who did it? Romans?"
"Men."
He waited, while she shuddered and hid her face against his
neck. She said they beat the man over his head, knocking him
down, and then they exposed his genitals and with leaded straps
literally tore them from his body. They beat him until he could
not walk and then they dragged him. She was weeping. She was
breathing against him, "I hate men! Not even the beasts are so
cruel!" She looked up at him with wet anguished eyes. Would the
Christians, she asked, ever kill that way in the name of their Christ?
"What man can say!"
Wasn't that, she asked, all that he had to learn?-if this new
faith would torture and kill as the old had done.
Damon called for another decanter of wine. "Let's get drunk,"
he said. "Who can look into the depths of man and stay sober?"
211
She was weeping again. Damon suspected that the tragic expe-
rience in her life, whatever it had been, had risen to the surface.
His thoughts turned to his mother. He had to think of her while
thinking of this Christian who had been tortured and beaten to
death. If Christians survived and won and became the Pharos
lighthouse would they persecute and torture and slay in the name
of their God?
They went to their room and lay on the bed, holding one an-
other close. They fell asleep. When Ayla awoke, Damon thought
she would be all right but she was not. Again she wept. She could
not forget the man's face and the way he had looked at them.
She could not forget his eyes. Something in that man's eyes, the
childlike bewilderment, the reproach and hurt, because these, his
fellowmen, should wish to kill him, had gone into Ayla's soul.
She wept like one who had wept so many times that weeping was
almost a reflex. Damon said they would go eat and then take a
pleasure boat and a jug of wine to Canopus and lie on couches
under the stars.
She looked at him and said, "Don't ever leave me alone again.”
"Never," he said.
When she saw him looking at the dark color under her eyes
she told him that women were most the mother in the time of
their menses, and more easily disturbed; but what had upset her
so terribly was her wish to kill when she saw men killing. She had
not known that she could do that. In the dying Christian's eyes
she had seen a loneliness that having seen nobody could ever for-
get. His mute appeal had stricken her with pity, but when in a few
moments she saw him prostrate and men kicking his teeth out
while other men smote his genitals off, when she saw the savage
faces of the men, saw them not as men at all but as cowardly things
in a pack, venting their insane fury on a creature already helpless
and dying, then murderous rage came up in her and she looked
round blindly for anything to kill with. She would have killed,
she would have killed them all!
They went to Canopus but were wretched because of the in-
sects. In their room it was to be a struggle with insects night after
night: with flies, with ants which they tried to brush away, with a
host of bloodsucking things that they crushed between fingernails.
Eating in some of the inns was a continuous battle with creeping
or flying things. In the dead of night Ayla and Damon would rise
and shake their bedding to dislodge a horde of black fleas. At
212
mealtime they would stare suspiciously at their food. Alexandrians
seemed to be used to a constant fight for their lives against the
multitude of tiny enemies that attacked them day and night every-
where. Was that, Damon wondered, why they were such a gay
and lighthearted people? They consumed huge quantities of wine,
and some of them lived in stuporous half-drunkenness almost from
birth to death. Their orgies they had tried to immortalize in pic-
torial art. Damon and Ayla gazed at a painting of a slave holding
a silver basin, while his mistress discharged into it the contents of
her stomach; and another of a master supported by two slaves as
he staggered homeward to find his bed. The banquets at Canopus
and Tentyra were such brawls of shrieking and vomiting that they
reminded Damon of the Romans at their worst.
After searching the city over they found an inn that seemed to
be free of pests. It was here that by chance they met Barnabas.
This Christian, known over much of the world, had written an
epistle which Damon had read and on which he wished to draw
him out. He suggested that Barnabas should take dinner with
them and eagerly he accepted, for unlike most Christians, who
abhorred inns and taverns and their wines and food, Barnabas
nourished his body as well as his soul.
He was a rugged man in early middle age. One eye, his left, was
far out of focus with the other and looked off from his skull. Most
of the time when he talked he looked directly at Damon with his
right eye and kept his left eye closed, but when emotionally stirred
he opened his left eye. Then Damon was startled, for while the
man stared at him intently with the right eye, the other eye looked
away at nobody knew what.
Damon sat across the table from him, with Ayla on Damon's
right. Damon asked Barnabas if he would take wine and he said
he would. Teasing the man Damon told him that most of the
wines known to the world could be had there: there was, for in-
stance, the light Coptos for fevers and as an aid to digestion, or
the exquisite Mareotic as a diuretic. Or possibly he would prefer
the dark yellow Teniotic, which aroused carnal passions, though
that was hardly a wine for Christians. "The thick heavy Laconia?"
Damon asked, trying to elicit his preference. "Or the wine of
Phocis? It is said to stimulate the mental faculties, especially
when mixed with the juice of the pine cone."
Barnabas turned his right eye on Ayla and winked. This inn
had, he said, the rarest vintages of Smyrna, which were decanted
213
under the very nose of the mother of the gods; it had the white
wines of Lesbos and Thasos, whose delicate flavors had so delight-
ed Apollo. He turned again to Ayla, his face expressionless, and
said, "We'll take Chian."
Damon was set back. The rogue had chosen the costliest wine
of all, a wine so celebrated that it had its own medals, with a
sphinx in a cluster of grapes on one side, on the other an amphora.
Wealthy persons drank it from an amphora, of silver or of gold,
deeming it too precious to be quaffed from baser metals. Wonder-
ing about this man, Damon ordered Chian for him and for Ayla;
but for himself he chose one, the mixing of which fascinated him.
A eunuch stood before his table, looking proud and pompous,
and pounded into a small stone mortar various intoxicating es-
sences and moistened them with the juice of an Egyptian flower.
He poured the Phocis into a chased silver amphora, added the
paste, sprinkled over it all some pungent spices, and then mixed
the contents of the bowl.
Damon meanwhile told Barnabas that he had read his epistle,
that he was thinking of writing a book about the Christians, if he
could ever determine just who they were and what they believed.
"In your book you say your Lord came to reveal a new system,
that the system of the Jews had been discarded—”
Barnabas silenced him with an impatient gesture. "This is what
I wrote: But you will say, yes, the people are circumcised for a seal.
But so is every Syrian and Arab, and all the priests of idols. Yes,
the Egyptians also. The Jews are a degenerate, lost and abandoned
people. What is their god?" asked Barnabas, fixing Damon with
his right eye.
"I've heard it all a dozen times."
"Their god is the victim of passion, error, caprice, jealousy,
hatred. The god of Abraham, what can he be but the incarnation
of the evil principle?"
He had been speaking with his left eye closed. Now the wide

wine and said:
"We're the only people in the world who have the truth."
"I've heard that a thousand times," Damon said.

I waited for a lying jade

XXI
Because winter was an unpleasant season in Judea Damon said
they would remain in Alexandria until spring, though after talk-
ing with Barnabas and other gnostic leaders he was eager to talk
with Jews. Alexandria had Christian leaders at one another's
throats, each convinced that he alone had the truth. One day
Damon heard that somewhere in the land of the Jews the story
had been written according to one called Matthew. He was eager
to read that. He could find no copy of it in Egypt nor any Chris-
tian who had seen it. To fill his time he read in science and phi-
losophy and then the day came to be off.
"To where?" asked Ayla.
"You know where. To the land of the Jews first. Then to
Caesarea, Rhodes-"
"My life, my soul!” she cried, using an expression she had picked
up in Alexandria. "Are we never to settle down and have a little
house, a garden, some permanent friends?"
"Some day."
"Some day!" she said. "But I'm getting old. Look at the gray in
my hair."
Damon was eager to find a copy of the book by Matthew; for
what, he asked himself, would the story be like if written by one
who lived in the land of the Jews? It might be written by a Jew
himself. It took him a week to find a copy and he read it while
standing, breathless, his gaze devouring the lines, his astonishment
growing. He then tried to find someone who knew the author or
had known him, or where he lived, or when this book had been
written. He was told by a Christian that Matthew had been one
of the Twelve but Damon perceived on the second reading that
220
this book had been put together by more than one person. No
man could have written Joseph's pedigree and the account of the
virgin birth; nor have Jesus attack Jewish laws and censure the
slightest departure from them. What a strange book it was!
Out in a park, with the book in his hand, he looked up at the
sky, trying to see clearly and whole the map of the phenomenon—
of all the places in the world where this new religion was develop-
ing, of all the sources on which it was feeding—the multitude of
myths, traditions, customs that were going into its making. What
a wonderful thing it was! Or was it?
Ayla said: "I don't see why it moves you so. Isn't it just a hash
of old religious cookery?"
"I see it as a tree with the trunk in Israel. The trunk was trans-
planted and now the tree is becoming immense, with a multitude
of branches; and one is Mark and one is Luke and one is Barnabas
-No," he said, breaking off, "it's not that way. One is Orphic,
one is Greek, one is Egyptian, one is Jewish- And that's not it,
for even those are all mixed up, intermingled, blended but usually
not very well blended. And another thing I see now."
"Yes, peppercorn. What is that?"
"There probably never was a Matthew. It doesn't matter. There
probably never was a Jesus hanged on Passover eve. I see now that
it doesn't matter."

"No."

"As a literal being, it doesn't. It's the myth that counts. The
things called facts are the fences we build. Truth, what is it but
our meanings housed in myths?”

putting a hand to her brow. "Are you going mad?"

Wandering around Caesarea Damon thought he now under-
stood why Christians hated the Jews. The Jews had their myth,
which had taken their poets centuries to develop. The Lord our
God, the Lord is One. There is no other God. He has no son. He
is One: transcendent, immutable, eternal: one all-encompassing
truth in an envelope of myth which Jews had perfected, taking
what they needed from other peoples. Because they had been en-
slaved again and again, because they had lost their unity as a na-
tion, they had developed out of need and yearning the belief that
a messiah, a David-king, would come to deliver them. The Chris-
tians, unable to make a god of a mortal king (though Mark had
tried!), had turned, guided by Paul and then by many, to the
222
savior-god concept of other peoples. In Alexandria a Christian
had told Damon that all of human history and all the holy books
of the Jews had been only preparation for the establishing, by God,
of the Christians above all other people. It was plain to him that
the Christians were taking ideas and values from other people
here, there, everywhere; but whether they were discarding the out-
worn and the debauched and giving to truth a new radiance he
did not know. He was sure that what was going on around him was
colossal arrogance or colossal poetry. It seemed to him that the
two were badly mixed.
Were Christians taking old, gross and degenerating materials
and refining them and then working them into a nobler myth? He
was confused, but so were the Christians everywhere. Would they
ever bring all the discrete elements into a synthesis? Would they
ever reconcile their multitude of childish contradictions? Would
they go on forever having Jesus say turn your other cheek and I
came not to bring peace but a sword? In the Matthew story he
found things as fine and tender as any in Luke, but he also found
what seemed to him to be fantastic silliness. The story said, for in-
stance, that Jesus came to life after three days and nights because
that was the period of time Jonah spent in the belly of the whale.
He found evidence in Matthew for his notion that Christians
were putting in the mouth of their Lord any moral sentiments that
pleased them. This version had Jesus say that the foxes had holes
and the birds had nests but man had no place to lay his head: these
words, uttered by Tiberius Gracchus two hundred years earlier,
had become a commonplace of speech. It had him say that who-
ever exalted himself would be humbled, whoever humbled himself
would be exalted. That was an old and familiar Jewish maxim.
As for returning good for evil, philosophers had been teaching
that doctrine for centuries-Lao Tse, Buddha, Plato, Socrates, Pit-
tacus.
In nothing, it seemed to Damon, had Christians become more
confused and ridiculous than in their fierce and foolhardy effort
to represent their Lord as a Jew. They had Jesus take a coin and
say, Whose image and superscription is this?-but at that time
there were no coins in the whole land of Israel bearing the image
of the emperor. In so many matters their indifference to historical
facts limned them in the ridicule and contempt of all enlightened
people.
