drugged, as though half-paralyzed in her senses. Again he saw
the strange wild look in her eyes. He did not mention her seizure
and her long sleep; he had hidden the book by Matthew, so that
she would not see it. Sometimes in her sleep she talked, now mut-
tering, now saying a word or two clearly, but always with intense
emotion that seemed to him close to hysteria and violence. Night
after night. . . . He forced her to eat a little. He tended her
faithfully, never leaving her; and at last from what she said in her
sleep, and when awake he put the story together, or most of it:
she had been married to a Christian, a morbid fanatical man, who
spent most of his time in prayer or in reading a few pages from
Christian writings. One night with an axe he slew his entire fam-
ily, his parents, his brothers and sisters, his two children, Ayla's
children. Ayla alone had escaped from a cottage drenched with
blood. He thought possibly he understood now the source of the
strange things in her during all these years. . .
She was tortured in her sleep but if he touched her she shud-
dered and drew away from him. Night after night. . . . The
poor lost thing! he would say to himself, looking at her still
white face, at the pathetic inertness of her hands. No wonder she
had never wanted to talk about Christians. For if it was those
words her husband had seen, there they still were and they were
incredible:
And the brother shall deliver up the brother to death, and
the father the child; and the children shall rise up against
their parents, and cause them to be put to death. . . .
227
During her convalescence he sat by a window most of the time,
day after day, staring out, asking himself over and over, What does
it all mean?. . .
228
XXII
As if out of her depths a horrible memory had been dragged that
now would not return to the depths, Ayla became a brooding and
unhappy woman. Only once again did she speak of the expe-
rience and then only briefly. Yes, it was those words; her husband
had taken them literally and had killed the whole family, all but
his wife, who fled before him in the night and hid out for a week
without food or drink. Her last picture, looking back, had been
of her children, a boy and a girl, both almost cut in two by the axe.
She told Damon that she would never speak of it again and he was
never to speak of it. Then she shocked him by saying that in
his obsessive interest in the Christians and their faith he was be-
coming more and more like her husband.
"You want me to forget about it?"
"You couldn't.”
"You don't want me to go to Jerusalem?"
"You have to."
"You don't wish to go with me?"
"You promised you'd never leave me alone."
Damon tried to turn his face away from Jerusalem and go back
to Antioch but Ayla was right, he had to go. She was right: this
thing had gone into him deep, deep into both his emotions and
mind. To avoid going overland the whole distance they took a
boat to Jamnia, which, though inland from the sea, had a harbor.
Jamnia was in a barren coastal area: Damon wondered again if
the bleak terrain where Jews had lived so long in any way ex-
plained their intense preoccupation with God and their wish to be
isolated from other peoples.
Lodgings in Jamnia were simply not to be had: the place was
229
completely occupied by devout Jews, who detested inns, taverns
and nearly all public places. Damon was able to buy food but he
and Ayla had to go beyond the village and sleep under the stars.
He had long known that Jamnia was the home of the Jewish
Academy and the seat of Jewish intellectual life. Its learned men
swathed themselves in the rabbinic gulta, a large striped rectangu-
lar cloak of wool, which covered the head as well as the body and
fell loosely from shoulders to the sandals on their feet. Blue and
white fringes trailed from its four corners. Soon after arriving
Damon saw at a distance a group of the Jewish sages and they
looked so aloof and unbending that he doubted he would ever have
the courage to approach them.
But there was one whom he had in mind, about whom in various
places he had gathered information, and would gather more here,
from the common people roundabout. He wanted to learn more
about Hyrcanus and all that he could about that man's favorite
pupil, Akiba. Damon had been told by Jews that a more arrogant
man than Hyrcanus had never walked the earth. Though born
slave-poor he had become extremely wealthy, and extremely vain
and ill-tempered, with an inordinate, it seemed an insatiable, love
of praise. Unbendingly conservative and a member of the landed
aristocracy, he had immense holdings on which he produced flax,
grapes, dates, olives and palmtrees. His disciples, Damon had been
told, thought him more precious in Israel than God's gift of rain.
Hyrcanus had no time for non-Jews nor for proselytes, whom he
suspected of lying with beasts. For him all non-Jews were heathen,
in whom he found no good. Even if one of them did an occasional
good deed it was only that he might boast of it and from no love
of goodness. Hyrcanus believed in long prayers and justified them
by saying that Moses prayed for forty days and nights without
pause. He held women in contempt and resisted all efforts to give
them more freedom. He bitterly hated Rome and the Samaritans;
of the latter he said that eating their bread was a worse sin than
eating the flesh of pigs. He believed that man's moral nature
could be improved only by study of the Torah, the Jewish books
about Moses and his time; but to this study no woman was ad-
mitted. He thought that refusal to beget children was a worse
sin than murder.
That was Hyrcanus, as Damon's notes revealed him. He knew
that he would never dare to approach this man.
Akiba ben Joseph was a different sort. In the depth of his mind,
230
the subtlety of his imagination and the kindliness of his heart he
was, Elisha had said, the greatest Jew Damon would ever meet.
At this time he was about fifty-five years old. Elisha said he had
been born in the most wretched poverty and for forty years had
lived on little more than barley bread, cabbage and turnips.
When a child, at harvest time he had gathered sand and pretended
that it was his tithe to the priest, though whether from childlike
eagerness or from contempt Damon did not know. In his early
manhood he had hated the Pharisaic and other priestly orders
with such venomous bitterness that he had said: "Would that I
had a scholar in my hands, I would bite him like an ass!" As an
ass would bite, Elisha had explained. This bitterness had lodged
in his soul because of the contempt which Jewish scholars had for
the common people. According to one of these scholars a Jew
had to have a capital of ten mina or five times the dower of a virgin
before priestly rules allowed him to eat cooked vegetables; twenty
mina before he was allowed to eat fish; fifty before he was allowed
to eat meat once a week; and one hundred mina before he was al-
lowed to eat meat daily. Maybe, Ayla said, it was such things that
impelled the philosophers to ask people to give all their property
away.
Akiba got no schooling at all until long after he was grown. He
was not converted, that is, reconciled, to the way of life of devout
Jews until he was forty. He then pitched in with astonishing zeal
to become a learned rab, and got by heart the entire book called
Leviticus, as well as many other parts of the Jewish holy writings.
He who had scorned learning and the wise man had come to be-
lieve that as much reverence ought to be paid to a Jewish scholar
as to God himself. Some of his disciples were saying that the truths
which the Holy One had withheld from Adam and Moses were
now being revealed to Akiba. Elisha had told Damon that the
man had become so eager to learn that he had followed a sage
named Joshua into a privy, and there learned, he afterward re-
ported, three good habits. "I didn't inquire,” said Elisha drily,
"into the nature of them."
Akiba disdained the unsanitary habit of passing the wine-cup
from mouth to mouth. He scorned those who made a spectacle
of themselves when praying. You might leave a man in a corner,
he said, and find him in another, after he had wandered through
the multitude of his kneelings and prostrations. He thought no
man served a worthy purpose in life who did not study the Torah
231
day and night. Worship was an expression of love; study was the
highest form of worship. Study of the Torah was his hobby, pas-
time, sport, theater, profession and trade. He said, as some learned
rabs had said before him, that God himself faithfully studied the
Torah and hugely enjoyed it.
Akiba spent a part of his time settling disputed matters. If a
human corpse was found midway between two cities it had to be
taken to the city to which it was nearer; but from what part of
the body was the measurement to be taken? From the navel, one
sage had said; but Akiba said from the face, for that was the image
of God. He thought the messiah would restore Israel to glory
in a natural manner and that the messianic era was close at hand.
But on the duration of the days of the messiah he argued with his
master: Hyrcanus said it would cover three generations; Akiba,
that it would cover exactly forty years. In that day when the son
of David came the House of the Assembly would be turned into a
brothel, Galilee would be devastated, the wisdom of the wise
would turn to decay, and the face of a man would look like the face
of a dog.
Damon also learned that Akiba's love for his wife Rachel and
his devotion to her had set him apart with the Plutarchs among
men. Though he was away from her much of the time, the better
to give his mind wholly to his studies, he was reported to have
said over and over that all that he was he owed to her patience and
goodness. He thought it against the will of God to set slaves free
but unlike Hyrcanus he was kind to them and to the poor. He
taught that the three cardinal principles of human experience were
love of God, the sanctity of human life and the purity of the home.
During the days when he was gathering information about him
Damon saw him now and then at a little distance and thought that
in appearance he was an extraordinary man. His huge skull in
the one moment when Damon glimpsed it seemed to be completely
bald. His frame, larger than that of most men, was rugged and
powerful, and Elisha had said that his hands were enormous. His
eyes, under heavy shaggy gray brows, were large and set far apart,
and unlike those of Hyrcanus, which were piercing and suspicious,
they could be luminous with kindliness and good will. His beard
was long and very heavy and streaked with gray.
Having gathered all the information about him that he could,
Damon lay with Ayla under the stars and said: "I'm afraid to talk
to him. I'll stutter or be tongue-tied."
232
"Do you think he's so wise?"
"Formidable. So sure he has the truth. The most terrible thing
on earth is a man convinced that he has the truth."
"Please don't ever become convinced of it."
"I'll force myself to see him tomorrow."
"Will he have things to tell you that you don't know?"
"I'm sure he will."
Damon sent a messenger to ask if it would be all right for Ayla
to be present. Akiba said it would. But when the hour came for
the first of the meetings Damon found himself trembling, and his
voice trembled when the three of them sat under an old tree and
Akiba fixed him with his large eyes. Those eyes were so steady and
probing, the face so stern, the whole body so motionless that Da-
mon found it almost impossible to speak. But he went desperately
ahead to tell of his many years in libraries, his discussions with
many persons, his travels, his knowledge of different Christian
sects, his wish to find the truth and set it down in a book. When he
had concluded, Akiba astonished him. Suddenly the man's face,
his manner, his whole being softened and warmed, and he moved
his bench toward Damon until their knees almost touched and he
began to talk with both hands and his tongue and his eyes and his
whole face. Damon had on his lap the book by Matthew. When
he tried to interrupt for a question-
Yes, yes, Matthew's pedigree was only an imitation of the genea-
logical tables in a book called Chronicles. It was intended to be
edifying.
Yes, yes indeed, the story of the Magi was merely a homiletical
illustration of a verse in the book called Numbers-and there
shall come a star out of Jacob, quite as, This is my beloved son in
whom I am well pleased was a paraphrase of words in a psalm and
a verse in Isaiah. No, of course beasts for sacrifice were not sold
in the temple! No, of course the stalls of the money-changers were
not in the temple! The light that shone on Jesus? Had it not
shone on all the great teachers, including Hyrcanus, whose face
when he was expounding the Torah gave off rays of light like those
from the face of Moses?
Akiba was so eager, so boyish almost; he so pressed forward with
his knees and gestured under Damon's nose with such huge hands
that Damon, embarrassed, tried to draw away. How seldom it was,
Akiba said, that those who wished to learn about these things came
to learned Jews, for whom the meanings were as simple as their
233
own names! Would he answer all the questions Damon had to
ask? Indeed he would.
Damon sent Ayla away to fill their skin with water and while
she was gone he asked Akiba about those dreadful words in Mat-
thew. Akiba's face, or the unbearded part of it, awoke in a great
smile of light. How simple it was! The passage was a slight para-
phrase of certain words in a little Jewish book called the Sotah;
and from this book Akiba recited, “In the time of the messiah the
people will be impudent and given to drinking; the wisdom of
the scribes will be stinking. The young men will shame the old,
the old will rise against the young; the son will despise the father;
the daughter will rise against the mother, the daughterinlaw
against the motherinlaw, and a man's foes shall be they of his
own household. You see," said Akiba, so formidably dynamic, his
eyes glowing, "you see that some of the words were not changed at
all."
"Yes," said Damon, looking for a moment into Akiba's eyes.
Akiba touched the book by Matthew and said, “This was writ-
ten by Jews."
"Jews? Then that would explain some things in it.”
"Yes."
"The hatred of Jews?"
"That also."
Damon looked down at a few notes which he was turning over
and over. He said: "What I'd like to know most is this, is there
anything new in this faith?"
"Of the things they have Jesus say I know of nothing new. Shall
I blanch a man's face?" With a swift gesture he added: “That is
our way of speaking: it means, Shall I point out to you the follies
of these people? A moment ago you asked, Is the messiah the suf-
fering servant? No! They took that wrong notion from Isaiah—
the one despised and rejected of men, a man of sorrows and ac-
quainted with grief. Oh, but Isaiah meant Israel, not the messiah!
Our prophets have often spoken of Israel as a man, sometimes as
a suffering servant, meaning the one, all of us, on whom rests the
burden of being priests and exemplars to all men. Do you see, do
you see?"
"Yes. But I've known that they misunderstand your writings."
"Or misinterpret. I shall give you instances, but how can we
tell when they misunderstand, when to suit their purpose they mis-
interpret? The book called Matthew there speaks of an ass and
234
the foal of an ass. They took that from Zechariah, who wrote, an
ass, even upon a colt the foal of an ass. Did they misunderstand
our word even to mean and?"
"What man can say? In books called Mark and Luke only a colt
is mentioned. Why did Matthew make it two?"
"Possibly because he misunderstood our word. Or had he in
mind the god Bacchus riding on two asses? Surely,” said Akiba a
little reproachfully, "you know the Greek sign of the constellation
cancer is an ass and its foal.”
"Of course!" cried Damon, staring at Akiba. "Why didn't I ever
think of it?"
Akiba was delighted. "There is so much to think of." He
wagged a finger at Damon. He had an attentive and apt pupil, he
loved to instruct. "It says there," Akiba went on, indicating the
book, "it says, And she shall bring forth a son and you shall call
his name Jesus. This was done, it tells us, that it might be ful-
filled which was spoken of the Lord by the prophet, saying, Behold,
a virgin shall be with child and shall bring forth a son, and they
shall call his name Emmanuel, which being interpreted is, God
with us. So it is written there," said Akiba, his eyes glowing with
the light in his mind. "These Christians, you see, are trying to
prove their case with our ancient prophets."
"I understand that."
"Very well. But what does it say in Isaiah? It says, Therefore
the Lord himself shall give you a sign: behold, the young woman
shall conceive and bear a son and shall call his name Emmanuel.
Our word almah does not mean virgin, never, under any possible
interpretation. It means simply a girl, a young woman. The
word used in that book is parthenos, which does mean virgin.
But our word for virgin is bethulah, not almah. If we point these
things out to Christians will they listen?"
"They don't dare to," Damon said. "They made the fantastic
mistake of calling their savior-god a Jew. All savior-gods are born
of virgins. So now they have to prove by your writings that Jesus
was virgin-born."
"You have said it!" Akiba cried and his face turned cold. "Oh,
let them write as they please. We do not care. We do care, though,
when they distort and corrupt our writings, and then hate us be-
cause we won't accept the distortions."
"Nobody can blame you for that." Damon looked round to see
if Ayla was coming. He was feeling at ease.
235
"But if we protest, what do they say to us? They say our Holy
One, blessed be he, has rejected us and we are headed for hell!
Have you heard it?"
"Oh, many times."
"We are headed for hell because we won't say that almah means
virgin?"
The eyes twinkled. Damon met the twinkle with a smile. "Sir,
do you think there was actually a Jesus?"
"Oh, that is possible. Many have made the claim, if indeed this
man made it. The Romans have crucified quite a number of them
and among them there could have been one or several called
Jesus. But of course the Christian story of his death is com-
pletely false to our customs."
Damon was taking notes and wishing that Ayla would come.
"Tell me about that," he said.
The sternness, the coldness left Akiba's face. He was again
warm, eager, almost terrifyingly alive. "Very well. If you wish
to make notes I'll try not to talk so fast. Now-what they call their
last supper is a travesty, a blasphemous travesty on our Passover
meal. No Jew, not even an ignorant Jew in Galilee, could ever
have said some of the things they have Jesus say. They are super-
imposing heathen customs on ours."
Writing, Damon paused to ask: "But aren't some things they
put in his mouth Jewish?"
"Oh, yes. Some are from Hillel, from the prophets, the psalms.
Some are old apothegms. These Christians," he said, with a quick
smile, “are impartial: they take from us or from other people, as
suits their purpose." With a swift impulsive gesture toward Da-
mon, warm and ingenuous and without guile, he went on: "You're
going to write a book about these people. You'll need to know
many things, if your readers are to understand what is taking
place."
Damon looked into the large glowing eyes and nodded.
Like a schoolmaster, which indeed he was, Akiba said: "You
will now take notes." He looked at Damon's hands and writing
materials and waited a moment. "It is written, The scepter shall
not depart from Judah, nor the ruler's staff from between his feet,
as long as men come to Shiloh. Christians say with what ig-
norance or guile you can imagine—that Shiloh means their Christ.
It is written, There shall step forth a star out of Jacob, and a scepter
shall rise out of Israel, and shall strike through the corners of
236
Moab and break down all the sons of Seth. Again, this is their
Christ!" He was speaking slowly, with pauses, and Damon was
making notes. "It is written, A prophet will your God raise up
unto you, from the midst of you, of your brethren, like unto me;
unto him you shall hearken.”
Damon glanced up to say, "Their Christ, of course.”
"Yes. King David sang a hymn to Israel and his God; but for
Christians he was singing to the forthcoming Christ. When Solo-
mon spoke of an eternal celestial reign, he meant, they tell us, the
reign of their Christ. If in a psalm the word ascension appears, lo,
it foretold their risen Lord. If some Israelite was rejected by the
elders, what can it mean but the rejection of Jesus by the Pharisees?
And so on: I can't tell you in how many instances they distort or
misread our writings.
"In our twenty-fourth psalm it is God who enters our temple,
not Jesus. In our forty-second psalm it is written, Yea, mine own
familiar friend, in whom I trusted, who did eat of my bread, has
lifted up his heel against me. What can that mean but Judas!
Now the idea of the faithless trusted servant runs through many of
our books and is to be found in the books of many people."
"That is true," Damon said, writing.
"Shall I go on?"
"Oh yes, if you will, please.'
The warmth left Akiba's face and he seemed to be brooding. To
encourage him to speak again Damon said softly:
"In this Matthew book they have Jesus say, This that is written
must yet be accomplished in me. It is plain that they are deter-
mined to have Jesus fulfill everything that they think was a
prophecy."
"If Jesus does that, then of what use is Israel?"
"Of no use, they say. And are you aware that they don't all in-
terpret or misinterpret alike? They already have many contradic-
tions. For instance, they first said Jesus was a Galilean born in a
place called Nazareth. Then someone read Micah and decided
that he would have to be born in Bethlehem. It is interesting to
see how they got him up there. One has the parents fleeing from
the wrath of Herod. Another-"
"Yes," Akiba said, raising a huge hand to interrupt him. "There
will, of course, be many contradictions: Christians unknown to
one another living in different places are busy with our books.
Well, now to go on. This Matthew story has a flight into Egypt,
237
a clumsy attempt to fulfill Hosea, who had in mind Israel, not
the messiah. Matthew has Jesus applauded by childern after he
is said to have driven the money-changers out of the temple. He
got the idea, I think, from our eighth psalm where it is written,
Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings you have called forth
praise. Do you find it very childlike?"
"Very. But in their way aren't they poets?"
"Poets? They may be. But what kind of poetry is this of the be-
trayal? In this book you have a Jew take thirty pieces of silver,
then go to the chief priests and the elders to tell them he has
sinned. He flings the money to the temple floor and goes off to
hang himself. The priests decide that this money, being the
price of blood, cannot go into the treasury, and so buy with it a
plot of ground for the burial of strangers. Matthew reveals his
source for this when he says, Thus was fulfilled the prophecy of
Jeremiah.
“We shall never know what Jesus said or did. For Christians
that doesn't matter. They will invent. They will fulfill."
"Isn't that the greatness of the story, that they don't know what
he said? For now they can have him speak the words of the wise
from all lands and people."
Akiba stood up. Startled, perplexed, almost dismayed, Damon
arose, wondering if he had offended him. Akiba then turned away,
saying that they would meet again, and Damon stared at his broad
back and looked round him for Ayla. I offended him, he was think-
ing: what did I say?
But the next day Akiba sent word that he would see him again.
When Damon came to the bench under the tree he found the wise
man thoughtfully stroking his beard.
Akiba looked up at him and said: "Do you wish me to say more?”
"Yes sir, if you will."
Akiba waited until Damon sat and took up his writing mate-
rials. Then: "It is said that on the cross he was offered vinegar to
drink. It was the custom with certain women in Jerusalem to
offer a pain-killing drink to any condemned man on a cross. But
what does your author there do? He turns to the words in one of
our psalms where it is written, They made me eat gall. So, sub-
stituting bitter gall for the merciful opiate, he fulfills another
prophecy."
Damon looked up to say: "Do you think Jesus was taken before
King Herod?"
238
"I think it very improbable. Perhaps they took the idea from
our psalm where it is written, The kings of the earth and the
great ones have assembled together against the Lord-will they
never understand that Lord in our holy writings always means
God? They have Pilate intercede. In Isaiah it is written, He inter-
ceded for the guilty. When Jesus gives up the ghost they have him
say, Father, into thy hands I commend my spirit. Those words
are from one of our psalms.
"Now in regard to this childlike fulfilling of prophecies," Akiba
said, his face darkening, "you must understand, in this book you
write, that they were not intended to be prophecies at all. Not in
the sense of foretelling the future. Our prophets have never been
prophets in that sense. And in any case, it is fulfillment a pos-
teriori, as the Romans would say."
Akiba was silent. Damon looked up and waited.
Akiba said: “Do you seek still more evidence?"
"I don't wish to weary you. I suppose you could talk a long time
about these things."
"I'll give you a few more instances. In the book you have there
Jesus cures the sick. That, they tell us, fulfills Isaiah where it is
written, He took upon himself our infirmities and bore the burden
of our diseases. They have him cast out demons, again fulfilling
Isaiah. This author bases John the baptizer on Isaiah where it is
written, The voice of him that cries in the wilderness, prepare the
way of the Lord. But the voice is Israel's. They have soldiers cast
lots for his garments, for in a psalm it is written, They part my
garments among them, and for my vesture they cast lots. They
have his hands and feet pierced, for in a psalm it is written, They
pierced my hands and feet. They say there was desolation over the
earth, for in Amos it is written, And the high places shall be deso-
late, and the sanctuaries of Israel shall be laid waste. They call
him a Nazarene, for in our book called Judges it is written, For,
lo, you shall conceive and bear a son; and no razor shall come on
his head; for the child shall be a Nazarite unto God from the
womb.
"What we resent most," Akiba said, “is their telling all people
that our Father has rejected us and trying to prove it by our own
books. They turn again to Isaiah, I am sought of them that asked
not for me; I am found of them that sought me not. Do I have to
tell you that our Father there is speaking to Israel?"
"No, I understand that."
239
"Very well, now I have spoken to you these things. What do you
say?"
Damon looked up and met his eyes. "I say that you have made
this part of their story very plain.”
"Of the way they misread our books what do you say?”
"Sir, I don't know if it is deliberate and therefore contemptible,
or only childlike."
"What do you say further?"
Damon's face reddened. He was ill at ease: what did the man

will make a great poem out of all this."
"In your book will you say that they have blasphemed our Holy

"I will say it."
Akiba moved forward and touched Damon lightly. "If they

"Do you think they will become mighty?"
“What man can say? They were few when I was a child, now
they are many. Having become many they are now trying to per-

"I know that some of them are. But isn't it true that many Jews

"If that is so, which of us has more justice? We don't care what

us we don't understand our own books. They take the heathen
savior-god and call him the son of our Father. They say our
Father has abandoned us. Do you understand that we are fighting
only to keep what is ours?"
"Yes sir, I will say that.”
"If you would find all the truth in these things you will need a
long life."
He stood up and Damon stood with him. Damon looked into
his eyes and said, "Sir, I am more grateful than I can ever tell you.
If some day I need to have other matters explained may I come
again?"
"You may. Take the blessings of our Father with you."
240
Akiba bowed slightly and Damon bowed. Akiba turned away
and Damon looked at him as long as he was in sight, feeling, as

XXIII
Because Jerusalem was only about thirty miles distant Damon said
they would walk. They went toward the rising sun over hills where
they saw only stone and thistle, and now and then the lonely shep-
herd. They took food with them and a skin of water but the heat
was dreadful and Damon wondered on their second day if they
would die of thirst. He had thought there would be springs burst-
ing from some of the hills but they found only filthy mudholes
where sheep had drunk.
Weary, almost exhausted, they sank to the earth, their mouths
and throats dry, their lips cracking. They tried to eat but found
it impossible to choke anything down. They were barely able to
speak. Ayla stared round her at the desolate land, hating it; and
Damon looked at it too, wondering about it and its people. When
dusk came they lay on their backs with hands clasped and after a
while they gazed at the glory of the sky; in the dry atmosphere the
stars were numerous and brilliant. Now and then Damon squeezed
her hand and waited to have the pressure returned. When at last
it was not returned Damon knew that she slept and for hours he
looked at the sky and thought about Jews. Why for so many cen-
turies had they chosen to live in this area and why were they so
devoted to it?
They awoke at daybreak and heard a bird singing. Ayla sat
up and tried to laugh. Her effort to laugh choked and convulsed
her and she bent forward, coughing. When she was done with
coughing she massaged her throat and turned on Damon eyes
filled with tears.
"God!" she whispered. "How much farther?”
"I don't know."
242
"We'll die in these awful hills."
"A lot of people have.”
They still walked into the east, watching the marvelous trans-
formations as the upper light came down and diluted the dusk;
and the delicate yellow that slowly colored the haze; and the round
golden sun when at last it floated above a hilltop. They scanned
the ravines to right and left for signs of water and walked up and
down, over hill and hill. Damon knew that Jerusalem was east
and a little south of Jamnia but he was afraid that they might be
lost. He admired Ayla's pluck; she set the pace, leading, and he
followed. Now and then she would stop to point, moving her ex-
tended arm slowly to right or left until Damon, looking up at the
sky and all around, trying to determine the directions, said,
"There." After sighting along her arm at a distant object to fix
the course she would walk again. All around them was the land of
Israel: why, Damon wondered, had Moses led his people to such a
God-forsaken waste?
It was about noon when Ayla began to run. Still, she did not
run but went forward in an ungainly tottering manner, and Da-
mon staggered after her, suspecting that she had seen water. They
came to it at last, a tiny spring at the foot of a hill, where beasts
had watered, leaving an unsightly mudhole. They threw them-
selves down. They formed a small pool and to clear it of floating
sediment Ayla dipped with her hands. She put her lips to the water
to taste it and then drew back, whispering, "You first."
Damon began to drink and at once she seized his hair to pull
him away. He would be sick, she whispered at him.
The water was warm and brackish but Damon thought it tasted
better than Chian wine. After drinking a little and studying their
inner sensations and drinking again they took dried friut and fish
and ate. It was plain, said Ayla, why Jews worshiped God as a
rain-giver. They never would have, in the well-watered area of
Damascus. Here under the hot and destroying sun rain was the
most eternal of all truths.
"And that's about all it is," she said, chewing a fig.
"People are what their religion makes them."
"Country makes the religion. You'd be proud and lonely too
if you lived in this land.”
"Fat and jolly where land is rich?"
Looking round her Ayla said: “How could they have a divine
mother here? The Mother is in the valleys of vision where fruits
243
hang thick and luscious. The Father is the lonely shepherd on the
dry hills."
Damon was staring at her and sucking at dry fish bones. "Won-
derful words," he said. "Go on talking.'
""
"That's all. When fat and prosperous the world turns to the
Mother. When it starves and thirsts it loves the Father more."
"You said it better the first time." He looked at her fair face,
coated with dust; at her lips pursing like a flower round the pit
of a date; and then at her eyes, all the while wondering about her.
The woman he lived with, it seemed to him, was still a stranger.
After eating and again lying on their bellies to drink they
climbed the hill and went down and climbed another hill, and
there before them was Jerusalem. Or what was left of it. The
sight of it came to Damon with a sense of shock and grief. It was
the ugly sprawling ruins on two hills that Titus and the Roman
legions had left. They stood arm to waist looking at it.
"Six hundred thousand Jews were killed, Josephus says. Tens
of thousands were sold as slaves."
"Men did it, peppercorn, not women. Where was the temple?”
"On the eastern hill."
They crossed the vale and climbed the eastern hill and found
the ruins of the temple, blackened stones in demolished walls.
Trying to look round him without drawing Ayla's attention he
wondered where Jesus had walked, where he had prayed, where
Gethsemane was. He had told himself that Gethsemane was only
a part of the myth, the poem, but now found himself believing in
its actual earth, as a great emotion began to well up in him.
An elderly man came along and Ayla spoke to him in the lan-
guage of Jews. After a few moments she said to Damon: "He has
never heard of people called Christians."
"No? Of Nazarenes?"
Ayla and the man talked again. "Long ago, but he says there
are none now."
How the whole thing slipped away into the mists! "Has he ever
heard of Jesus the Nazarene who was hanged on a tree?”
"He says there was once a Jesus ben Pandira who was stoned
and hanged. That was long ago. .. Wait. He says Mark Antony
scourged and hanged someone named Antigonus."
"Ask who he was."
"A man who called himself the king of the Jews."
244
"Don't let him go yet. Find out when this other Jesus was

"He says long ago. Long ago, peppercorn.'
Long ago! "How long?"
"He says a hundred, two hundred years. Wait.
He says he
heard of another Jesus, Jesus bar Abbas. That means son of the
father."
Son of the father! King of the Jews! "How it all comes out of
the mists of the past, yet still eludes us! How long ago was Jesus
bar Abbas?"
After talking a few moments Ayla said, "He doesn't remember.
Long ago. It has been a long time, he says, since a false messiah
was stoned and hanged."
"Hold him! Ask if he ever heard of a Jesus who had twelve
disciples and performed great wonders and aroused the hate of

scourged and crucified."
Ayla and the man talked. "He says no. He has never heard
of that one."
"Of that one! Well, give him this coin and ask where we can
fill our pouch."
After they had filled their pouch they went outside the broken
walls and looked into the pale distant haze of Samaria, of Moab.
The terrain on all sides of Jerusalem was stony and barren and
desolate. Why in the name of their Father had they built their
holy city, the navel of the world, in such a spot!
Beyond the north wall were two roads, one going down to Jer-
icho, the other into the northwest and to Joppa. Damon had
heard that along both roads bandits lurked who would cut a man's
throat for the smallest coin. He sat with Ayla on dusty stones and
considered what they should do. He forced himself to think of
this, to keep his grief down. He was stunned to realize, now that
he was in the holy city of the Jews, that he had not yet accepted
it as myth and poem, but was looking for flesh and father.
"How far to Joppa?" she asked.
"Forty miles or fifty."
"Well, here we are," she said with a little unhappy laugh.
"Dusty and dirty and sad.”
"Yes," he said, staring round him.
"Do
you think now there was a Jesus?"
He wished she had not asked the question. It came too close
245
to his heart. "I don't know," he said trying to keep all emotion
out of his voice. "I ask myself, did he once stand where we are
now? Where did he go off alone to pray?"
"This earth has all been prayed over, Damon. Think how many

“Yes. So much of the earth is hallowed and we so seldom re-

close. "I've lived with him a long time. Even if there was never
such a man, even if he never said a word that they have him say,
there is such a man for me now."
"Maybe that's the poem."
"Maybe. Or maybe that's only the grief. Maybe it all started
from nothing but human yearning but here it is now in the hearts
of thousands of people." He glanced at her face. "I must talk.
I'm trying to understand something. I can believe now that there

there on a cross, tortured, feeling forsaken, crying out to God.
Yet Paul was right."
"If there was no such man there should have been? Is that

"Paul would say no. The great masses of humanity would say

"I don't think women understand it."
"Oh, surely women can understand this sense of presence. You

symbol in a poem were to have a fence built around it-if it were
to be only a spot of ground in Jerusalem, instead of all the spots

here, what happened here; but men will go on with the story until
at last they say, There the cross stood, there he prayed, there Peter
denied him, there the soldiers cast lots for his garments, there he

Ayla looked hard a moment at her man's face and then again at

Damon stood up and looked back at the ruins, over at the west-

246
of it actually happened. It's so hard to go along with Paul's vision,

"For things in a man that a woman doesn't understand very well.

"Maybe."
"But the scholar—?”
"What about him?" he said, looking at her.
"Which one in you is going to come out on top, the scholar or

"Which should?”
"Don't ask a woman.”
"Well, the scholar says with a shrug, he was never here; the

"Aren't you being pretty sentimental? Whether he should have

"I suppose I wouldn't.”
"Just what are you looking for anyway? Can you tell me? For

"They've been driven to it.”
"Oh, don't be so silly! They were hating before Christians came

"I don't know."
"None of the philosophers found one?"
"Oh, the philosophers! They hate one another too."
Ayla reached into her pouch to take out a fig. She munched
the fig and said, "Well, don't expect too much from the Christians.
They talk all the time about love but they may become worse
killers than the Romans. People who talk so much about love

247
"Maybe."
"I was that way. When I met you I hated all men. I learned to

love until God is a woman."
Damon had been looking at her. He was thinking again that this
woman was a stranger.
She gave him a little smile and said, "Don't let your vanity be
wounded. You men, you know, have created God in your image,
and it is you men who have war and torture and slaves and the
smell of blood in all the arenas from Rome to Babylon. Men
think their God finds such things good. Even the Jews. But how
can there be love until the world understands Diana?"
"You may be right," he said.
"You know what I think is the trouble with men?"
"I know it isn't flattering."
"You get lost in ideas. The other day I was reading some of
your notes. The Stoics say personality is single, Plato that it is
dual. What does it matter? I have heard you say that the logos is
the creative and shaping principle but you haven't a ghost of an
idea what the logos is. I know that I love you and that means some-

"And more to me," he said.
"Think of that idiot out in the desert, buried to his chin. Is

"What?"
"He talked to us about love but never once used the word."
She brought forth a piece of fish and blew the dust off it. She
nibbled at it and looked at Damon, saying at last, "You feel
awfully lonely here. Why?"
He gestured round him. "Good God, it's a lonely place."
"No place is lonely except to the lonely person."
248
"Well, maybe that's what I am."
"And maybe that's why Jesus means something very special to

"He means loneliness?"
"To you. Salvation to the numskull. But women, we never

don't feel," she said, looking round at the ruins and emptiness. "I

here. There have been many gods but only God in a place like
this. Only a place like this could have made the Jews and only
the Jews could have made God."
"I've been thinking that,” he said.
"Diana for me," she said, eating fish, "is just the earth in all its
beauty and fruitfulness. She makes me feel brimming and full,
like a tree bent under its fruit, like Artemis with her thousand
breasts. She'll mean so much when you let me have a child. But
you can't, I suppose, as long as you are a lonely son hunting for his
Jesus-father."
When darkness came they moved back to the wall to find a
sheltered spot for the night. With loose stones Damon built low
walls, so that they would be concealed when they slept; and after
he had drunk and eaten he went away from the walls and stood
looking round him. Ayla went out and stood by him.
“I want to walk around," he said.
"Alone?"
"Yes, I think so."
"I'll wait here. Don't be too long."
"I won't," he said.
He went down the hill to the ruins of the temple, thinking of
the books written by Jews—of Ruth and Job and Isaiah and Ec-
clesiastes; he was trying to realize the simple historic fact that
Jews had lived in this place for centuries, had produced here
their great poets, their concept of God, their wish to be a people
set apart. A little later he looked across at a hill called the Mount
of Olives and his mind turned to the books that had been written
about Jesus. Because he felt so lonely and empty and lost, so
feeble and ineffectual, so wasted and cast off by his travels and
searching and the passing of time, and so close to tears, he began
to speak in a low voice. .
"Then Judas went to the chief priests and said, What will you
249
give me to deliver him to you? It never happened. It is a parable
but what does it mean. If the betrayal story is the heart of the
Jesus story, then betrayal of what?
is
"They sat and ate a meal somewhere around here in an upstairs
room. Jesus broke bread and gave it to them saying, This is my
body. He said nothing of the kind. He poured wine and said, This
my blood. No Jew ever said that. Yearning to conquer death,
many people eat and drink their gods, trying to make their essence,
their immortality, a part of themselves. But how absurd to put
that here in Judea!
"After I am risen again I will go before you into Galilee. A
symbol of what? Before the cock crows three times you will deny
me: Peter is the rock and the rock is the womb: who denies whom
and why? Peter strikes off a man's ear. Jesus tells him to put up
the sword. Women hang things from their ears because, a phi-
losopher has said, the ear is a female genital symbol. The ear and
the sword. Peter, the womb, cuts off the genital. What does it
mean?
"They took Jesus and they spit in his face and they smote him;
and three times Peter the womb denied that he had ever known
him. Male and female symbols. They brought Jesus to trial and
the people clamored for his life and he was delivered to them and
they spit on him and mocked him and scourged him. Another
parable, but what? Now the Christians are trying to make all these
things literal. Poets create a poem and theologians reduce it to
dogma.
""
Was that it? he wondered, looking round him, his eyes misted
over. Christian poets, taking their materials from all peoples, were
creating a poem-myth. Literal dullards like Ignatius were turning
it into prose. After it had all been debased to the level of the
common understanding it would then be only another superstition.
He was standing on the eastern hill in the night, with the ruins
of the temple around him, tears gathering to his lashes. He
thought, If Jesus was only an illiterate fanatic then something
has happened, for here I am, looking for him! He wanted to choose
a place where he could imagine Jesus had stood, and throw him-
self down with his lips to the earth and weep his heart out. But
he only went up the hill, slowly, toward Ayla; thinking, He was
never here, not in the flesh; yet he is here and will always be here
now. They say that the Jews missed him. They are missing him
too, as they missed both Job and Hosea.

XXIV
After a night under the walls and the ruins they rode with a cara-
van to Joppa, with Damon looking back and Ayla looking at
Damon. From Joppa they went up the coast to Caesarea and
lingered there until autumn, while Damon, impatient with his
literalness, searched for a man named Matthew.
There was no Matthew. They arrived at Antioch before the
chill of winter and Damon looked round for a house. He knew
that his weary woman wanted to journey no more: she had tried
to share his vagaries and fancies and illusions, now she wanted to
settle down and have a child. Damon found a handsome cottage
and furnished it and settled her in it, and then went forth to look
for his old friends. He had not been in Antioch for more than
fifteen years.
He had gray hair now and deep lines but he expected to find
his friends as he had left them. Luke and Levilla were much the
same. Levilla's body had thickened, her movements were slower,
but there was little sign of age in her tranquil face.
"You don't look a day older," Damon said.
"You do," she said. "Have you been wicked?”
"Oh yes. Is the fatted calf ready? And how is Murdia?"
"Murdia belongs to our Lord. You leave her alone." She
came closer, searching his face. "Have you married?"
"Of course."
"A Christian? Are you going to let me meet her?"
"If you wish. We could have you and Luke in for dinner.”
Looking into his emotions Damon told himself that for him
Levilla was dead; seeing how fat and thick she was he understood
how dull she had always been. He wondered about Murdia.
251
Days passed before he saw her and then he was shocked and in-
credulous. Most of the city's rabble lived in a kind of park, where
they slept on the earth under trees and ate what they could find.
It was a filthy place, aswarm with the diseased and the old, with
beggars, orphans--and there among these people he found Murdia,
looking unclean, ragged, undernourished, her hair in snarls, her
hands roughened. He might not have known her but for her
eyes. They were the same lovely eyes with a hint of the old mis-
chief.
"Murdia?" he said softly, looking at her. At the moment she
was putting a forearm to her brow. At her feet was a wretched
thing, barely recognizable as human.
She came up to Damon. "Is it you?"
"Yes."
She looked at his eyes. Then her gaze moved over his face, gentle
with wonder, tender with memory. She smiled a little. Her voice
was low when she said, "It has been a long time."
"Murdia, what are you doing here?"
"Here? Where do you think I should be?" She turned slowly,
looking at the people and at last at the poor old thing that had
been at her feet. "Our Lord, Damon, commanded us to help
one another. Are you helping?”
With shock growing in him Damon thought, This is not the
woman I used to know. This woman was behaving strangely, in
a quiet perplexed way, or in the way of one who was weary. Her
gaze again moved over his face, questioning his eyes, studying his
mouth, in a sort of detached way, as if only a part of her were aware
of him, as if most of her had gone into these people to whom
she ministered. The pain in Damon became more and more
acute, until at a loss for words and with something hot behind his
eyes and in his brain he drew forth money and offered it to her.
Like a child she accepted it. Again she gave him a little smile that
awoke in him poignant memories.
"What are you, Murdia?" he asked gently. "A kind of mother
to all these people?"
"I just help them," she said. "Our Lord asked us to." She
seemed to have forgotten the money. Now, becoming aware of it
in her hand, she secreted it in her garment and went to the pros-
trate creature and knelt.
Curious, Damon went over and looked down. He saw a little
old withered man, almost naked, little more than a skeleton; un-
252
speakably filthy, loathsome with disease. He saw kneeling by the
creature a woman, a Christian, the Murdia who had belonged to
one of the noblest and wealthiest of Roman families-kneeling
here in the dirt, unclean and ragged herself; trying with a probe to
dislodge parasitic insects that had crawled into infected sores. He
recalled her saying to him many years ago, "Do you expect a wo-
man to feel ardor for you with all that hair sticking out of your
nose?" He recalled her saying, "How can I trust that perfidious
place where the seed of my trouble was sown?" And now she knelt
in the dirt here, her arm around a ghastly creature that was dying.
Once she had said, "Damon darling, you are awfully dull." Now
she said, "Could you take the pouch over there and bring me water
from the creek?" He hastened away and returned, and she tried
to pour water down the scrawny throat. Gently, very gently, she
lowered the little old man to the earth and, rising, said, "He will
soon be with our Lord."
Damon wanted to get away. The stench and the sight of the
people had made him ill. He could not look at Murdia while
thinking of the beautiful and dazzling woman she had once been.
O God, what a thing life was! He had to go and was afraid he
would offend her; but when he turned away, saying only, "Good-
bye" and expecting her to protest she did not protest at all, she
only looked at him in her sad quiet detached way and said, “Good-
bye." And then he was not able to go. Then he felt his blood hot
and angry that life could be so brutal, that a person once so lovely
had come to this, that memories so poignant could still rise to
choke his heart and throat. He had loved her but could not love
this, nor remain with it nor leave it, nor know what to do about it.
"I'll come again,” he said.
There it was, the faint smile that was only a shadow of the smile
he had once known; the look in the eyes, for a moment so clear
and tender, so like what he had seen so many times in Rome, long
ago!
"Yes, Damon," she said, "come again."
Again with shock he realized that she was hardly aware of him;
aware only in those moments when she gave the smile, when her
eyes turned tender, and aware only dimly, vaguely, even then-as
though something had touched the quick of memory a moment,
and gone. He turned and went away without looking back, for
he knew that she would not be looking after him. He went to a
tavern and gulped wine, remembering, "Damon, when you die
253
and go to hell shall I come down and get you?" Remembering,
"If a man doesn't keep his eyes tidy what is a woman to think of
the condition of his priapus?"
He sat for a long time thinking of her and the Christians.
A few days later he went again and found her busy with the sick,
with the smell of disease in her garments; found her only feebly
aware of him, as though memory of him were candlelight to one
who labored here in the sun. He would stand and watch her and
wait; and after she had ministered to an ailing one she would come
to him for a moment. She would give him a faint smile which
said, in its inexplicable way, that the past was dead and they were
strangers.
He went a third time and Murdia said to him, "We must all
bear witness." She meant that all Christians should seek death
as martyrs. Bishop Ignatius had told them that.
"Does Bishop Ignatius want people to kill him?”
Ah, they would kill him, she said, some day. Only those would
inherit the highest glory who died as Jesus died. Ignatius said, Let
the beasts rend me or let them crucify me or burn me alive. In the
most casual way she told him these things. Some thought the
witness ought to be crucified but Ignatius said it did not matter,
if the person died horribly. Had he not known that his mother
was a witness? She was in the highest glory now, grieving for her
son and waiting for him.
When Damon told Ayla that many of the Christians here wished
to be martyrs she was appalled. Were they renouncing God and all
the beauty of his earth?
"I don't know. It's all a riddle to me."
"Ignatius must be insane."
Damon talked to Ignatius but could not tell if he was insane.
The bishop told Damon what Murdia had told him: Christians
who stood as witnesses would inherit the highest glory. The ag-
onies of death were a trivial price to pay for the eternal blessings
that such death would bring.
Damon looked into the man's glowing black eyes. "Do you
mean that you seek to be arrested, tortured and killed?”
He did not have to seek, Ignatius said proudly. Those worthy
to stand as witnesses would be called. Their Lord had said, Take
up your cross and follow me.
"But he wasn't speaking literally, was he?"
254
“Are you, a heathen, the one to say?—or I, a bishop in our
Lord?"
"I only asked you a question. It seems to me that most Chris-
tians take all these things pretty literally. Since seeing you I have
read many books-"
Ignatius cut him off. Satan, he said, had stood at the elbow of
Paul, at the elbow of Mark—
"And Luke?"
"Luke has been deceived."
While Ignatius was explaining in what ways Luke had been
deceived, Damon was thinking about the Christians in Antioch:
they hated Paul; they had rejected the word messiah, their favorite
term now being Jesus Lord; and they refused to use the phrase
son of man. They recognized Jesus as the divine head of their mys-
tery cult, and themselves as his initiates or mystae and God's chosen
people. The word christ for them had become a proper name:
their Lord was not Jesus the Christ any longer but Jesus Christ,
as though the word christ were patronymic.
"But if all this is true," Damon said, interrupting him, "the
best thing that could happen to you Christians is for your ene-
mies to kill all of you."
"Oh no! Only a few are chosen as witnesses."
"Have you a sign that you have been chosen?"
"Indeed I have."
"To be killed in some horrible way?"
"In one of three ways."
Of course he was mad, Ayla said: there was more of God in the
birth of a baby than in all the men on earth like that.
"We'll have three children," Damon said, looking at her.
"Why three?"
"For most people that's the favorite number."
"And women know why because men have it."
Damon tried to laugh it off. "But the source of the trinity idea
is probably the father, mother and child."
"Maybe," she said, dusting.
"Then we'll have two or four."
She turned to look at him. "Why have any as long as you keep
chasing over the earth after phantoms? Are there any more books
you want to see?"
"O Lord!" he said and groaned. "Books and people."
He wanted to see Demonax, the famous disciple of Demetrius;
255
the younger Pliny; Epictetus, the philosopher; Plutarch, possibly,
it seemed to him, the most civilized of all living persons; and Jo-
sephus, the Jewish historian-or was he dead? The wicked Domi-
tian was dead and Trajan was now emperor.
"We'll settle down," Damon said. "We'll buy a fine house. You
can have children and sink deep into the fruitful Diana. I'll write
the book. When our children are grown we'll journey again.”
"It's no fun to travel when old. Will you be happy if we settle
down now?"
"Women, not men, are born to be happy."
And so it was agreed. They bought a house and furnished it and
Ayla set aside a study where Damon could write. He was surprised
to learn what a house and the prospect of a baby could do for her
cheeks and eyes and the warmer depths in her womanly soul.
Motherhood sang in her its litanies of tenderness but she kept a
sharp eye to Damon's comforts and interests, or signs of boredom.
Now and then when sunk in a book or busy pouring over notes he
would look up to find her beautiful eyes gravely regarding him.
Then he would tell her again that all was well.
One day she said, "I see the signs. We must have someone in."
"I'm all right," he said.
"Oh no, I can feel the wanderlust in you, hear it when you sleep.
Last night it was Plutarch, the night before it was Murdia. Shall
we have Luke and Levilla?"
"If you wish."
"Please be gracious. It makes no sense to argue with Christians
all the time. There are other things to talk about."
"What?"
“Damon, look. If you're going to abuse them and shout at them
I don't want it."
He drew her to him and kissed her. "Ayla's Damon is not the
gracious kind," he said. “But I'll try.”
256
XXV
Damon had never known what to make of Luke: the man was
quiet and indrawn-and was it slyness in him or an elusive sub-
tlety that sometimes had the face of slyness? What Damon liked
best in him was his love of learning and books. Art Luke de-
tested, for like most Christians he thought painting and sculpture
idolatrous. Always before he came to their house Ayla concealed
the few art objects which she had.
Luke and Levilla came one evening to a sumptuous dinner and
then sat with Damon and Ayla in the parlor. Damon had been
explaining that his ideal of the historian was Thucydides, not
Herodotus or Livy, and that he was in no way emotionally en-
gaged by the book he proposed to write. When he said that, his
wife gave him a little snort of unbelief.
"You think that isn't true?" he said, looking at her.
"I think you set out to find some great good in this new faith.
You'll imagine you have."
"You don't think I'll write an honest book?"
Well, it would be, Damon admitted, a difficult story to write.
It varied so from place to place. Already there were fifty or more
different sects, at least forty different gospel stories, and a score
of different church organizations. In one place the presbyter was
the most important official; in another, the bishop; in a third, the
apostle or prophet. And everywhere to their shame and to the joy
of their enemies they were bitterly assailing one another, each
sect saying that it and it alone had the truth. Some had taken the
Jewish sabbath, some Sunday the day of the sun, as their holy day
of the week. Some said Jesus died at the spring equinox; some, on
Passover eve; and some said he was still alive. They even had dif-
257
ferent names for the twelve disciples, the twelve whom Paul had
never mentioned.
Damon turned to the wine. When Luke and the women de-
clined he filled his cup. Luke, he said, passing the aroma back
and forth under his nostrils and looking at Ayla, had asked him
what kind of book he intended to write. "Christians will think
it should be a consistent and logical story. But how can it be?"
He had placed Luke so that the light from a large brazier fell on
his face. Now, looking at him, Damon said: “In your version of
the story are some beautiful parables. They're not in Mark.
They're in none of the other gospel stories that I have read. Did
you invent them?"
Luke was silent a few moments. Then he said: "I don't feel
that I am inventing when the holy spirit is speaking through me."
"Very well, but what am I to say about the holy spirit? In the
language of the Jews it is feminine, the Ruha Kadisha. In the
gospel story according to the Hebrews Jesus speaks of the holy
spirit as his mother. Maybe," he said, looking over at Ayla, “that
is what you meant by Diana. But when this gospel according to the
Hebrews was translated into Greek the holy spirit became neuter,
as it is in Greek. Yet Paul identified it with the Christ. So what
in the world is it?"
Levilla said quickly, "Luke didn't come here to be questioned.
You promised to tell him about this book you're writing."
"I don't know how I can tell about Luke in the book if I don't
know what Luke believes. I don't know how Christians can ex-
pect to make proselytes if they fill their books with obvious his-
torical errors. I don't know—”
99
"What errors?" asked Levilla, her tone sharp and her eyes un-
believing.
"Levilla, there are many.”
"Name one."
"All right. In some of the Christian books the death of John
the baptizer is related to Herodias, who is there called the wife of
Philip. She was not the wife of Philip but of his step-brother
Herod, son of the second Mariamne, the daughter of the high
priest Simeon ben Boethus."
"Does Luke say she was the wife of Philip?"
"It's been a long time since I read Luke's book. I'll undertake
just the same to find historical inaccuracies in it.”
"If there are," said Luke quietly, "Satan was present."
258
Damon relaxed and smiled. "All right, I'll say in my book that
you said that but it won't take many things like that to have the
whole world laughing at you."
"We expect to be laughed at," Levilla said. "Our Lord said
we'd be reviled and hated."
"Well, now let me talk with Luke. Luke, as I see it, you Chris-
tians are reworking some old legends. You have Jesus hanged be-
tween two thieves. So was the Egyptian impostor Inarus. So
was the Persian saint Hitzibouzit. Have you any idea what the
old myth means?"
"Damon!"
"All right, all right. My wife thinks the matter too indelicate
for Levilla's ears. So let's take another, the parallels between your
story and the story of Krishna. Each was born of a virgin. Each
was of royal descent on the father's side. Each became a deity in
human form. In both cases the virgin mother was acclaimed by
angels, and the birth in each case was announced by a star. The
mothers have the same name, Maya and Mary. Both Krishna and
Jesus were born in a cave or stable and were adored by shepherds.
Wise men were guided to the birthplace by a star. The earthly
father of each was a carpenter. Each was preceded by a forerunner
preparing the way and after the birth of the divine child a wicked
ruler took the forerunner's life. In each case the wicked ruler
sought to kill the divine child. In both cases the parents had to
flee. Both Jesus and Krishna were tempted by Satan. Both had
twelve disciples, chosen largely from fishermen. Both miraculously
fed a multitude with fish. Both walked on water. Both descended
into hell. Both were crucified and the sun darkened at their
death. Both arose from death and ascended to heaven.
"Luke, for thinking people such parallels can't be laughed away.
You say that Satan contrived the Krishna story to dupe the un-
wary. But will enlightened people believe that? In the cata-
combs near Rome I saw a drawing of Jesus as a babe, in a basket
under a shed, with an ox and an ass at his feet. Dionysus is at-
tended by an ox and an ass. You would tell us that Jesus fulfilled
a prophecy in Isaiah, that the ox knows his owner and the ass his
master's crib. But aren't you Christians in danger of making your-
selves ridiculous with such things? Understand me, I want to write
a sympathetic book about you people but I'm under no obliga-
tion to make your nonsense appear reasonable."
Luke said, "Damon, you still seem not to understand. Satan has
259
been busy a long time. He foresaw that our Lord would come, and
to confuse and deceive he prepared all these parallels. They are
the work of the great Deceiver. If people are going to think us
childish and our faith nonsense, that also will be Satan's work."
Damon filled his cup and drank. Then, staring at Luke, he said,
"Honest to God our Father, do you expect intelligent people to
accept such evasions? Why in the name of the seven heavens don't
you Christians admit that you're reworking old legends, as Philo
reshaped the Jewish concept of God, as Paul—”
"Do you intend to put in your book such things as you're now
saying?"
"I suppose I shall."
"Then," said Luke, with a touch of anger, "put this in, that
Satan has been busy in the world a long time; that he had fore-
knowledge of all things to come; that he is wonderfully cunning
and artful; and that for generations, knowing that our Lord would
come, he has been preparing snares and pitfalls. Some man wrote
the story of Krishna and Satan guided him. It was Satan who in-
vented for his own wicked purpose the stories of Dionysus and
Mithra and the other false saviors. People are confused, as you are,
as Satan intended you to be. Will you put all that in?”
"Yes," Damon said, looking into the man's eyes. It was plain
to him that Luke believed with all his blood and soul what he
had just said. “Luke, the thing I had in mind was this, that such
Christians as you are poets busy with old myth and legend, giving
new and shining life to old things. That seems to me a greater
story than the way you want it."
"Not to me," Luke said. "You seem to have little grasp of the
nature and scope of evil in the world."
"All right, let's go on. What about fish? Some Christians eat
them Friday as part of their holy meal. I've seen inscriptions that
represent Jesus as the great fish; there are Christians who call
themselves the little fishes. I learn from books that people have
worshiped the fish a long time. It must be a symbol, isn't it? Some
Christians worship Jesus in the form of a serpent. Isn't that a sym-
bol?"
"Damon." It was Ayla. Her tone was quiet but he heard the
warning in it.
His face turning red, Damon cried, "How can a man write an
honest book if at every turn he spares somebody's sensibilities? I
shall say that Paul gives us no account of the scourging and the
260
crown of thorns and all that. I shall say that he lifted the story
into the realm of Greek philosophy. And if Luke were to read
my book he would be offended, for he belongs to those who are
filling in all the details of birth, childhood, parentage, brothers,
sisters, manhood, death. If," he said, still looking at Ayla, “you
think this story can be written—”
"Oh, never mind it! Go on with what you were saying."
"I sometimes wonder," Damon said, turning to Luke, "if books
would ever be written, if women had their way. But this is not
a woman's story. Because the story, as I understand it, is simply
this: there probably was no Jesus; there certainly was no Judas,
no trial such as you people have, no last supper such as you have.
Paul was poet enough to reject all that. Paul is dead now and his
great vision of poetry in myth is being debauched with a lot of
physical details. You don't believe myth is the vehicle in which
we house truth? Homer and Virgil did it. The Jews did it. Philo
did it. Philo, being a poet, refused to accept the Jewish holy books
as literal: he said the preference of Jacob over Esau was that of
reason over sensuality; that the daughters of Lot were merely
symbols of sensual indulgence. And so on, but you don't argee?"
"The Jews don't accept Philo."
"They haven't sense enough, the stupid asses. As my friend
Elisha says, Jews are disputing over the bones of the myth, after
the flesh has wasted away and the spirit has fled. If you Christians
were to put flesh on the myth again the spirit would return. Paul
tried to but everywhere you are rejecting Paul."
Luke was gravely shaking his head no. "I think I understand
what you are saying but I can't accept it."
"May I tell you what Paul tried to do, as I see it, after all these
years of reading and thinking about it?"
"Why yes, of course."
"In his own creative way he tried to reconcile his own Jewish
heritage with that of the Greeks. Maybe reconcile isn't the word.
He tried to blend, to integrate, to fuse-to fuse in poetic vision.
Did he fail? Anyway he took stuff from the mystery cults, but
only as a mold to hold his poetry. Taking from both Jeremiah
and Philo he raised the circumcision of the flesh to the circumci-
sion of the heart, as the new covenant between God and men. Was
not that an act of sublime poetry? He did not abolish the Law,
he only tried to bring it up to date. Do you Christians grasp his
vision? You, Luke, are now writing a book and in it you have
261
the mythical Peter say that Jews are the children of the prophets
and of the covenant which God made when he said to Abraham,
In your seed shall all the people of the earth be blessed. Luke,
my friend, that wasn't Paul's vision! Paul raised the new truth
above the Jews, above all people, to make it a truth for all people.
Are Christians going to reject his vision?”
"I can't speak for Paul," Luke said. "I speak the truth as the
holy spirit reveals it to me."
Damon smiled. "That's the way with all of us, isn't it? Luke,
what I'm saying is that you're a poet but not yet as big as Paul.
Paul nowhere ever tells us a single thing that Jesus said. So how
can you know what he said?"
"As the holy spirit guides me."
"You've never seen a source of his sayings, written by one who
knew him?"
"Has any man?"
"I think not. Well, at least you're clearing up one point for me.
When you have Jesus say that new wine should be put in new
bottles-"
"The holy spirit guided me."
"I understand that. You used an old Jewish saying. Elsewhere
you may have Jesus utter an old Persian maxim or a Greek or a
Roman. You mean the holy spirit guides you to choose wise words
from any
and all sources. You're working as a poet but you're not
a poet when you invent stories to discredit the Jews, such as your
Samaritan story. Nor can I understand what your purpose was
when you have Mary rushing around to find her son, convinced
that he is mad. Was Satan busy? Surely a mother doesn't think
a son is mad and also of divine birth.

The hour was late. Luke said they had to go but the next after-
noon Levilla slipped over to see Damon. She looked anxious. The
previous evening Damon had seen her glance many times at Ayla's
pregnant belly and he was not surprised when Levilla said:
"You don't believe a woman can have a child without the help
of man?"
"Good heavens, Levilla! Are you going to?"
"Oh, don't try to sound witty. Answer me."
"No, I don't believe it but a lot of people do. A lot of people
think the female can conceive by means of the wind, sun, fire,
water, fish, serpents, or the divine breath. Did you know that in
the Babylonian story of creation a wind hovers over Tiamat, that
262
is chaos, and impregnates her? That the holy spirit Kneph impreg-
nated a queen by holding a cross to her mouth? Philo tells us
that God impregnated Sarah, Leah, Rebecca, Sipporah and others
and that Moses found his wife with child by God when he took
her."
"You don't believe any of it?”
"No. As poetry, not as fact. Let me tell you more. Mutianus,
a Roman consul, swore he knew an elephant that could write
Greek. Mucianus tells us of a fish that stopped a boatload of boys
headed for castration. The lion can tell by the odor of the lioness
if she has been faithless; horses tricked into incest kill themselves;
and a woman had twelve children at one time." Watching Levil-

spring the dam should be covered while facing north. Sexual inter-
course is good for dim eyesight. In Persia mice are found preg-
nant while still in the womb.
"No, I don't believe such things but I do pause now and then
when I remember that a man as learned as Aristotle thought that
the octopus copulates with its mouth. That's as silly as the belief
that Isis was impregnated by a flash of lightning, or Queen Maut-
mes by holding the cross to her mouth. Why are you so disturbed
by the matter?"
She took a deep breath and said firmly, "If other gods are virgin-
born why deny ours was?"
"I don't. I merely asked Luke why Paul knew nothing about
it. Paul thought Jesus was the son of Joseph and became divine
through the spirit of holiness. You know what some people are
saying about you?"
"What?"
"That you're having Jesus virgin-born to cover up his bastardy."
Levilla stood up, trembling. "Oh, you wicked man! May God
have mercy on you!"
"Sit down! I told you what some people are saying. If you
don't believe it, go and learn for yourself.'
"They're wicked people!"
""
"All right, they're wicked. I think you're wicked because you
think celibacy is better than love. How can Luke have Jesus say
that of such as children is the kingdom of heaven, yet tell us that
children are conceived in sin? I abhor and execrate that doctrine.
Give people a chance to be decent. Don't say a baby comes into
the world under the curse of sin and depravity. Give us a positive,
263
not a negative, faith. Give to women the high position they
should have. All these things I would have said to Luke but he
went home."
Staring at him as though fascinated she said, "Your own mother
was a Christian.”
"Oh, my mother! She had no notion of what it meant. Love
she said, but here are you Christians all over the world, hating
one another with an infernal passion. If you teach love why in
hell don't you practice it?"
"You don't think Eve brought sin into the world?”
"No, and I don't think an Egyptian brother and sister commit-
ted incest in their mother's womb. That silly ancient myth about
a man and a woman and a penis in a garden!"
"Serpent," said Levilla, coloring.
"All right. Levilla, I've lived a long time, I'm getting old. I
don't know what to believe but I think we'll be saved by good
deeds, rather than repentance and grace. The washing away of sins
with water is too simple for my mind or my mind is too simple for

"If he's a Christian."
"You weary me."
"Oh, and you weary me! You always have."
"Levilla, you'll never give up until you bring me to what you
call the light. Will you? They that wait upon him shall renew
their strength; they shall mount up with wings as eagles; they shall
run and not be weary; and they shall walk and not faint. A Jew

prisoners from the prison, and them that sit in darkness out of

makes me lie down in green pastures; he leads me beside the still
waters. He restores my soul. Though I walk through the valley
of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil, for he is with me. Or
take Job: Man who is born of woman is of few days, and full of
trouble. He comes forth like a flower and is cut down; he flees as

Hosea-"
264
Swiftly Levilla moved toward him and before he could divine

she ran away from him. He stared after her as she ran, too aston-
ished to move, too shocked to breathe.
