XXVI
During these years Damon was writing his book. Now and then,
when the meanings eluded his grasp, he despaired, feeling that
he had wasted his life. He would read over a page, over and over
and hurl it from him, and then go find his friend Elisha. It was
Elisha who had said to him, long ago, "All religions are myth and
legend." Who had said, “A person can believe in Jesus or in the
Christ but not in both." Who had said, "This new faith can never
appeal to enlightened people but it can destroy them." In his
later years Elisha had come to believe that his own people were
doomed to extinction: he had grown weary of their casuistries,
their aloof proud arrogance, their hidden hurt, and turning away
from them had become a lonely man. His mental faculties had
sharpened, his insights were subtler, though touched more and
more with the sardonic: Damon found him a delightful compan-
ion: he would look at him and listen to him and think what a
contrast he was to the Jews in Jamnia, so sunk in their myth, so
covered over by the fables their prophets had spun, that they were
like creatures living in a dark cave, half-blind, and feeling rather
than seeing.
One thing Damon had tried to discover was the reasons why
this new faith appealed so enormously to people. He supposed
there were several reasons. For one thing, it was a weary and suf-
fering world, and a humanized and suffering god had far more
appeal than a remote and impersonal deity. Their Lord had been
weary, as weary as Demeter when, in her search for Persephone,
she sank down by the well, sore at heart and despairing. If their
Lord could suffer and endure so cruel a life, should they not lift
up their hearts and their courage?
266
Another thing was the intense craving everywhere for a life
after death. This life, people said, was not worth living but they
could bear its anguish and sorrows if beyond death their lot
would be better. There was the horror of the lonely one, cut off
from the living and cast forgotten into a grave. People wanted to
be remembered, and this desire, as deep as the beating heart, Cicero
had used in his argument for a life after death. Even some of the
philosophers, for all their scoffing at vulgar superstitions, made
provision in their wills for an annual remembrance at their graves.
In his journeying over the world Damon had seen on tombstones
a thousand times the words, eternal sleep. A few, more cynical
than their fellows, left a quip or a jest to be engraved on the stone,
or in their last moments shared the spirit of a dying Roman em-
peror who whispered, "I think I'm about to become a god." The
loneliness at the prospect of death had gone deep into Damon, as
his hair turned gray and all the signs of age came upon him: the
thought of eternal oblivion filled him now and then with such
despair that he felt an urge to take his life and get it over with.
Yes, and there was in people an insatiable, almost a frenzied,
hunger for wonders, marvels, miracles; and of these the Christians
offered more generously than any other faith. All had their won-
ders, but where the votary of Dionysus or Mithra or Isis had one,
the Christian had ten. Striving for proselytes the Christians would
cry, "Behold! Who performed the greater wonders, Osiris or
our Lord?" Not to be outdone, indeed, to be supreme in this
matter, they not only embellished old wonders but with all their
ingenuity invented new ones.
And still another reason, it seemed to Damon, for the rapid
spread of this faith was the simplicity of its ritual and the ease
with which it offered salvation. A person had only to believe, to
be baptized, to partake of the Lord's supper and to understand
the lesson of meekness, humility and love. Then the holy spirit
was poured upon him. Initiation in other mystery cults was so
expensive that only the wealthy could afford it; but Christians
flung wide the door to all men, even to those with only the rags
on their backs. Blessed are the poor! That cry was ringing through
the whole Roman world.
Among Jews only the aristocrats had superior holiness. These
believed that the multitude of Jews, ignorant of the Law, were ac-
cursed. Judaism had little to offer to the ignorant, the poor, the
weak, the sinful and fallen. But Christians said, Blessed are the
267
meek and the poor, the accursed and the outcast! If they suffered
in this life they would inherit in the next and the last would be
first.
Aristocrats everywhere (Damon wrote in his book) divided man-
kind into the people and the rabble; the latter included all the
loafers, beggars and common scum that was to be found by the
temples, basilicas and baths, or lying in their vermin on mats at
the gates or at the entrances to buildings. The scholar who had
been born a slave, as he had been, was held in that contempt that
covered all the lowly, including physicians and artists.
So far as Damon had been able to compute it, three-fourths of
the people in the Roman world were slaves or little more than
slaves. They had no religion. Religion had always been the spe-
cial privilege of aristocrats. Until now. Now to these despairing
and hated millions the Christians said, Take up your burden and
come with us. Come with us to our Lord, who loved the poor;
who ate with the publican, lifted up the harlot and blessed the
sinner. What had Luke written in his book?-Blessed are you
when men shall hate you; blessed are you that hunger for you
shall be filled; blessed are the poor for theirs is the kingdom. He
had written: Woe unto you who are rich!
It was as though trumpets were blowing all over the world,
calling the whore from the brothel, the beggar from his mat, the
leper from his loneliness, the slave from his master, the criminal
from his prison house. It was as though all over the world a great
wind was rising, with God's voice in the wind, calling to his chil-
dren. Everywhere, so far as Damon could tell, people were com-
ing to the voice. A tide was rising. In Antioch now and then he
went to watch Murdia labor with the sick and dying or to see the
Christian multitude meet at daybreak in a park. A more ghastly
aggregation of people he had never seen. They were the diseased,
the crippled, the feeble and old, the insane, the blind or dumb
and the stench of them was overpowering; but something was deep-
ly stirring in these ragged and emaciated and repulsive creatures
who lifted their faces to the rising sun and opened their hearts
in song:
O our Lord! O our Lord!
I will keep your commandments, O forsake me not! . . .
He heard them sing:
Oh, let it then break forth to thee,
our Father and our Lord,
268
our only consolation here,
our future and reward.
•
•
Impartial in this as in all things they had taken songs from the
heathen:
Awake, you sleeper,
rise up from the dead,
and he who is Christ our Lord will receive you!
The sun of the resurrection
begotten before the dayspring
now gives life with his beams!
Unaware, it seemed to Damon, of themselves as sun-worshipers
they lifted their hands and sang to the sun, and then gathered for
their holy meal, unleavened bread as hard as stones, herbs, roots
and water faintly colored with wine. What an outpouring of
thanksgiving for so little!
We give you thanks, O our Father,
for life and knowledge
which you have made known to us by Jesus!
As this bread was scattered on the mountains,
then gathered in to become one,
so let us all be gathered from the ends of the earth
to your kingdom.
Such heartfelt praying! It rose in a holy chant to heaven, and
when the prayers were said they turned, wild with rapture, to kiss
one another. It was at these meetings that Damon saw the full
stature of Luke, the deeper beauty under the piety of Levilla.
Luke was the shepherd-poet, the physician of souls. Levilla was
a kind of mother, solicitous and devoted; as with Murdia, no open
sore was too loathsome, no odor too vile to turn her away from a
sufferer. It was Damon who turned away, shamed by his wealth
and indolence.
Here, he wrote in his book, was the Christian faith at its best:
it opened the temples of holiness to the unholy; gave hope to the
hopeless; offered another life to the toiling slaves who labored to
enrich the few; and brought God's son down to suffer with them
and show them how to carry their cross. Yes, but he could not
convince himself that this impassioned renunciation of life for
the hope of another life in another world could lead to more than
a deeper hopelessness. He had not faith in the survival of body or
soul after death. The solution of the world's problems, Elisha had
said, would only be found in a system of compassion and justice,
269
as broad as mankind. Yet Damon recognized that for people who
had nothing here and could hope for nothing the Christian prom-
ises were the oasis in the desert. What bleak and awful misery
would be theirs if, having found the promise, they were to lose it!
"All true enough," Elisha said one evening, stroking his gray
beard. "The common people should have a religion, even if it is
nothing but illusion."
"But don't you think that mankind is steadily moving toward
a better life?"
"I've no idea. The vast riddle of life envelops us like a fog and
the only candle in it is the mind. But look! Even those with the
brightest candles today can see no farther than the one saw who
wrote Ecclesiastes."
"But from generation to generation doesn't the fog lift a little?
For instance, don't Christians take moral jewels from other people
and give them more brilliance?"
"In what?"
"The old saying, Physician, heal thy own lameness. Christians
say, Physician, heal thyself. He who has compassion upon men,
upon him God has compassion. Christians say, Be ye merciful,
even as your Father is merciful.”
"Those are both old Jewish sayings. You mean Christians are
making them more pithy."
"Sometimes. He who gives alms in secret is greater than Moses.
Christians say, When you give alms let not your left hand know
what your right hand does. Have you ever seen the fowl of the
forest and the beast of the field laboring for their sustenance?
Christians have changed it to, Behold the fowl of the air, for they
sow not, neither do they reap, nor gather in barns, yet our heaven-
ly Father feeds them."
"I don't see much choice there," Elisha said.
"Here's another. Jews say, The Sabbath is given into your
hands; you were not given over into her hands. Christians say,
The Sabbath was made for man, and not man for the Sabbath.
Isn't that better?"
"Possibly."
"Jews say, If a man said to his neighbor, take the beam out of
thine eye, he would answer, take first the beam out of thine own
eyes. Christians have changed it. They say, You hypocrite, first
cast the beam out of your own eye. Jews say, What should a man
do that he may live? Let him die. What should a man do that
270
he may die? Let him live. Christians have rewritten it. He who
finds his life shall lose it, and he who loses his life shall find it."
"Well, yes, they are more concise. Do they have their Jesus ut-
ter nothing but old apothegms?”
"That's about all. Not every one that is near remains near; not
every one that is afar off remains afar off. There are elect who are
rejected and again brought near; but there are also elect who, once
rejected, are never brought near again. Christians put all that in
seven words: Many are called but few are chosen."
"Not quite all."
"No, that's true."
"Does it seem probable that Jesus went around uttering old
moral precepts?"
"Hardly," said Damon, answering Elisha's faint smile. "Chris-
tians are just having him say what they think he should have said.
For instance, one of your prayers goes this way: Our Father which
art in heaven, be gracious unto us, O Lord our God hallowed be
thy name, and let the remembrance of thee be glorified in heaven
above, and upon earth here below. Let thy kingdom reign over
us now and forever. The holy men of old said, remit and forgive
unto all men whatsoever they have done against thee. And lead
us not into temptation, but deliver us from the evil thing. For
thine is the kingdom, and thou shalt reign in glory forever and
forevermore.
"Someone supposed to have been named Matthew has rewritten
it. Our Father which art in heaven, hallowed be thy name. Thy
kingdom come. Thy will be done in earth, as it is in heaven. Give
us this day our daily bread. Forgive us our debts as we forgive our
debtors. And lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from
evil; for thine is the kingdom and the power and the glory, for-
ever."
"They have Jesus say
"Yes."
that?"
"Do they rewrite everything they touch?"
"No, not everything. The old apothegms, yes. For instance,
Tiberius Gracchus wrote, The beasts that prowl in Italy have
hole and lurking places where they can make their bed. You who
fight and die for Italy enjoy only the blessings of air and light.
Homeless, unsettled, you wander to and fro. This Matthew took
those words and made them say, The foxes have holes and the birds
271
of the air have nests; but the son of man has not where to lay
his head."
"Do you find it rather remarkable that a Jew in Galilee knew
what Gracchus said?"
"Oh, or what Plato said. There's an old platonic saying that no
man can be very good and very rich at the same time. They have
Jesus say that it is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a
needle than for a rich man to enter heaven. They've taken not
only from your people but from Seneca, Pittacus, Buddha, Plato
and more. They have Jesus say all of it.”
"They certainly make a remarkable man of him, don't they?”
"A remarkable philosopher and poet."
Damon took to Elisha the gospel story according to Matthew and
asked him to read it. After he had read it Damon asked him what
he thought about it.
"He tries awfully hard to discredit the Jews, doesn't he? You
have heard that it was said, Love your neighbor and hate your
enemy. Nowhere in writings by Jews can they find those words.
Damon, you've given most of your life to this thing. Has it been
worth it?"
"I don't know."
"Do they have a single original statement for their Jesus?"
"I suspect not, though I haven't yet found a parallel to every-
thing they have him say.”
Elisha was playing with his beard in a way he had; he would
draw it sharply between his fingers and then stare down at it, as
though astonished to find it gray. Then suddenly he would look at
Damon with strange lonely eyes.
"Did you know that the Christians are liars and forgers?"
"Forgers?" Damon had never thought of it that way.
"Don't they say that lies and deceit are fine if they serve their
purpose? That's the curse of all moralists. Paul said it. Paul said,
For if the truth of God has more abounded through my lie unto
his glory, yet why am I also adjudged a sinner? The end justifies
the means? What else are they doing but forging a whole literature
to try to prove that they alone are right?"
"Don't other people? Yours did.”
"Fables and parables."
"All right, poets are myth-makers. How about Homer and
Virgil?"
"They wrote about myths. Are you saying it's all right for
272
Christians to forge a whole batch of letters between Seneca and
Paul? Do you put that with Homer? And what about all the let-
ters which they say were written by Jesus and Mary and Joseph?"
"Doesn't it depend on whether good or evil comes of it?"
"My friend, you're opening the door to all things. Take an in-
stance. Where did they get the story of Jesus feeding the multi-
tude?"
"Krishna?"
"The Sibyls."
"The Sibyls?" said Damon, staring. The Sibyls were legendary
prophetesses who had tried to foretell events. Their followers
were the more illiterate and superstitious people.
Elisha took a sheet of parchment from his desk. “Read it,” he
said.
Damon read:
With five loaves at the same time, and with two fishes,
he shall satisfy five thousand men in the wilderness.
He shall still the winds by his word and calm the sea.
He shall walk on the waves. He shall raise the dead.
"I'll be damned!" Damon said, rereading. "So that's where they
got those things. I hadn't known they stooped as low as the Sibyls."
"Representing their Lord as the greatest of the wonder-workers
would they overlook anything?"
"Is your fable of Ruth truth?"
"Oh, come now! Do you put the feeding of five thousand with
two fish on the same level as the Ruth story? Is the parable of
Jonah or Samson or Joshua no better than forged letters between
Seneca and Paul? The Christians have even forged letters in the
name of Tiberius, yes, and in the name of the Roman senate. It's
one thing to build a myth. It's another thing to traduce dead peo-
ple to support it.”
"Well, yes. But can we expect a childlike people to distinguish
between the two? These people, Elisha, are fanatics, as your peo-
ple over in Jamnia are. Come flood and earthquake and bloody
moon, they're heaven-bent."
"You mention Jamnia. Did you know that Hyrcanus has been
thrown out of the Sanhedrin and the Pharisaic order? But to
come back. Will future generations accept all these forgeries as
documents on a par with the letters of Cicero or Aristotle's physiol-
ogy?"
273
"Maybe as they accept Homer. Will they accept the myths of
your people?"
"My dear friend, Joshua's stopping the sun my people probably
long ago took as fact. Now they take it as a parable. We should
recognize things for what they come to be. Do my people over in
Jamnia? Many of them don't. Will Christians ten or twenty cen-
turies from now take the loaves and fishes as fact or fable?"
"I've no idea. It may all be too childish for words. But can we
see it this way, that Aristotle dealt with facts, Job with truth? One
wrote a treatise, the other a poem."
"Maybe. But Job didn't arouse hatred of other people. Is a
poem good that stirs up bitterness? If the Christians have new
truths, or old truths in a more acceptable form, as you seem to
think they have, will people be comforted or inflamed? I haven't
abandoned the thought I expressed to you many years ago, that
all these fabrications can never appeal to enlightened people."
"Jewish fabrications have also offended many."
“Oh, I know it. I don't defend certain dismal idiocies in our
writings."
"Well, your point of view, then, is that Christians, like Jews, yes
and like Greeks, will so mix the true and the false as to make an
unholy mess of things."
"No, they will write the poem down to the common understand-
ing. That will destroy it." Stroking his beard and looking at
Damon, Elisha went on: “I've never understood why you should
spend your life on a book about them. With your zeal and energy
you might have been a philosopher or a great teacher."
Damon filled their cups. "It may be that I've wasted my life.
But it has seemed to me that I lived right in the midst of an im-
mense flowering of human hope, of the creation of a stupendous
new poem; and that, since my talents as scholar are greater than
my talents as poet or philosopher, I might observe as clearly as I
could what was happening here and set it down. For me it has
been a labor of love. Or should I say it has been a labor of dis-
cipline?"
Elisha regarded him a few moments. Then his stern face soft-
ened and he smiled. "Very well, my friend, I'll be the last to say
that you have wasted your time. I also have felt the need of dis-
cipline-not from authority outside but within the self. Do you
remember the star you said you would live by?—If one should tell
you that he has sought and found, do not believe him; but be-
274
lieve him who tells you that he found without seeking. We have
both sought, we are getting to be old men now, gray, lonely, tired.
And we know no more than we knew forty years ago.
"One thought and we must be off to bed. If this new religion
can combine the best in the Jew with the best in the Greek it may
be worth all that it will cost. Philo tried and failed. Paul tried
and maybe he failed. Men must fail as long as priests and theo-
logians imprison what philosophers and poets labor to set free.
And now, old bones must sleep.'
""
275
XXVII
Feeling that possibly he had wasted his life Damon put his work
aside and tried to forget the Christians. Their first child came
and then another, a son and a daughter, and with Ayla he took
delight in watching the unfolding of their emotions and minds.
He thought he might give his remaining years to a history of
philosophy and he kept an eye out for wandering philosophers in
Antioch. When he learned that Oenomaus of Gedara had come,
Damon at once sought him out and begged him to come to dinner.
Compared to Demetrius or Akiba, Oenomaus was a formidable
and pitiless adversary. He was big and bald and dirty and rude,
but he was rude because he believed that truth had to fight for
its life against priests and politicians. He was still young but
looked like a man in middle age. He looked, Damon said to Ayla,
like one whose dam had been the good rough strong-smelling
earth, and whose sire had been Prometheus.
"What," he roared at Damon, his beard wagging and his eyes
bulging, "have the philosophers told us? That rulers use religion
to enslave and debase and debauch the masses! Man, do you doubt
it? All governments keep their people immured in ignorance the
better to exploit them. Is it not so? What emperor or king has not
been a wicked fraud, what priest a scoundrel? Man, I tell you
there'll be no civilization on this earth until all minds have been
freed from the primitive jargon which every child is forced to
learn at its mother's breast!"
He glowered at Damon. He swung to glower at Ayla, his large
eyes glowing with anger. He brooded a few moments and sud-
denly erupted.
"Gods, demons, oracles, prophets, miracles, wonder-workers,
276
prodigies, signs, portents, auguries-what a burden of darkness our
language carries! It is all for the purpose of duping the ignorant.
Man, do you doubt it? I tell you that every man has free will and
inalienable rights and liberties; yet look at him today, enslaved,
starved, degraded, exhausted and driven out to die! Is this the
kind of world your religions have produced? Is this the thing your
priests sanctify? Is all this obscene and shameful degradation of
man by man all that you have to show for a hundred centuries of
belief in gods and priests and sacraments?”
On and on during a long evening he thundered at them, admon-
ished, rebuked, counseled, his words biting, burning, consoling;
shook them with his bursts of contemptuous laughter; challenged
and insulted and silenced them with his wide angry gestures, his
eyes bulging as he blew out his immense scorn, this man already
famous over most of the world.
"What is civilization at the top but brigands and tyrants and
thieves, liars and assassins, slave-dealers and whoremongers? Un-
der them what do we have? Under the starved and goaded and
broken ox in the yoke, the sweating and stinking and despairing
millions, driven by their desperate sorrow to renounce this life,
this, their only life, and to hope in their pitiable abject dumb way
for another life beyond! Before whom do they crawl, begging
for a chance to lift up their hearts, fill their hunger, lay their souls
before some holy thing? Before the sly and fornicating priests of
Mithra, Osiris, Isis, Dionysus, Adonis, Cybele, Zeus or the Caesars
or this Jew-god from Galilee.
On and on, silencing Damon when he tried to speak.
"It won't always be this way, no! Not forever-not forever will
the multitudes stagger sick and disease-ridden and famished and
terrified from the womb to the grave, under the whips and scourges
of their masters! Some day there will be an end of that. What did
Cato say?—that a priest turns away to snicker when he sees another
priest. I tell you the time must come, the time will come, when
the last sword will be used to spike the last tyrant to a wall, and
the last holy book to choke the last priest. . . .
99
Ayla was sitting with her daughter on her lap, her son at her
side. Both children stared popeyed at Oenomaus. Suddenly he
looked at them and turned to Damon and bellowed, "What have
you named these children?"
"Why" said Damon, unable to recall the name of either one.
"Demetrius and Diana," Ayla said.
277
The sneers of Oenomaus could be more dreadful than his rage.
"Demetrius?" His black eyes burned on Damon. "For the De-
metrius?"
"Yes."
With a sweep of his arm Oenomaus indicated the luxurious
furnishings. "What a prince you are in the hierarchy of hypo-
crites! Behold this wealthy father, hugging luxuries to his fat
gut, glowing with pride when he walks in his fine raiment, stretch-
ing his lazy bones on his costly linen, stuffing his gross and dis-
tended belly with breast of partridge and Falernian—and naming
his son for Demetrius! Does this parable of your own dishonor
express for you the two-faced world where ninety per cent of the
people groan in their filth and stench and cry out to nonexistent
gods, while your own emotions grow fat and thick and you write
a book about the Christians?" He shook with unholy mirth. "My
son?—nay, my servant lies at home sick with the palsy, grievously
tormented. Lord, heal him. Blessed are the meek. Bah! With this
debauching of truth, yes! If they weren't meek how would you
manage to keep all these things you have stolen from their toil?
Blessed are the poor. By the gods, yes! They will inherit the
earth after their masters have sucked it dry. Blessed are the poor.
What a fraud upon truth these Christian pieties are! What cow-
ardice against the helpless is this sermon on a mount! By all
means write about these people. They are working out a system
that will allow tyrants to degrade and defraud them for another
thousand years, the rich to keep their loot and add to it, the power-
ful their power, the priests their tithes and temples, and the poor
with their faces to their masters' backsides their forlorn hope for
another life." He scowled a moment at Damon and said, "What
will you write?"
"What will I write? You mean-"
"In your book. That ecclesiastical tyrannies can be a hundred
times worse than those of the Caesars?"
After this violent man had left their house Ayla was frightened
and ill. Damon was dazed. He walked around, staring at the evi-
dence of his wealth, knowing that deep in the heart of things the
Cynic was right: the powerful had so much and the poor only their
hope. How bitter to confess it, who had been born a slave!
"I dodge and evade," he said to Ayla. "My kind is common.
There are only two or three like Oenomaus in a generation, these
clear strong voices that speak out of conscience and momentarily
278
disturb our complacent souls. I pass the burden to God. I refuse
to pay the scapegoat his fee. This, I say, is God's world; he ordered
it and made it; being all-powerful he can change it. Shall I interfere
with the divine plans?" He looked at Ayla and laughed. “Will
breast of partridge ever taste the same again?"
"Oh, don't be so damned silly. Give slaves the wealth and they
will be twice the tyrant."
"No doubt. But that is not what Oenomaus proposes."
"The silliest of all the notions with which you have furnished
your mind in a prim and tidy way is this, that the rich are more
wicked than the poor. They are smarter or luckier, that's all."
"Yes, no doubt."
"If," she went on, chiding him, "people are good as they are
enlightened, think what the world would be if the common people
ruled. Is that the kind of nightmare you want?"
After a few weeks Damon forgot Oenomaus; he cultivated his
garden, watched his children play, had friends in now and then for
partridge and Falernian and idle talk, until one day when he was
shaken out of himself. He was told that in Ephesus there was a
new gospel story that was wholly unlike the one by Mark, Luke
and others. It was so different that it seemed not to be Christian
at all.
Damon begged Ayla to permit him one more journey, prom-
ising that this would be his last, and after a week of unhappy bick-
ering days and sleepless nights she yielded and they were off.
Ephesus was on the Lydian coast, across the sea from Athens. They
found it a pleasant city, with squares, gardens, promenades, the-
aters, and its great temple of Artemis, the largest in the world.
Proud citizens told them that this temple was more than four
hundred feet in length, two hundred in breadth; that it had a
hundred and twenty-seven columns sixty feet tall; that its theater
seated twenty-five thousand.
"Diana's temple," Damon said.
Ayla shrugged. “Diana's temple is the earth."
Christians were hated in Ephesus. Because of their abhorrence
of animal sacrifice they had almost ruined the cattle-market. They
had almost ruined the silversmiths. Damon went from person
to person before he found one who would talk about the Chris-
tians: then he learned that the new gospel story had been written
by one named John, who was said by his disciples to have been
one of the twelve. Damon employed a scribe to make a copy of the
279
gospel and then in his room at the inn read it with amazement.
This author had boldly reshaped the whole Jesus story and iden-
tified the lowly Galilean Jew with the Logos!
Even on his first reading Damon noted the book's arrogance
and anti-Jewishness: how could impertinence go farther than to
have the chief priests say, "We have no king but Caesar"? He per-
ceived that this writer had omitted or played down everything in
the other gospel stories that had manifested the humanity of Jesus:
in John's book he was no longer the suffering servant or the man
who became god: from first to last he was the pre-existent god
with mighty powers: all that was most human and appealing, that
was lovely and tender in the stories by Mark and Luke had been
stripped away. On his second reading it seemed to Damon that a
gnostic must have written this book, or a man under the influence
of Philo.
He was busy with his third reading when Ayla asked what he
thought of it. Damon muttered, "God knows. A person can be-
lieve in Mark and Luke or John but not in both."
"Are we going home now?"
"I want to see the author."
"O my God! Isn't his book enough? Are we going to be away
from our children ten or fifteen years?"
“Ayla, be reasonable. It will take me only a few days.”
But seeing John, Damon learned, was not easy. A very old man
who lived a secluded life, he had been elevated to a saintly dignity
and remoteness. Damon sent a Christian to beg the old man to
see him and the Christian went a second time, and John consented
then only after it was explained to him that a scholar from Antioch
was writing a book about his people.
The old man—was his name John? Damon wondered-had long
snowwhite hair and beard; his teeth were gone; his invisible mouth
was so deeply sunken that his speech was barely intelligible; and
his trembling hands were covered with distended veins and with
brown spots. His eyes were inflamed; the lower lids had sunk and
moisture ran from them, which the old gentleman had constantly
to wipe away.
Was this the lengendary Elder John?
Manifesting the utmost respect, Damon waited until the old
man asked him to sit; and he then said that he had read this new
gospel story a number of times. Was he the author? If he was
not, had he known the author?
280
With a hand that looked as bloodless as a mummy's, in spite of
the swollen veins, the old man waved the question away. Begin-
ning to feel that the whole thing was unreal, Damon looked at him
and waited, sensing at last that he lived so completely in a world
of myth and logos and gnosis, and what he might have called the
higher incarnations, that actual people had little meaning for him.
On this visit Damon gave up. On his next he brought with him a
Christian named Simon, who had known the old man a long time.
When they were all seated Simon nodded to Damon to proceed.
Speaking more to Simon than to the old man, who seemed not
to listen, Damon wondered aloud if the author of this new gospel
had read the others. If he had read Mark and Luke, for instance,
he surely had not taken them seriously: they, for instance, had
limited the ministry of Jesus to one year but John had it cover
several years; they had put it in Galilee, John chiefly in Judea;
they had sent him to Jerusalem once, John had sent him five times;
they had him die on the 15th of the Jewish month Nisan, John on
the 14th; they had John the baptizer say that he was Elias, John
had him say that he was not. For Luke and Matthew Jesus was
virgin-born; John said nothing about that. For them he was es-
sentially a man; for John, a god from the beginning, the Logos
incarnate. He wondered if the old gentleman here was familiar
with the writings of Philo.
Simon looked at him expectantly, the old man gently inclined
his head, looking at Damon while he wiped at his eyelids. His
old hands trembled when he took them from his lap.
Damon had the feeling that he was speaking not to a human
being but to a phantom. He forced himself to say that Mark's story
was very literal, to wonder if John's was an allegory. He was de-
lighted when the old white head gave a gentle and barely per-
ceptible assent.
Again wondering aloud, as much to Simon as to the old man,
as much to the universe as to either, Damon said that Luke and
Matthew had done their best to fulfill what they took to be ancient
prophecies, including some in the Sibyls; and in a way John had
also done this. He had made Jesus the Jewish paschal lamb and
said that no bone of him should be broken: did John have in
mind Exodus where it was written, Neither shall you break a bone
thereof? There was no reply. The rheumy old eyes were looking
at him. Damon tried again. Jesus was laid in a tomb at the scene
of his death: did John have in mind that the lamb should be killed
281
and eaten at the same spot? John had the side of Jesus pierced by
a lance: was that to fulfill-
Simon touched Damon softly and said, "God fulfills."
Damon felt unreal, ineffectual, almost stupid. He thought, I
should go now. He looked back and forth from Simon to the old
man and said at last, "If he'd tell me what the logos is—for he
makes Jesus the logos. Some say it is Hermes, some, Mithra,
some, Thoth. Did he have these other religions in mind when
he replaced the Jewish Passover lamb with the christ-sacrifice, and
transformed the descendant of David into the logos? Or was he
trying to discredit and overthrow the other gospel stories? Or was
he deliberately assimilating Jesus to Plato's demiorgos? I need to
know these things."
Again Damon waited, again there was no reply. He found that
he was sweating. The scene for him was becoming more and more
eerie, as the old man wiped at his eyes and gazed at him like a
strange bird, while Simon stared at the old man. Damon was
thinking, This gospel has displaced all the others: how can I tell
the story if I don't know this man's purpose?
Damon turned to Simon and said, "John has raised the story
to the plane of philosophy. That's where it'll have to be if it
lives. Was that his deliberate intention?”
Simon said, "He obeyed God's will."
The old man, so frail under his mass of snowy hair, would look
at Damon with one eye while wiping at the other; when, having
cleansed the one, he would fix it on Damon and apply the cloth
to the other eye.
Damon tried another approach. John's book, he said, was very
Jewish but showed hatred of Jews. In raising the story to a Pla-
tonic level was it necessary to place the Jews in such a bad light?
If his purpose was to liberate the story, to elevate it to poetry and
philosophy, then why hadn't he done a complete job of it? Da-
mon paused. In the old face there was nothing that he could read;
in Simon's, only bewilderment. Philo, Damon went on, worked
in allegory. He wanted his people to accept the culture of the
Greeks, the Greeks the moral earnestness of the Jews. He wanted
these two great peoples to support one another at their points of
weakness, enrich one another at their summits of strength. If
that was John's purpose, then why have Jews call Caesar their
king, why identify their paschal lamb with the heathen messiah?
Why hadn't John taken all the dross and slag from the story?
282
Simon touched Damon and said, "He grows weary, have you
more to say?"
"I've learned nothing yet," Damon said, turning to Simon.
"Oh, but you have," Simon said. "When he does not protest,
you are learning."
“May I ask just a little more?"
"Very well."
"It seems to me a great poem is being created. I wonder why
it should be so imperfect. For Philo the great power serving God
is the logos, the divine intelligence operating in the universe.
Why does John identify the logos with a Jew in Galilee? Why
does he keep anything Jewish, except what is universal?"
Simon looked expectantly at the old gentleman's face, waiting
for a reply of some kind. Damon waited too, and then said:
"I don't want to weary him. I'll go soon, but just another ques-
tion or two, if I may. Philo thought the soul of man a kind of
emanation of godliness; that flesh is the source of evil; that evil is
innate in man; that the chief goodness is the renouncing of desire
and passion; that life should be simple for the reason that in com-
plexity evil is fertilized. John seems to accept those views. Or does
he? Philo calls the logos the ambassador of God, the image of God
and the son of God. For Jews God has no son. What bothers me
most is that John should take the story so far from Israel, yet keep
so much of Israel in it."
Simon said gently to the old man, "Sir, do you wish to answer?"
The old man gave some sign that Damon missed but that Simon
understood. Simon said to Damon, "God wills."
"God wills keeping incongruous and grotesque things from
earlier stories? That doesn't seem logical to me."
Looking anxiously back and forth between Damon and the old
man, Simon said: “Give him an instance of what you mean."
"Well, all right. For Jews the messiah is only a man, a king.
Why does John say the Jewish messiah and the logos are the same
thing?”
The old gentleman raised a hand and Simon said, "He wants
us to go now." Simon arose. Damon stood up and bowed to the
old man and thanked him. Outside he thanked Simon and gave
him a gold coin and returned to the city, feeling as never before
in his life that he had been talking to something that simply did
not exist. He had stood in Jerusalem and said, "Was Jesus ever
here? Somewhere on this hill or that hill or over on the Mount
283
of Olives did he kneel and pray? Somewhere was he hanged on
a tree?" Damon had wanted to fling himself to the earth and
weep, because the whole thing was getting away from him, slipping
off into the mists. And now, here was Jesus as Logos!
He returned to Ayla and sat on the bed and bowed his head to
his hands. "I can't see it!" he muttered. "I can't see it!"
"What, peppercorn?"
"This thing," he said, looking at her. "This thing that started
as a simple little story in Israel, becoming a little less literal with
Mark, again less with Luke, and now lost in John and the Logos!"
"There's another way to look at it."
"What?"
"Damon's pilgrimage from Pella to John."
"Oh?"
"From a peasant Jew to the Logos."
"Maybe."
"From the son of Mary and Joseph to Philo and Plato."
"O Lord," he said, “yes, that's it. Where will it all end?”
"For us let's let it end now. Let's go back to our children and
forget it."
"I suppose," he said, again hiding his face.
"Shall we go at once?" she said.
She knew that he didn't hear. She knew that his mind was else-
where. His mind was somewhere in the mystical sunlight-upon-
clouds realm with a very old man, whose veins were swollen and
blue and whose hair was snowwhite.

They would go home, Damon said, they would leave at once, but

When Damon told Ayla he wanted to see Papias she threw up

"He is a very prominent bishop—-”

285
It was a long and wearisome journey to Hieropolis and the
moment Damon saw Papias he wondered if he had been a fool to
come. He could not recall having seen a Christian like this man.
About forty-five years old, of average height, slender and stooped,
with a short brown beard and restless brown eyes, Papias had
only one thing in his mind and he talked about it morning, noon
and night. Talking was his life; words poured out of him in a
continuous and impassioned torrent. Utterly convinced that
only he and his followers had the truth he was eager to bring Da-
mon or any other person to the light.
And what a story Papias had to tell! Early in his first meeting
with him Damon mentioned the death of Jesus and Papias cut
him short. They were sitting. Papias now hopped up and faced
Damon, speaking with his whole body as well as with his tongue.
The man was alive and throbbing; with his shoulders, with a
vigorous wagging of his head, with his hands, with an eloquent
flow of words that never ceased, he overwhelmed Damon and
silenced him.
The death of Jesus! He looked at Damon with pity and horror.
"Man, where have you been? Jesus did not die on a cross. That
falsehood has been spread by Jews. He died in bed. I tell you that
Jesus died in bed!”
"Yes!" said Damon, gasping.
"He was a very old man when he died. I tell you he was a hun-

was an old man and his hair was as white as snow and he was almost

"In Hieropolis!"
"Will you listen? I knew him. I—”
"You knew him!"
"I knew him. I tell you if you'll listen that I knew him. I will
tell you these things, that you may know the truth. . .
Damon was staring up at the heated face and the impassioned

Jesus had lived to be an old man, had died a natural death. But
he had never met one. Here was one before him, and a famous
bishop at that; and he was scoffing and snorting at the story of

"You knew him?" Damon muttered, numb with astonishment.

all, who through him are born again to God-infants and children

On and on he talked until he had worn Damon down; until,

mind. Papias was now telling him that no longer in the world was

290
been made in the fullness of time. People had only to believe, to

When Papias made the sign of the cross Damon shouted at him
that the Christians had taken this from the Egyptians; but Papias
scoffed and said that was only another of Satan's mischiefs. At
last, with his senses swimming, his mind almost paralyzed, Damon

in the world would ever come from all these disparate things,
whether a poem or a bedlam. Was all of it, taken together, wildly
beautiful in its effluence and its soaring; or did it manifest those
things in human nature that compelled a man, when solitary and
thoughtful, to bow his head? If a great poet did not appear with
the vision and grasp to shape all these materials into a philosophic
whole would there be only a multitude of sects souring the sweet-
ness and extinguishing the light?

XXIX
After their return to Antioch the years passed and their children.
grew up and married; and Damon resolutely put his book aside
and his interest in Christians until one day . . . Until that day
when the Christians in Antioch were beside themselves with rage
because the emperor Trajan, yielding to Jewish entreaties, gave to
Jews permission to rebuild their temple in Jerusalem. This, it
seemed to Damon, was no business of Christians but they made
it their business; he heard that all over the Roman world they were
denouncing the emperor and Jews and making a colossal nuisance
of themselves. In Ephesus they had been so violent that a number
of their leaders had been put to death. In Antioch, Bishop Ig-
natius, now an old man, was inciting his people to open revolt.
The Christians held a huge meeting, with Ignatius as the chief
spokesman. Damon was present and he was horrified by the bit-
terness of the man's harangue. Ignatius told his people that the
holy spirit possessed his voice, that his words came straight from
the Throne. He said that any Christian bishop should be regarded
by Christians as the Lord himself. The multitude was cheering.
Ignatius went on to say that he was the child whom Jesus took in
his arms, where Mark had written, And he took a child and set
him in the midst of them. Jesus, according to the legends, had
been dead more than eighty years: Ignatius though an old man,
was not, Damon decided, that old.
Hearing such words from a man's mouth, Damon began to have
the sense of unreality that had enfolded him when listening to
Papias. For him it was all fantastic, for it was plain that Ignatius
was eager to be killed. Over and over he told his people that he
was the Lord's wheat, that the teeth of wild beasts in the colosseum
293
would grind him into the flour of holiness. He was aflame with a
wish to die as a martyr and he begged all those who heard him
to do nothing to circumvent his arrest. Because he so openly de-
fied the Roman authorities and so recklessly incited his people to
violence he was arrested soon after Damon heard him speak.
When this happened in the fifteenth year of Trajan's reign Ig-
natius was the most widely known of living Christians. On hear-
ing that he was to be taken to Rome, this man whose name was
known to the whole world, Damon wanted to go along to see
what was done. He asked Ayla if she wished to go with him.
"You promised that you'd never go chasing again."
"Oh, but this is another matter. They'll kill this man. I want
to see if his faith sustains him the way it sustained my mother.”
Ayla shook her head wearily. She had been in poor health for
years. "I don't want to go," she said.
Ignatius was placed in the custody of ten Roman soldiers. Da-
mon was surprised to learn that most of the way the prisoner was
to be taken overland. This was a concession to the old man; he
wished to pause at certain places to confer with Christian leaders
or to speak to the multitudes. He was also to be allowed to dis-
patch letters to groups to tell them he was coming.
Those going with the prisoner and his guards took ship to Lycia,
bypassing Tarsus and a number of other Christian centers; and
from Lycia moved inland. Day after day Damon thought of this
strange old man. Ignatius was able to induce trancelike states, in
which, he told his people, he was most completely possessed by the
holy spirit. Damon had seen him in a trance. After announcing
to his people that the holy spirit was descending to fill him, Ig-
natius began to shake like a man in the agonies of poison; great
and dreadful tremors filled his frame and convulsed him, until
he was barely able to stand. Pushing in close, Damon looked at
the man's eyes. They had seemed to enlarge and the pupils to
dilate, and to shine with an unearthly light, while apparently
blind to all earthly things. The tremors seized and shook him for
several minutes, when he became very quiet, his gaze still on the
sky. At last in a loud voice he began to speak, and the message
went from tongue to tongue that he was speaking for God.
He told his people what the holy spirit was revealing to him.
In Ephesus, he said, a Christian elder had been killed. The dead
man's heart had been taken out and cut into small pieces, and on
every piece had been found the name of Jesus, written in letters
294
of gold. He told them that a deacon had been sentenced to death
by fire. He was bound to the stake. The fagots were kindled, and
though at once the flames enveloped the man they did not touch
him, but rose above his head in the form of the cross. The fire not
only would not burn this Christian; it formed a kind of womb in
which he was sheltered, with the cross of flame above him; and
people were amazed and then enraged. They dragged him from
the fire and tore him limb from limb. Damon did not know if
Ignatius was inventing these fantastic wonders, or repeating stories
that he had heard about martyrs.
After they left the ship, Damon caught a glimpse of Ignatius
and was shocked. Had the guards deliberately tortured him, or
used brutal methods to quiet his frenzies? Heavily bound with
chains, the bruised and bloody prisoner was placed in a cart, and
three guards rode ahead, three behind, and two on either side.
Damon was able to approach close enough to see Ignatius: both
eyes were cut and swollen almost shut; there were blood stains all
over his gray beard, and he lay sprawled in the cart like a man
more dead than alive. At Smyrna for some strange reason he was
allowed to speak to a multitude of Christians who had gathered
to see him.
Do nothing without the bishop, he told them. Keep their
flesh as the temple of God. Love unity, flee from divisions. Be
imitators of Jesus Christ as he was also of his Father. Then for
several minutes Ignatius dwelt on a matter which, it seemed to
Damon, obsessed him: Christians were to respect the deacons and
elders even as they respected their Lord; and above all, they were
to revere their bishop as God, for in a way he was God. The
bishop presided after the likeness of God, the prebyters after the
likeness of the apostles. The bishop, being a type of the Father,
was supreme above all. "Wheresoever the bishop shall appear,
there let the people be; even as where Jesus may be, there is the
catholic church.' Damon made a note of that: never before had
he heard the word catholic applied to any of the Christian sects.
The old man's eyes were so swollen that he could barely see but
he talked on and on, gesturing, shouting, whispering, weeping,
exhorting. Suddenly he changed to the Roman tongue and sang
to them, and Damon wrote the words down:
Gaude Virgo! Mater Christi,
quae per aurem concepisti,
Gabriele nuntio!

""
Ignatius then prayed for them, tears washing down over his
cheeks and his bloody beard; and the moment the prayer was con-
cluded he raised both hands to the sky and thundered, “Come,
fire and cross! Come, grapplings with wild beasts, cuttings and
manglings, wrenching of bones, hacking of limbs, smoking of

His words were so wild
and horrible that Damon bowed his head. "Come, cruel tortures
of Satan to assail me, only let it be mine to attain unto our Lord
Jesus Christ! Do nothing to prevent my departure from this city!
I am writing to all the churches along the way that I am eager to
die for God and they must do nothing to hinder me! Food for
wild beasts let me be, that I may have access to God! . . ." Da-
mon had closed his eyes on the pain of it. "God's wheat am I,
and the teeth of wild beasts will grind me, that I may be formed
purest bread of the Christ! Caress the beasts, for they will be my
tomb! Let no fragment of my body remain, for I want to give
no man the trouble of burying it! For I shall truly be a disciple
of Christ our Lord!"
Some of those who heard him were weeping, some were pray-
ing, their faces tense and white.
"Do I hunger? Do I thirst? I have no taste for corrupt food, nor
for the pleasures of this life! I hunger for the bread of God, which
is the flesh of Jesus Christ, and for drink I would have his blood,
which is incorruptible love! There is only the one flesh of our
Lord Jesus Christ, and one cup for union in his blood; and only
one altar, as there is only one bishop, surrounded by the presby-
ters and deacons. The glad tidings are the flesh of Jesus, and I
do not hunger, for I am filled.
99
Damon turned away, thankful to heaven that Ayla had not
come. Looking back, he saw many of the people hysterically weep-
ing, saw many kneeling in prayer; saw Ignatius, standing in his
chains, which ran from bracelets on his arms to bracelets on his
ankles. Pale, haggard, feeble and old, was he, Damon wondered,
to be admired or pitied? Was he mad? Would he be killed? I
have read all the philosophers, Damon mused, and I'm an old
man, but when I listen to Ignatius I realize that I know no more
than the boy who watched his mother die. What strangeness was
it in men that sought violence? What was the terrible thing in
an old man clamoring to be burned alive or torn apart for a sad
and lonely figure in a myth? Was it that men were afraid of life
and did not want to live it; or was there some incurable sickness
296
in their souls? He could not ask Elisha, for Elisha was dead; and
Luke was dead; and Levilla and Murdia were dead and he would
soon follow them, having found no answer to the riddle that had
tortured Ayla, why by the tens of thousands men crucified one
another, burned, gutted, skinned and embalmed one another,
castrated one another, cut off hands and feet, gouged out eyes,
pulled out tongues and ran redhot iron rods up the anus and clear
to the heart. All in the name of what they called God!
Poor old Ignatius. Back through memory Damon searched all
the philosophers he had read and all the wise men he had listened
to but there was no answer. Most of the night he lay awake, think-
ing, thinking; and the next day the melancholy group, with Ig-
natius at its center, took its way to Rome. Bound with chains
and his life almost jolted out of him he was hauled overland to
Alexandria Troas and there took ship to Neapolis, the port of
Philippi. At Troas a score of other Christian leaders, shackled
and condemned to death, were taken aboard.
Damon was not allowed to talk to them but he could imagine
what they were saying. We were lame in understanding, our
whole life was nothing but death; and he pitied us, he took com-
passion on us, for he saw that we had no hope of salvation but in
him. He called us when we were not, and from not being he
willed us to be. Let us be earnest to be imitators of our Lord.
Or were they perhaps murmuring Paul's incomprehensible words?
-God forbid that I should glory save in the cross of our Lord, by
whom the world is crucified unto us and I unto the world.
From Troas to Italy Damon did not see Ignatius and he won-
dered if the guards had slain him and thrown him into the sea.
But no: when led off the ship, jangling his chains, he seemed more
alert and vigorous than he had been at Smyrna. Somewhere along
the way he had written to the Christians in Rome, and a body of
them had come to meet him. They hailed him as though he were
the Savior; they followed him to Rome; they took up vigil before
the building that was to be his temporary prison.
Ignatius was brought forth the next day, and while a mob
wildly clamored for his death he was questioned by a captain of
police. Damon thought the captain a very decent fellow. He
told Ignatius that if he would say but a few words he would be
freed.
"What harm is there in saying, Caesar is Lord, and in offering
incense to him? If you will say, Caesar is Lord and will burn
297
incense to him on one of the altars we'll return you to Antioch."
Ignatius shook his old gray head. The captain, or by turn his
assistant, tried to persuade him to yield. What harm was there in
saying a few words and burning a little incense? Millions of
people did these things. Practically all people did these things but
the Jews and Christians. Was there some sin in swearing by the
genius of the emperor and acknowledging him as one of the gods?
All the while the mob howled, "Away with the atheist, throw
him to the lions!"
They then returned Ignatius to his cell. The next day he was
brought before a magistrate, who said to him, "Swear the oath to
Caesar and I will release you. Deny the one you call Lord, for
he is only a phantom. Will you acknowledge the emperor? Will
you deny the one you call Christ?"
In a loud firm voice Ignatius gave his reply. "I am an old man.
All my life I have been his servant and he has been my Lord and
Savior. How can you ask me to deny my King?"
When again urged to swear by the emperor, Ignatius said: “I
am a Christian." For an hour this was his reply to all entreaties.
When the magistrate's patience was exhausted he told Ignatius
that if he did not yield he would be thrown to the beasts. Ignatius
then shouted, "Hurl me before them! I have come to shed my
blood for our Lord!" During part of still another day the magis-
trate tried to reason with him, to appeal to his loyalty to the em-
pire, or to frighten and intimidate him. But Ignatius was adam-
ant. He was determined to be degraded, broken, destroyed.
When at last he was led away to the stadium, Damon began to
feel sick. He did not share the general fondness for bloody scenes
in the arenas, and least of all for the torture and killing of men;
but he forced himself to go, to look, to think. Gathered in the
stadium was a mob of thousands, howling for blood. Most of them
were men but there were hundreds of women, and the women, it
seemed to Damon, were more frenzied than the men. When Ig-
natius was led forth and the multitude saw him it yelled and
shrieked, faces convulsed by passion, eyes bulging. What form of
death did the people decree? Some shouted for the stake and
fagots, some for the beasts. There was dreadful confusion. Igna-
tius was standing back with guards, and Damon thought he looked
untroubled and unafraid. His chains were now taken off. The
one who seemed to be in charge of the execution was still trying
to determine the will of the people. It was impossible to deter-
298
mine it: if thousands shouted for the beasts, other thousands
shouted for fire. There was much scurrying around by the execu-
tioner and his advisers. At last it was announced that the punish-
ment would be by fire.
Men set a stake. They seized Ignatius and thrust his back against
the stake and were preparing to bind his hands and feet when,
raising a hand for silence, he said in a voice loud and clear, “No,
let me stand free. I will not run. I will not falter. Bring on your
fire and cover me with flames, for my Lord wills it so." Again
there were conferences and delays while throats shrieked for the
old man's death.
It was decided not to bind him. So there he stood, erect and
undaunted, his back to the stake, his gray head high and proud,
his gaze on the sky. Damon began to feel terribly ill. He felt sick
with shame clear to his depths, sick with the knowledge that these
insane shrieking creatures around him were men and women,
whose kinship he had to accept. He looked at their faces but they
were not human faces.
A great pile of kindling was heaped around the old man and
drenched with oil. In a mist that was a mist of tears Damon saw
a man run forth with a torch. He saw the first flames leap in the
pile of kindling. Then he had to bow his head and hide his eyes,
for he was remembering his mother now and he could endure no
more. All around him were the savage yells and the throaty gloat-
ing that was worst of all. The infernal sounds beat round him
and upon him until his senses darkened and his mind sickened;
but all the while he was telling himself that he must look once
again, to see if the old man was still there.
He was still there. Damon could look for only a moment at
the horrible sight. The flames had completely enveloped him,
there was fire in his hair and beard. He was there, he was not
bound and he had not moved. Damon then forced himself to
look once again at the faces that were not human and he hated
them and he recognized in this moment that he was a Christian, as
he would have been a Jew if he had been present when the holy
city was sacked; as he would be in any situation of torture what
was dearest on earth to the one tortured. Was not that what it
all meant?
The people, still shrieking out of their incredible frenzies, were
leaving their seats and the stadium; with their blood up and their
passions overflowing they came in roaring tides past Damon,
299
knocking him off the bench and trampling him. The mob poured
out and over him and he was unable to help himself but he found
comfort in thinking that out there was the blackened skeleton of
a brave old man, who in this final hour and at least for this final
hour had made Damon a Christian. Half-conscious, bruised,
bloody, trampled, he tried to struggle up to his knees and to bow
and to pray. Or was he praying? Or was he talking to Luke, or
Elisha, or Jesus? Or was he saying:
"It is all gone in the mists now, it is all gone; it is all lost to us,
it is nostalgia, it is heartache; it is hope and yearning and eyes
misted over. Was he once there by the sea of Galilee? In Geth-
semane did he kneel heartbroken and pray? We can never know
now, for the past has closed over it, as the grave closes over the
beloved, and all is swallowed in the mists but memory; and the
memory itself fades and pales and at last is gone, is
gone..
