XXX
"We can never know now," Demetrius read, and for a little while
sat still, looking away, thinking of his father and of this book
which his father had written. "It is all lost now, it is heartache,
it is hope and yearning and eyes misted over. Was he once there.
He read:
The story had its simple and humble beginning in the land of
the Jews—and yet not there, for the story is ancient. Was there
a lowly one named Jesus who was hanged as a false messiah? I
put that question to my friend Elisha and he said that nobody will
ever know now and it does not matter. Was there an Orpheus,
was there, let us ask, a Buddha, or have the gods been only glori-
fied images of ourselves? If there was a Jesus he has now been
swallowed by the mists, like a lonely figure climbing a high moun-
tain, that vanishes from sight and forever when the clouds en-
fold him; and what he taught, or to whom, or how he lived and
died we can never know.
At first, it seems, there was a Jewish cult in Jerusalem, com-
prised wholly of Jews who believed that the messiah had come.
That was more than a hundred years ago. This handful of people
was persecuted by other Jews, including one named Paul, who
later after his conversion became the cult's principal apologist and
teacher. But Paul, though a Jew (or was he only half-Jew?), was
a Greek by training and tastes, and he undertook to free the cult
of certain practices which non-Jews found offensive. In doing this
he sacrificed Jesus to the Christ.
After absorbing Greek influences the new faith became a mys-
tery cult, offering salvation by supernatural means; and because
301
there were so many mystery cults, this new one began almost at
once to diverge into various sects, as different minds made their
contributions to it. Writers began to appear, and after a while
there were many versions of the gospel story; and in Rome, Anti-
och, Corinth, Ephesus, Alexandria and other places the sects began
to promote their own peculiar points of view. A Jewish cult thus
became transformed into a sacramental cult, which then took
Greek ideas into its doctrines; and this reached, in my lifetime,
its highest development in Ephesus, where the Lord was identified
with the Logos.
The myth had by this time moved a long way from Jesus in
Galilee. If there was such a Jew, he must have been (no matter if
tenderness and the years may ennoble him) a rather illiterate and
simple man who taught simple things, such as the need of re-
pentance and the imminent end of the world. Those were Jewish
ideas. He could never have foreseen even a tithe of all the things
now attributed to him and taught in his name! Least of all could
he have foreseen how the spirit of anti-Jewishness would develop
in the cult, from Mark to Luke to Matthew to John. He could
never have known how the gospel legends would be worked over,
year after year, generation after generation, as the story took and
assimilated materials from all peoples, striving to make itself in-
vulnerable to any objections which it could not overthrow. Above
all, he could never for a moment have dreamed, this lowly one in
Galilee, that a hundred years after his death there would be a hun-
dred sects in his name.
To write a biography of him, if he did indeed live and teach
and suffer, must be forever out of the question. Not a single one
of his chief followers has ever tried to. His story is generally and
widely known as the mystery, and a mystery it must continue to
be. If his votaries, putting together the strands of oral tradition,
had been intent on weaving a biography, they would have tried
to make it consistent in itself, and the different biographies with
one another. That they have nowhere tried to do this proves that
the actual facts of his life never concerned them.
What is he now, this dim figure receding into the mists? Is he
only another vegetation-god, slain on the sacred tree? Is he only
another sun-god, like Mithra, in the rock tomb? If this is so, his
people must one day assign to him December 25th as his birthday,
for that is the birthday of all the sun-gods. If only one could look
into the future to see how this myth will develop!
302
For a little while Demetrius sat in thought. Had his father
known that some sects fixed the birthday on April 25th, some
on May 25th, and still others on January 6th? He read again.
Like the sun-gods he now has twelve disciples, and enters a city
on an ass or the foal of an ass, the signs of cancer. This much we
know, that Christians have taken heathen rituals, mysteries, myths:
they have their Lord resurrected from a rock tomb, like Mithra;
turn water into wine, like Dionysus; walk on the waves, like
Poseidon; lie in a manger, like Ion; come to birth in a stable, like
Horus, and from a virgin mother, as with all the gods. Like Mi-
thra, Thoth and Hermes he has been incorporated with a mystical
principle, the Logos; and has become personalized wisdom, after
Plato and Philo. But certain Jewish elements strangely persist!
We must wonder what he taught, if anything at all. Of the say-
ings attributed to him there is not a single one of any consequence
for which parallels did not exist long before his time. His fol-
lowers have drawn impartially from all sources. Their contempt
for, even rejection of, this world; their severe censure of all irregu-
lar sexual behavior; their belief that no good can come of kind-
ness if it is not offered with a pure heart-these and other matters
they took from the Stoics. Indeed, they took so much from this
philosophy that in an effort to conceal their pilfering they forged
letters from Seneca to Paul. Their sweet benevolence toward the
sick and needy; their orders, now being established, of monks and
nuns in a life of moral contemplation and self-denial; their mystic
principle of the union of the individual with his God-these they
have taken from Buddha.
Striving to establish their cause with such strength that neither
powers nor peoples can overthrow it, they have employed amazing
ingenuity. They have invented wonders to prove that their Lord
was the greatest wonder-worker of all. They have endeavored to
have him fulfill everything that they have construed as ancient
prophecy. They have gone so far that they now have a gospel
which they say was written by Jesus himself. They have contrived
countless letters which purport to have been written by him, or by
his mother or by Joseph or by the apostles; by distinguished Ro-
mans about him, in which his uniqueness is admitted; as well as
many other documents, all forgeries, with which they would prove
their contentions in all matters under dispute. In the invention
of documents they have been deterred only by the limits on their
talents and their inventive faculties.
303
In converting allegories to their needs or in contriving allegories
of their own they have shown the same zeal and creativeness. The
words of Odysseus, The rule of many is not good, let there be one
ruler, they interpret to mean only one god. They argue that
Homer's poems were written to show the mischief that comes
from having many gods. When Homer tells us that Hephaestus
represented, on the shield of Achilles, the earth, the sea, the sun
that rests not, and the moon full-orbed, Christians contend that he
was teaching the divine laws which he learned from Moses! The
extent to which they have allegorized ancient writings to suit
their purpose is almost beyond belief.
With the same fantastic zeal, as my earlier chapters have tried
to demonstrate, they have in almost countless instances plundered
and corrupted the Jewish holy books. After having Jesus say in
Luke and some of the other gospels, this which is written must be
fulfilled in me, they enthusiastically proceed, indifferent alike to
historical facts and the meaning of words, to have him fulfill all
things. In this they obviously are trying to give their story a his-
torical basis and a prophetic warrant. Hosea, for them, foretold
the return from Egypt; Joel, the outpouring of the holy spirit
upon him; Obadiah, his coming to the mount; Jonah, his resurrec-
tion. . . . Learned Jews point out (but in vain!) that it was not
a man, not a prophet, not the messiah who is meant in the passages
they cite, no, but Israel. Jews say Israel, he, using the singular
masculine pronoun to refer to all the people. My God, my God,
why hast thou forsaken me! is Israel's cry. They parted his gar-
ments: that is, Israel's. They pierced Israel, scoffed at Israel, made
Israel's grave with the wicked. Christians will not listen when you
tell them these facts, nor raise the slightest blush of shame when
you point out that a defeated and crucified messiah is utterly re-
pugnant to all Jewish thought and belief.
But see what they do! Having placed their Lord in Israel they
are faced with a multitude of difficulties. They read in Micah, But
thou, Bethlehem, though thou be little among the thousands of
Judah, yet out of thee shall he come forth unto me that is to be
ruler in Israel—and they go south from Galilee clear across the
land to put his birthplace in Bethlehem! They read in Zechariah
that the king will ride on an ass, and on an ass they have Jesus
enter the holy city! Are we dealing here with childishness or with
poetry? They read in Isaiah, I (Israel, that is) laid me down and
slept: I awaked: for the Lord sustained me—and they have him
304
rise from the dead. Poor lowly Jesus, what did you actually do?
They read in Isaiah, I (meaning God) was visible to them that
asked not for me, I spread out my hands to a disobedient people—
and lo, non-Jews are saved and Jews are rejected!
One shudders to think how much mischief may come from that
one misreading of Isaiah.
These instances could be multiplied but I can feel the weary
reader begging indulgence. It is clear that we are dealing here
with an incredibly naive or an incredibly perverse people. Elisha,
who sits in my house as I write these words, thinks they are only
desperately trying to prove their case. He points out that only a
learned Jew can understand Jewish modes of language and
thought. Since most Christian writers have been non-Jews it is not
surprising that they have so misunderstood Jewish books—that
they were unaware, for instance, that Jewish prophets did not
foretell, but only admonished, exhorted, announced. To pile con-
fusion on confusion, absurdity on absurdity, each Christian writer,
working independently of the others, has chosen what seemed
to him most appropriate to be fulfilled. Because Mark has Jesus
cry, My God, why have you forsaken me! is no reason that Luke
should not have him say instead, Into thine hand I commit my
spirit.
Sensing that he was being watched, Demetrius looked up and
saw fixed on him the grave eyes of his wife.
"I'm just reading my father's book," he said.
"I know. For a little while we have had daylight between two
darknesses."
He flushed with anger. “That's a woman's point of view," he
said.
After she had gone away he sat a few minutes, wondering about
her. An estrangement had grown up between his father and his
mother, because his father had given his life to a book about the
Christians. He was afraid that his own wife would destroy this
book. He would hide it. He read again.
This indifference, often arrogant, to the labors and interpreta-
tions of one another, has produced a host of contradictions. If
we set different Christian books side by side we find it said that
few find the gospel, or that all men are drawn to it; that salvation
is very easy, or very difficult; that the kingdom comes slowly like
an ear of corn, or is ushered in suddenly with a cataclysm; that it is
material, or wholly spiritual; that it is impending, or a thousand
305
years away. In their details of the life of Jesus their stories are in-
consistent with one another at a thousand points. Depending on
which version you read Jesus is a bigoted Jew or a cosmopolitan
Hellene; a lover of the common people or a gnostic hater of their
ignorance; a pleader for love of one's enemies or a bitter denouncer
of opponents. Depending on the story, he promises or denies tem-
poral blessings; avows or conceals his messiahship; calls for for-
giveness of all sinners or casts the erring to perdition. In short, no
matter what he may or may not have been he is now, a hundred
years later, the product of a thousand different minds, imagina-
tions, insights. All this sets it apart as a great story, a great poem,
because so much of human history, faith and yearning have gone
into its making.
Do we not have here, essentially, the old old cry, My God, my
God, why have you forsaken me? The lonely lament of unhappy
and fearful and haunted people who cry daily to the heavens for
some proof that this wretched life with its injustice, its inequities,
its terrors is not all that the babe is born to, who did not ask for
this life or come into it by his own will. I can understand the cry
with its burden of sadness and loneliness, for I am nearing my
end; and though I do not believe these things, yet with what re-
luctance, almost with what bitter outcry, I shall go down to my
grave!
Again Demetrius sat, thinking of his father. He supposed that
Damon had felt all the bitterness of despair and heartbreak when
the insane mob trampled him to death, after shouting their ob-
scene insults at a brave old man in flames. Ayla had told her son
how his father had wandered in Jerusalem, in tears, his heart
but not his mind saying, Did he walk here? Somewhere on that
hill did he kneel and pray?
He read again.
I turn to my friend Elisha and ask, Is that all of it? Do we come
out of darkness and go down into darkness, knowing no more of
the riddle than when we came? Is the cry to God which your peo-
ple have uttered in one beautiful poem after another, and which
supplicates from every Christian book, to have no answer? Have
we no language, Elisha, but loneliness, no labor but this desperate
strife, no anchors more substantial than these apparitions? Have
we, my friend, no better lantern than the myth? You come from a
a great people. Down through the centuries your prophets have
306
wrung their hands at the sky and cried their hearts out to God.
Yet, look!
Yes, look! Demetrius thought. Had his father and Elisha fore-
seen what was to come? For the Jews were again in trouble, in
very grave trouble. Ever since the destruction of their temple the
hotblooded zealots among them had been plotting vengeance while
impatiently awaiting the messiah. Now most of them believed that
the messiah had come. He was one Bar Kokhbah, the Son of the
Star, who had roused a revolt against the mighty Roman empire.
Even Akiba, the old and wise Akiba, who occupied a whole chapter
in Damon's book, had acclaimed him as the long-awaited one,
the son of David, who would deliver the Jews from their oppressors
and establish them as the rulers of the earth. Tens of thousands of
Jews had wildly acclaimed him, crying, The Star hath trodden
forth out of Jacob! With guerrilla bands Kokhbah had defeated
the first Roman army sent against him, and the enthusiasm of his
followers had mounted to the last pitch of frenzy. Then-De-
metrius thanked God that his father had been spared this knowl-
edge—then the land of Israel was again drenched with blood, the
holy city was razed and covered with salt and the survivors dis-
persed to the ends of the earth. Again the Christians were wild
with gloating.
...
What had Elisha and Damon said, on this day many years ago?
No people on earth, Elisha, have been more faithful to their
God than your people. None have so glorified his name, or striven
with such zeal to keep his laws. Yet see what has come over them,
again and again! There is no Yahweh: we both know that. So we
must ask if a myth is worth the horrors which it calls down upon
those who believe it.
My good old friend sits, slumped in a chair, his long beard, not
white but rock-gray, spread out before him, his handsome skull
as bald as old bones. His beard runs up his cheeks and across above
his ears and around his head, making it seem that he wears a huge
bib. He looks at me a few moments and then speaks.
You, he says, have argued that myth is a vehicle for truth.
It may be a vehicle for error. No doubt there are some good things
in the myth my people have elaborated these two or three thou-
sand years; but there must be more evil than good in it or it would
not have called down on us all the hate and enslavement. In its
name we shall probably be wiped off the earth, and jubilant Chris-
tians will say this proves their own myth to be right. No other
307
people have so much right to be cynical, yet we are not. That may
be our strength. We have tried to outgrow the scapegoat.
As you know, says Elisa, looking at me in his dry way, every
year on the 14th of March in Rome a man clad in skins is led
through the streets and beaten with long white rods and driven
out of the city. In Greece a slave used to be flogged with the rods
of the agnus castus and abandoned with the cry, Out with hunger,
in with health and wealth. Whether it is a slave or the old god
Mars or a goat for Azazel makes little difference. People still
have the notion that someone else will accept the burden of their
sins.
Christians have their scapegoat. They call him their Lord. To
his head in death they transferred their sins. Can any religion
based on the scapegoat idea serve an enlightened mankind? Must
it not inevitably lead to prodigious excesses of indulgence and
easier and easier ways of atonement? People seek a great deal for
a little and at last are unwilling to pay even that. For Christians
their Lord is a ransom paid to Satan, instead of a symbol of the
triumph of the moral law. There is all the difference in the world
between the two. A philosopher named Socrates once asked you
when people would be willing to pay the scapegoat his fee; and
after all these years you have no answer.
I say to Elisha, No doubt, no doubt; but is there not a degree
of moral triumph? I have written it this way. The faith called
Christian teaches sympathy for the sick, the afflicted, the sorrowing;
it has set on foot plans for the relief of suffering and want; it
has appealed to the most generous impulses in the human heart;
it has risen or it tries to rise above the tyrannies of sexual and
property lust. It would set man free of vengeance and punishment
as a cure-all for human ills and wrongs. Forsaking coercion and
retaliation-
Elisha raises a hand to interrupt me. Do you, he asks, say they
have forsaken retaliation when many of them daily pray for the
extermination of my people? If they have risen above the tyranny
of sexual lust, what have they risen to? An ideal of celibacy and
the extinction of all human life? Damon, my friend, do not try
to see what is not there, like an orphan searching the shadows for
the face of his father. If religious appeal is to the less intelligent
part of mankind, then uncommon people must eventually become
its servants and be forced to prostitute their talents to the common
level. Mind and courage in that case will abdicate.
308
You say myth is the proper vehicle for truth and so it may be;
but the horrible thing about myth is that its bones remain for
the exegetes after its spirit is dead. That is what has happened to
the myth created by my people. Philo tried to give it new flesh
and soul, and for the fanatics who were at Jamnia he was a heretic.
Our scholars are busy with the skeleton of Moses. This myth,
created by the best intelligence of its time, served a purpose, but
has long since been abashed by certain truths in philosophy and
science. If the Christian faith, or any other, had within it, insep-
arable from its own life, the power to evolve, to absorb and in-
corporate truths from all fields of knowledge, as these become
known, it need not become mere theological bones over which
the exegetes waste their lives. Did I depart from my people's ways?
Yes, because there the wise men sat, studying the bones of their
ancient myth and disputing over the bones. Long ago, it may be,
those bones were more deeply fleshed, more completely infused
with the spirit, than any other myth-bones known to man. But it
is only the poets who can put flesh on human loneliness and hope.
There is some fine poetry in Paul and in some of the other Chris-
tian writings but I'd think the question you must ask it this: for
their time do these poets have the stature Moses had for his time,
or Job or the Preacher?
I admit to Elisha that possibly they haven't. He then goes on:
Like my people in Jamnia, reverently allegorizing over the
bones, powerless to raise their faith to the next level, so Christians
may dispute endlessly over their Jewish-heathen hybrid, spinning
sophistries long after their myth is empty and the last truth has left
its house. Many of my people today want to fight the Roman em-
pire. The idea of a king-messiah was all right when my people
were enslaved long ago, but the Greeks have made the idea ridicu-
lous for our time. So here my people are, behaving for all the
world as if eager to get themselves exterminated. The same fate
must some day come to Christians, if they struggle against a higher
enlightenment and a nobler ideal.
I ask: Didn't Philo try to raise the myth to a higher level?
Yes, indeed, Elisha says; but when he took Greek philosophy he
forgot Greek science. He got so entangled with Plato that he threw
himself and never got up. And Paul—well, Paul assimilated a Jew
to the heathen savior-god; and though, says Elisha, smiling at me,
that for a Jew is an extraordinary feat, for thoughtful men it must
always remain an extraordinary silliness. He ruminated a few
309
moments and said: Possibly a great poet will rise to fuse Greek and
Jew in a new myth.
We discussed the matter, wondering if a poet of such stature
would come-if all the aimless and sunless tributaries flowing out
of mankind's yearning would ever converge in one deep river un-
der a full sun.

Those were the last words his father had written, who had spent
his life trying to discover the sources of the streams. Demetrius
had heard that new Christian leaders had risen. He had not seen
them. He sometimes wondered if he should try to complete his
fathers' book. While thinking of the matter he looked up at two
paintings on the wall: Ayla had had these done when Damon took
his last journey: on hearing that he had been killed she gave the
paintings to her son. One she called the Mother. It showed a gar-
den, much like the gardens that Ayla had had, deep and lush and
drenched with its own colors, with the head and breast of a god-
dess above it, as a kind of emanation of it. At her breast she
clasped a rosy infant. Only her head, breast, hands and child
were visible; the remainder of her fell downward in a kind of
vaporous substance, suffused with the colors of earth, flowers,
fruits, sky and water, so that she seemed to emanate from the
earth yet remain with it, to have her roots and being in it, but
to be above it as the presiding genius over its fructifying powers.
The other painting she called the Father. It showed a piece
of wasteland with the Father above it, in the manner of the Mother
above the garden: his countenance was craggy and strongbeaked,
with wild hair and angry terrifying eyes, a being out of Jeremiah,
lonely, solitary, childless, unwedded and unloved. Demetrius
looked at them in turn, as he had so many times; at the happy,
cheerful, voluptuously warm and tender mother, looking down
at the child and at the succulence of the good earth: she smelled
of kitchens, babies, jams, fruits, pure water and warm winds,
odorous forests and the valleys of vision; and he smelled of desert
heat and burning earth, hot stones and moistureless hills and a
merciless sun. Demetrius recalled how his mother had sat day
after day with the artist, evoking for him the two visions as she
saw them; suggesting a little more color in the mother's ripe
cheeks, more roundness in the fat vegetables, more richness in
the shading of earth, plant and flower; more prominence in the
bone structure of the father's face (who was not a father, having
310
no son) more bleakness in the curve of his nose, more of the desert
in his eyes..
...
The Father and the Mother, Ayla had said; but had she seen
too little, her son wondered, while seeing so much? They were as
distinct as the desert, with its pathetic tithe of sperm and growth,
and the valley, its womb open to the sun: was it really the way of
the man, driven to cruelty and lusts by his loneliness, and the
way of the woman, crooning to her babe with the ripe fruits bend-
ing around her? Would a poet ever rise to fuse these two? So
strange that his father had seen his, his mother her, vision so
clearly, yet had never seen one another; so strange that he, looking
at both and taught from childhood to understand both, should
be so much more deeply moved by the Father!
For this was a man's quest, this search for God. He suspected
that the time would come, some day, when he would have to rise
and go.
311
NOTES AND COMMENTARY
The great question in theology in 1904, von Dobschutz wrote, was,
What is Christianity? "Harnack tells us that the answer can be found
only by historical research. Thus we come to ask what the beginnings
of our religion were. What was the primitive Christianity?" A host
of scholars have given the answer, keeping in mind Paul's admoni-
tion, "We can do nothing against the truth, but for the truth." His
motto, said Edmunds, in his fine study of Buddhist and Christian Gos-
pels Compared, "has been: buy the truth and sell it not."
It is not true, as is so commonly believed, that Christianity alone
routed paganism. "Human reason," Gibbon tells us, "had already ob-
tained an easy triumph over the folly of paganism"; and Robertson
Smith: "The final disintegration of antique religion in the count-
ries of Graeco-Italian civilization was the work first of the philosophers
and then of Christianity." It was not, says Loisy, "the gospel of Jesus
that conquered the pagan world, but a mystery of salvation based on
the death of Jesus conceived as redemptive." Shotwell has put it
better than any other: "There is no more momentous revolution in
the history of thought than this, in which the achievements of thinkers
and workers, of artists, philosophers, poets and statesmen, were given
up for the revelation of prophets and a gospel of worldly renuncia-
tion." "All things are yours," said Paul.
Christianity developed in and through myth. "All Christian tradi-
tions are myths," says Kalthoff. "Why," asks Cheyne, "should I fear
to recognize mythic forms of speech in the New Testament?" "Create
a belief in the theory," says Jastrow, "and the facts will create them-
selves." In almost every case, says Robertson Smith, speaking of re-
ligion in general, "the myth was derived from the ritual, and not the
ritual from the myth." Or as Frazer says: "all peoples have invented
myths to explain why they observed certain customs." Of Bauer
Schweitzer says, "he did suspect how strong was the influence upon
the formation of history of a dominant idea which moulds and shapes
it with a definite artistic purpose"-as the Logos concept from the
Greeks moulded Christianity. "How far," ask Jackson and Lake, “does
tradition create, rather than commemorate, in its description of great
personalities?" They give as an instance that of St. Francis as an
indication "of what is needed in a more complete collection of narra-
tives necessary to elucidate the problem of the way in which tradition
almost unconsciously alters facts in the process of its development"-
312
see Coulton's essay in Vol. II, Jackson and Lake (eds.), The Begin-
nings of Christianity, on Francis.
All men, says Henry Osborn Taylor, "except fools, have their irra-
tional side. Who does not believe what his reason shall labor in vain
to justify?” True. But we must remember with Baur that “There can
be no true objective criticism until a man stands more or less indiffer-
ent to the result, and frees himself as far as possible from all sub-
jective relations to the object of criticism." That many Christian
scholars have not been able to do. "The whole history of Jesus-only
a fanatic or a stupid knave would deny it," said Voltaire, “should be
examined in the light of reason." It is true, as Jevons says, that “The
time has happily gone by when the mere idea of comparing Christian-
ity with any other religion would have been rejected with horror
as treasonous and treacherous." "It is perhaps not quite so super-
fluous," says Emmet, "as might at first appear to emphasize the fact
that we can no longer approach such a subject as this [NT]on the old
basis of a belief in verbal inspiration. For neither those whose main
interest is the defense of the accuracy of the New Testament writings,
nor those whose ideal is that of the impartial historian, always realize
what is implied in the changed point of view. Both still show at
times the subconscious influence of presuppositions which in theory
have been long abandoned." As for Catholic scholars, Coulton says:
"There are certain historical points (e.g. the question whether St.
Peter was ever bishop of Rome) on which the orthodox Roman Cath-
olic has no right even to enquire; he must admit here that Cardinal
Manning was right in repudiating the appeal to history as 'both a
treason and a heresy'. And, on every question which concerns the
honour of European civilization during those centuries when his
Church dominated society, the orthodox Roman Catholic has neces-
sarily an even stronger bias than the Protestant ecclesiastic; since this
latter can afford to admit the grossest errors of his Church in the
past, so long as she is willing henceforth to correct them, whereas the
former claims to have been infallibly guided, on all essentials of faith
and morals, for nineteen centuries.”
But scholars who are not Catholics also make the most absurd state-
ments-as Klausner when he says: "Whatever of primitive Christian-
ity is not derivable from Phariseeism, may be sought for in Essenism."
Or W. K. L. Clarke: "Christianity and Judiasm are so intertwined in
their origins and destinies that neither can be understood without the
other": the origins of Judaism have nothing to do with Christianity.
Even a scholar of the eminence of Harnack could say: "Paul wrecked
313
the religion of Israel on the cross of Christ." Thirteen pages later he
says truly enough, "Such an injustice as that done by the Gentile
church to Judaism is almost unprecedented in the annals of history”
but then adds: “But viewed from a higher standpoint, the facts ac-
quire a different complexion. By their rejection of Jesus, the Jewish
people disowned their calling and dealt the death-blow to their own
existence." A statement so false calls to mind Tylor's: "Among the
reasons which retard the progress of religious history in the modern
world, one of the most conspicuous is this, that so many of its approved
historians demand from the study of mythology always weapons to
destroy their adversaries' structures, but never tools to clear and trim
their own."
In a study of Christian origins scholars must go to Jewish as well
as to non-Jewish sources. Abrahams says: "Jewish students of the OT
have gained much from the researches made by Christian scholars.
the Jew may be able to return the compliment, and help Chris-
tians to understand certain phases of the Gospels." But too many
Christian scholars have refused the help. Speaking of Oesterley's
translation of Pirke Aboth Herford says: "It is intended to help
Christian readers to a better understanding of the New Testament. A
most excellent object, for such information is badly needed by most
Christians who write about the NT." "I have often heard the wish
expressed," says Schechter, "that a history of the rise of Christianity
might be written by a Jew who could bring Rabbinic learning to bear
upon the subject." Yes, but when such a Jew as Klausner attempts a
history of Paul he comes up with most of the Christian prepossessions!
There have been too few studies like Chwolson's, which, says Schechter,
"attempt to treat what one may call the Halachic part of the NT with
the thoroughness and devotion usually bestowed only on doctrinal
points." And Danby says, "For a critical knowledge of the Jewish back-
ground of the Gospels the Christian can never wholly dispense with
Jewish scholarship." In preparing for this novel, as for the two which
next preceded it, I read many Jewish scholars and many ancient Jew-
ish writings.
In spite of the labors of literally hundreds, indeed, thousands of
scholars not all the problems of Christian origins have yet been settled;
but many of them have, and Jackson and Lake tell us that "It may,
indeed, be prophesied that the writers of the future will be less oc-
cupied with the collection of material, its analysis and synthesis, than
with the application of psychology to the problems." I have, I am
aware, made the protagonist a remarkable man for his age, giving to
314
him the high moral calm of a Seneca and the restless intellectual curi-
osity and almost the learning of a Plutarch. If the reader felt that I
went too far in this I must beg his indulgence.

AGE, the Christians have delighted in picturing the Roman world
as utterly degenerate. Ramsey: "It was rotton to the
heart. So said Paul; and so must everyone feel." Even a scholar as
great as Lea says: "The world into which Christianity was born was
hopelessly corrupt." Sekles: "all the vices of heathenism appear in al-
luring forms." Aust: "A degenerate age stands unintelligent before
the ruins of its faith...." Draper: "The social fabric was a festering
mass of rottenness aristocracy was demoniac . . . city was a hell.
No crime... left unperpetrated . . . even virgins were guilty of in-
conceivable immodesties." Charlesworth points out that Christian his-
torians have taken Tacitus at his word: one of his typical passages:
"Horrible and hideous sights were to be seen everywhere in the city...
blood and piles of corpses, side by side with harlots and the compeers of
harlots. There were all the debauchery and passion that obtain in a
dissolute peace, every crime that can be committed in the most savage
conquest There is much truth in the above judgments but as
Enslin says: "The picture of the moral bankruptcy of the gentile world
has been grossly overdrawn by Christian apologists." Dill sums it up:
"The truth is that society in every age presents the most startling moral
contrasts, and no single comprehensive description of its moral condi-
tion can ever be true. This has been too often forgotten by those who
have passed judgment on the moral state of Roman society Or
Cumont: "The laxity of morals . . . has been exaggerated but it was
real. Many unhealthy symptoms told of a profound moral anarchy
weighing a weakened and irresolute society." Many writers of the
time, says Cadoux, took a “very black view” of things. It is true, as
Oesterley says, that everywhere (except among Jews) "there was a
crumbling of the religious beliefs of peoples" but it is true as Halliday
says that "The general tone of the age is inspired by the Stoic ideal of
the brotherhood of men." It is true above all, as Wenley says, that
"The Roman Empire . . . has long been a favorite field of controversy
alike with the apologists and critics of Christianity. The former never
weary of reminding us that social corruption then attained unprece-
dented proportions; the latter are as insistent that in the chief writers
of the time . . . thoughts and aspirations may be found more exalted
than any recorded in early Christian literature."
·
Though as Krüger says, religious institutions "which had the pres-
tige of centuries behind them, fell to pieces and were obliged to assume
new forms," Uhlhorn reminds us that "no reproach could be more
unjust than to call the old world irreligious. On the contrary, Chris-
tians, to the heathen, must have seemed irreligious; and often enough
316
they were thus reproached!" "Not because the heathen were irreli-
gious," says Willoughby, "but because they were so incurably and
tenaciously religious, Christian propagandists actually made little
headway with them at first." For, said Tertullian, "even the brothels
and cook-shops and prisons have their gods." The Vestal Virgins, says
Tucker, “are in a large measure the ancient prototype of the modern
nun, and their house is the prototype of the convent." Whether Nero
governed, or Aurelius, says Friedlander, the paramount question for
all men and women "was whether the blue would win or the green."
(And so it was only recently with the Giants and the Dodgers). Com-
pared with the present it was a grossly superstitious age. Pliny the
Younger wrote Sura: "I am extremely desirous to know whether you
believe in the existence of ghosts." "Superstition," cried Plutarch,
"the worst of all terrors!"; but, says Case, though he “may fairly be
called the best educated man among the Greeks" he wrote: “It has
been mantained that no man in his senses ever saw a ghost.... But if
Dion or Brutus... could place so much faith in the appearce of specters
as to give an account of them to their friends, I see no reason why
we should depart from the opinion of the ancients." For the Christian,
says Workman, "the miraculous was so common that it ceased to be
miraculous. . . . Augustine . . . solemnly asserts that in his own diocese
of Hippo, in the space of two years there had occurred no less than
72 miracles, among them five cases of restoration of life." "Business
with the supernatural," says Case, "was conducted on a large scale
and with great energy." Glover: "The flight of birds, the entrails of
beasts,rain, thunder, lightning, dreams, everything was a means of di-
vination.” As for Roman cruelty, Tucker says: "the Romans were in
fact similar to those modern spectators who love to see a man plunge
from lofty trapeze into a narrow tank, with a reasonable chance of
breaking his neck." "Executions accompanied by torture,” says Fried-
lander, "have at all times been attractive shows." In the human soul,
says Stekel, "cruelty crouches like a beast, chained, but eager to spring.'
Every nation, said Paldamus, “must pay for despising common human-
ity and natural feeling; and the Romans paid more dearly than any
other"-until the 5th decade of the 20th century! So great was the
demand for slaughter of men by men or by beasts that some of the
emperors restricted the number exposed; but even so, Trajan had
10,000 men fight for 123 days and under Titus 5,000 beasts were killed
in one day in the arena. Augustine said later that these bloody shows
had a fatal fascination for many Christians. Dramas in the theater,
317
says Teuffel, "were in general of an obscene character, especially seduc-
tion, scenes of adultery, cheating of husbands or fathers . . ." Phallic
amulets, says Kiefer, "are in almost every museum of antiquities
throughout Europe" and modern man “does no justice to the deep
original meaning of the symbol." To men of that time, says Block,
"seeking greater friction-titillation it was finally all one whether this
was obtained by coitus analis cum puella or cum puero . . . the latter
form was practiced on an appalling scale by the ancient Greeks and
Romans." But Goethe is only one modern who has written, "When
I've enough of the girl, she'll play the boy for me, too"; and we have
the Kinsey reports. Any age must be judged by the position of its
women. Though the position of women was high under the Romans,
Cato said: "If you were to catch your wife in an act of infidelity, you
would kill her with impunity without a trial; but if she were to catch
you, she would not venture to touch you with a finger, and indeed she
has no right."
AKIBA Finkelstein ranks him with Moses, Isaiah, Maimonides, Spin-
oza-"dominates the whole scene of Jewish history for 18
centuries.... the most revered, as well as the most beloved, of talmudic
sages."
ALLEGORY Farrer: "Aristobulus and Philo frankly surrendered
...
...
the literal truth of Genesis, and rationalized the Scrip-
ture stories of Jehovah just as the Greeks had rationlized the Homeric
tales of Zeus." Christians did likewise, as Schmidt says, to save the OT
"in its conflict with Greek thought. a method that allows the in-
terpretor to read into the Bible the theistic speculations, the psychol-
ogy and ethics of Greek philosophy... .Ultimately it is no longer the
thought of the Biblical writers that is to him authoritative, but the
thought that he himself . . . has imported into the text." Many Chris-
tian leaders argued "that there is a vast difference between the con-
ception of God in the Mosaic legislation and that presented by Jesus."
Without allegorizing, says Harnack, "a great deal of the OT would
have been unacceptable to Christians." In allegorizing, says Enslin,
Christians found a "very valuable means of discovering traces of Christ
in the OT.... No serious student pays any heed to it today." Moody:
"With the weapon of allegorical exegesis in her hands, the Church
was able to defend the OT, and to read into it whatsover she judged
to be 'spiritual' truth." The Church, says Taylor, "fixed special sym-
bolic meanings upon the OT narratives, so as to make them into pre-
figurative testimonies of the truth of Christian teaching." It was with
318
such uncritical methods, says G. F. Moore, "that Christians from the
beginning found the distinctive doctrines of Christianity expressed or
implied in all parts of the OT" and modern scholarship, which cor-
rects such arbitrary interpretation, "orthodoxy, whether Jewish or
Christian, has resisted with the instinct of self-preservation.” Abba Sil-
ver: "The major effort of most of the NT writers . . . was to 'Christian-
ize' the Bible, and the Rabbis were contantly engaged in opposing their
views." That is, Christians allegorized everything they found offensive.
The author of the Clementine Homilies wrote: "Far be it from us to
believe that the Master of the universe, the Maker of heaven and
earth, 'tempts' men as though he did not know-for who then does
foreknow? and if he 'repents', who is perfect in thought and firm in
judgment?" And Origen asked: "What man of sense will suppose
that the first and the second and the third day, and the evening and
the morning, existed without a sun and moon and stars? Who is so
foolish as to believe that God, like a husbandman, planted a garden
in Eden?" Hatch quotes and then comments: "whereas Philo was
mainly concerned to show that the writings of Moses contained Greek
philosophy, the Christian writers endeavored to show that the writings
of the Hebrew preachers contained Christianity. . . . [the OT's] anth-
ropomorphisms, its improbabilities, the sanction which it seemed to
give to immoralities, the dark picture which it sometime presented of
both God and the servants of God, seemed to many men to be irre-
concilable with both the theology and ethics of the Gospel.... the
allegorical method. . . largely helped to prevent the OT from being
discarded." Celsus in his famous attack on Christianity said the ex-
planations were even less plausible than the matters explained; and
Origen's equally famous reply, says Hatch, "is weak: it is partly a
Tu quoque
Christian allegorizing led to the most fantastic
things: Dembitz says of the Epistle to the Hebrews, for instance, that
its author "not only misconstrues every verse, but also misreads several
words of the Scriptural text." But Jewish efforts to correct Christian
"exegesis" were in vain: Christians went ahead, as Halliday puts it, to
lay a "premium on the fantastic, or even the irrational." Christians
never understood, as M. Joseph points out, that "Some of the biblical
stories are clearly legends."
""
ALEXANDER I have placed this incredible impostor a little before
his time: his cult, says Dill, almost conquered the
Roman world, and it was Lucian who at the risk of his life exposed
him.
319
ANTINOMIANISM Farrer: "Antinomianism, or the doctrine that
a state of spiritual perfection exempted men
from the obligations of the moral law, and that it was lawful to sin
that grace might abound, undoubtedly produced in the early church
the same reveling in sin that the same doctrine produced afterwards
among the Anabaptists." Tertullian said: "As for your saying that
we are a most shameful set, steeped in luxury, avarice, and depravity,
we will not deny it is true of some, but it cannot be said of all, or even
of a majority of us." The Notes of my next novel will deal with this at
greater length.
ANTIOCH Derwacter: "Antioch was the place . . . where Jews and
Gentiles lived much of the time in mutually irritating
contact. The Christians were caught between the two." Though
Breasted says no ancient streets were lighted, Bouchier says of Antioch:
"At night all streets were brilliantly illuminated." Tucker says the
Street of the Colonnades "is said to have been lighted at nights." Ency.
Biblica: "In no city was pleasure more earnestly pursued." It was in
Antioch that Christians were first called by that name.
APOSTLES Enslin: "That he formally called twelve men and then
conferred upon them especial powers is perhaps doubt-
ful..." Jackson and Lake point out that the "scenes and labors" of
the Twelve were unknown to the Church historian Eusebius 4 cen-
turies later. Streeter: "Thus most of the Twelve are mere names; and
even the list of names varied with the tradition current in different
localities." If there were twelve, says Loisy, Jesus did not choose them:
"They were the committee to which the first community of believers
entrusted the management of their affairs" and the number twelve
"seems to betray a symbolic intention."
AZAZEL
(See Frazer's statement on title-page). Loisy: "the sin of
the guilty human race is done away with in the body of the
Man-Christ, who was himself untouched by it. Childish dreams worked
up into a theological nightmare and adapted, by hook or crook, to a
lofty moral conception!" Idelsohn: "During the time of the Temple,
on every Yom Kippur, a scapegoat (Azazel) was sent from the Temple
into the desert, to carry away the sins of the people (Leviticus xvi,22).”
Hooke: "The ritual by which the sins of the community are trans-
ferred to the 'scape-goat,' which is then driven out into the desert
for Azazel, bears the closest resemblance to the common Babylonian
puhu, or substitution ritual." Buchanan Gray: "the goat which re-
320
ceives upon itself and carries away the sins of Israel is called the 'goat
for Azazel'..." Toy: "The demon-figure in the OT which is most
clearly defined and which made the most serious effort to maintain
itself in the national thought is Azazel.” A R S Kennedy: "these sins
were conceived as not only symbolically but actually transferred to the
head of the goat." Oesterly and Robinson: "Azazel was originally a
god of the flocks-just as Astarte was a cow-deity . . ." Speaking of
customs in Borneo, India and elsewhere, Cheyne says: “The natives
of these regions firmly believe that the demons who bring disasters
and other calamities can be driven away by means of loud cries ad-
dressed to some quasi-sacrifiical animal such as a goat..." Reminding
us of the two men and two women buried alive in the Forum, in or-
der, says Plutarch, to appease demons, Case adds: "In a later day there
were Christians who could talk in a similar way about the sacrificial
death of Jesus as a price paid to the devil." Dujardin: “the epistles
of St. Paul . . . [teach] that Jesus was loaded with the sins of the world,
which then died with him on the cross. . . . the god is put to death
in order that he may return to life again regenerated." Reinach:
"Ever since St. Paul, the ruling idea of Christianity has beeen that of
the redemption of man, guilty of a prehistoric fault, by the voluntary
sacrifice of a superman." Gray: "The general principle of transferring
sins physically to some animal or other medium, and, by then dismiss-
ing the medium, getting rid for good of the sins, is now recognized as
a widespread practice associated with a stage of belief far nearer the
primitive than the religion either of Ezra or of Moses." Robertson:
"The goat of the Hebrew ritual-mystery, which has perplexed so
many commentators, is really a myth duplicate of the other ritual-
mystery of the red heifer, which in the Egyptian mythology stood for
Typhon, the Evil One." G. Stanley Hall: "The Jesus of Paul, who was
mainly a divine sacrifice for us. . . . The folk-soul has always sought
to deceive God and evade the claims of justice by many a fictive chab-
lone sacrifice instead of a genuine one." How Christian scholars resist
the obvious origin of the sacrificed-god idea!
BAPTISM G. F. Moore: "The origin of the requirement of baptism
is not known." Lake: "Obscure. Jesus neither prac-
ticed nor enjoined baptism of any kind." J. E. Carpenter: "The pre-
cise origin of Christian baptism is obscure." Latourette: "Just how
or why baptism came to be the method of admission to the Christian
community we do not know." E. F. Scott: "It may be accepted as
certain that the rite of baptism was not instituted by Jesus." Goguel:
321
·
...
"Tradition has so little belief that baptism goes back to Jesus, that the
4h Gospel after quoting a statement that Jesus had baptized, itself
corrects this (John iv, 2)." Schmiedel: "Baptism and the repetition
of the Last Supper were no ordinances of Jesus." Hatch: "Baptism
and . . . the Lord's Supper. are found outside Christianity."
Many scholars, including Pfleiderer, Anrich, H. Holtzmann, Harnack,
Gunkel, Wrede think Paul was the first to interpret baptism as a sac-
rament. Many Christians objected to baptism saying the Twelve were
not baptized; to which Tertullian replied later that they were sprinkled
when the spray dashed over the boat, and Peter was immersed when
he tried to walk on the waves. Baptizing in the 'Name' goes back, in
the belief of such as Deissmann and Heitmuller, to the ancient belief
in magical ideas associated with the name of a person. Whatever the
origin, says Gilbert, "Baptism in any form has no basis in the teaching
of Jesus." Loisy: "Baptism is presented as an 'illumination' of which
Jesus, needless to say, is the sun. . . baptism was not instituted by
Jesus." Justin spoke of baptism as an illumination; Barnabas identi-
fied the water with the cross; later, Christians spoke of the 'new creating
wave', 'the union with immortality'; and Clement of Alexandria said,
"Being baptised we are illuminated; being illuminated we become
sons; being made sons, we are made perfect; being made perfect we are
made immortal." The Didache (which see) said: “If thou hast not
living water, baptize into other water; and if thou canst not in cold,
then in warm. But if thou hast neither, pour water thrice upon the
head into the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy
Ghost." Walker: "As to the method of baptism, it is probable that
the original form was by immersion, complete or partial." Stanley:
"Baptism was not only a bath, but a plunge-an entire submersion in
the deep water, a leap as into the rolling sea or the rushing river.”
Reinach: "Baptism was originally a simulacrum of drowning." Pflei-
derer: "baptism was a symbol of that fundamental of Christian ethics,
'Die and become!' . . ." Farrer: "Baptism preceded initiation into
the mysteries of both Isis and Mithras. . . . Even infant baptism was
an ordinary Roman rite." Harnacak: "It was as a mystery that the
Gentile church took baptism from the very first. . . . It was a mys-
terium salutare, a saving mystery; but it was also a mysterium tremen-
dum, an awful mystery, for the church had no second means of grace
like baptism." Dujardin: "Baptism itself is a rite of initiation. .
The novice must tread the path of the god; as the god dies and is re-
born, the novice dies to his old life and is born again to the new. St.
Paul ennobled the idea." Hunkin: "The plunge beneath the water
322
suggests death-death to the old pre-Christian manner of living.'
Moxom: "Seems to have been intimately associated with the idea of
regeneration." Moody: "It was understood that in baptism sins were
somehow washed away. But the meaning of the washing was never
clear." Lippert: "the Church associates baptism with exorcism and
emphasizes the 'expulsison of demons'." McGiffert: "Whether in-
fants were baptized in the apostolic age, we have no means of deter-
mining.” In primitive Christianity, says C. H. Moore, it was a ritual
act of purification but by the "second century Christianity had be-
come a mystery in the Greek sense" and then attached to it was the
notion of immortality. Dieterich: "One must keep clearly in mind
that the primitive thinking of man does not know the idea of develop-
ment but conceives of the natural processes of change (like puberty)
as religious transformations, and a 'conversion' of any kind as a defi-
nite moment of change in man, and thinks of it all as the coming into
existence of a new man." That says clearly what so many seem to have
missed.

CELSUS His famous and brilliant objections to certain features of
Christianity have perished except the parts that survived
in Origen's famous reply. Christian writers have traduced him for
centuries but Glover says: "He was above all a man of culture-can-
did, scholarly, and cool."
CHRISTIANS Canfield: "The supporters of Christianity were from
the lower classes." La Piana: "The earliest Chris-
tians were for the most part of humble and probably of servile des-
cent." Jackson and Lake: “The right distinction between words, and
the correct use of language, is the product of technical education, not
of religion, and the Christian writers show no signs of having had this
education." Christian intolerance. Moxom: "Christians
from the first intolerant of religions other than their own." Walker:
"Christians looked upon themselves as a separated people, a new race,
the true Israel, whose citizenship was no longer in the Roman em-
pire..." Reville: "The Christians were 'the saints'; all other men
were
